# Reconciliation queue

Generated by `scripts/merge.py` from disputes whose reconciliation status is `queued`, plus items added by the
reconciliation passes. Status is always **not yet reconciled** — never "contradiction". Each item lists the
sides and candidate readings. Verification level of the underlying entries is shown.

_Last generated: 2026-09-29 22:47 IST. Items: 425._

## RQ-U38-10 — Are the Abhidharma treatises the word of the Buddha?  
`dsp:abhidharma-as-buddhavacana` · skeleton

- **lin:vaibhasika**: Yes: the Buddha taught the Abhidharma scattered through his discourses; Kātyāyanīputra and others gathered it, as Dharmatrāta gathered the Udāna verses.
- **lin:sautrantika**: No: the treatises have known human authors; the 'Abhidharma basket' consists of particular sūtras such as the Arthaviniścaya.
- Candidate readings:
  - P5-neyartha: both agree the characteristics of dharmas were taught by the Buddha; the disagreement concerns the authority of the compiled treatises.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: whether the Abhidharma treatises are the word of the Buddha.

## RQ-U44-acceptance-of-kalacakra — Was the newly arrived Kālacakra to be accepted as authoritative, and the other tantras read in its light?  
`dsp:acceptance-of-the-kalacakra` · skeleton

- **lin:kalacakra**: Yes: the Kālacakra is the Ādibuddha's definitive teaching; the 'bodhisattva commentaries' read the Hevajra and Cakrasaṃvara through it.
- **lin:vajrayana**: Some Indian paṇḍitas are reported to have resisted the new tantra when Kālacakrapāda introduced it (Tibetan histories; low confidence).
- Candidate readings:
  - P5-neyartha: the Kālacakra as definitive, other tantras as provisional (the Kālacakra's own claim)
  - P3-path: parallel systems for different practitioners
- Note: Not yet reconciled; the historical debate is known only through later reports.

## RQ-003 — Is knowledge of the pure self by the ultimate standpoint the whole path, so that outward rites, worship and monastic authority are not needed? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:adhyatma-niscaya-and-ritual` · skeleton

- **lin:adhyatma-jain**: The soul's own pure nature, known by the ultimate standpoint and self-experience, is the path; ritual formalism, flower offerings and the authority of bhaṭṭārakas and monks are not the path. (In his early phase Banārsīdās abandoned conduct altogether; his mature view holds both standpoints with the guṇasthānas.)
- **lin:svetambara**: The pure self is the goal, but conduct, image worship, the monastic order and scripture are its indispensable means; the Adhyātmīs err in rejecting them (and in Digambara doctrines such as the kevalin's not eating).
- **lin:digambara**: Both one-sided niścaya and one-sided vyavahāra are wrong views; the disciple who understands both becomes impartial and gains the full fruit of the teaching.
- Note: Within the tradition the two standpoints are held together: niścaya names the real goal, vyavahāra the means proper to each guṇasthāna (PSU 8; Samayasāra Nāṭaka).

## RQ-U50-05 — Is Advaita Vedānta — the doctrine of māyā — 'Buddhism in disguise' (pracchanna-bauddha)?  
`dsp:advaita-crypto-buddhism` · skeleton

- **lin:bhedabheda**: Bhāskara: the doctrine of māyā — disputed, cut off from its root and 'sung by the Mahāyāna Buddhists' — leads people astray.
- **lin:dvaita**: Madhva and Jayatīrtha: the māyā-teacher and the Mādhyamika are both to be ignored — what is the void for the voidist is Brahman for the māyā-teacher; both ultimates are attributeless and defined only by exclusion; the voidists' saṃvṛti is the māyā-teachers' māyā, alike beginningless, indeterminable, concealing and projecting. Liberation cannot come from knowing the void.
- **lin:puranic**: The Padma Purāṇa (Uttarakhaṇḍa), in Śiva's list of heretical teachings: 'māyāvāda is a false teaching, called Buddhism in disguise; I taught it in the Kali age in the form of a brāhmaṇa'.
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Jīva Gosvāmī: the doctrine that the world is false and the Lord's lordship imagined by ignorance is the view the Gītā (16.8) calls godless; it conflicts with the Lord's word and is rightly called māyāvāda, which the Padma Purāṇa calls Buddhism in disguise. Caitanya (as the Gauḍīya texts present him) rejects Śaṅkara's commentary as hiding the sūtras' direct sense.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: Vedānta Deśika opens the Śatadūṣaṇī urging followers of Rāmānuja's path to 'strive on every side for victory over the crypto-Buddhists (pracchanna-bauddha)'.
- **lin:samkhya**: Vijñānabhikṣu: the māyā doctrine of the 'modern' Vedāntins is not the Brahmasūtra's teaching; with the Padma Purāṇa he counts it among heterodox (crypto-Buddhist) teachings.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Advaita is not Buddhism: Śaṅkara refutes all three Buddhist doctrines — the realist, the idealist and the void — and dismisses the void as opposed by every means of knowledge; against the idealist he upholds external objects and the undeniable, self-established witness; memory proves one enduring knower. The non-dual reality free of knower, known and knowing 'was not spoken by the Buddha': the Buddhist denial of external objects is only near to non-duality, which is to be known from the Upaniṣads alone. Brahman is being (sat) and consciousness, the self that no one can deny — not a void and not a momentary cognition.
- Candidate readings:
  - P8-six-marks: read by opening and close, repetition, novelty, fruit, praise and reasoning, the purport of Śaṅkara's commentaries is the Upaniṣadic Brahman as existent (sat), which the Buddhist texts deny — Advaita's own claim; the critics apply the same marks to find māyā/void as the purport.
  - P1-level: both systems use a two-truths frame; the charge concerns what is placed at the ultimate — the critics treat 'attributeless' as equivalent to 'void', Advaita and the Buddhists both reject that equivalence.
  - P6-upaya (the Padma Purāṇa's own framing): māyāvāda as a teaching given at the Lord's command to delude the asuras — a Purāṇic account the Advaitins do not accept as describing their teaching.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the critics hold that Advaita's attributeless Brahman and māyā equal the Buddhists' void and saṃvṛti; Advaita holds that Brahman as being-consciousness and the undeniable witness are exactly what the Buddhists deny.

## RQ-005 — To which tradition does the Tirukkuṟaḷ belong — Jain, Śaiva, Vaiṣṇava, Buddhist, or none?  
`dsp:affiliation-of-tirukkural` · skeleton

- **lin:jainism**: The Kuṟaḷ is a Jain work, its author identified by Jain tradition with Elācārya (Kundakunda).
- **lin:tamil-saiva-siddhanta**: The Kuṟaḷ is Śaiva: Tiruvaḷḷuvar is honoured as a Nāyaṉār; its God of eight qualities is Śiva with the eight attributes of the Āgamas; its 'two deeds' and 'feet of the Lord' are Śaiva teaching; Śaiva authors (Umāpati's Tiruvaruṭpayaṉ) follow its form.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: The Kuṟaḷ belongs to the Vedic-Vaiṣṇava world: it alludes to the one who measured the worlds with his feet (610) and the world of the lotus-eyed one (1103); its three books follow dharma, artha and kāma, as the Vaiṣṇava commentator Parimēlaḻakar reads it.
- **lin:mahayana**: A Buddhist reading (notably by the Tamil Buddhist revivalist Iyothee Thass, recent) takes the Lord of the first chapter as the Buddha, walker on lotuses and turner of the dharma-wheel, and the ethics as Buddhist.
- **lin:tamil-saiva-siddhanta**: Non-sectarian reading (much modern Tamil scholarship): the Kuṟaḷ names no sectarian god and is 'the common scripture' (potumaṟai) for all; each tradition reads its own deity into epithets the author chose to leave open.
- Candidate readings:
  - Jain author (Zvelebil and others lean this way on internal evidence)
  - Śaiva author
  - Vaiṣṇava/Brahmanical milieu
  - Buddhist (modern claim)
  - an author of the shared Tamil ethical milieu drawing on Jain and Brahmanical ideas, deliberately non-sectarian
- Note: Not yet reconciled: authorship is a historical question no principle settles; the ethical teachings are shared by all claimants.

## RQ-006 — Are the Āgamas and tantras authoritative — equal to, above, or outside the Veda?  
`dsp:agama-veda-authority` · skeleton

- **lin:mimamsa**: Only the authorless Veda is an independent authority; the dharma-teachings accepted by the Sāṃkhya, Yoga, Pāñcarātra, Pāśupata, Buddhists and Jains are not grounded in the Veda and are set aside.
- **lin:mantramarga**: Śiva's scripture is the higher knowledge revealing the Lord; the Veda is the lower knowledge revealing the bound soul and its bonds; they differ by the eligibility of their hearers; Vedic religion is a lower stream than the Atimārga and Mantramārga.
- **lin:pancaratra**: The Pāñcarātra is valid because the omniscient Lord spoke it; it is Vedic in root (the Ekāyana branch, per Vedānta Deśika); condemnations of 'tantras' concern the Śaiva and other schools, not it.
- **lin:vaikhanasa**: Vaikhānasa temple worship is itself Vedic: every rite follows the śruti and is performed with Vedic mantras by brahmins of the first of the sūtras.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Where an Āgamic system contradicts scripture (e.g. the origination of the soul in the Pāñcarātra, the Lord as mere efficient cause in the Pāśupata/Māheśvara systems) it is rejected; its devotional worship may be accepted where it does not contradict.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: the Kāmika itself grades Veda and Āgama as lower and higher knowledge for different adhikārins, which could frame the relation as one of eligibility rather than of truth.
  - P2-standpoint: Veda as general and Āgama as special teaching (a formula of Tamil Śaiva Siddhānta, to be sourced by U18).
  - Partial acceptance: Mīmāṃsā and Advaita accept Āgamic teaching only where it does not conflict with the Veda.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the sides disagree on what makes a scripture authoritative.

## RQ-007 — What appears in perceptual error ('this is silver' seen in shell)?  
`dsp:akhyati-or-viparitakhyati` · skeleton

- **lin:prabhakara-mimamsa**: Nothing unreal appears: there are two cognitions, a perception of 'this' and a memory of silver that has lost its 'that', and their difference is not grasped (akhyāti); every cognition as such is valid.
- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: Error presents a thing as other than it is: the shell and the silver are real, only the relation shown is unreal (viparītakhyāti); non-apprehension cannot explain the double moon or a mistaken direction.
- **lin:nyaya**: Error apprehends the object as possessing a character it lacks (anyathākhyāti), the silver being known through a special contact.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: both Mīmāṃsā schools agree that the elements of error are real and differ on whether a single false relational cognition occurs; each rejects the other's analysis.
- Note: Not yet reconciled; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-008 — Is the perfected, indestructible body the goal of yoga, or is it surpassed by the body dissolved in the liṅga (the void)?  
`dsp:allama-goraksa` · skeleton

- **lin:natha**: U21 contribution (the Nāth side in its own texts): the yogī's body, filled with the moon's nectar and made adamantine, does not decay or die; the great siddhas 'broke the staff of Time and roam the universe'; yet the same texts place this within samādhi, the samarasa of breath and mind, and non-duality in the supreme state.
- **lin:virasaiva**: The void body of the one dissolved in the liṅga is higher: the sword that rings off Gorakṣa passes through Allama; powers are not the goal (as in U20's record).
- **lin:natha**: Through yoga the body is made adamantine and deathless (as Gorakṣa's position is portrayed in Vīraśaiva texts; the Nāth tradition's own statement to be supplied from its texts by U21).
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: the perfected body as a stage and the dissolution of body-consciousness (samarasa / liṅgaikya) as the goal — the Nāth texts themselves place samādhi above the powers (GŚ 94–100; Akulavīra B.84), so the Nāth side might accept this reading of its own ladder, though not the Vīraśaiva narrative of defeat.
  - P2-standpoint: kāya-siddhi spoken from the standpoint of the body (mode), dissolution in the void from the standpoint of the ultimate (substance).
  - P4-stage: the perfected body as a stage below dissolution in the liṅga (the Vīraśaiva reading, which the Nāth tradition would reject).
  - The two traditions define the goal differently (kāya-siddhi vs liṅgaikya).
- Note: not yet reconciled; the Nāth texts join body-perfection to samādhi, while the Vīraśaiva account sets them against each other

## RQ-009 — Do meritorious public works — building tanks and temples for the god — lead to Śiva, or only work offered as worship with the liṅga and knowledge? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:allama-siddharama-works` · skeleton

- **lin:virasaiva**: Siddharāma (before his initiation): works of public benefit done for Mallikārjuna are the path.
- **lin:sarana-vacana**: Allama: such works bind; the liṅga must be found within; Siddharāma receives liṅga-initiation.
- Note: The tradition's own narrative presents Siddharāma's works as a stage superseded by initiation and knowledge, while work itself is kept as kāyaka offered to the liṅga.

## RQ-010 — Do the effortful methods of haṭha (breath-retention, mudrās, mantra, meditation on objects) bring the mindless state, or does it arise only effortlessly by the guru's grace? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:amanaska-critique-of-hatha` · skeleton

- **lin:hatha-yoga**: (Amanaska) The laborious methods do not bring the mindless state and may cause pain and disease; it arises of itself through the guru's grace and an objectless gaze.
- **lin:hatha-yoga**: (Haṭhapradīpikā) Kevala retention brings rājayoga; the great śakti awakened by postures, retentions and mudrās dissolves breath in the void, and then the natural state arises of itself; kuṇḍalinī wakes 'by the guru's grace'.
- Note: Both agree the state is effortless and spontaneous (sahaja); they differ on whether preparatory effort is needed — a question of the student's stage.

## RQ-011 — Is Amitābha a reward-body buddha in a reward land, or a transformation body in a land where ordinary beings and sages dwell together?  
`dsp:amitabha-land-reward-or-transformation` · skeleton

- **lin:mahayana**: (Jingying Huiyuan and other Sui scholars, as reported) Sukhāvatī is a land manifested for beings; the upper grades are sages of the stages; ordinary beings are born into a lower land.
- **lin:pure-land**: (Daochuo, Shandao) Amitābha is a reward buddha and Sukhāvatī a reward land; the old view is 'a great error'; ordinary beings enter by the vow's power; all nine grades are ordinary beings.
- **lin:tiantai**: (Tiantai four-land scheme, as later Pure Land writers use it) Sukhāvatī is chiefly the land where ordinary beings and sages dwell together, while also containing the higher lands.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: 'reward' from the Buddha's side (fruit of the vow), 'land shared by ordinary beings' from the beings' side
  - P4-stage: the land appears according to the capacity of those born (Tiantai four lands)
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the two sides classify the same land within different three-body/four-land schemes.

## RQ-012 — Is the sacrifice of animals meritorious?  
`dsp:animal-sacrifice` · skeleton

- **lin:vedic-srauta**: Animal sacrifice performed according to the Veda is meritorious and not killing in the forbidden sense.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: A sacrifice in which living beings are killed is not praised; King Mahāvijita's model sacrifice killed no animals; giving, refuge, precepts, going forth and knowledge are sacrifices of greater fruit.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage / P3-path: the Vedic tradition itself ranks the inner sacrifice of knowledge above material offerings (BhG 4.33; ChU 3.16) — close to DN 5's hierarchy of sacrifices
  - P7-arthavada: praise of animal sacrifice read as arthavāda by some later Hindu authors
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Vedic ritual prescribes what the suttas reject.

## RQ-013 — Is the killing of animals in Vedic sacrifice dharma?  
`dsp:animal-sacrifice-epic` · skeleton

- **lin:epic-teaching**: For animal sacrifice: in the dispute over the injunction 'one should sacrifice with aja', the gods held that aja means a goat (MBh 12.324.3); Syūmaraśmi, from within the cow, defends the Vedas' authority against Kapila (12.260).
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Against it: the seers held that aja means seed-grain; Vasu fell for siding with the gods (12.324); Kapila, Tulādhāra and Vicakhnu praise non-violent sacrifice and say slaughter was introduced by the greedy (12.253–257, 12.260); the gleaner's merit is destroyed by the thought of killing (12.264); the mongoose ranks the gleaner's gift above the horse sacrifice (14.92–93); the Nārāyaṇīya's worship is without slaughter.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: animal sacrifice for householders with desires, non-violent worship for renouncers and ekāntins (Kapila: the āśramas' duties have one aim, 12.260.12)
  - yuga reading: bloodless sacrifice belongs to the Kṛta age (12.336.58; Tulādhāra's 'as the good sacrificed of old')
  - P7-arthavāda: the praise of non-violent offerings as praise rather than prohibition
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the epic records both the authority of Vedic animal sacrifice and a strong teaching of bloodless sacrifice.

## RQ-014 — What does a word mean: a real universal, or the exclusion of others (apoha)?  
`dsp:apoha` · skeleton

- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Mature form (Śāntarakṣita, Ratnakīrti): the word-meaning is neither bare negation nor a real universal but a positive (conceptual) object qualified by the exclusion of others; exclusion is grounded in the particulars' capacity for similar effects.
- **lin:nyaya**: Exclusion cannot be the meaning: to exclude 'non-cow' one must already know 'cow'; the word 'cow' makes one understand a positive cow, not a negation (Uddyotakara, as recalled).
- **lin:mimamsa**: A real universal (ākṛti) — MS 1.3.33; Kumārila's apohavāda rejects exclusion: an exclusion presupposes a positive grasp of what is excluded.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Words signify by excluding what is other (anyāpoha); there are no real universals (Dignāga, Dharmakīrti).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: a positive class-meaning and an exclusion-meaning may be two descriptions of how words delimit; both sides deny it.
- Note: Not yet reconciled; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-015 — Is Madhva's interpretation of the Brahmasūtras (especially its opening adhikaraṇas) faithful to the sūtras and the Upaniṣads?  
`dsp:appayya-vijayindra-controversy` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Appayya Dīkṣita's Madhvatantramukhamardana argues that Madhva's reading of the opening adhikaraṇas departs from the sūtras and the Upaniṣads they summarize.
- **lin:dvaita**: Vijayīndra Tīrtha defends Madhva's readings point by point and counter-attacks the Advaita readings.
- Candidate readings:
  - {"principle": "P8-six-marks", "reading": "Decide the sūtras' purport by the six marks — accepted by both, applied differently."}
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U34-09 — Ārdraka's debates with rival ascetics (Sūtrakṛtāṅga 2.6)  
`dsp:ardraka-debates` · skeleton

- **lin:jainism**: Ārdraka: true non-harm requires knowledge and restraint in every act; intention alone does not excuse killing; feeding brāhmaṇas or killing one elephant a year does not free one from harm; the Jina's teaching of many to crowds is without attachment.
- **lin:ajivika**: Gośāla (as reported by the Jain text): Mahāvīra changed from solitary ascetic to teacher of crowds, which is inconsistent.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: Buddhist monks (as reported by the Jain text): the moral quality of an act lies in intention; an unintended killing is not sinful.
- **lin:vedic-srauta**: Brāhmaṇas (as reported by the Jain text): feeding two thousand snātakas brings heaven.
- **lin:sramana**: Elephant-killing hermits (hastitāpasa), as reported: killing one large animal a year harms fewer beings than daily gathering of food.
- Candidate readings:
  - The Buddhist position on intention is itself stated more carefully in Buddhist texts; compare with dsp:is-there-fruit-of-action and the Buddhist account of cetanā
- Note: Not yet reconciled; the opponents are known here only through the Jain text's report.

## RQ-017 — Do the Vedic mantras have meaning, or are they effective sound only? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:are-mantras-meaningful` · sourced

- **lin:vedanga**: Kautsa: the mantras are meaningless — their wording and order are fixed, their ritual use is fixed by the Brāhmaṇas, they state impossible or mutually inconsistent things, address insentient objects and contain unintelligible words; so the study of their meaning is useless.
- **lin:nairukta**: Yāska: the mantras are meaningful, because their words are the same as in ordinary speech; a rite is perfect when the recited verse fits the act; fixed order is found in ordinary speech too; if the blind do not see the post, the fault is theirs; one who recites without understanding is a load-bearing post.
- **lin:vedic-srauta**: Kautsa: 'the mantras are meaningless' — their words and order are fixed, their form prescribed by the Brāhmaṇa, their sense sometimes impossible or mutually opposed (vipratiṣiddha), and they address those who already know.
- **lin:vedic-srauta**: Yāska: the mantras are meaningful, for their words are the words of ordinary speech; fixed order and apparent opposition occur in ordinary speech too; obscurity is not the post's fault if the blind man cannot see it; one who recites without understanding is a post bearing a load.
- Note: Kautsa speaks from the standpoint of ritual application (fixed by the Brāhmaṇas), Yāska from that of meaning; both grant the fixed ritual use.

## RQ-018 — Are the sense and action capacities made of the elements, or products of ahaṃkāra?  
`dsp:are-the-senses-elemental` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: Products of ahaṃkāra (classical Sāṃkhya).
- **lin:nyaya**: Elemental: the senses come from the elements.
- **lin:samkhya**: Elemental, per the Sāṃkhya teacher Pañcādhikaraṇa (a dissenting Sāṃkhya view).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: constitution (Nyāya: what the organ is made of) vs function (Sāṃkhya: the organ as instrument of the I-sense).
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-019 — Are all things momentary?  
`dsp:are-things-momentary` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: No: recognition shows persistence; momentary things could not stand in causal relation.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Yes: whatever exists is momentary (the Buddhist pramāṇa school).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: substance vs mode (as in dsp:existence-of-pradhana).
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-020 — Can an arhat fall back from arhatship? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:arhat-retrogression` · skeleton

- **lin:sarvastivada**: Yes: of six kinds of arhat, five ('liberated by occasion') can fall back from the fruit; the unshakeable cannot; stream-enterers cannot fall back; one fallen does not die before regaining it.
- **lin:sammitiya**: Yes (Kathāvatthu commentary on Kv 1.2 names Sammitiyas, Vajjiputtakas, Sabbatthivādins and some Mahāsaṅghikas).
- **lin:sautrantika**: No: defilements abandoned by seeing do not return, and there is no falling back from arhatship either; what is lost is only the enjoyment of attainments.
- **lin:theravada**: No: the arahant does not fall away (Kv 1.2).
- **lin:mahasanghika**: Arhats cannot fall back; stream-enterers can (Vasumitra).
- Note: All sides agree that the unshakeable (akopya) arhat and the 'falling back from enjoyment' of attainments are not in question; the remaining disagreement is whether the fruit itself can be lost.

## RQ-021 — Are the four orders of life a free choice, a fixed sequence, or is only the householder's order valid? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:asrama-vikalpa-samuccaya-badha` · sourced

- **lin:dharmasastra**: Option (vikalpa): after completing studentship one may enter whichever order one wishes; each, lived by its rules, leads to safety.
- **lin:sannyasa**: Option by dispassion: one may renounce from studentship, household or forest — on the very day one becomes dispassionate.
- **lin:dharmasastra**: Sequence (samuccaya): student, householder, forest-dweller, renouncer in due order; the debts to seers, gods and ancestors must be paid before the mind is set on liberation.
- **lin:dharmasastra**: Sublation (bādha, aikāśramya): there is only one order, the householder's, because only it is directly enjoined in the Veda and the others do not beget offspring; the division of orders was made by the asura Kapila to rival the gods.
- Note: The later synthesis grades the orders by qualification: the sequence for most, early renunciation only for one whose dispassion is complete.

## RQ-022 — Is the astrologer to be honoured or excluded at a śrāddha? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:astrologer-at-sraddha` · skeleton

- **lin:dharmasastra**: Manu lists 'one who lives by the asterisms' among those to be kept away from a śrāddha.
- **lin:jyotisa**: Varāhamihira: the twice-born who knows this whole science in text and meaning should eat first at a śrāddha, honoured as a purifier of the row; the reader of it does not go to hell.
- Note: Manu excludes one who makes a living by the stars; Varāhamihira praises the brahmin learned in the whole science — his praise reads as arthavāda encouraging study, and the two refer to different persons (tradesman vs learned brahmin).

## RQ-023 — May monks and renunciants practise astrology or live by it? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:astrology-for-renunciants` · skeleton

- **lin:early-buddhism**: No: predicting eclipses, the courses of moon, sun and asterisms, meteors and earthquakes, fixing auspicious days and performing pacification rites are 'low arts' by which some ascetics live wrongly; the Buddha abstains, his follower must not practise astrology, and monks may not learn or teach such arts.
- **lin:jainism**: No: the monk is one who does not live by the sciences of portents, sky-signs, earth-signs, dreams, bodily marks, sounds and the like.
- **lin:dharmasastra**: The Vedic renouncer too must never seek alms by portents, omens, astrology or palmistry.
- **lin:jyotisa**: Jyotiṣa is the eye of the Veda and needed by all: even forest-dwelling renunciants without possessions consult the knower of the lights; the chart itself shows who will renounce and into which order; one who condemns the science without knowing it goes to hell.
- Note: The Buddhist, Jain and Manu rules forbid the renunciant to live by astrology; the jyotiṣa texts address householders, kings and the rite. The rule is one of adhikāra (the renunciant's stage), not a verdict on the science for all.

## RQ-024 — Is the Veda threefold (trayī), or is the Atharvaveda a Veda of equal (or higher) rank? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:atharvaveda-status` · sourced

- **lin:vedic-srauta**: The Veda proper of the sacrifice is threefold — ṛc, yajus, sāman — the Vedas of the hotṛ, adhvaryu and udgātṛ; 'trayī' is the standing name of Vedic knowledge.
- **lin:sakha-saunaka**: The Atharvaveda is the fourth Veda and, as the Brahmaveda, belongs to the brahmán priest who oversees and repairs the whole rite; it is listed fourth among the Vedas and received by Sumantu from Vyāsa.
- Note: From the standpoint of the śrauta liturgy the Veda is threefold (the three chanting and reciting priests); from the standpoint of the whole revealed corpus it is fourfold.

## RQ-025 — Who may be initiated into the Atimārga observances: brahmin men only, or anyone?  
`dsp:atimarga-initiation-eligibility` · skeleton

- **lin:pasupata**: Only brahmin men: the disciple is a tested brahmin; 'no brahmin returns' restricts the path to brahmins; the practitioner must not speak with women or śūdras.
- **lin:kapalika**: As reported by opponents: by entry into initiation one becomes a brahmin at once, and by the Kāpāla vow a man becomes an ascetic.
- **lin:svetambara**: Jain doxographers report that the Śaiva bharaṭas take the vow without regard to varṇa, and that even a slave who keeps the Śaiva initiation for twelve years attains nirvāṇa (report of the Naiyāyika-Śaivas' saying).
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage (adhikāra): each school defines qualification differently - which is itself the point at issue.
  - Different Śaiva orders at different periods may simply have differed in rule.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Pāśupata restricts eligibility by birth; other Śaiva groups (as reported) do not.

## RQ-026 — Is the Atimārga's liberation final, or a lower level beneath Śiva? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:atimarga-liberation-rank` · skeleton

- **lin:pasupata**: By this rule the brahmin reaches Rudra's proximity and does not return; the end of suffering by the Lord's grace is final, certain and absolute.
- **lin:mantramarga**: Pāśupatas reach the level of Īśvara (Aiśvara pada) and skull-observers the Dhruva; they are not re-created, yet all that the Atimārga attains is attained with ease in Śiva-knowledge, which is higher.
- Note: Both sides agree the Atimārga follower is not reborn; the Mantramārga's own graded scheme reads that goal as a high but not highest stage.

## RQ-U16-7 — Is the individual self atomic or all-pervading?  
`dsp:atomic-or-pervasive-self` · skeleton

- **lin:dvaitadvaita**: Atomic: the soul is minute, dwelling in the heart and pervading the body by its knowledge (Daśaślokī 1; BS 2.3.19ff read literally). Vallabha and the Gauḍīyas agree.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Advaita (and Nyāya, Sāṃkhya): the self is all-pervading; atomicity is said of the self as delimited by the intellect.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level (Advaita's own: atomic as conditioned by buddhi) — rejected by the Vaiṣṇava schools
- Note: not yet reconciled.

## RQ-028 — Is the material world composed of eternal partless atoms?  
`dsp:atomism` · skeleton

- **lin:vaisesika**: Yes: earth, water, fire and air consist of eternal, partless, spherical atoms which, moved by adṛṣṭa under the Lord's will, combine into dyads and larger wholes; division of wholes must end in them.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: No: unconscious atoms cannot begin to move (adṛṣṭa being unconscious), inherence leads to regress, and atoms with colour and other qualities would be non-eternal and gross (Śaṅkara on Brahma Sūtra 2.2.11–17, as usually cited).
- **lin:yogacara**: No: atoms joining on six sides would have six parts; if they do not join, aggregates would be no larger than an atom (Vasubandhu).
- **lin:jainism**: Atoms exist but are of one kind of matter (pudgala), taking the form of any element, and bond by their sticky and dry qualities.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: Advaita and Yogācāra could allow atomic analysis at the empirical level while denying it ultimacy; Vaiśeṣika rejects that frame.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U16-1 — Is the difference between Brahman and the soul due to adjuncts, natural, or inconceivable?  
`dsp:aupadhika-vs-svabhavika-bhedabheda` · skeleton

- **lin:bhedabheda**: Bhāskara: non-difference is natural, difference is due to real adjuncts (aupādhika); when the adjuncts fall away at death the soul is one with Brahman.
- **lin:bhedabheda**: Yādavaprakāśa: both are natural (svābhāvika); Brahman itself becomes Īśvara, souls and matter (as reported by Rāmānuja).
- **lin:dvaitadvaita**: Nimbārka: both difference and non-difference are natural and eternal, like snake and coils or sun and rays; not due to adjuncts.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: Rāmānuja: both forms are faulty — Bhāskara's makes Brahman itself suffer through its adjuncts, Yādava's makes Brahman itself undergo change and imperfection; the relation is that of soul and body (inseparable attribute).
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Jīva: the relation is inconceivable (acintya) — neither adjunct-made nor comprehensible by logic — because the Lord's powers are inconceivable.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: part–whole (difference) vs substance (non-difference) as complementary standpoints — rejected by Bhāskara, who makes difference conditional, and by Rāmānuja, who denies non-difference in substance
  - P1-level: difference at the conventional level, non-difference at the ultimate — this is Advaita's reading and all these schools reject it as making difference unreal
  - Gauḍīya 'acintya' as a meta-position: the tension is itself affirmed as beyond reason
- Note: not yet reconciled: each school's account of the cause of difference is a claim about what is real, and each rejects the others'.

## RQ-030 — Is suffering ended by burning off past kamma through austerity, and which action is most blameworthy?  
`dsp:austerity-and-past-kamma` · skeleton

- **lin:jainism**: All experience is due to past deeds; by austerity past kamma is worn away and by not doing new kamma nothing flows in; bodily action ('rod') is most blameworthy.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: Not everything felt is due to past deeds; self-mortification is an ignoble extreme which did not lead the bodhisatta to awakening; kamma is intention, so mental action is most blameworthy; striving is fruitful by abandoning craving, not by torment.
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path: both value restraint (the suttas call restraint 'tapa', Snp 2.4) and differ in method
  - P2-standpoint: the Jain analysis of karma as substance versus the Buddhist analysis of karma as intention
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the two traditions differ on the nature of karma (matter shed by tapas vs intention) and the value of austerity.

## RQ-U45-1 — Are the Old Tantras (rnying rgyud) authentic Indian scripture?  
`dsp:authenticity-of-nyingma-tantras` · skeleton

- **lin:kadam**: Critics of the later diffusion questioned tantras translated in the early period for which no Indian original was known, and condemned practices they held to be perversions of tantra.
- **lin:nyingma**: The Old Tantras were translated under imperial sponsorship by Indian masters and Tibetan translators; their Indian lineages are recorded; the Guhyagarbha has Indian commentaries and a Sanskrit manuscript was reported found at Samye; later editors placed an Old Tantra section in the Kangyur.
- Candidate readings:
  - Indian texts whose originals were lost in India (Nyingma).
  - Compositions made in Tibet within an Indian framework (critics).
  - Scripture validated by its lineage and fruit whatever its place of writing (a reading some Nyingma apologists also offer).
- Note: A question of history and canon for which no reconciliation principle applies; not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U45-2 — Are revealed treasures (gter ma) genuine teachings of Padmasambhava and the buddhas?  
`dsp:authenticity-of-terma` · skeleton

- **lin:sakya**: Sceptics (Sakya Paṇḍita among others) warned against texts 'dug from the earth' or received in dreams whose origin cannot be verified, and noted false revealers.
- **lin:nyingma**: Treasures are concealed by Padmasambhava, sealed in the revealer's mind by aspiration and entrusted to ḍākinīs; genuine revealers are prophesied and authenticated, and the tradition itself distinguishes true from false treasures.
- Candidate readings:
  - Authentic by the tradition's own tests (prophecy, lineage, fruit).
  - Valid as skilful means for their time whatever their mode of origin (P6-upaya reading; not accepted by critics).
  - Tibetan compositions (sceptics).
- Note: Turns on criteria of scriptural authority on which the parties differ; not yet reconciled.

## RQ-033 — Is the Abhidhamma the word of the Buddha?  
`dsp:authority-of-abhidhamma` · skeleton

- **lin:theravada**: Yes: the Buddha taught it in the Tāvatiṃsa heaven and gave the method to Sāriputta; the Kathāvatthu was expanded from the Buddha's outline; it is teaching in ultimate terms.
- **lin:sautrantika**: The sūtras are the authority; the Abhidharma treatises are compositions of disciples (as the Sautrāntikas are described in Abhidharma literature; U38).
- **lin:sarvastivada**: Their own seven Abhidharma books are the Buddha's teaching compiled by disciples (U38).
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: the Theravāda's own distinction between teaching in conventional terms (suttas) and in ultimate terms (Abhidhamma)
  - The tradition's account and the scholarly account (later compilation) are kept apart as metadata, not reconciled
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-034 — Are regional practices that the cultured disapprove of authoritative in their own regions?  
`dsp:authority-of-regional-custom` · sourced

- **lin:dharmasastra**: Yes, in their region: each of the five southern and five northern practices is followed where it is customary (BDh 1.1.2.1–6); marriage follows the customs of peoples and villages (ĀśGS 1.7.1).
- **lin:dharmasastra**: No: 'that is false', says Gautama; neither set is to be respected, since they conflict with the tradition of the cultured (BDh 1.1.2.7–8); customs are valid only when not contrary to the Veda (GDh 11.20).
- Candidate readings:
  - the rule of GDh 11.20 (custom valid unless contrary to the Veda) as the tradition's own resolution
  - regional validity as a matter of standpoint (P2)
  - strict rejection in favour of the śiṣṭa tradition
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-035 — Are the sixty-four (Kaula) tantras valid guides, or deceptive and non-Vedic?  
`dsp:authority-of-sixty-four-tantras` · skeleton

- **lin:srividya**: (Samaya view, Lakṣmīdhara) The sixty-four deceive even those who know them, aim at worldly powers, are far from the Veda and are for those outside Vedic eligibility; the Vedic Samaya way of the five auspicious āgamas is to be followed, the 'mixed' and Kaula ways abandoned.
- **lin:kaula**: (Kaula view, as quoted by Bhāskararāya) 'Kaula is higher than the Siddhānta; nothing is higher than Kaula'; the tantras of the Mothers are Śiva's own teaching, and those teaching Sundarī and other deities are rooted in Upaniṣads.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: Lakṣmīdhara himself assigns tantras to different classes of eligible persons.
  - P7-arthavada: 'beguiled' in SL 31 may be praise of the Goddess's tantra rather than a doctrinal condemnation of the others (a Kaula reading).
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U16-8 — Are the Śaiva (and Pāñcarātra) Āgamas equal in authority to the Veda?  
`dsp:authority-of-the-agamas` · skeleton

- **lin:siva-visistadvaita**: Śrīkaṇṭha: there is no difference between the Veda and the Śivāgama; both are Śiva's word.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Śaṅkara (BS 2.2.37–45): the Pāśupata doctrine of the Lord as mere efficient cause is refuted, and parts of the Pāñcarātra are rejected where they contradict the Veda.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: Yāmuna and Rāmānuja: the Pāñcarātra is fully Vedic and authoritative; the Pāśupata and other Śaiva systems are rejected.
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path: each Āgama authoritative for its own path of worship
  - P7-arthavada for praise of one's own Āgama
- Note: not yet reconciled.

## RQ-037 — What authority do the Purāṇas have beside the Veda? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:authority-of-the-puranas` · sourced

- **lin:puranic**: The Purāṇa is the fifth Veda, remembered by Brahmā before the Vedas, and the Bhāgavata is the essence of all Vedānta.
- **lin:mimamsa**: Smṛti, including Purāṇa, is authoritative only as far as it rests on (and does not contradict) the Veda; much of it is arthavāda.
- **lin:puranic**: The Purāṇa was composed for those to whom the Veda is not accessible - women, śūdras and fallen twice-born.
- Note: Under P4 the Purāṇa's authority is framed by adhikāra: it carries Vedic truth to those without access to the Veda (BhP 1.4.25).

## RQ-038 — Should a medical text be accepted because a sage composed it, and must the followers of Caraka and Suśruta exclude each other?  
`dsp:authority-of-the-samhitas` · skeleton

- **lin:ayurveda**: Partisans (as reported by Vāgbhaṭa) who prize only sage-composed texts, or only Caraka or only Suśruta.
- **lin:ayurveda**: Vāgbhaṭa: the power of a substance does not depend on who states it; drop jealousy and be impartial; if sagehood were the criterion, Bheḍa and the others would be read; accept what is well said.
- Candidate readings:
  - P7-arthavada: Utt 40.81 as praise of the text rather than a rule against examination
  - P2-standpoint: authority of tradition (āgama) and of results (pratyakṣa) as two grounds the verse itself names
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the same chapter also asks that the text be applied 'like a mantra, not to be questioned' (Utt 40.81).

## RQ-U14-08 — Is Advaita's avidyā — beginningless, indescribable, located in and concealing Brahman — tenable?  
`dsp:avidya-saptavidha-anupapatti` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Yes: avidyā is beginningless, neither real nor unreal (anirvacanīya), located in Brahman (Vivaraṇa) or the individual (Bhāmatī); it conceals and projects; it is known by experience ('I am ignorant') and by postulation, and is removed by the knowledge arising from the great sayings.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: No: it is untenable in seven ways — locus, concealment, nature, indescribability, proof, remover and removal; bondage is due to karma, which scripture calls avidyā.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: Advaita's avidyā is a vyāvahārika account; Viśiṣṭādvaita denies levels of reality
  - P2-standpoint: both locate bondage in the soul's ignorance of its relation to Brahman
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-040 — Should the questions about the eternity and extent of the world, soul and body, and the Tathāgata after death be answered? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:avyakata` · skeleton

- **lin:sramana**: The wanderers of other sects each hold one answer as 'this alone is true, all else is false' (the world is eternal; the soul is the body; the Tathāgata exists after death …), and quarrel over it.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: The Buddha sets the ten questions aside: they are not beneficial and do not lead to nibbāna; the Tathāgata has abandoned speculative views; being liberated from reckoning by the aggregates, 'reborn' and 'not reborn' do not apply; answering Vacchagotta 'there is a self' or 'there is no self' would side with eternalism or annihilationism.
- Note: Reconciled under P6 (the Buddha's silence fitted to the questioner, SN 44.10) and P1 (the questions presuppose a self or Tathāgata that is not found in truth, MN 72, SN 22.86); the rival sectarian positions themselves are not reconciled.

## RQ-U32-ayurdaya-methods — By which method is the length of life to be computed?  
`dsp:ayurdaya-methods` · skeleton

- **lin:yavana-jataka**: Maya, the Yavanas, Maṇittha and Śaktipūrva (Parāśara): each planet gives fixed years (19, 25, 15, 12, 15, 21, 20) when exalted, half when fallen, proportionately between.
- **lin:jyotisa**: Jīvaśarman: each planet gives an equal share of the maximum life.
- **lin:jyotisa**: Satya: reckon by the navāṃśas traversed; Varāhamihira calls Satya's teaching best, but warns against the many multiplications others add.
- Candidate readings:
  - Varāhamihira's own verdict for Satya (BJ 7.13).
  - Later horā assigns the methods to charts according to whether the ascendant, Sun or Moon is strongest (a P2/P4-type reconciliation; source not verified).
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-042 — Was being (sat) or non-being (asat) first, at the origin of the world? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:being-or-non-being-first` · sourced

- **lin:upanisadic**: Being alone was in the beginning, one without a second; being could not come from non-being (Uddālaka).
- **lin:upanisadic**: Non-being was in the beginning; from it being was born; it made itself (TU 2.7); non-being became being and an egg (ChU 3.19); one who knows brahman as non-being becomes non-being (TU 2.6).
- Candidate readings:
  - asat as the unmanifest state (Brahma Sūtra 2.1.17)
  - two cosmogonies preserved side by side
  - asat as beyond the being/non-being pair (cf. RV 10.129.1)
- Note: The Brahma Sūtra (2.1.17) reads 'non-being' in these passages as the unmanifest state of name and form, as shown by the passages' own continuation ('it made itself', TU 2.7), so that the purport of both is being.

## RQ-043 — Is the Bhāgavata's final purport non-dual knowledge or devotion?  
`dsp:bhagavata-purport-knowledge-or-devotion` · sourced

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Non-dual knowledge: the Bhāgavata's mark is the oneness of Brahman and self, its sole aim kaivalya (12.13.12); forms are superimposed (1.3.30-33); Śuka's last word is 'I am Brahman' (12.5.11); devotion purifies for knowledge.
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Devotion: bhakti is the supreme dharma (1.2.6); devotees refuse the five liberations, even oneness, without service (3.29.13) and desire not even freedom from rebirth (11.14.14); only bhakti wins the Lord (11.14.20); Kṛṣṇa is Bhagavān himself.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: the devotional passages speak at the conventional level, the non-dual ones at the ultimate (the Advaita reading; rejected by Vaiṣṇavas).
  - P4-stage: knowledge is a stage within devotion, or devotion within knowledge (each school's own subordination).
  - The text's own synthesis: BhP 1.2.11 names one non-dual reality as Brahman, Paramātman and Bhagavān, and 11.2.42 has devotion, experience and detachment arise together.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: each school reads the other's key verses as subordinate; P1 (level) and P4 (stage) are candidate principles but both traditions reject them.

## RQ-044 — Is devotion or knowledge the higher and more direct means, and is oneness with the Lord to be desired? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:bhakti-jnana-precedence` · sourced

- **lin:puranic**: Devotion alone wins the Lord; motiveless devotion is greater than liberation; pure devotees refuse even oneness without service.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Knowledge of the non-dual Self liberates; even the practice of samādhi is bondage from the absolute standpoint; the jñānī is dearest to the Lord.
- **lin:ramanandi**: Devotion is the lamp without which knowledge does not shine; from devotion knowledge, realization, dispassion and liberation follow quickly.
- Candidate readings:
  - by temperament (P3)
  - by stage, devotion maturing into knowledge (P4)
  - devotion as the end in itself (Vaiṣṇava)
  - knowledge as the end (Advaita)
- Note: The Uddhava Gītā itself assigns knowledge, action and devotion to different dispositions (11.20.6-8), and the Devī Gītā grades devotion upward into non-dual knowledge.

## RQ-045 — Is ignorance (avidyā) a positive entity (bhāva-rūpa) that is the material cause of the world-appearance, or is it superimposition/non-apprehension itself?  
`dsp:bhavarupa-avidya` · skeleton

- **lin:vivarana**: Positive: ignorance is a beginningless, indescribable, positive power, proved by perception ('I am ignorant'), inference and presumption; Padmapāda calls it an insentient power.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Maṇḍana: ignorance is non-apprehension and misapprehension residing in the jīvas. Swami Satchidanandendra (recent): for Śaṅkara avidyā is superimposition itself (BSBh intro), not a positive root-ignorance; the later doctrine departs from Śaṅkara.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level/P6-upaya: both are empirical-level explanations of the one error (traditional compendia).
  - Historical reading: Śaṅkara's usage is phenomenological (adhyāsa), the Vivaraṇa's is causal-explanatory; they answer different questions.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the question concerns what Śaṅkara taught and whether a positive ignorance is required; the prakriyā principle is invoked by traditionalists but rejected by the modern critic.

## RQ-U24-bhavas-in-kali-yuga — Are the vīra and divya dispositions (and the five tattvas) permitted in the Kali age?  
`dsp:bhavas-in-kali-yuga` · skeleton

- **lin:kalikula**: (Kālīvilāsa) No: the divya and vīra dispositions 'do not exist, do not exist' in the Kali age; all classes worship in the paśu disposition (4.2-3); divya belonged to Satya–Tretā, vīra to Tretā–Dvāpara (6.10-16).
- **lin:kalikula**: (Mahānirvāṇa) Yes, with restrictions: the five tattvas are proper to Śakti worship (5.22-24), the tantric path is the only one for this age (2.7-15), but in the Kali age the last tattva is only with one's own wife and householders use the sweet triad (6.14; 8.170-171).
- **lin:kaula**: (Kulārṇava) The Kaula is highest without any age restriction, though only for the tested and eligible (2.7-8; 2.30-36).
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: the Mahānirvāṇa's substitutes for householders show a graded permission; the Kālīvilāsa's rule may address ordinary worshippers of the four classes.
  - P2-standpoint: the Kālīvilāsa speaks from the standpoint of the age (cosmic), the Kulārṇava from that of the qualified individual.
  - Not reconcilable if the Kālīvilāsa's 'does not exist' is taken as a universal prohibition.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Kālīvilāsa denies outright what the Mahānirvāṇa and Kulārṇava allow under conditions.

## RQ-047 — On what terms were women ordained, and can the Theravāda bhikkhunī order be restored?  
`dsp:bhikkhuni-order` · skeleton

- **lin:theravada**: The canonical account: the Buddha, after three refusals, granted Mahāpajāpatī's going forth on condition of the eight principles of respect, affirming that women can realise all four fruits, and predicted a shorter duration of the true Dhamma; at the First Council Ānanda was charged with having urged it. (Traditionalist position, recent:) the Theravāda bhikkhunī lineage lapsed and cannot be revived, since ordination requires an existing bhikkhunī Saṅgha of the same lineage.
- **lin:theravada**: (Revivalist position, recent:) the order can be restored with the help of bhikkhunīs of a lineage held to descend from Saṅghamittā (the Dharmaguptaka lineage of East Asia), or by monks alone under the Buddha's allowance for monks to ordain nuns; the canon affirms women's capacity for arahantship and records a flourishing Bhikkhunī Saṅgha (Thig).
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: the garudhammas as contextual rules of the founding situation (revivalist reading)
  - Strict Vinaya reading: the procedural requirements are not open to reinterpretation (traditionalist reading)
- Note: Not yet reconciled: a living Vinaya question within Theravāda.

## RQ-U38-14 — Can the full ordination of nuns be introduced in the Mūlasarvāstivāda (Tibetan) tradition?  
`dsp:bhiksuni-ordination-revival` · skeleton

- **lin:mulasarvastivada**: Only by a procedure valid under the Mūlasarvāstivāda Vinaya, which requires a quorum of bhikṣuṇīs of the same lineage; such a lineage is absent in Tibet (conservative view).
- **lin:dharmaguptaka**: The Dharmaguptaka bhikṣuṇī lineage, living in East Asia, can supply the nuns' side of a dual ordination (reformist view).
- Candidate readings:
  - Vinaya reading: dual ordination with Dharmaguptaka bhikṣuṇīs and Mūlasarvāstivāda bhikṣus.
  - Vinaya reading: ordination by bhikṣus alone, as permitted at the order's beginning.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: whether and how full ordination of nuns can be conferred where the Mūlasarvāstivāda bhikṣuṇī lineage was never transmitted.

## RQ-049 — How should Digambara image worship be performed, and are the bhaṭṭārakas authorities? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:bisapantha-terapantha` · skeleton

- **lin:bisapantha**: Worship with fresh flowers, fruits, lamps and sweets, seated; the bhaṭṭārakas are legitimate heads; the attendant deities (Padmāvatī, Kṣetrapāla) may be honoured.
- **lin:terapantha-digambara**: Offer only non-living substances (rice, dry fruits), standing; reject the bhaṭṭārakas; worship of attendant deities is wrong faith.
- Note: Both worship the same Jina images and accept Kundakunda; the manner of offering is a difference of ritual path.

## RQ-050 — Does liberation contain bliss (and consciousness)?  
`dsp:bliss-in-liberation` · skeleton

- **lin:nyaya**: Mainstream Nyāya: liberation is absolute release from pain; there is no proof of an eternal pleasure manifested in it, and one who strives for liberation out of attachment to pleasure is not freed; the liberated self has neither pleasure nor cognition. Pleasure bound up with pain is to be relinquished like food mixed with honey and poison.
- **lin:vaisesika**: Liberation is the absence of contact with a body and of new appearance when adṛṣṭa is exhausted; a quiescence like fire whose fuel is burnt (no special qualities of the self remain).
- **lin:nyaya**: Bhāsarvajña (a minority within Nyāya): liberation is the absolute cessation of pain qualified by the experience of eternal pleasure.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Liberation is the realization of the self as Brahman, being-consciousness-bliss; a liberation without consciousness or bliss would be no better than a stone's state — a charge pressed against Nyāya (e.g. in Śrīharṣa's Naiṣadhīyacarita, as usually cited).
- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: Pārthasārathi: no — release has no cognition and no bliss; it is like dreamless sleep without return, a human goal because pain ceases; Kumārila: release is not an enjoyment of pleasure, which would be a perishable heaven.
- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: Some Bhāṭṭas (reported in the Śāstradīpikā): yes — the self's innate bliss, overpowered in saṃsāra, becomes manifest and is enjoyed through the mind in release; Kumārila's verse states another's view.
- **lin:prabhakara-mimamsa**: No — scripture does not make known accomplished things, and no supreme bliss is experienced by the embodied; release is valuable as the end of sorrow.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Yes — liberation is the realization of Brahman, whose nature is bliss.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint / terminological: Nyāya denies pleasure as an adventitious quality produced by contact with objects; Advaita affirms bliss as the self's nature, not a produced quality — the two may partly talk past each other.
  - P1-level: Advaita could read Nyāya's account as describing liberation negatively from the empirical standpoint.
  - The Śāstradīpikā itself notes that on either view release is a human goal and differs from saṃsāra by the absence of pain.
  - P1-level: Advaita's bliss belongs to the absolute self, not to an experience of a knower — a reading the Mīmāṃsakas reject.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U24-blood-sacrifice — Should blood offerings (bali) be made to the Goddess?  
`dsp:blood-sacrifice` · skeleton

- **lin:bengal-assam-sakta**: (Kālikā Purāṇa) The Goddess is pleased by offerings; by them liberation and heaven are won and a king conquers his enemies; mantra-purified blood becomes nectar (67.1-12; 67.19-22). The same text restricts them: brāhmaṇas must not offer wine or certain victims, a human offering needs the king's permission, and pumpkin, sugarcane or spirits are 'equal to' a victim (67.23; 67.46-52; 67.113-117).
- **lin:kalikula**: (Kālīvilāsa) In the Kali age worship is to be performed in the paśu disposition, with pure food and without meat (nirāmiṣa) (4.3).
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: (Vaiṣṇava) Killing animals is not true worship; the Bhāgavata warns that animals slain in sacrifice await their slayer in the next world (Bhāgavata 4.25.7-8, recalled), and the Goddess is Kṛṣṇa's power acting by his will (Brahmasaṃhitā 5.44); Bengal Vaiṣṇavas reject bali.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage / adhikāra: the Kālikā Purāṇa itself grades offerings by class and permits substitutes; the substitutes (anukalpa) could be read as the tradition's own path for those who will not kill.
  - P7-arthavada: the promise of liberation through bali (67.5) as praise of the rite rather than doctrine of liberation.
  - Not reconcilable while one side holds killing to be an offering and the other a sin (ahiṃsā).
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Kālikā Purāṇa enjoins what the Vaiṣṇava and paśu-bhāva positions forbid.

## RQ-052 — Must one hold a prātimokṣa vow to take the bodhisattva vow? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:bodhisattva-vow-prerequisite` · skeleton

- **lin:kadam**: Only those who always keep one of the seven kinds of prātimokṣa vow have the fortune for the bodhisattva vow, not others.
- **lin:sakya**: There are two bodhisattva-vow traditions: the one from Asaṅga (Cittamātra) requires a prior prātimokṣa vow; the one from Nāgārjuna and Śāntideva (Madhyamaka) can be given to anyone with the aspiration; each is to be conferred by its own rite (Sakya Paṇḍita).
- Note: Atiśa's condition applies to the rite of the Bodhisattvabhūmi (Asaṅga's tradition), which is the rite he names; the Madhyamaka rite has different entry conditions.

## RQ-U45-3 — What is Bön's relation to Buddhism: an independent older revelation, or a Tibetan religion that took over Buddhist doctrine?  
`dsp:bon-and-buddhism` · skeleton

- **lin:bon**: Bön is the teaching of Tönpa Shenrab, a fully enlightened teacher of Ölmo Lungring in Tagzig, far older than Śākyamuni; it reached Tibet through Zhang Zhung, was persecuted and hidden, and recovered as treasure; its parallels with Buddhism reflect one truth or Buddhist borrowing from Bön.
- **lin:gelug**: Buddhist historians (e.g. Thuken's Crystal Mirror of Tenets, early 19th c.) describe Bön as a pre-Buddhist religion of rites and spirits that later 'transformed' itself by adopting Buddhist scriptures; Buddhist accounts of Samye report Bön priests defeated and exiled.
- Candidate readings:
  - Two independent lineages converging on the same Great Perfection (P2 reading, rejected by both as a statement of origin).
  - Mutual borrowing over centuries (neither tradition accepts this fully).
  - Bön as the older source (Bön position).
  - Bön as derivative (Buddhist polemical position).
- Note: Both traditions insist on distinct identities; P2 could present the shared Dzogchen as one truth from two standpoints, but that would erase a distinction both sides insist on — not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U16-2 — Does Brahman itself transform into the world, and if so does it change?  
`dsp:brahma-parinama-vada` · skeleton

- **lin:bhedabheda**: Bhāskara (and Bhartṛprapañca): Brahman really transforms into the world through its power; the effect is real, different and non-different from the cause.
- **lin:pustimarga**: Vallabha: Brahman becomes the world without change (avikṛta-pariṇāma), as gold in ornaments; creation is manifestation (āvirbhāva).
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Jīva / Caitanya: only the Lord's external power transforms (śakti-pariṇāma); the Lord remains unchanged like the touchstone that yields gold; vivarta is rejected.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Advaita: a real transformation would make Brahman changeable and composite; the world is an apparent transformation (vivarta) of Brahman, a real transformation (pariṇāma) only of māyā.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: Advaita itself admits pariṇāma of māyā at the vyāvahārika level — rejected by all four pariṇāma schools, for whom the world is not merely conventional
  - Gauḍīya śakti-pariṇāma and Vallabha's avikṛta-pariṇāma as convergent answers to Advaita's objection of change in Brahman (a partial convergence among the Vaiṣṇava schools)
- Note: not yet reconciled: the pariṇāma schools and Advaita disagree on whether the world's reality is ultimate.

## RQ-U14-10 — Is Brahman (God) the material cause of the world, or only its efficient cause?  
`dsp:brahman-material-cause` · skeleton

- **lin:visistadvaita**: Both: Brahman with souls and matter as its subtle body is the material cause, and as their self the efficient cause; the change belongs to the body, not to Brahman's essence.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Both, but as the substrate of an apparent transformation (vivarta-upādāna): the world appears on Brahman through māyā.
- **lin:dvaita**: Only efficient: prakṛti is the material cause; Viṣṇu, eternally distinct, rules it.
- **lin:nyaya**: Only efficient: God arranges eternal atoms, which are the material cause.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: 'material cause' under different analyses of cause (substrate of change, of appearance, of arrangement)
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-056 — Why did Viṣṇu appear as the Buddha (and how are the Buddhist and Jain teachings to be regarded)?  
`dsp:buddha-avatara-purpose` · sourced

- **lin:puranic**: To delude the enemies of the gods and lead them away from the Veda (BhP 1.3.24; VP 3.17-18 Māyāmoha; Agni 16).
- **lin:alvar**: Later Vaiṣṇava poetry (Jayadeva's daśāvatāra hymn) praises the Buddha avatāra as compassion condemning animal sacrifice.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: The Buddha is the fully awakened teacher of the true dharma, not an avatāra of Viṣṇu.
- Candidate readings:
  - P6-upāya: the Purāṇic 'delusion' as a skilful means for beings of a certain temperament (a Purāṇic reading, rejected by Buddhists).
  - Two Vaiṣṇava readings (deluding vs compassionate) recorded side by side.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Purāṇic account is an opponent's report; the Buddhist tradition rejects the avatāra framing altogether.

## RQ-057 — Do insentient things (walls, tiles, grasses) have buddha-nature or preach the Dharma? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:buddha-nature-of-insentient` · skeleton

- **lin:chan**: Nanyang Huizhong: the buddha-mind is walls, tiles and pebbles; the insentient preach the Dharma, heard when free of the feelings of consciousness.
- **lin:mahayana**: (Objection voiced by a monk, citing the Nirvāṇa-sūtra) Buddha-nature is what is apart from walls and insentient things; buddha-nature is permanent, mind impermanent.
- **lin:soto**: Dōgen: whole being is buddha-nature; impermanence is buddha-nature.
- Note: Huizhong's own answer: 'deluded, they differ; awakened, they do not' — like ice and water — distinguishes the standpoint of delusion from that of awakening.

## RQ-058 — Is buddha-nature a true self (permanent, blissful, pure, not empty of qualities) or another name for emptiness? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:buddha-nature-self-or-emptiness` · skeleton

- **lin:tathagatagarbha**: Buddha-nature is the true self: 'self means the tathāgatagarbha'; the dharmakāya has the perfection of self; the garbha is not empty of the inseparable buddha-qualities.
- **lin:mahayana**: The tathāgatagarbha is not the self of the tīrthikas: the buddhas teach it meaning emptiness, the reality-limit, nirvāṇa, the unborn, so that the foolish may not fear no-self; its purpose is to remove five faults.
- **lin:tathagatagarbha**: (East Asian reading from the same sūtra) Buddha-nature is the emptiness of the highest meaning, which sees both empty and non-empty — the middle way.
- Note: The texts themselves distinguish levels — the Mahāparinirvāṇa separates worldly and supramundane 'self', and the Laṅkāvatāra and RGV state the teaching's purpose — so 'self' and 'no-self' refer to different objects (the dharmakāya's qualities vs the imagined self of the aggregates).

## RQ-U34-08 — May monks reside permanently in temples and manage their property?  
`dsp:caityavasa` · skeleton

- **lin:caityavasi**: The temple-dwelling monks (as reported by their opponents): residence in and care of temples serves the tradition and the laity.
- **lin:kharatara-gaccha**: No: monks must live in lodgings given by laypeople, wander, and own nothing, as the Āgamas prescribe.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: settled clerics serving temples and wandering mendicants as different roles — the later yati/mendicant distinction partly institutionalized this
- Note: Not yet reconciled; the temple-dwellers' side survives mainly in their opponents' accounts.

## RQ-U39-1 — Can all beings attain buddhahood, or are there beings (icchantikas, those without lineage) who never will?  
`dsp:can-all-beings-attain-buddhahood` · skeleton

- **lin:tathagatagarbha**: All sentient beings have buddha-nature, including the icchantika; there is no fixed icchantika; all will attain buddhahood.
- **lin:yogacara**: Beings have distinct lineages (gotra); some belong to the śrāvaka or pratyekabuddha lineage and some have no lineage (agotra) and do not attain buddhahood; the teaching of universal buddha-nature was given with a hidden intention.
- **lin:mahayana**: Faxian's six-fascicle Nirvāṇa Sūtra (418) and its first Chinese readers: icchantikas are excluded from buddha-nature.
- Candidate readings:
  - P5 (Yogācāra reading): 'all have buddha-nature' is neyārtha, spoken to encourage beings of indeterminate lineage.
  - P5/P6 (Tathāgatagarbha and Tiantai reading): the five-lineage teaching is a skillful means; the one vehicle and universal buddha-nature are nītārtha.
  - P4 (Mahāparinirvāṇa juan 36 and Laṅkāvatāra p. 28 themselves): 'icchantika' names a present condition, not an eternal nature — the Tathāgata's power can revive the roots of good.
  - P1 (Faxiang's own distinction, reported from memory): buddha-nature as principle (lixing) all beings have; buddha-nature as practice (xingxing) some lack.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: each side uses the tradition's own P5/P6 hermeneutic to make the other's teaching provisional, so the principle cannot decide between them.

## RQ-061 — Does the statement 'all things are empty' refute itself?  
`dsp:can-emptiness-be-asserted` · skeleton

- **lin:nyaya**: If all things lack own-nature, the statement 'all things lack own-nature' also lacks it and cannot negate anything; if it has own-nature, not all things are empty. Either way the thesis fails.
- **lin:madhyamaka**: My words are dependently arisen and so empty, yet they function, as empty carts and pots do their work; they negate as one magical man stops another. And I have no thesis, so the faults of a thesis do not touch me.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: words function conventionally without own-nature, so the statement works at the conventional level (Nāgārjuna's own answer).
  - P2-standpoint: from the realist's standpoint a functioning statement must be real; from the Mādhyamika's, function does not require own-nature — two standpoints, not one truth-claim.
  - No-thesis reading (VV 29): the Mādhyamika only shows the opponent's inconsistencies, so no self-refuting thesis is asserted.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Madhyamaka answer relies on the two truths, which the realist does not grant.

## RQ-062 — Can every soul eventually be liberated, or are some souls by nature never to be liberated?  
`dsp:can-every-soul-be-liberated` · skeleton

- **lin:dvaita**: Souls are of three innate classes: those fit for liberation, those who transmigrate forever, and those fit for eternal blinding darkness; this gradation is beginningless and unalterable, and liberation and darkness are both final.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Every soul is in truth Brahman; bondage is ignorance, and any soul that gains the knowledge of identity is liberated; no self is by nature excluded from that knowledge.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: All bound souls are capable of liberation through the Lord's grace, won by devotion or surrender; the tradition knows no class of souls eternally excluded from it.
- Candidate readings:
  - {"principle": "P4-stage", "reading": "The 'classes' describe stages of spiritual maturity, not fixed natures."}
  - {"principle": "P7-arthavada", "reading": "The passages on eternal darkness are hortatory warnings (praise and blame)."}
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-063 — Can practices other than the nenbutsu bring birth in the Pure Land?  
`dsp:can-other-practices-lead-to-birth` · skeleton

- **lin:pure-land**: Yes: the three meritorious acts, the sixteen contemplations and all good roots dedicated to birth lead there (the sūtras; Chinese masters).
- **lin:jodo-shu**: (Hōnen) They are set aside; the primal vow's practice is the nenbutsu alone. (Chōsai, as reported) The various practices too are vowed in the primal vow. (Benchō's line, as usually stated) They can bring birth but are not the vow's own practice.
- **lin:jodo-shinshu**: They lead only to the transformed land (19th vow).
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: other practices for those unable to entrust exclusively (Shinran's provisional gate)
  - P6-upaya: other practices taught to lead beings toward the nenbutsu (Senchakushū 4's first reading)
- Note: Not yet reconciled among Hōnen's disciples' lines.

## RQ-064 — Can an unconscious pradhāna, without a conscious director, bring forth and order the world?  
`dsp:can-unconscious-pradhana-create` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: Yes: prakṛti acts spontaneously for puruṣa's purpose; no Lord is needed or established.
- **lin:vedanta**: No: the inferred pradhāna cannot be the cause, because an unconscious cause cannot produce orderly arrangement or activity.
- **lin:nyaya**: No: effects such as the world require an intelligent maker (Īśvara) as their efficient cause.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint (causal): prakṛti as material cause and a Lord as efficient cause — the theistic-Sāṃkhya reading; rejected by classical Sāṃkhya (SS 1.92) and by Advaita, for which Brahman is both material and efficient cause.
  - P2-standpoint (purpose): Sāṃkhya's teleology 'for puruṣa' already locates the orienting factor in consciousness (puruṣa's presence), which Vedānta places in the Lord.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: whether a conscious agent is required is the point at issue.

## RQ-U34-02 — Did the original Jain scripture survive?  
`dsp:canon-survival-jain` · skeleton

- **lin:svetambara**: Yes, largely: the eleven Aṅgas and the other Āgamas were preserved through the councils of Pāṭaliputra, Mathurā and Valabhī and written down under Devarddhigaṇi; only the Dṛṣṭivāda with the Pūrvas was lost.
- **lin:digambara**: No: knowledge of the Aṅgas and Pūrvas dwindled until 683 years after the nirvāṇa no complete Aṅga was known; only parts survive in the Ṣaṭkhaṇḍāgama and Kaṣāyapāhuḍa; the Śvetāmbara Āgamas are later compositions of a group that had relaxed the discipline.
- **lin:yapaniya**: The old canon survives in a form the Yāpanīyas accepted and cited (Āvaśyaka, Chedasūtras, niryuktis).
- Candidate readings:
  - The two accounts may describe the same decline from different sides: the Śvetāmbaras preserved a recited canon with admitted losses, the Digambaras stressed the loss and preserved other portions
  - Scholarly account (labelled, not a reconciliation): the Śvetāmbara canon contains old material of many periods; the Digambara āgamas are independent early works
- Note: Not yet reconciled: a question of transmission history on which the accounts differ.

## RQ-066 — Is purity and worth determined by birth into a class (varṇa), or by conduct?  
`dsp:caste-and-purity` · skeleton

- **lin:vedic-srauta**: The brahmin is the highest class, born from the mouth of the cosmic Person / of Brahmā, Brahmā's heir; the classes are ordained by birth, and purity belongs to the brahmin class.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: One is a brahmin or an outcaste not by birth but by deeds; all four classes can do good and evil and all can be purified; the classes arose from occupations; the Dhamma, not birth, is best among people.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Brahmanical varṇa as a ritual and social order (ethical-social standpoint) versus spiritual worth by conduct (seeker standpoint) — the Brahmanical tradition itself sometimes defines the true brahmin by conduct (e.g. the Yakṣapraśna, Vajrasūcī Upaniṣad)
  - P1-level: birth-based duties as conventional, liberation as open to all — but the suttas also reject birth-based rank conventionally
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Buddhist texts deny that birth confers purity, which the Brahmanical sources affirm.

## RQ-067 — May people of low birth know and expound the meaning of scripture and approach God on equal terms? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:caste-and-the-varkari-saints` · skeleton

- **lin:dharmasastra**: Vedic study, the exposition of the Veda's meaning and certain rights of worship belong to the twice-born by birth (adhikāra); the children of a sannyāsin who returned to household life are outcaste; a śūdra must not teach the Veda's meaning.
- **lin:varkari**: Before God birth counts for nothing: the name is open to all; the devotee, whatever his birth, knows the Veda's meaning; the low-born saints are revered as equals; the Gītā itself promises the highest to women, vaiśyas and śūdras who take refuge.
- Note: The orthodox rules govern entitlement to Vedic ritual and study; the saints' claim concerns the path of the name and devotion, which scripture itself (BhG 9.32) opens to all.

## RQ-U50-02 — Does the effect pre-exist in its cause (satkārya), is it newly produced (asatkārya/ārambha), is it an apparent transformation of an unchanging cause (vivarta), a real manifestation of consciousness (ābhāsa), a transformation of God that leaves him unchanged (avikṛta-pariṇāma), or dependently arisen and ultimately unarisen? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:causation` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: The effect exists in its material cause before it is produced (satkārya): because what does not exist cannot be produced; because a definite material cause is taken for a definite effect; because not everything arises from everything; because a capable cause produces only what it is capable of; because the effect has the nature of its cause. Production is manifestation.
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Past and future exist in their own form, differing in their time-paths; change is transformation of property, time-mark and state in an enduring substrate; the instrumental cause does not set the material causes going but only removes the obstruction, as a farmer breaches a dyke.
- **lin:nyaya**: The effect is a new thing, non-existent before production (asatkārya, ārambhavāda): before it is produced the fruit is not existent in the Sāṃkhya sense, for existence and non-existence are opposed; it comes about from inherent, non-inherent and instrumental causes and is the counterpositive of its prior absence. Nor does existence arise from non-existence as such (the sprout from the destroyed seed).
- **lin:vaisesika**: From the absence of the cause follows the absence of the effect, not the reverse; a thing before it is produced is non-existent (prior absence) — the effect is a newly made whole.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: The effect is non-different from its cause, 'a verbal handle, a name' — as pots are only clay (CU 6.1.4; BS 2.1.14). Brahman is both material and efficient cause; the world is its apparent transformation (vivarta), not a real modification (vikāra): the snake is only the rope. From the highest standpoint nothing whatever is born.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: Brahman is both the material and the efficient cause; the world is non-different from Brahman as pots from clay, but the effect is a real state of Brahman, whose body is the sentient and non-sentient; real transformation (pariṇāma) touches Brahman's body, not its essence.
- **lin:bhedabheda**: Brahman makes itself into the world by real transformation of its power (ātmakṛteḥ pariṇāmāt); the effect is real, both different and non-different from its cause (Bhāskara; Nimbārka).
- **lin:pustimarga**: Brahman, transforming itself, is the material cause of the world, yet this transformation involves no change in Brahman (avikṛta-pariṇāma).
- **lin:dvaita**: Viṣṇu is the independent cause who creates, maintains, dissolves and controls; the world is real and its material is prakṛti, dependent on him; the doctrine of apparent transformation is refuted, since the witness establishes what is real.
- **lin:kashmir-saivism**: Things that appear in the present as external are possible only if they already exist within consciousness; the Lord, by his will and infinite powers, makes them appear (ābhāsa) — this is his creatorhood. Consciousness, free, is the cause of the universe, which she unfolds on her own screen: a real self-manifestation, not an illusion.
- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: The world, spoken of as 'he, she and it', undergoes origination, maintenance and dissolution, so it has an agent; it resolves into and re-emerges from its cause — Śiva as efficient cause working through real māyā as material.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: Formations occur through ignorance and the other conditions; the wheel of becoming has no maker such as a Brahmā: things arise dependently, neither from a self-made nor an other-made cause.
- **lin:madhyamaka**: No existent whatever is ever found arisen from itself, from another, from both, or without a cause (MMK 1.1); the effect is neither made of the conditions nor of non-conditions; dependent arising is itself emptiness, and emptiness, dependent origination and the middle way have one meaning.
- **lin:jainism**: The existent is endowed at once with origination, cessation and persistence: a thing is in some respect existent and in some respect non-existent before its production. Time, own-nature, destiny, former deeds and human effort, each held one-sidedly as the cause, are wrong views; taken together they are right view.
- **lin:vyakarana**: Brahman, without beginning or end, whose essence is the Word, appears (vivartate) as objects; the world is the Word-principle's appearing (Bhartṛhari — the earliest use of vivarta as a technical term recorded here).
- **lin:upanisadic**: Time, own-nature, fixed order, chance, the elements, the womb and the person are weighed as causes and none suffices; it is the greatness of God in the world by which the wheel of Brahman turns.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint for Sāṃkhya vs Nyāya: satkārya speaks of the material cause's capacity (the substance standpoint), asatkārya of the product as a new whole (the mode standpoint) — rejected by both, each of whom argues the other's thesis is incoherent.
  - P1-level for Advaita vs Kashmir Śaivism: both deny change in the ultimate consciousness; they differ on whether the appearance is real self-display (ābhāsa) or indeterminable (vivarta) — the Trika insists on the reality of manifestation, Advaita on its sublatability.
  - P1-level for Advaita vs the theistic Vedāntas: transformation (pariṇāma) true empirically, vivarta true ultimately — rejected by Rāmānuja, Nimbārka, Vallabha and Madhva, who deny degrees of reality.
- Note: Partly reconciled by the traditions' own devices: Advaita and Madhyamaka accept causal change or dependent arising at the conventional level while denying real origination ultimately (P1), and the Jains hold the pre-existence and the novelty of the effect true from the substance and mode standpoints (P2); the Sāṃkhya–Nyāya and vivarta–ābhāsa disagreements are not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U38-09 — Does the destruction of a conditioned thing have a cause?  
`dsp:cause-of-destruction` · skeleton

- **lin:vaibhasika**: Impermanence is one of the four real characteristics that acts on each dharma; destruction is characterized, though things are momentary.
- **lin:sautrantika**: No: destruction is causeless and spontaneous; what arises perishes of itself; apparent destruction by fire is non-arising of a similar series.
- **lin:pudgalavada**: Some formations are momentary, others endure for a time (Vasumitra).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the characteristic 'impermanence' names what the Sautrāntika calls the thing's own perishing — rejected by both as to the reality of the characteristic.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Vaibhāṣika holds a real entity where the Sautrāntika sees a designation.
