## RQ-070 — What causes the owner–owned relation between puruṣa and prakṛti? (views recorded in the Sāṃkhya Sūtra) (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:cause-of-purusa-prakrti-relation` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: Karma: the relation is caused by karma and is beginningless like seed and sprout (SS 6.67).
- **lin:samkhya**: Non-discrimination (Pañcaśikha, SS 6.68).
- **lin:samkhya**: The subtle body (the teacher Sanandana, SS 6.69).
- Note: The sūtra records the three as alternatives without rejecting any; they name the relation's cause at different removes (non-discrimination at the root, karma as its working, the subtle body as its carrier).

## RQ-071 — What occasions the descent of power (śaktipāta): the equality of karma, the maturing of impurity, or the Lord's free will alone? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:cause-of-saktipata` · skeleton

- **lin:mantramarga**: Its time is the equality of the soul's karmas; Śiva waits for that time because of the soul's beginningless karma, yet remains its lord and cause, as the sun is of the opening lotus.
- **lin:pancaratra**: Viṣṇu's compassion — his power of grace called śaktipāta — arises for the suffering soul; by his glance its karmas become equal and fall silent, and it is lifted out of saṃsāra.
- **lin:trika**: Abhinavagupta rejects karma-equality and the ripening of impurity as causes: the descent depends on the Lord's independence alone.
- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: The ripening of mala (malaparipāka) is the occasion; the Lord, not the mala, is the cause.
- **lin:tamil-saiva-siddhanta**: Both: when the two deeds are balanced (iruviṉaiyoppu) and āṇava has ripened (malaparipākam), grace descends in four grades.
- Note: Under P2 the Kiraṇa's own image (sun and lotus) states the event from the causal standpoint (karma-equality as the time) and the divine standpoint (the Lord as cause); the Ahirbudhnya states it from the divine standpoint, with karma-equality as effect.

## RQ-U38-05 — What caused the first schism between the Sthaviras and the Mahāsāṃghikas?  
`dsp:cause-of-the-first-schism` · skeleton

- **lin:theravada**: Laxity in discipline: the Vajjiputtakas' ten points were rejected at Vesālī; the defeated monks formed the Mahāsaṅgītika and altered the texts.
- **lin:sarvastivada**: Doctrine: Mahādeva's five points lowering the arhat divided the Saṅgha under Aśoka.
- **lin:mahasanghika**: Vinaya recension: an elder enlarged and rearranged the Vinaya; the great community kept the original.
- Candidate readings:
  - Historical reading: several successive disputes (the ten points at Vaiśālī, the five points, Vinaya recension) each remembered by the side it justified — the traditions do not frame it so.
  - P2-standpoint: each account is true from the standpoint of its community's discipline — rejected by each, which regards the other side as having departed from the Dharma-Vinaya.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the schools remember different causes for the first division of the Saṅgha.

## RQ-073 — Is recitation of the Buddha's name compatible with Chan? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:chan-and-nianfo` · skeleton

- **lin:chan**: The deluded recite the Buddha's name seeking the West; the awakened purify their own minds — the Pure Land is the pure mind (Platform Sūtra); guarding one's own mind surpasses recollecting another buddha (Xiuxin yao lun).
- **lin:fayan**: Joint practice: Chan and recitation together, as advocated by Yongming Yanshou, Zhongfeng Mingben, Yunqi Zhuhong and the Ōbaku school; Musang opened his assemblies with recitation.
- Note: Recitation as a method for devotional temperaments and as a huatou ('who recites?') within Chan practice.

## RQ-074 — Is Chan a special transmission outside the scriptural teachings, or one with them? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:chan-and-the-teachings` · skeleton

- **lin:chan**: A special transmission outside the teachings, not established on words and letters; the twelve divisions of scripture are only expressions; one must not seek the meaning in words.
- **lin:heze**: Chan and the teachings coincide: Zongmi correlates the three Chan tenets with three doctrinal teachings; Yanshou takes the one mind as the source of both.
- **lin:seon**: Hyujeong: Chan is the Buddha's mind and the teachings his words; first understand through the teachings, then enter Chan through the live word.
- **lin:chan**: The Platform Sūtra: those who say 'do not use words' slander the scriptures, for 'not using' is itself words.
- Note: Teachings as the provisional means and first stage, Chan as direct realization of what they point to (Hyujeong's sequence).

## RQ-075 — Should Chan meditation and Pure Land recitation be practised together? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:chan-pure-land-dual-practice` · skeleton

- **lin:pure-land**: Yes: both return to the one source; Chan is the Chan of Pure Land and Pure Land the Pure Land of Chan; practise one gate deeply; all good practices share one end.
- **lin:jodo-shu**: Mixing practices loses birth; exclusive practice alone is right.
- **lin:chan**: Recitation is for those of lesser capacity, or a huatou; seeking a Buddha outside is contrary to seeing one's nature (as reported).
- Note: Zhuhong's 'return to the source is not two, expedient gates are many' reconciles by path, while urging depth in one gate.

## RQ-076 — Is Chöd — a practice first taught by a Tibetan woman and concerned with spirits and the gift of the body — authentic Buddhadharma with an Indian source?  
`dsp:chod-orthodoxy` · skeleton

- **lin:chod**: Chöd is the practical application of the Perfection of Wisdom: its view is that of the sūtras, its instructions come in part from the Indian siddha Padampa Sangye and from Āryadeva the Brahmin's Grand Poem, and Machig is the emanation of the Great Mother. Indian scholars who came to test her accepted it, and it is the one Dharma that went from Tibet to India.
- **None**: A practice composed in Tibet, lacking an Indian tantra or sūtra as its source and centred on dealings with spirits, cannot be accepted as the Buddha's teaching merely on the authority of a Tibetan founder.
- Candidate readings:
  - P5-neyartha: authenticity judged by agreement with the definitive Perfection of Wisdom rather than by place of origin (the Chöd tradition's own argument).
  - P6-upaya: the spirit-dealing form as skillful means for cutting self-clinging in Tibetan conditions.
  - Historical question (origin) and doctrinal question (conformity with the sūtras) treated separately.
- Note: Not yet reconciled; the critics' side is known only through the defending tradition.

## RQ-077 — Is the mind a series of momentary cognitions, or one enduring mind with many objects?  
`dsp:citta-momentary-or-enduring` · skeleton

- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: The mind is one, has many objects and endures; properties inhere in an enduring property-bearer.
- **lin:sautrantika**: All conditioned things, cognition included, are momentary; continuity is that of a series (santāna), not of an enduring substrate.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: substance (dravyārthika) vs mode (paryāyārthika) descriptions of mind.
  - Leave distinct: Vyāsa explicitly refutes momentariness.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-078 — Is consciousness a product of the combined elements of the body?  
`dsp:consciousness-from-elements` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: No: consciousness is not found in the elements separately, nor in combination; puruṣa, other than the body, exists.
- **lin:carvaka**: Yes: consciousness arises from the combination of elements, as intoxicating power from ingredients.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: the materialist account as a description of embodied consciousness only (rejected by both).
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-079 — Is it the same consciousness that runs and wanders through the rounds of rebirth?  
`dsp:consciousness-transmigrates` · skeleton

- **lin:early-buddhism**: (Sāti) 'As I understand the Dhamma taught by the Blessed One, it is this same consciousness that runs and wanders on, not another' — the one that speaks and feels and experiences the results of deeds.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: (The Buddha) Consciousness is dependently arisen; without a condition there is no arising of consciousness; it is reckoned by its condition as fire by its fuel.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: continuity of a conditioned series spoken of conventionally (later Theravāda: rebirth-linking consciousness as a new moment conditioned by the last)
  - The Pudgalavāda 'person' as the carrier (U38, dsp:pudgala)
- Note: Not yet reconciled as a doctrinal question beyond the text: the sutta itself rejects Sāti's view.

## RQ-080 — Was the world created, and does it undergo total dissolution?  
`dsp:creation-and-dissolution` · skeleton

- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: No means of knowledge establishes a first creation or a dissolution of everything; a bodiless Prajāpati could not will, a compassionate one would create only happiness, a purposeless one would not act; creation and dissolution are to be understood 'as today'.
- **lin:prabhakara-mimamsa**: Things arise and perish gradually; people were always born of parents; no Īśvara moves atoms by will.
- **lin:nyaya**: Īśvara creates the world from atoms at the beginning of each cycle and dissolves it at the end; his existence is inferred from the world as an effect.
- **lin:puranic**: Creation and dissolution recur in cosmic cycles (kalpas) under the Lord.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: Advaita reads creation-talk as empirical (vyāvahārika) — Mīmāṃsā accepts no such levels.
  - Kumārila's own Tantravārttika speaks of Manus in each kalpa, suggesting that cyclic time is not denied as such, only a total destruction and first creation; unresolved within his works.
- Note: Not yet reconciled; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-081 — Is the outcome of life decided by fate (daiva) or by human effort (pauruṣa)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:daiva-or-paurusa` · sourced

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Effort is everything; fate is nothing but one's own former effort and is overcome by present effort guided by scripture and the good; reliance on fate is delusion.
- **lin:ajivika**: Everything is fixed by destiny (niyati); human effort has no power (as reported by Buddhist and Jain opponents).
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Both fate and effort are needed, as seed and field; effort is the field without which fate bears no fruit (the common epic and smṛti position).
- Candidate readings:
  - past-and-present effort (YV)
  - seed and field (epic)
  - strict destiny (Ājīvika, reported)
- Note: The Yoga Vāsiṣṭha itself redescribes fate as past effort, so 'fate' and 'effort' name one causal process seen from past and present standpoints.

## RQ-082 — Should the Goddess be worshipped in the right-hand (dakṣiṇa) mode, or is the left-hand/Kaula (vāma) mode with the five tattvas higher? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:daksina-vs-vama` · skeleton

- **lin:kaula**: The seven modes rise from Veda to Kaula; Vāma is higher than Dakṣiṇa and Kaula highest of all (Kulārṇava 2.7-8); in the Kula dharma enjoyment itself becomes yoga (2.23-24); worship of the primordial Śakti without the five tattvas is fruitless 'like seed on rock' (Mahānirvāṇa 5.22-24).
- **lin:kalikula**: (Kālīvilāsa) In the Kali age all classes must worship in the paśu disposition, bathing thrice daily and eating no meat; the vīra and divya dispositions do not exist in this age (Kālīvilāsa 4.2-3).
- **lin:srividya**: (Samaya Śrīvidyā, Lakṣmīdhara) The Kaula and 'mixed' modes are to be abandoned: the sixty-four tantras aim at worldly powers, deceive even their knowers and are far from the Veda; only the inner Samaya worship is Vedic.
- Note: The texts themselves tie the modes to the practitioner's disposition and eligibility (bhāva, adhikāra) and provide substitutes, so right- and left-hand worship are prescribed for different practitioners.

## RQ-U26-07 — Was Dāmodaradeva a disciple of Śaṅkaradeva, or an independent founder?  
`dsp:damodaradeva-independence` · skeleton

- **lin:ekasarana**: (Mahāpuruṣīyā account) Dāmodaradeva was initiated by Śaṅkaradeva and belongs within his dharma.
- **lin:brahma-samhati**: (Dāmodarīyā account) Dāmodaradeva was an independent teacher whose line need not be subordinated to Śaṅkaradeva's.
- Candidate readings:
  - Historical: he received initiation but developed an independent institutional line.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: competing lineage histories.

## RQ-084 — Are precepts and practice needed once one has awakened? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:daruma-shu-precepts` · skeleton

- **lin:daruma-shu**: (Reported by its opponent Eisai) There are no precepts to keep and no practice to do: the mind is buddha from the beginning.
- **lin:rinzai**: Eisai: the precepts are the foundation of Zen; one who does not keep them cannot practise Zen.
- **lin:chan**: The Platform Sūtra: 'when the mind is level, what need to keep precepts?' — yet it gives formless precepts and repentance.
- Note: From the ultimate standpoint there is nothing to keep; at the conventional level precepts remain the foundation — the Platform Sūtra gives both.

## RQ-085 — Should the devotee say 'I am He' (so'ham) or 'I am your servant' (dāsō'ham)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:dasoham-soham` · skeleton

- **lin:sarana-vacana**: Not 'I am He' but 'I am your servant': the devotee's stance is humble service to Śiva and his devotees.
- **lin:pancacarya**: At the māheśvara stage the worshipper rejects total non-duality because Lord and servant must differ (10.37–43), but in śivayoga-samādhi the supreme self is contemplated as 'I am He' (12.21–24), at aikya the feeling 'I am Śiva' establishes oneness (14.5–7), and one who no longer thinks 'I am servant, Śiva master' stands in non-duality (14.37).
- Note: The SSM itself assigns servanthood to the stages of practice and 'I am He' to śivayoga and union (P4); the vacana speaks from the devotional standpoint, the SSM's aikya verses from the absolute (P2).

## RQ-086 — Is birth settled only at death with the Buddha's welcome, or already in this life? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:deathbed-welcome-or-settled-in-life` · skeleton

- **lin:jodo-shu**: Right mindfulness at death and Amida's welcoming descent are sought; the nenbutsu is continued so that the practitioner is welcomed.
- **lin:pure-land**: Deathbed rites and assisted recitation secure right mindfulness; Daochuo's wax seal: the moment life ends is the moment of birth.
- **lin:jodo-shinshu**: The welcome belongs to those of various practices; the person of shinjin is settled at the moment of shinjin and need not wait for the moment of death.
- Note: Shinran himself assigns the welcome at death to practitioners of the nineteenth vow and settlement in life to those of the eighteenth — a distinction by the practitioner's stage.

## RQ-087 — May a debater refute without holding a thesis of his own (vitaṇḍā)?  
`dsp:debate-without-thesis` · skeleton

- **lin:nyaya**: Cavilling without a counter-thesis is a recognized but inferior form of debate, permitted only to protect truth; a caviller who states his purpose or meaning thereby adopts a thesis, and one who rejects all examples cannot refute anyone.
- **lin:madhyamaka**: The Mādhyamika refutes the opponent's theses by consequences without advancing a thesis of his own: 'if I had a thesis the fault would be mine; since I have none, there is no fault' (Nāgārjuna).
- Candidate readings:
  - P6-upaya: the Madhyamaka's thesis-less refutation as a skilful means aimed at the opponent's clinging.
  - P1-level: absence of a thesis 'ultimately' while using conventional language.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-088 — Can the categories and means of knowledge be coherently defined?  
`dsp:definability-of-categories` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: No: every definition proposed by the Naiyāyikas (of valid cognition, of the means of knowledge, of the categories) fails on examination; the world of distinctions is indeterminable (Śrīharṣa's Khaṇḍanakhaṇḍakhādya), argued on the opponent's own rules.
- **lin:navya-nyaya**: Yes: definitions can be made exact by specifying delimitors, relations and counterpositives; Gaṅgeśa's Tattvacintāmaṇi answers such objections.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: Śrīharṣa's critique targets the ultimate status of distinctions, while Navya-Nyāya defends their empirical coherence.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-089 — Does dependent origination describe rebirth across three lives, or a process in present experience? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:dependent-origination-three-lives-or-present` · skeleton

- **lin:theravada**: The commentaries expound the twelve links across three lives: two past causes, eight present, two future — twenty modes and three links; the wheel has no maker or experiencer.
- **lin:suan-mokkh**: The whole chain arises in daily life at every unmindful contact; 'birth' is the birth of the ego; reading it only across lives makes it useless for practice.
- **lin:theravada**: The Vibhaṅga's Abhidhamma method already shows the links within one moment of consciousness, alongside the sutta method across lives.
- Note: The canonical Abhidhamma itself gives both an across-lives and a single-moment exposition; each reading is a standpoint the tradition already contains.

## RQ-090 — Where does the path of the gods lead: to the conditioned (kārya) Brahman or to the supreme Brahman (BS 4.3.7–16)?  
`dsp:destination-of-devayana` · skeleton

- **lin:vedanta**: Bādari: to the conditioned Brahman, since movement to the all-pervading is impossible (Śaṅkara accepts this as siddhānta, with krama-mukti).
- **lin:vedanta**: Jaimini: to the supreme Brahman, the primary meaning of the word.
- **lin:vedanta**: Bādarāyaṇa: the guide leads those who do not meditate on symbols (Rāmānuja takes this as the final view: the path leads to the supreme).
- Candidate readings:
  - Advaita: the path serves saguṇa meditators and ends in krama-mukti; nirguṇa knowers do not travel.
  - Viśiṣṭādvaita: all released souls travel to the supreme Brahman.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: Advaita and Viśiṣṭādvaita take different sūtras as the conclusion.

## RQ-091 — Should forest dwelling, alms-food, rag robes, tree-root dwelling and abstention from fish and meat be compulsory for life? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:devadatta-five-points` · skeleton

- **lin:early-buddhism**: (Devadatta) These five practices conduce to few wishes, contentment, effacement and energy; they should be binding on all monks for life.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: (The Buddha) Whoever wishes may dwell in the forest, live on alms, wear rag robes; the root of a tree is allowed for eight months; fish and meat are allowed when not seen, heard or suspected to have been killed for the monk.
- Note: Under P3 the Buddha's own ruling makes the austerities optional according to temperament; Devadatta's party rejected this and split the Saṅgha.

## RQ-092 — Does birth (caste) determine a devotee's worth, eligibility and place — in the temple, in scripture, and among devotees? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:devotee-birth-and-caste` · skeleton

- **lin:alvar**: The servants of the Lord's servants, even caṇḍālas of caṇḍālas, are our masters; Veda-learned brāhmaṇas who revile devotees become outcastes; the Āḻvārs themselves include a Vellāḷa, a pāṇar bard held untouchable, a Kaḷḷar chieftain and a woman, and the Lord had the bard carried into his sanctum.
- **lin:bhakti-sastra**: Among devotees there is no distinction of caste, learning, beauty, family, wealth or occupation, because they are the Lord's own; those of every birth down to the lowest are eligible for devotion; yet scripture is still to be kept after conviction.
- **lin:puranic**: A dog-cooker who has given mind, speech, acts, wealth and life to the Lord is better than a brāhmaṇa with twelve virtues who is averse to him.
- **lin:tenkalai**: Judging a devotee by birth is among the gravest offences (bhāgavata-apacāra); the Tamil Veda of Nammāḻvār, born outside the brahmin class, is equal to the Sanskrit.
- **lin:vadakalai**: After surrender the duties of one's class and stage are still to be performed, as the Lord's command and as service, not as means.
- **lin:vedanta**: The śūdra is not eligible for the Upaniṣadic knowledge, because hearing, study and performance of the Veda are forbidden to him and because of smṛti (Brahma Sūtra 1.3.38); knowledge may come to such persons through the epics and Purāṇas.
- Note: The bhakti texts themselves pair equality among devotees (the soul's belonging to the Lord) with continued conventional duty (NBS 12–14 with 72–73; Deśika), so the two claims can be read at the ultimate and conventional levels.

## RQ-093 — Is suggestion (dhvani, vyañjanā) a distinct power of words, and is it the soul of poetry? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:dhvani-vyanjana` · skeleton

- **lin:alankara**: Yes: beyond denotation and indication there is an implied meaning — fact, figure or rasa — conveyed by suggestion, which is the soul of poetry (Ānandavardhana, Abhinavagupta, Mammaṭa).
- **lin:alankara**: No: what is called suggestion is inference from the expressed meaning; words have one power, denotation (Mahimabhaṭṭa); or rasa is conveyed by the purport of the sentence (Dhanika); or it is included in striking expression (Kuntaka).
- **lin:vyakarana**: Nāgeśa accepts suggestion (vyañjanā) as a third power of words beside denotation and indication.
- Note: Both sides grant that poems convey implied meanings and rasa; they differ on the cognitive operation (suggestion or inference/purport) — an analytic standpoint difference about one experience.

## RQ-U38-12 — Does sense perception grasp the external object directly, or only its form (ākāra) in cognition?  
`dsp:direct-or-representational-perception` · skeleton

- **lin:vaibhasika**: Directly: sense consciousness grasps a present, really existing object, without an image; consciousness always needs an existent object (the argument for three-time existence).
- **lin:sautrantika**: Through its form: cognition arises bearing the object's form; the external object, already past when cognition arises, is inferred (as summarized in later doxography; low confidence for the Indian sources).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint — rejected by both as to the structure of cognition.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: whether perception grasps its object directly or through the object's form in cognition.

## RQ-095 — Are all nouns derived from verbal roots? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:do-all-nouns-derive-from-verbs` · sourced

- **lin:nairukta**: Yes (Śākaṭāyana and the convention of the etymologists): every noun is derived from a verb; derivation should always be attempted, by meaning if not by form.
- **lin:vyakarana**: Not all (Gārgya and some grammarians): only where accent and formation fit a root and the derivative is regular; words like 'cow', 'horse', 'man' are conventional, else anyone who moved would be called 'horse'.
- Note: The etymologist's derivation by meaning and the grammarian's derivation by form answer different questions; Yāska ranks formal derivation first and semantic derivation second (2.1).

## RQ-096 — Does initiation (dīkṣā) itself liberate, or is knowledge the direct cause of liberation?  
`dsp:does-diksa-liberate` · skeleton

- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: Initiation liberates, by Śiva's power working through the ācārya; knowledge alone cannot remove a substance.
- **lin:tamil-saiva-siddhanta**: Initiation by the guru (in seven modes) destroys stored karma, and knowledge of Śiva by grace brings union; both are Śiva's grace, and the jñāna-pāda is the culmination of the four.
- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: Liberation comes through the initiation rite, which destroys the bonds by mantra-power; initiation is the cause of the other means, and the incapable are liberated by it with devotion.
- **lin:trika**: Knowledge is the direct cause of liberation; initiation liberates by bringing about knowledge (recognition) of one's identity with Śiva.
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path: the Kiraṇa itself says some are fit for ritual, others for knowledge, others for conduct, each liberated accordingly (6.5–8).
  - P1-level: ritual as the conventional means, knowledge as the ultimate one — a reading the Saiddhāntikas would reject.
- Note: Not yet reconciled with the Trika position; the Tamil emphasis on jñāna is within the Siddhānta frame.

## RQ-097 — Does killing prescribed in Vedic sacrifice incur demerit?  
`dsp:does-sacrificial-killing-incur-demerit` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: Yes, slightly: the Vedic means are impure because of the killing; the general prohibition of harm still applies.
- **lin:mimamsa**: No: killing enjoined by the Veda for the sacrifice is dharma; the specific injunction prevails over the general prohibition.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: ritual standpoint (the act fulfils the sacrifice and is dharma) vs ethical-karmic standpoint (the act harms a being and leaves a trace of adharma) — Vācaspati's own 'different objects' argument; Mīmāṃsā denies the second.
  - P4-stage: Mīmāṃsā addresses those qualified for heaven-bringing rites; Sāṃkhya those seeking final release.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: whether any demerit remains is the point at issue.

## RQ-U14-04 — Does the Lord relish (doṣa-bhogya) or overlook the faults of those who take refuge in him?  
`dsp:dosa-bhogya` · skeleton

- **lin:tenkalai**: He relishes them, as a cow licks the dirt from its newborn calf, out of motherly love.
- **lin:vadakalai**: He overlooks them (does not see them); faults cannot be objects of relish for one opposed to all evil.
- Candidate readings:
  - P7-arthavada: 'relishing faults' as praise of the Lord's motherly love rather than a doctrine about faults
  - P2-standpoint: the devotee's experience of being accepted whole (T) vs the Lord's purity (V)
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-099 — Does the world exist only while perceived (dṛṣṭi-sṛṣṭi), or is it created by Īśvara before and apart from individual perception (sṛṣṭi-dṛṣṭi)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:drsti-srsti-vada` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Dṛṣṭi-sṛṣṭi (Prakāśānanda): the world's being is its being perceived, like a dream; no creation theory is needed.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Sṛṣṭi-dṛṣṭi (majority, e.g. Vedāntaparibhāṣā, Śaṅkara's BSBh 2.2.28–29): Īśvara creates the empirical world, which exists whether or not an individual perceives it; waking differs from dream.
- Note: Later Advaitins present dṛṣṭi-sṛṣṭi as a teaching for advanced students and sṛṣṭi-dṛṣṭi for the rest; both lead to the uncreated (GK 3.15).

## RQ-100 — Must jhāna precede insight, or can insight proceed on momentary concentration (dry insight)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:dry-insight-or-jhana-first` · skeleton

- **lin:mahasi**: Continuous noting develops momentary concentration strong enough to suppress the hindrances; the pure-insight vehicle (sukkhavipassaka) reaches the paths without prior jhāna.
- **lin:pa-auk**: At least access concentration, preferably jhāna, is needed; its light lets the meditator see ultimate materiality and mentality, without which insight is into concepts.
- **lin:thai-forest**: Calm and insight are developed together; the citta must converge in samādhi to have the strength for wisdom, but wisdom can also lead to samādhi.
- Note: The Visuddhimagga itself recognizes two vehicles — calm-first and pure insight — suited to different practitioners.

## RQ-101 — Is liberation only the cessation of suffering, or also the attainment of lordship?  
`dsp:duhkhanta-cessation-or-lordship` · skeleton

- **lin:pasupata**: It is twofold: cessation and lordship; elsewhere liberation is only cessation or isolation, but even those liberated by Sāṃkhya-Yoga remain paśus.
- **lin:samkhya**: Liberation is isolation (kaivalya) of the person from matter (position as characterised by the Pāśupatas; the Sāṃkhya unit holds the texts).
- **lin:nyaya**: Liberation (apavarga) is absolute release from pain; Yāmuna even reports the Pāśupatas' end of suffering in these terms, as the destruction of the soul's special qualities.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the impersonal aspect (cessation) is common ground; the personal aspect (lordship) is the Pāśupata's distinctive claim.
  - No reconciliation: the Pāśupatas deny that the Sāṃkhya-liberated are free.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Pāśupatas insist their goal exceeds cessation and isolation.

## RQ-102 — How long is the road of the dead to Yama's city?  
`dsp:duration-of-the-road-to-yama` · sourced

- **lin:puranic**: Twelve days: the dead sees his house for twelve days, then reaches Dharmarāja's city (MkP 10.69-76).
- **lin:puranic**: A year: sixteen cities are passed with the monthly piṇḍas (Garuḍa Pretakalpa 2.5.85-154).
- **lin:puranic**: Two or three muhūrtas for ninety-nine thousand yojanas (BhP 3.30.24).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint (ritual): the Garuḍa's year follows the ritual calendar of monthly śrāddhas; MkP's twelve days the daśāha/dvādaśāha rites.
  - Different accounts for different kinds of sinner (the Bhāgavata speaks of the attached householder dragged at speed).
  - P7-arthavāda: the durations dramatise the terror of the road.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: three texts give different durations; recorded as a textual divergence rather than a historical debate.

## RQ-U14-06 — Must the surrendered keep the scriptural duties, and are deliberate offences after surrender to be expiated? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:duties-after-prapatti` · skeleton

- **lin:vadakalai**: Yes: duties are the Lord's command (ājñā); neglecting them offends him; deliberate offences require expiation or repeated surrender as expiation.
- **lin:tenkalai**: Duties are kept as service (kainkarya) and for the example of the world, not as means nor under threat; the Lord's grace covers offences, though offences against devotees are gravest.
- Note: Both schools require the surrendered to keep scriptural conduct; they differ on its motive and on expiation — the ethical-social practice converges.

## RQ-104 — Is the Great Perfection the 'sudden' Chan of Moheyan, rejected at Samye, in another guise? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:dzogchen-and-chan` · skeleton

- **lin:sakya**: Critics (notably Sakya Paṇḍita in his Clear Differentiation of the Three Vows) held that teachings claiming liberation by recognizing the mind's nature without accumulations or analysis — the 'white panacea' and a 'Chinese-style Great Perfection' — revive the view of the Chinese master defeated at Samye.
- **lin:nyingma**: The Nyingma deny the identification: Nupchen's Lamp for the Eye in Contemplation already ranks the simultaneous approach of Chan below Mahāyoga and Atiyoga; the Great Perfection is recognition of awareness, not the suppression of thought; its lineage is Indian (Garab Dorje, Mañjuśrīmitra, Śrī Siṃha, Vimalamitra).
- Candidate readings:
  - Dzogchen and Chan are independent 'sudden' teachings with different views of non-conceptuality (Nyingma position).
  - Dzogchen absorbed Chan elements in the 8th-10th c. (critics' position).
- Note: The 'effortless' teaching and the gradual path are addressed to different capacities, as the Kunjed Gyalpo itself states (ch.44); the question of historical derivation is not reconciled.

## RQ-U32-earth-rotation — Does the earth rotate, or do the stars revolve around a fixed earth?  
`dsp:earth-rotation` · skeleton

- **lin:jyotisa**: Āryabhaṭa: as one in a moving boat sees fixed objects moving backward, so the fixed stars are seen moving westward at Laṅkā — the earth turns.
- **lin:jyotisa**: Brahmagupta (and Lalla and others): if the earth moved a minute of arc in a prāṇa, where would it go, and why would tall things not fall?
- Candidate readings:
  - Later siddhāntas (SS, Bhāskara II) keep a stationary earth-globe held by Brahman's or its own power; Āryabhaṭa's verse was reinterpreted by some commentators as describing appearance only.
  - P2-standpoint: description of relative appearance (Āryabhaṭa) vs cosmological account (SS 12) — whether the texts support this is not settled.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U47-2 — Are Tsongkhapa's eight 'difficult points' genuinely Candrakīrti's Prāsaṅgika?  
`dsp:eight-difficult-points` · skeleton

- **lin:gelug**: Prāsaṅgika uniquely rejects an ālaya distinct from the six consciousnesses and reflexive awareness even conventionally, rejects autonomous inference, accepts external objects conventionally, holds that śrāvakas realise the selflessness of phenomena, that grasping at the self of phenomena is an affliction, that disintegration is a functioning thing, and so presents the three times uniquely.
- **lin:sakya**: Sakya critics (Taktsang Lotsawa, Gorampa, Shākya Chokden) held several of these to be Tsongkhapa's innovations, charging contradictions (Taktsang's 'eighteen great burdens'): e.g. accepting conventional valid cognition and external objects under the name of Prāsaṅgika, or making disintegration a thing.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: Gelug systematises what conventional designation requires once inherent existence is denied; the critics speak from the standpoint of the unexamined worldly convention.
  - P5-neyartha: the disagreement is over which passages of Candrakīrti are definitive.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the dispute concerns which positions are Candrakīrti's.

## RQ-107 — Is there one jīva or are there many? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:eka-jiva-aneka-jiva` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Eka-jīva-vāda (Prakāśānanda; surveyed by Madhusūdana): there is one jīva; other beings are its dream-like projections; with its knowledge all is liberated.
- **lin:bhamati**: Aneka-jīva-vāda: jīvas are many, each with its own ignorance or inner organ, so one is liberated while others remain bound.
- Note: Both are empirical-level accounts; at the ultimate level neither one nor many jīvas exist (GK 3.48); the tradition lists both among the methods.

## RQ-108 — When an Ekādaśī is 'pierced' by the Daśamī, on which day should the fast be kept? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:ekadasi-viddha` · skeleton

- **lin:dharmasastra**: The Smārta digests decide by the extent of the tithi at sunrise and in the relevant part of the day, with their own rules on piercing (vedha).
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Vaiṣṇavas reject an Ekādaśī touched by the Daśamī even at dawn (aruṇodaya) and fast on the following day (mahādvādaśī rules).
- Note: Each community keeps the timing rule of its own path: the Smārta rule for the general householder, the Vaiṣṇava rule for devotees who take the vow as devotion.

## RQ-109 — Śiva or the Jina — the contest at Abalūr  
`dsp:ekanta-ramayya-jainas` · skeleton

- **lin:jainism**: The Jainas of Abalūr defended the Jina's supremacy against Rāmayya (known only through the Śaiva record: reported by the other side).
- **lin:virasaiva**: Ēkānta Rāmayya staked his head on Śiva's supremacy, cut it off and received it back; the shrine passed to Śiva.
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path: devotion to different chosen ultimates (Śiva, the Jina) — rejected by both parties, each of which denies the other's ultimate.
- Note: not yet reconciled: a contest over supremacy that each tradition settles in its own favour

## RQ-110 — Is exclusive devotion through the name, without Vedic rites, images or brahmin priests, a valid dharma? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:ekasarana-and-brahmin-orthodoxy` · skeleton

- **lin:smarta**: Rites, image worship, brahmin priesthood and the worship of the gods enjoined by smṛti are obligatory; a teaching that sets them aside and initiates all castes is heresy.
- **lin:ekasarana**: In the Kali age the name of Kṛṣṇa is the one refuge and the true purport of the Vedas; rites without devotion are empty (the Brahmin wives who fed Kṛṣṇa).
- Note: Ritual and devotional paths are distinct and suit different seekers; the Bhāgavata the Ekaśaraṇa follows itself praises the name above rites for Kali.

## RQ-U26-06 — Must the devotee of Kṛṣṇa abstain from worshipping other deities (and from animal sacrifice to them)?  
`dsp:ekasarana-and-other-deities` · skeleton

- **lin:sakta**: The Goddess is to be worshipped with the offerings vowed to her, including animal sacrifice; neglecting her brings harm.
- **lin:ekasarana**: Worship of Kṛṣṇa alone suffices, as watering the root of a tree nourishes all its branches (BhP 4.31.14); worship of other gods breaks the single refuge.
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path (iṣṭa-devatā): each tradition's chosen deity — rejected by Ekaśaraṇa's single refuge.
  - Ekaśaraṇa's own formula (root and branches): all gods are satisfied through Kṛṣṇa — which the Śākta side would not accept as honouring the Goddess.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: Ekaśaraṇa's exclusivity rejects the plurality a P3 reading would assume.

## RQ-112 — Are the gods eligible for the knowledge of Brahman and for meditations such as the honey-meditation (BS 1.3.26–33)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:eligibility-of-gods` · skeleton

- **lin:vedanta**: Jaimini: not eligible for the honey-meditation etc., since they themselves are its objects.
- **lin:vedanta**: Bādarāyaṇa: they are eligible for Brahman-knowledge, having the capacity and desire.
- Note: Śaṅkara restricts Jaimini's denial to meditations in which the gods themselves are the objects, while all beings with capacity are eligible for Brahman-knowledge.

## RQ-113 — How are the means of knowledge themselves established?  
`dsp:establishment-of-pramanas` · skeleton

- **lin:madhyamaka**: If means of knowledge establish objects, what establishes the means? Other means lead to a regress; self-establishment would equally establish objects without means; the lamp does not illuminate itself (Nāgārjuna, Vigrahavyāvartanī).
- **lin:nyaya**: The 'three times' objection defeats itself; denying all means of knowledge undermines the denial; a means of knowledge can also be an object of knowledge, like a balance; means are established like the lamp's light.
- **lin:madhyamaka**: U40 contribution: the means of knowledge establish nothing by own-nature. If they establish objects, what establishes them? Other means lead to infinite regress; self-establishment fails, as fire does not illumine itself; establishment by their objects makes each depend on the other, so neither is established.
- **lin:nyaya**: U40 contribution (as the Vigrahavyāvartanī's objector puts it): things are established by perception and the other means of knowledge; to deny them one must apprehend them by these very means, which the denier therefore cannot reject.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: Candrakīrti accepts the worldly means of knowledge conventionally while denying their ultimate establishment; Nyāya's defence could be read as a defence of conventional validity.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-114 — Is sound (the word) eternal?  
`dsp:eternality-of-sound` · skeleton

- **lin:nyaya**: No: sound has a beginning, is sense-perceived and is spoken of like produced things; it is a non-eternal quality of ākāśa. The word–meaning relation is conventional (later: the Lord's convention).
- **lin:vaisesika**: No: sound is produced from conjunction, disjunction and sound; understanding meaning from words is conventional.
- **lin:mimamsa**: Yes: the word (and its phonemes) is eternal and its relation to meaning original; what is produced is only its manifestation by utterance.
- **lin:vyakarana**: The meaning-bearing word (sphoṭa) is eternal, manifested by sounds (Bhartṛhari).
- **lin:mimamsa**: Words — the letters — are eternal and only manifested by utterance: utterance serves to convey to another; the same word is recognized everywhere; utterances are counted as repetitions of one word; no cause of destruction is known; the Veda speaks of eternal speech.
- **lin:nyaya**: Sound is a non-eternal quality of ether: it has a beginning, is perceived by a sense and is spoken of as produced; the Veda's authority comes from its reliable author, not from eternal words.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Everything produced is momentary; words signify by convention; no eternal, authorless word exists.
- **lin:vyakarana**: The word is eternal, but as the sphoṭa manifested by sounds, not as the audible letters.
- **lin:vyakarana**: Word, meaning and their relation are eternal (Kātyāyana's first vārttika); the eternal word is the sphoṭa, while the audible sounds that manifest it arise and perish.
- **lin:mimamsa**: The letters themselves are eternal and only manifested by utterance.
- **lin:nyaya**: Sound is a non-eternal quality of ether: it has a beginning and is spoken of as produced.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: distinguish produced sound (dhvani) from the word as a type; Mīmāṃsakas grant that manifesting sounds are produced, and Naiyāyikas that the universal of a phoneme is eternal.
  - P2-standpoint: grammarians and Mīmāṃsakas agree on eternality and differ on its bearer (sphoṭa vs letters); Nyāya's 'sound' may be the audible noise (dhvani) that Mīmāṃsā too treats as produced (MS 1.1.17) — a partial overlap each side would qualify.
  - P1-level: what is eternal (the word as meaning-bearer) and what is produced (the audible sound) are distinguished by the grammarians; Nyāya speaks of the audible sound only.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U32-exalted-malefics — Do exalted malefic planets make a king?  
`dsp:exalted-malefics-rajayoga` · skeleton

- **lin:yavana-jataka**: The Yavanas: with malefics in their exaltations a king is born, but cruel-minded.
- **lin:jyotisa**: Jīvaśarman: with (exalted) malefics one does not become a king.
- Candidate readings:
  - Varāhamihira's subsequent rules (BJ 11.2 ff.) count exalted planets without excluding malefics, which leans to the Yavana view.
- Note: Not yet reconciled; Varāhamihira records both views before giving his own royal yogas.

## RQ-116 — May the nenbutsu be practised exclusively, setting aside all other practices and deities?  
`dsp:exclusive-nenbutsu-controversy` · skeleton

- **lin:jodo-shu**: Yes: the primal vow selected the nenbutsu alone; set aside the holy path, the miscellaneous practices and the auxiliary acts.
- **lin:faxiang**: (Kōfukuji, Jōkei) No: exclusive nenbutsu slights Śākyamuni and the kami, hinders all good practices, misunderstands the nenbutsu as mere calling, harms the order and disturbs the nation (nine errors).
- **lin:huayan**: (Myōe) The Senchakushū discards bodhicitta and calls holy-path practitioners bandits.
- **lin:nichiren**: (Nichiren) The nenbutsu leads to the Avīci hell; the Senchakushū's 'set aside' is slander of the Lotus (Risshō ankoku ron).
- **lin:jodo-shinshu**: (Shinran) The persecution was contrary to the Dharma; he became neither monk nor layman.
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path: exclusive nenbutsu as one temperament's path, all others valid for others (Hōnen's own Seven-Article Pledge forbids disparaging other schools)
  - P4-stage / age: selection as fitted to the last age (Daochuo's time and capacity)
- Note: Not yet reconciled: an exclusivist claim about the one practice for this age against the view that exclusivity denies the other teachings.

## RQ-U38-02 — Do past and future dharmas exist?  
`dsp:existence-in-three-times` · skeleton

- **lin:sarvastivada**: Yes: all dharmas exist in all three times as real entities; the times are distinguished by activity (kāritra).
- **lin:sautrantika**: No: only the present exists; the conditioned 'arises having not been, and having been ceases'; results arise from the transformed series preceded by the karma.
- **lin:kasyapiya**: Partly: past karma that has not yet given its fruit exists; the rest of the past and the future do not (the Vibhajyavāda position).
- **lin:theravada**: No: 'everything exists' is refuted.
- **lin:mahasanghika**: No: past and future have no real existence (Vasumitra).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Vaibhāṣika speaks of a dharma's own-nature (substance, dravyārthika) which does not change, the Sautrāntika of its activity and moment (mode, paryāyārthika); the Vaibhāṣika itself distinguishes the times only by activity — rejected by both, since each affirms or denies real existence, not a standpoint.
  - Soteriological reading: both explain how past karma bears fruit and how past defilements can be known and abandoned — by existence (Vaibhāṣika) or by the transformed series (Sautrāntika).
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Sarvāstivāda affirms the real existence of past and future dharmas; the Sautrāntika, Mahāsāṃghika, Mahīśāsaka and Theravāda deny it, the Kāśyapīya partly.

## RQ-118 — Is there a store-consciousness (ālaya-vijñāna) distinct from the six consciousnesses?  
`dsp:existence-of-alaya` · skeleton

- **lin:yogacara**: Yes: without a continuous, neutral consciousness holding the seeds, one could not explain the appropriation of a new body, the survival of seeds through the mindless attainments, the simultaneous occurrence of several cognitions, the karmic process and death.
- **lin:sarvastivada**: No: there are six consciousnesses; the continuity of karma is explained by possession (prāpti) and by the dharmas existing in the three times; mind re-arises after cessation from the prior mind.
- **lin:sautrantika**: No separate store is needed: seeds are carried by the mental series (with the body) itself, and mind re-arises after cessation from seeds in the body-mind series.
- **lin:prasangika**: The ālaya was taught with an intention, for those who need it; it is not ultimately established and not even conventionally accepted as Yogācāra teaches it.
- Candidate readings:
  - P5-neyartha: Prāsaṅgika reads the ālaya teaching as provisional; Yogācāra reads it as definitive — the disagreement is about which teaching is provisional.
  - P2-standpoint: the ālaya answers the question of continuity from the standpoint of the series (santāna); the Sautrāntika seed-in-series may describe the same continuity without reifying a separate consciousness.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the schools differ on whether a store-consciousness exists.

## RQ-119 — Does a single, unconscious primal matter (pradhāna) exist as the material cause of the manifold world?  
`dsp:existence-of-pradhana` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: Yes: the unmanifest is inferred from its effects, which pre-exist in it.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: No: a single permanent cause cannot produce successive, momentary effects; the arguments from homogeneity and pre-existence fail.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: substance (dravyārthika) vs mode (paryāyārthika) — enduring prakṛti vs changing modifications; both traditions reject this: Sāṃkhya holds the modifications real transformations of one substance, Buddhists deny any enduring substrate.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: permanence and momentariness are both insisted upon.

## RQ-120 — Are there objects external to cognition?  
`dsp:external-objects` · skeleton

- **lin:yogacara**: No (as the pūrvapakṣa is stated): the notion of means and objects of knowledge is like the notion of objects in a dream, or like magic, a gandharva city or a mirage.
- **lin:nyaya**: Yes: the idealist thesis lacks a reason; dream-cognition depends on prior experience like memory; false cognition is removed by true knowledge as dream-notions are on waking, which presupposes real objects.
- **lin:sautrantika**: External objects exist but are not directly perceived; they are inferred from the images they cause in cognition (the 'Sautrāntika' position in later doxography).
- **lin:vaibhasika**: External objects are directly perceived: sense-cognition grasps the aggregate of atoms.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: The object-condition cannot be external atoms (Dignāga); blue and its cognition are necessarily apprehended together and so are not different (Dharmakīrti); but Dharmakīrti also argues at the level where external objects are assumed.
- **lin:yogacara-madhyamaka**: Conventionally there are no external objects (mind-only); ultimately mind too is neither one nor many.
- **lin:mimamsa**: Yes: waking cognitions are not overturned as dream cognitions are; cognition is formless while the object has form; a cloth-cognition regularly arises only from threads (the Vṛttikāra in Śabara; ŚV nirālambanavāda); in dreams too the objects are real ones of other times and places.
- **lin:yogacara**: No independent external object is established: cognition and its object are always apprehended together (sahopalambha-niyama) and cognitions arise as in dreams from latent impressions.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: Yogācāra's own three natures leave room for conventional objects.
  - P4-stage: some Buddhist doxographies treat mind-only as a stage leading beyond itself.
  - P1-level: Yogācāra may be read as describing the ultimate while conceding conventional objects — Mīmāṃsā admits no level beyond the empirical and rejects this.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-121 — Is outer worship (of the Śrīcakra or image) necessary, or is inner worship alone the true worship? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:external-or-internal-worship` · skeleton

- **lin:kaula-srividya**: Outer cakra-worship is done daily even by Śiva and by all knowers, since it produces the conviction of non-difference; the inner and the supreme follow.
- **lin:samaya-srividya**: Only inner worship in the heart-space is admitted; outer worship is Kaula and rejected.
- **lin:srividya**: True worship is the contemplation of the non-difference of knower, knowing and known; the offerings are inner acts (Bhāvanā Upaniṣad); supreme worship is not the offering of outer flowers but abiding in the non-dual (Saṅketapaddhati quoted in the Dīpikā).
- **lin:puranic**: The Devī Gītā teaches both: outer worship, Vedic and tantric, and inner worship, which is higher.
- Note: Most of the texts themselves grade outer, meditative and non-dual worship as stages (Yoginīhṛdaya's three pūjās; the Devī Gītā), so outer and inner worship are read as suited to different stages.

## RQ-U21-1 — What is liberation? (the Amaraughaśāsana against rival definitions)  
`dsp:false-views-of-moksa` · skeleton

- **lin:natha**: Liberation is not the cutting off of karma, Veda recitation, the supportless state, meditation on form, bindu or nāda, the bliss of Kaula union, the movement of kuṇḍalinī or an even gaze; it is the mind seeing the mind in innate absorption, liberation while living.
- **lin:mimamsa**: The Veda's injunction is the means of knowing dharma, and its enjoined acts bring their results (the position the AmŚ lists as 'liberation rests on Veda recitation').
- **lin:yogini-kaula**: Kaula rites of worship, wine, meat and union bring enjoyment and liberation (the position the AmŚ lists as 'bliss of union is liberation'); Kaula texts themselves speak of bhukti and mukti from the rites.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: the rejected practices as preparatory, liberation as sahaja-samādhi (the AmŚ denies them the name 'liberation', not all value).
  - P1-level: the rival definitions speak of conventional attainments, the AmŚ of the ultimate state.
- Note: not yet reconciled; the Amaraughaśāsana states its rivals only in its own summary, and their own strongest statements of liberation are still to be supplied

## RQ-123 — What moves events — fate, time, nature, the Lord, karma, or human effort? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:fate-or-effort` · skeleton

- **lin:epic-teaching**: Nature (svabhāva): all states arise and cease by their own nature; human effort is not the cause (Prahlāda).
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Time (kāla): Time takes and gives all; do not boast of valour (Bali).
- **lin:epic-teaching**: The Lord's ordaining: the Lord in the heart moves all beings as if mounted on a machine (BhG 18.61); the warriors are already slain by him — be the instrument (11.33); Draupadī complains that the Creator plays with beings like puppets.
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Human effort (puruṣakāra): nothing is gained without effort; fate does not succeed without effort as a seed without a field (Draupadī; Bhīṣma).
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Karma: the serpent, Death and Time each disclaim causing the boy's death until his own past action is named the cause (Gautamī).
- Note: The Gītā gives five causes of every action — the body, the agent, the instruments, the activities and daiva (18.14) — so each position names one causal factor from its own standpoint; Bhīṣma's seed-and-field image joins fate and effort (13.6.7).

## RQ-124 — May fish be eaten with milk?  
`dsp:fish-with-milk` · skeleton

- **lin:ayurveda**: Bhadrakāpya: all fish may be taken with milk except the cilicima.
- **lin:ayurveda**: Ātreya: no fish at all with milk, the cilicima especially.
- Candidate readings:
  - Bhadrakāpya names the most dangerous case; Ātreya generalizes the rule (degree, not kind)
- Note: Not yet reconciled by a principle; the tradition follows Ātreya's stricter rule.

## RQ-125 — Does the arhat still have imperfections (the five points of Mahādeva)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:five-points-of-mahadeva` · skeleton

- **lin:mahasanghika**: Yes (as reported by opponents): an arhat can be tempted by others to an impure emission, still has non-knowing and doubt, is brought to understanding by others, and the path arises with an utterance.
- **lin:purvasaila**: Yes (Kathāvatthu commentary on Kv 2.1–2.6 names the Pubbaseliyas and Aparaseliyas).
- **lin:sarvastivada**: No: these are heresies; Mahādeva was a sinner who caused the schism (Vibhāṣā). Yet the Kośa itself holds that only the Buddha has destroyed non-defiled ignorance.
- **lin:theravada**: No: each of the points is refuted.
- Note: Points two and three (non-knowing, doubt) can be read as non-defiled ignorance and uncertainty about matters outside liberation, which the Sarvāstivāda itself attributes to arhats (AK 1.1 bhāṣya); the other points remain not yet reconciled.

## RQ-126 — How do the four yogas of Mahāmudrā correspond to the five paths and ten grounds — in particular, at which yoga is the path of seeing (the first ground) reached?  
`dsp:four-yogas-and-the-grounds` · skeleton

- **lin:kagyu**: One strand places the path of seeing with the arising of simplicity, when the mind's nature is first seen as unborn.
- **lin:kagyu**: Another strand places it later (with one taste) or holds that the yogas and grounds cannot be matched one-to-one because persons progress differently.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: the correlations differ because they describe gradual and simultaneous persons (Ājñāsamyakpramāṇa 271a.3)
  - the four yogas and the ten grounds are different maps not meant to be aligned one-to-one
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the correlations of individual Kagyu masters are not recorded reliably here.

## RQ-U16-5 — Is the ultimate an attributeless Brahman to be realised by 'that thou art', or the personal Bhagavān reached by devotion? (Caitanya against the Advaita renouncers)  
`dsp:gaudiya-critique-of-advaita` · skeleton

- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Caitanya (CC 1.7, 2.6): the Vedānta's direct sense teaches a personal Lord with transcendental qualities and powers; Brahman is his effulgence; the world is pariṇāma of his power; the great saying is Oṃ; Śaṅkara took a secondary sense; atheism that relies on the Veda is worse than Buddhism; love, not liberation, is the goal.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Advaita (Sārvabhauma and Prakāśānanda before their conversion, as presented by the Gauḍīya texts; and Advaita's own texts): Brahman is attributeless; 'that thou art' teaches identity; forms and qualities belong to the conventional level.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level (Advaita's reading: saguṇa at the conventional level) — explicitly rejected by the Gauḍīyas
  - BhP 1.2.11 (one reality, three names) as a shared text read by both, each ranking the names differently
  - P3-path
- Note: not yet reconciled: a direct disagreement on the nature of the ultimate.

## RQ-U16-6 — Do the Gauḍīyas belong to Madhva's lineage (Brahma-Mādhva sampradāya)?  
`dsp:gaudiya-sampradaya-affiliation` · skeleton

- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Tradition (Kavikarṇapūra's Gauragaṇoddeśadīpikā, 1576; Baladeva's Prameyaratnāvalī): the guru-succession runs from Madhva through Lakṣmīpati Tīrtha, Mādhavendra Purī and Īśvara Purī to Caitanya; Baladeva's Govinda-bhāṣya gave the school its Brahma Sūtra commentary.
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Doubts within and about the record: the Six Gosvāmīs do not state the Madhva affiliation in their main works, and Jīva cites Madhva chiefly as a source of rare quotations; Gauḍīya doctrine (acintya-bhedābheda, the gopīs' love) differs from Dvaita; modern scholars regard the link as asserted later.
- **lin:dvaita**: The Mādhva tradition does not count the Gauḍīyas among its own maṭhas (summary; to verify).
- Candidate readings:
  - distinguish initiation-lineage (paramparā through the Purīs) from doctrinal school
  - treat the affiliation as the tradition's account and the doubt as the scholarly account (P8 not applicable)
- Note: not yet reconciled: a historical question of lineage on which the tradition's account and the doubts are recorded side by side.

## RQ-129 — What is the Gītā's principal teaching — knowledge, action, devotion, or their combination? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:gita-primary-teaching` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Liberation comes from knowledge of the self alone, steadfastness in knowledge preceded by renunciation of all works; action done as an offering purifies the mind for that knowledge; devotion leads to knowledge; the combination of knowledge and works is not the means.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: The Gītā teaches Nārāyaṇa, the supreme Brahman, reached by bhakti-yoga, which is produced by one's own dharma (karma-yoga), knowledge and detachment; the first hexad teaches the yogas of action and knowledge leading to vision of the self, the middle hexad bhakti-yoga; 18.66 teaches surrender.
- **lin:dvaita**: The Gītā teaches the supremacy of Viṣṇu and the eternal difference of the selves; liberation is by his grace through devotion grounded in knowledge of his supremacy, with desireless action as worship.
- **lin:kashmir-saivism**: Knowledge and action go together: the one established in knowledge acts and is not bound (reported for Abhinavagupta's Gītārthasaṅgraha).
- **lin:bhedabheda**: Knowledge combined with works (jñāna-karma-samuccaya) is the means to liberation.
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: The most secret teaching is pure devotion to Kṛṣṇa, the culmination of the Gītā (9.34, 18.64–66).
- **lin:epic-teaching**: (Recent) The Gītā's principal teaching is energetic action (karma-yoga) by the one who knows, for the welfare of the world, rather than renunciation.
- Candidate readings:
  - knowledge as the direct means, action and devotion preparatory (Advaita)
  - bhakti/prapatti as the means, action and knowledge preparatory (Viśiṣṭādvaita)
  - devotion grounded in knowledge of Viṣṇu's supremacy, by grace (Dvaita)
  - knowledge combined with action (Bhāskara; Abhinavagupta as reported)
  - pure devotion as the most secret teaching (Gauḍīya)
  - action by the enlightened for the world's welfare (Tilak, recent)
- Note: The Gītā itself names plural paths by temperament (3.3; 13.24–25) and grades them by capacity (6.3; 12.8–11), so all schools agree the four yogas are taught; which is the final, direct means of liberation is not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U33-6 — Was Gosāla Mahāvīra's disciple and a false Jina, or an independent Jina in his seventh reanimation?  
`dsp:gosala-mahavira` · skeleton

- **lin:jainism**: Gosāla was Maṅkhali's son and Mahāvīra's disciple for six years; he falsely claimed to be a Jina, burnt two monks, was undone by his own fiery power, and confessed on his deathbed.
- **lin:ajivika**: (Ājīvika claim, known only from the Jain report) The disciple Gosāla is dead; the one speaking is Udāyi Kuṇḍiyāyaṇa in his seventh reanimation, a Jina and the last tīrthaṅkara of the age; Mahāvīra has changed from a solitary ascetic to a teacher of crowds.
- Candidate readings:
  - None proposed; the Ājīvika side has no surviving voice.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: a question of fact and legitimacy recorded only by one party; no principle applies.

## RQ-131 — Are the liberated graded in bliss, or equal (or one)?  
`dsp:gradation-in-liberation` · skeleton

- **lin:dvaita**: The liberated are graded: each class enjoys a hundredfold more bliss than the one below, up to Brahmā and Vāyu; the five differences and gradation are not lost in liberation.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: In liberation there is only the one Brahman, whose bliss is without degrees; the graded blisses of scripture (e.g. Taittirīya 2.8) are portions of that bliss experienced under limiting adjuncts, not differences among the liberated.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: The liberated are equal to Brahman in enjoyment of his bliss, differing from him only in not governing the world; there is no gradation among the released.
- Candidate readings:
  - {"principle": "P1-level", "reading": "Gradation belongs to the relative level, equality or identity to the ultimate."}
  - {"principle": "P7-arthavada", "reading": "The hundredfold lists are praise of liberation, not doctrine."}
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U38-04 — Are the four truths realized gradually (in successive moments) or in a single moment?  
`dsp:gradual-or-single-realization` · skeleton

- **lin:sarvastivada**: Gradually: sixteen moments of acceptance and knowledge, dharma- and subsequent knowledge for each truth; fifteen are the path of seeing.
- **lin:theravada**: In one moment: path-knowledge penetrates the four truths simultaneously (Theravāda commentarial position; low confidence on the canonical Kathāvatthu reference).
- **lin:mahasanghika**: In one moment (ascribed to the Mahāsāṃghika side in doxographic summaries; low confidence).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the sixteen moments describe the phases of one path-event from the side of its objects (the truths and realms), the single moment from the side of its function (abandonment) — rejected by the Vaibhāṣika, for whom the moments are distinct dharmas.
  - P4-stage: gradual in the preparatory roots of penetration, single in the path itself.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the realization of the four truths as sixteen successive moments or as one.

## RQ-133 — Do the planets cause a person's fortune, or only reveal the karma that causes it? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:grahas-cause-or-sign` · skeleton

- **lin:jyotisa**: The planets are signs: the horā reveals (abhivyanakti) the ripening of karma earned in a former birth, as a lamp shows objects already in the dark; portents 'announce' (saṃsūcayanti) calamity that men's sins have prepared.
- **lin:jyotisa**: The planets are divine agents: Janārdana in the form of the planets gives beings the fruits of their karma; the happiness and sorrow of all creatures depend on them, and the rise and fall of kings; therefore they are to be worshipped and pacified.
- Note: Both accounts make one's own karma the ground of fruit; 'sign' is the causal-epistemic standpoint (what the astrologer reads), 'dispenser' the divine standpoint (who delivers it).

## RQ-134 — Are some Purāṇas sāttvika and authoritative, others rājasa or tāmasa and to be avoided? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:guna-ranking-of-puranas` · sourced

- **lin:puranic**: Purāṇas are graded by the guṇas according to the deity they glorify (Matsya 53.68-69); the Padma (as cited) makes the Vaiṣṇava ones sāttvika, the Śaiva ones tāmasa; the Garuḍa's Brahmakāṇḍa forbids seekers of liberation to hear the rājasa and tāmasa ones except their sāttvika parts.
- **lin:puranic**: All eighteen are Vyāsa's and establish dharma (KūP 1.11.279); each Purāṇa glorifies Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Sūrya and Rudra (Matsya 53.66); Śaiva Purāṇas exalt Śiva and reverse the ranking.
- Note: Under P7 the exaltations of one deity are praise (arthavāda) addressed to worshippers of that deity (P3), so the rankings need not deny the other Purāṇas' dharma.

## RQ-135 — Is the Four Tantras (rgyud bzhi) the word of the Buddha, or a treatise composed in Tibet?  
`dsp:gyushi-buddha-word` · skeleton

- **lin:sowa-rigpa**: The Four Tantras is the word of the Medicine Buddha, taught through his emanations Rigpé Yeshe and Yilé Kyé in Tanaduk, brought to Tibet and translated by Vairocana, concealed and recovered as treasure, and only edited by Yuthok; its form ('Thus have I heard…') is that of a tantra.
- **lin:zurkharpa-medicine**: A strand of Tibetan medical scholarship held (or seriously considered) that the Four Tantras was composed in Tibet — by Yuthok — drawing on Indian and other sources, pointing to Tibetan elements in the text and the absence of an Indian original.
- Candidate readings:
  - Buddha-word in the full sense (the tradition's account).
  - 'Blessed word': a teaching composed through the Buddha's blessing, counted as authoritative scripture though written down or arranged in Tibet (candidate reading; not attested here).
  - A Tibetan treatise (bstan bcos) whose authority rests on its efficacy and lineage rather than on origin.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U26-04 — Is the Haridāsī (Sakhī) sampradāya a branch of the Nimbārka sampradāya or an independent tradition?  
`dsp:haridasi-nimbarka-affiliation` · skeleton

- **lin:dvaitadvaita**: Svāmī Haridās was initiated by Āśudhīra in the Nimbārka line; the Haridāsīs are a Nimbārkī branch.
- **lin:haridasi**: Svāmī Haridās founded an independent path of grove-worship (and is himself Lalitā sakhī); the tradition owes nothing essential to other sampradāyas.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: formal initiation lineage vs doctrinal independence.
  - Historical: which branch (householder gosvāmīs or Ṭaṭṭī Sthān ascetics) holds which view needs sourcing.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the branches of the Haridāsī tradition themselves differ.

## RQ-137 — Is haṭha a necessary means to rājayoga, and has it any value apart from it? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:hatha-vs-raja` · skeleton

- **lin:hatha-yoga**: Haṭha and rājayoga need each other: haṭha is taught solely for rājayoga, both are practised to completion, and mere haṭha without knowing rājayoga is fruitless labour (the Haṭhapradīpikā and Śiva Saṃhitā).
- **lin:hatha-yoga**: Rājayoga needs no haṭha: the mindless state arises effortlessly by the guru's grace; breath-control, mudrās and similar methods are useless or harmful (the Amanaska, and in part the Yogatārāvalī).
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Rājayoga is Patañjali's yoga of cessation (asamprajñāta); haṭha, laya and mantra are auxiliary means subsumed under the eight limbs (the Jyotsnā's gloss; Nārāyaṇatīrtha).
- Note: The texts themselves assign methods by the student's fitness (SS 5.10–14) and say eight-limbed yoga and haṭha differ in practice, not fruit (DYŚ 119–120).

## RQ-U44-higher-consecrations-monastics — May celibate monastics receive the secret and wisdom consecrations?  
`dsp:higher-consecrations-for-monastics` · skeleton

- **lin:kadam**: No: forbidden by the Ādibuddha tantra; monastics may receive the master's consecration, study, teach and perform rituals.
- **lin:kalacakra**: The Kālacakra tradition ranks the fully ordained monk as the best vajra master (with novice and householder below) and gives the higher consecrations within its system (reported; low confidence).
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: monastics take the higher consecrations only in visualized form (a later Tibetan solution)
  - P3-path: monastics follow the perfection way with the lower tantras
  - Literal restriction by vow (Atiśa) versus ranking of monk-masters (Kālacakra)
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-139 — Is all activity the function of buddha-nature (Hongzhou), or must numinous knowing be recognised and cultivated (Zongmi)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:hongzhou-all-activity-buddha-nature` · skeleton

- **lin:hongzhou**: The Way needs no cultivation — only do not defile it; the ordinary mind is the Way; walking, standing, sitting and lying, responding to things — all is the Way; this very mind is buddha.
- **lin:heze**: Hongzhou equates every movement — greed and anger as much as compassion — with buddha-nature and so neglects the essential 'empty and calm knowing' and the need for gradual cultivation after awakening.
- **lin:chan**: Nanyang Huizhong criticises those who take seeing, hearing and knowing as buddha-nature and the body as its perishing shell.
- Note: Mazu speaks from the standpoint of the realized ('no cultivation, only no defilement'), Zongmi from the seeker who has awakened only in understanding and must still cultivate.

## RQ-140 — How is a cognition (and its validity) known?  
`dsp:how-cognition-is-known` · skeleton

- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: A cognition is not perceived; it is inferred from the knownness (jñātatā) it produces in the object; validity belongs to it intrinsically.
- **lin:prabhakara-mimamsa**: Every cognition is self-luminous and manifests knower, known and itself together (tripuṭī); validity is known with it.
- **lin:murari-mimamsa**: A cognition and its validity are grasped by the after-cognition (anuvyavasāya) that follows it (as reported by Gaṅgeśa).
- **lin:nyaya**: A cognition is known by the after-cognition; its validity is known extrinsically, by inference from successful action.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: all three Mīmāṃsā views keep validity intrinsic and differ only on the mechanism of self-knowledge; Nyāya rejects intrinsic validity itself.
- Note: Not yet reconciled; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-141 — How does each tissue nourish the next?  
`dsp:how-dhatus-are-nourished` · skeleton

- **lin:ayurveda**: Whole transformation, as milk becomes curd (kṣīra-dadhi-nyāya).
- **lin:ayurveda**: Serial irrigation, as water flows from field to field through channels (kedārī-kulyā-nyāya).
- **lin:ayurveda**: Selective uptake, as pigeons each take their own grain from the threshing floor (khale-kapota-nyāya).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: each model describes a different aspect (transformation, transport, selection)
- Note: Not yet reconciled: three commentarial models of Ca Ci 15.15-16 (tissue formed from tissue).

## RQ-142 — How does rasa arise from the union of determinants, consequents and transient states, and where is it? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:how-rasa-arises` · skeleton

- **lin:alankara**: Lollaṭa: rasa is the stable emotion intensified by the determinants, existing in the character (Rāma) and, by imitation, in the actor.
- **lin:alankara**: Śaṅkuka: rasa is the stable emotion inferred in the actor, who is taken as Rāma like a painted horse taken as a horse.
- **lin:alankara**: Bhaṭṭa Nāyaka: rasa is neither perceived, produced nor suggested; by the powers of bhāvakatva and bhojakatva it is enjoyed in a sattva-filled repose akin to tasting Brahman.
- **lin:alankara**: Abhinavagupta: rasa is manifested (suggested) and relished in the sensitive spectator whose latent stable emotion is awakened by generalized determinants; it consists in the relish itself (Mammaṭa and Viśvanātha follow).
- Note: Abhinavagupta himself presents the earlier views as rungs of a ladder of discrimination by which the intellect climbs to the truth — a reconciliation by stages of understanding, from the tradition's own mouth.

## RQ-143 — How should the Saṃhitās be interpreted — by the rite, the gods, the self, history, or one God? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:how-to-read-the-samhitas` · sourced

- **lin:vedic-srauta**: Yāska records several schools: ritualists (yājñika), etymologists (nairukta), legend-tellers (aitihāsika), the wandering ascetics and the teachers of the self (ātmapravāda), and reads verses on the planes of the rite, the deity and the self.
- **lin:mimamsa**: Sāyaṇa (with Mīmāṃsā) explains each hymn chiefly by its use in the sacrifice; the Veda makes known the unseen means to the desired.
- **lin:dvaita**: Madhva: every hymn has several levels of meaning and all ultimately praise Viṣṇu, the one denoted by all names.
- **lin:arya-samaj**: Dayānanda (recent): the Saṃhitās teach one formless God and contain no history of persons; Agni, Indra and the rest are names of God; the ritual-mythological reading is rejected.
- **lin:integral-yoga**: Sri Aurobindo (recent): the hymns are a symbolic record of inner spiritual experience (Agni the divine will, Soma delight, ṛta the truth-consciousness).
- Note: The tradition's own multi-plane exegesis (rite, deity, self) lets ritual, theological and inner readings stand as readings of the same verse from different standpoints.

## RQ-U42-huatai — Who is the true Sixth Patriarch — was the Northern school collateral?  
`dsp:huatai-true-lineage` · skeleton

- **lin:heze**: Bodhidharma's robe and the true transmission passed from Hongren to Huineng; Shenxiu's line is collateral and its teaching gradual.
- **lin:northern-chan**: Shenxiu was the recognised heir of Hongren, honoured by the court as 'Dharma lord of the two capitals'; Puji held the patriarchal succession in the capitals.
- **lin:chan**: The Platform Sūtra itself: 'the Dharma is one school, people are northern and southern'; Shenxiu praises Huineng's 'wisdom without a teacher' and sends students to him.
- Candidate readings:
  - P6-upaya: the Platform Sūtra's own irenic reading — one Dharma, different people.
  - Both lines descend from Hongren; the 'sixth patriarch' title was contested in the 8th c.
  - Scholarly reading: the robe and verse-contest stories were composed in Shenhui's campaign.
- Note: A question of lineage legitimacy rather than doctrine; no reconciliation principle applies directly.

## RQ-145 — Who is Matsyendranātha — a Śaiva siddha, a form of the bodhisattva Karuṇāmaya, or the Buddhist siddha Lūipa? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:identity-of-matsyendra` · skeleton

- **lin:natha**: Matsyendra is the disciple of Ādinātha and guru of Gorakṣa, bringer of the Yoginīkaula; Bhairava himself as fisherman (Macchaghna) recovered the Kaula scripture.
- **lin:newar-vajrayana**: In the Kathmandu valley the deity brought from Kāmarūpa to end Gorakṣa's drought is Karuṇāmaya (Avalokiteśvara), called Buṅga-dyaḥ and Macchindranāth.
- **lin:mahasiddha**: Tibetan tradition relates Matsyendra to the siddha Lūipa ('fish-gut eater') or Mīnapa among the eighty-four.
- Note: One revered figure is described from the Śaiva, Newar Buddhist and Tibetan standpoints, each within its own lineage; the traditions agree on the fish-legend and the siddha's power.

## RQ-146 — Should consecrated images of the Jinas be worshipped? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:image-worship-and-inner-worship` · sourced

- **lin:murtipujaka**: Yes: the image is the Jina 'placed' (sthāpanā) and a support for recalling his qualities; material worship by householders leads to inner worship, and the good of the act outweighs the slight harm.
- **lin:digambara**: Yes (Bīsapantha and Terāpantha), though the Terāpantha offers only non-living substances and rejects attendant deities.
- **lin:sthanakavasi**: No: the thirty-two Āgamas contain no injunction to worship images; worship with water, flowers and lamps harms one-sensed beings; the canon's 'caitya' does not mean image-shrine in the passages cited; devotion is to the Jina's qualities and to living mendicants.
- **lin:terapanthi**: No, with the Sthānakavāsīs.
- **lin:taranapantha**: No: the shrines hold scriptures, not images.
- **lin:upanisadic**: Yogins see Śiva in the Self, not in images; images are imagined for the ignorant; for the renouncer image-worship leads to rebirth.
- **lin:ramanandi**: Forms are given to the formless for the worshippers' sake; the deity is worshipped in yantra and mantra, and without the yantra is not pleased.
- Note: All sides agree that liberation comes from the inner state (bhāva) and that the Jina grants nothing; they differ over whether material worship, which involves some harm, is a legitimate means for householders.

## RQ-U42-patriarchs — Is the Chan list of 28 Indian patriarchs authentic?  
`dsp:indian-patriarch-lineage` · skeleton

- **lin:chan**: The Dharma passed from Mahākāśyapa through 28 patriarchs to Bodhidharma (Baolin zhuan; Platform Sūtra; Qisong's defence).
- **lin:tiantai**: Critics relying on the Fu fazang yinyuan zhuan held that the transmission ended with Siṃha's execution, so the Chan continuation is unfounded.
- **lin:chan**: The Dunhuang Platform Sūtra itself gives a different list.
- Candidate readings:
  - Mind-to-mind transmission does not depend on a written list (P5-neyartha).
  - Scholarly: the list is an 8th–9th c. construction.
- Note: A historical claim; not yet reconciled.

## RQ-148 — Does a son own ancestral property from birth or only on the father's death?  
`dsp:inheritance-by-birth-or-death` · sourced

- **lin:mitaksara-school**: By birth (janmasvatva): sons have ownership in ancestral property from birth; kinship (sapiṇḍa) is sharing particles of one body.
- **lin:dayabhaga-school**: By death (uparamasvatva): ownership passes only when the father dies, falls or renounces; heirship follows the capacity to benefit the deceased by piṇḍa offerings.
- Candidate readings:
  - regional validity: each school authoritative in its region (Bengal vs elsewhere)
  - different standpoints on ownership (worldly vs scriptural)
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U38-03 — Is there an intermediate existence (antarābhava) between death and rebirth?  
`dsp:intermediate-existence` · skeleton

- **lin:sarvastivada**: Yes: existence continues without interruption like the series of a grain; the gandharva at conception, the non-returner who attains nirvāṇa 'in between', and the sūtra of the destinies prove it; the intermediate being has the form of its next life and feeds on smells.
- **lin:sammitiya**: Yes (Kathāvatthu commentary on Kv 8.2 names the Pubbaseliyas and Sammitiyas).
- **lin:theravada**: No: an intermediate existence is none of the three kinds of existence; rebirth follows death immediately.
- **lin:mahasanghika**: No (Vasumitra).
- **lin:mahisasaka**: No (Vasumitra).
- Candidate readings:
  - Scriptural reading: the sūtra terms (gandharva, antarāparinirvāyin) read literally (Sarvāstivāda) or otherwise (Theravāda: one who attains nirvāṇa in the first half of life) — no principle settles which is definitive (P5-neyartha contested).
- Note: Not yet reconciled: a disagreement about what happens between death and rebirth.

## RQ-150 — Is 'the one consisting of bliss' (ānandamaya, TU 2.5; BS 1.1.12) the supreme Brahman or a sheath?  
`dsp:is-anandamaya-brahman` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Śaṅkara (in his final view, BSBh 1.1.19): it is a sheath — '-maya' means modification and it has parts; the sūtras refer to 'Brahman the tail, the support'.
