- **lin:vedanta**: The Vṛttikāra (as reported by Śaṅkara), Rāmānuja and Madhva: the ānandamaya is Brahman, as the repetition of 'bliss' and the context show (BS 1.1.12).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: bliss as Brahman's nature (all agree) vs the 'bliss-sheath' as its adjunct-conditioned appearance.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: an exegetical disagreement about the sūtra's referent; each school denies the other's reading.

## RQ-151 — Can devotion to God be a full aesthetic rasa, or only a bhāva? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:is-bhakti-a-rasa` · skeleton

- **lin:alankara**: Classical poetics: love directed to a deity, sage, guru or king is a bhāva, not a rasa; the rasas are eight or nine (Mammaṭa).
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Rūpa Gosvāmī: love for Kṛṣṇa is the stable emotion of a true rasa, indeed the supreme one, in five primary and seven secondary forms.
- **lin:alankara**: Classical poetics (e.g. Mammaṭa's Kāvyaprakāśa): love directed to a deity is a bhāva, not a rasa; the rasas are eight or nine (with śānta).
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Rūpa Gosvāmī: love for Kṛṣṇa is the permanent emotion of a true rasa — indeed the supreme rasa — in five primary and seven secondary forms; its relish is transcendent (aprākṛta).
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Madhusūdana Sarasvatī (Bhaktirasāyana): devotion is a rasa, the melted mind taking the Lord's form (summary; to verify).
- Note: The poeticians speak of worldly spectators' relish of poetry, Rūpa of the devotee's relish of a transcendent object — different standpoints on 'rasa'.

## RQ-152 — Is blood (rakta) a doṣa alongside vāta, pitta and kapha? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:is-blood-a-fourth-dosa` · skeleton

- **lin:ayurveda**: Suśruta: the three doṣas with blood as a fourth sustain the body in origin, maintenance and dissolution; bodily diseases arise from imbalance of vāta, pitta, kapha and blood.
- **lin:ayurveda**: Caraka and Vāgbhaṭa: the doṣas are three; blood is among the seven dhātus (the vitiable); and Suśruta himself: blood is never provoked apart from the doṣas.
- Note: In the surgical standpoint blood is counted with the doṣas because it is vitiated and let out; in the internal-medicine standpoint it is a dhātu vitiated only through the doṣas — as Suśruta also says.

## RQ-153 — Is the aspiration for awakening (bodhicitta) required for birth in the Pure Land?  
`dsp:is-bodhicitta-needed-for-birth` · skeleton

- **lin:pure-land**: (Tanluan; the Larger Sūtra's three grades; the three meritorious acts) All aspirants must arouse bodhicitta, which is the vow to save beings; seeking birth for pleasure does not bring birth.
- **lin:jodo-shu**: (Hōnen) Bodhicitta is among the practices mentioned for the three grades in order to be set aside in favour of the nenbutsu alone.
- **lin:huayan**: (Myōe) Discarding bodhicitta is the first great error of the Senchakushū; without it the nenbutsu is not Buddhism.
- **lin:jodo-shinshu**: (Shinran) The true bodhicitta is other-power: shinjin itself, given by Amida.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: Shinran relocates bodhicitta to the Buddha's side, where Myōe's requirement and Hōnen's selection might both hold
  - P4-stage: bodhicitta as required for higher grades only (Senchakushū's parallel reading)
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the sides disagree whether bodhicitta is a practitioner's condition or is subsumed in (or given with) the nenbutsu.

## RQ-154 — Is a stable, undecaying body required for liberation in life?  
`dsp:is-bodily-immortality-required-for-liberation` · skeleton

- **lin:rasa-sastra**: Rasa Śāstra: yes — liberation after the body falls cannot be seen and is 'meaningless'; knowledge requires practice and practice a stable body; a body worn by age is unfit for samādhi; so the body must first be made divine through mercury, and then yoga yields brahmanhood here.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: The 'six systems' as the Rasa texts report them: liberation is realized at or after the fall of the body; and (Advaita, the SDS objector) liberation is the manifestation of being-consciousness-bliss by knowledge, for which no divine body is needed.
- **lin:ayurveda**: Āyurveda (Caraka): liberation is the cessation of all feeling and non-return through yoga, the recollection of truth and renunciation; rasāyana prolongs life but old age and death are natural and without remedy.
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path: Rasa as a body-breath path whose stated end (knowledge of identity with Śiva, the light of consciousness) overlaps the knowledge paths
  - P4-stage: the stable body as a preparatory condition (B2) rather than liberation itself — the Rasa texts themselves say liberation comes from knowledge (Rasahṛdaya 1.10)
  - P1-level: bodily stability belongs to the conventional level, identity with Śiva to the ultimate
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Rasa tradition insists on the undecaying body; the others do not require it.

## RQ-U14-09 — Can one be liberated while still living in the body (jīvanmukti)?  
`dsp:is-jivanmukti-possible` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Yes: with knowledge of Brahman ignorance ends; the body continues through begun karma, but the knower is liberated here.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: No: while the body lasts karma and bondage remain; the knower or the surrendered is assured of release, which comes when the body falls.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: both agree that begun karma runs its course and the knower does not return; they differ in whether to call the embodied knower 'liberated'
  - P1-level: for Advaita bondage was never ultimately real
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-156 — Is knowledge the means of devotion, is devotion a means to knowledge, or is devotion its own fruit? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:is-knowledge-the-means-of-bhakti` · skeleton

- **lin:bhakti-sastra**: Devotion is its own fruit and higher than action, knowledge and yoga; it is not knowledge, since even one who hates the Lord may know him; knowing about a king or food neither pleases the king nor satisfies hunger.
- **lin:bhakti-sastra**: Two views the Nārada Sūtra reports without naming holders: 'some' say knowledge alone is its means; 'others' that knowledge and devotion depend on each other.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Knowledge of the self alone removes ignorance and liberates; devotion purifies the mind and prepares for knowledge, and in its highest sense is the constant search into one's own nature.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: The knowledge the Upaniṣads enjoin for liberation is itself devotion — steady remembrance vivid as perception, knowledge that has become love, won by grace; knowledge and devotion are not two.
- **lin:epic-teaching**: One who has become Brahman attains supreme devotion; by devotion he knows the Lord in truth and enters him; by undivided devotion he is known, seen and entered.
- Note: Read as differences of path and stage: the Gītā places supreme devotion both after becoming Brahman and before knowing the Lord in truth, so knowledge and love can each precede the other according to the seeker's path and stage.

## RQ-U14-11 — Is the knowledge that liberates a knowledge arising from the scriptural sentence, or a sustained meditation (upāsana) that is bhakti?  
`dsp:is-liberating-knowledge-meditation` · skeleton

- **lin:visistadvaita**: It is meditation: the Upaniṣads use 'know', 'meditate' and 'worship' interchangeably; the knowledge enjoined is repeated, loving remembrance vivid as sight, i.e. bhakti, which pleases the Lord who grants release.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: It is the direct knowledge of identity produced by the great sayings; meditation (nididhyāsana) only removes obstacles to it; knowledge is not an act to be enjoined.
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path: knowledge-path and devotion-path descriptions of liberating realization
  - P4-stage: meditation as the means, knowledge as its culmination
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-158 — Is liberation only the cessation of the mind? (YBh 2.24)  
`dsp:is-liberation-cessation-of-mind` · sourced

- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: The one who is almost a teacher (ācāryadeśīya), answering the objector: with the cause of non-seeing absent the buddhi ceases; liberation is the cessation of the mind.
- **None**: The objector's tale of the impotent husband: knowledge that exists does not bring cessation of the mind; what hope that it will after it is destroyed? Vyāsa's own reply is not in this segment.
- Candidate readings:
  - both statements are objections recorded by Vyāsa; see YBh 2.25 for what the text says of the puruṣa's state
- Note: not yet reconciled: recorded as an argument within the text; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-159 — Is every life-span fixed, or is there untimely death? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:is-lifespan-fixed` · skeleton

- **lin:ayurveda**: Some: whoever dies dies at his time; the time one dies is one's fixed time.
- **lin:ayurveda**: Ātreya: life rests on daiva and puruṣakāra; some karma is fixed, some not; both timely and untimely death exist — otherwise mantras, medicines, precautions and rejuvenation would be pointless.
- Note: Fixedness belongs to strong daiva, openness to weak daiva and human effort — two standpoints on karma rather than one exclusive rule.

## RQ-160 — May Buddhists (monastics) eat meat? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:is-meat-eating-permitted` · skeleton

- **lin:mahayana**: No: the bodhisattva, seeing all beings as his only child and as former relatives, must not eat meat; the earlier permission of 'three-fold pure' meat was given step by step and is now withdrawn.
- **lin:theravada**: Meat that is pure in three respects (not seen, heard or suspected to have been killed for the monk) may be accepted; the Buddha refused Devadatta's demand to make abstention from meat compulsory.
- Note: The Mahāparinirvāṇa itself presents the three-fold-pure rule as a provisional, stage-by-stage permission later withdrawn for bodhisattvas.

## RQ-161 — Is medicine effective, given that some treated patients die and some untreated recover? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:is-medicine-efficacious` · skeleton

- **lin:ayurveda**: Maitreya: medicine is no better than no medicine, since both outcomes occur with and without it.
- **lin:ayurveda**: Ātreya (and Punarvasu in Vāgbhaṭa): medicine is effective for diseases curable by it; some diseases are incurable, some resolve alone and would resolve faster with help; treatment with the four pillars should not be doubted.
- Note: Maitreya's observation holds of incurable and self-limiting diseases; Ātreya's claim holds of the curable — the classes of disease are the standpoints.

## RQ-162 — Is the mind (cognition) self-luminous, or is it seen by a distinct, unchanging seer?  
`dsp:is-mind-self-luminous` · skeleton

- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: The mind is not self-illuminating because it is seen; the unchanging puruṣa always knows its activities.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Cognition is self-aware (svasaṃvedana): every cognition is aware of itself in being aware of its object; no separate permanent seer is needed.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: both reject an infinite regress — Yoga by a distinct seer, the Buddhist by reflexive awareness.
  - Leave distinct: each side denies the other's premise.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: Yoga's non-luminous mind with a separate seer against Buddhist self-aware momentary cognition.

## RQ-163 — Is perception only non-conceptual, or can a perception be determinate (conceptual)?  
`dsp:is-perception-non-conceptual` · skeleton

- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Perception is free of conceptual construction; any cognition joined with names or genera is conceptual and not perception; that perception is non-conceptual is itself established by perception.
- **lin:nyaya**: Perception arises from contact of sense and object and is 'determinate' (vyavasāyātmaka); later Nyāya admits both an indeterminate and a determinate stage, both perceptual.
- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: There is first a bare, non-conceptual 'seeing' (ālocana) and then a conceptual cognition of the same object; both are perception.
- **lin:vyakarana**: There is no cognition in the world that is not accompanied by word; all knowledge appears as if interwoven with language (Bhartṛhari).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Buddhists call 'perception' only the first, non-conceptual moment; Nyāya and Mīmāṃsā extend the word to the conceptual judgment that follows — partly a difference of definition.
  - P1-level: the Buddhist particular is the ultimately real; the schools that accept real universals describe the conventional object.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the schools define perception differently.

## RQ-164 — Does scripture (śruti) teach the Sāṃkhya's unconscious pradhāna as the cause of the world?  
`dsp:is-pradhana-taught-in-sruti` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: Yes: pradhāna is established by inference and confirmed by scripture; reliable testimony teaches what inference does not reach.
- **lin:vedanta**: No: the scriptural cause 'saw' (reflected) before creating, so the unconscious pradhāna is not scriptural; the passages cited for Sāṃkhya mean something else, and Kapila's smṛti yields where it conflicts with śruti.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: Sāṃkhya enumerates principles analytically to discriminate self from non-self, while the Brahma Sūtra asks what the Upaniṣads teach as the conscious first cause — answers to different questions (rejected by both: Śaṅkara denies pradhāna is scriptural; classical Sāṃkhya denies any conscious director).
  - P4-stage: Advaita accepts the discrimination of self from the non-self as preparatory while rejecting an independent pradhāna.
  - Theistic Sāṃkhya synthesis (Vijñānabhikṣu, recalled): prakṛti as material cause under the Lord taught by śruti.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: each side holds the other's reading of the same scriptures to be wrong.

## RQ-U14-02 — Is surrender (prapatti) a means (upāya) to liberation? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:is-prapatti-an-upaya` · skeleton

- **lin:vadakalai**: Yes: prapatti is an independent means, performed once with its five limbs and the principal act of self-offering, equal in fruit to bhakti-yoga; the Lord is the accomplished means and prapatti the means to be performed, which occasions his grace.
- **lin:tenkalai**: No: the Lord himself is the only means; surrender is the soul's acceptance of that fact, befitting its dependent nature; to treat it as a means is to rely on one's own act and is a fault.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: Rāmānuja reads Gītā 18.66 both as renunciation of agency within the yogas and as surrender in place of expiation for those unable to begin bhakti; the Gadyas present surrender as his own act of refuge.
- Note: Both hold the Lord to be the accomplished means and surrender to be done once; they differ in whether the soul's act is called a means — a difference of standpoint (seeker's act vs the Lord's agency).

## RQ-166 — Is every rasa blissful, even compassion and terror? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:is-rasa-bliss` · skeleton

- **lin:alankara**: Yes: in compassion and the other rasas supreme happiness arises; the experience of the sensitive is the proof — otherwise no one would turn to the Rāmāyaṇa (Viśvanātha, following Abhinavagupta).
- **lin:alankara**: No: rasa is of the nature of both pleasure and pain; the compassionate, furious, odious and terrible are painful, though spectators delight in the poet's and actors' skill (Rāmacandra and Guṇacandra).
- Note: The Nāṭyadarpaṇa grants delight in the artistry while calling the content painful; the other side speaks of the relish as a whole — two standpoints on one experience (content vs relish).

## RQ-167 — Is Śiva the material cause of the world, or only its efficient cause?  
`dsp:is-siva-the-material-cause` · skeleton

- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: Śiva is only the efficient cause; māyā (and bindu) is the material cause and his power the instrument; if Śiva were the material he would be insentient and changing and would share the world's impurity.
- **lin:tamil-saiva-siddhanta**: The same: the world arises from māyā as its material; the Lord, one with it by pervasion, is not transformed (Civañāṉa Pōtam 1 and its commentaries).
- **lin:kashmir-saivism**: Śiva manifests the world within his own consciousness as a reflection (ābhāsa), by his freedom; he is both its material and efficient cause without loss (as recorded by U19).
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Brahman is both material and efficient cause, the world being its apparent transformation (vivarta) through māyā (as recorded by U13).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: analytic realism vs absolute non-duality
  - P1-level: at the conventional level māyā is the material; ultimately only Śiva (rejected by the Siddhānta)
- Note: Not yet reconciled; a P2 reading (substance-standpoint realism vs absolute-standpoint non-dualism) is a candidate.

## RQ-168 — Is the self (knower of the field) all-pervading?  
`dsp:is-the-self-all-pervading` · skeleton

- **lin:ayurveda**: Caraka: the self is all-pervading and great, present in all wombs though abiding in one; it feels only through its own senses (Śā 1.79-81).
- **lin:ayurveda**: Suśruta: in the Āyurveda treatises the knowers of the field are taught as eternal but not all-pervading (Śā 1.16).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: Suśruta speaks of the embodied, treatable self; Caraka of the self in itself
  - textual: the Suśruta sentence is compressed and its reading depends on the commentary
- Note: Not yet reconciled: two classical compendia differ on the extent of the self.

## RQ-169 — Is the ultimate truth an object of knowledge, and is emptiness realised by valid cognition (perception and inference)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:is-the-ultimate-knowable` · skeleton

- **lin:gelug**: The ultimate truth (emptiness) is an object of knowledge: it is found by a reasoning consciousness analysing the final mode of being, first realised by inference and then directly by yogic perception, in which it appears without dualistic appearance.
- **lin:kadam**: Emptiness is not realised by perception and inference as Buddhist logicians define them — to say so is the talk of the near-sighted; otherwise all tenet-holders measuring by valid cognition would agree. It is 'seen' by not seeing, non-conceptually; the valid cognitions were set up to refute opponents.
- **lin:sakya**: The ultimate is beyond the scope of mind and words; it is not an object of knowledge in the ordinary sense, and is 'known' only as the non-dual state of meditative equipoise (Gorampa).
- Note: All three agree the direct realisation is non-dual and non-conceptual; they differ on whether that realisation may be called 'knowing an object' and on the role of inference beforehand (standpoint of conventional description vs. of the equipoise itself).

## RQ-170 — Is the ultimate of the nature of speech (śabdādvaita), and is all cognition word-permeated?  
`dsp:is-the-ultimate-speech` · skeleton

- **lin:vyakarana**: Yes: Brahman is the Word-principle appearing as the world; no cognition is free of the word; speech is the consciousness of beings.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: No: an eternal single Word-brahman cannot produce successive, various things; if the world is its transformation it is non-eternal, if its appearance it is unreal; perception is free of words and conceptual construction.
- **lin:digambara**: No: Prabhācandra refutes Word-monism among the doctrines opposed to the Jain view of many real substances and conceptual and non-conceptual cognition.
- **lin:mantrasastra**: Śabda-brahman is not the word nor the meaning, both insentient, but the consciousness of all beings — speech is consciousness, not consciousness speech.
- **lin:pratyabhijna**: Supreme speech is the reflexive awareness of consciousness, not the grammarians' paśyantī.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the grammarians, the mantra manuals and the Pratyabhijñā may all describe the reflexive, 'speaking' side of consciousness, differing on whether it is the whole of the ultimate.
  - P1-level: word-permeation may hold of conceptual (vyāvahārika) cognition, which the Buddhists also treat as word-bound, while the Buddhists deny it of perception.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: whether reality is Word, and whether cognition is word-permeated.

## RQ-U50-01 — Is there a self (ātman, puruṣa, jīva) — an enduring conscious subject distinct from body and changing cognitions — or only a stream of impersonal factors designated 'person'?  
`dsp:is-there-a-self` · skeleton

- **lin:nyaya**: There is a self: an eternal, all-pervading substance distinct from body, senses, mind and cognitions; cognition, desire, aversion, effort, pleasure and pain are its qualities and the marks by which it is known. To think 'the self does not exist' is itself a form of the false knowledge that binds.
- **lin:vaisesika**: The self is not perceived by ordinary perception but inferred: breathing in and out, the closing and opening of the eyes, life, the movement of the mind, the functioning of the other senses, pleasure, pain, desire, aversion and effort are its marks; the word 'I' applies to it, not to the body; selves are many, since their states differ.
- **lin:mimamsa**: A self distinct from body and cognition exists — the agent who enjoys the fruit of sacrifice in another world; without it the injunctions for heaven would be pointless. Kumārila: the self is known in the notion 'I'; Prabhākara: it shines forth in every cognition as its knower, never as its object.
- **lin:samkhya**: Puruṣa exists: aggregates exist for the sake of another; there must be something opposite to what has the three guṇas; there must be a controller, an enjoyer, and one for whose isolation activity takes place. Puruṣas are many, since birth, death and the instruments are allotted to each separately.
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: The seer is seeing alone, which appears to conform to the mind's cognitions; the mind is not self-illuminating, because it is seen; if one cognition were grasped by another there would be infinite regress and confusion of memories; a mind that were only momentary cognitions could not be made one-pointed.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: The self is the self-evident witness (sākṣin) of all cognitions, never an object and never deniable — no one thinks 'I am not'. Body, senses, breath, mind and intellect, and the agent-enjoyer, are not-self, superimposed on it. Against the Buddhists: memory and recognition ('I saw that, I see this') show one enduring knower; a momentary store-consciousness cannot hold impressions; the witness is not a momentary cognition.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: The self is a distinct, eternal, atomic knower, established in the awareness 'I', self-luminous; consciousness is its attribute, which belongs to someone and is of something — not the self itself; each self is a mode of Brahman.
- **lin:jainism**: The soul (jīva) exists; its defining mark is cognitive activity (upayoga); it is the doer and enjoyer of its karma, can be liberated, and is eternal as substance but non-eternal as mode (its states and births). One-sided permanence (as of the Naiyāyikas) and one-sided momentariness (as of the Buddhists) are both refuted.
- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: There is a soul within the body and distinct from it: it says 'not this' of each thing, speaks of 'my body', knows through the senses, and knows on waking that it slept; souls are many, their experience, bondage and release differing.
- **lin:pratyabhijna**: Against the Buddhist that only momentary cognitions appear and no permanent seer: memory is impossible if the remembering cognition is separate from the experience; the unity of cognitions at different times is the one conscious subject, Maheśvara, endowed with the powers of knowing, remembering and excluding.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: Each of the five aggregates is not-self: were it self it would not lead to affliction and one could say 'let it be thus'; what is impermanent and painful is not fit to be regarded 'this is mine, this I am, this is my self'. 'Being' is a designation, as 'chariot' is for assembled parts. Asked 'is there a self? is there no self?' the Buddha was silent: either answer would side with eternalism or annihilationism.
- **lin:theravada**: The person is not found as a real and ultimate fact; 'Nāgasena' and 'chariot' are designations; as the chariot is not found when each part is examined, there is no being in the ultimate sense, only mentality-materiality.
- **lin:sautrantika**: Apart from the series of aggregates there is no self; 'self' designates the series. Those outside this teaching are not liberated because they hold 'self' to be a separate entity rather than a designation for the series (Vasubandhu, AKBh ch. 9); only aggregates, conditioned by defilement and karma, enter the womb through the intermediate existence, like a flame.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: One who sees a self loves it as 'I', thirsts for its happiness, and that thirst hides faults and drives rebirth: the view of self is the root of saṃsāra. The selves of the Naiyāyikas, Mīmāṃsakas, Sāṃkhyas, Jains, Upaniṣadic thinkers and Vātsīputrīyas are examined and refuted; whatever exists is momentary.
- **lin:madhyamaka**: If the self were the aggregates it would arise and perish; if other than them it would lack their marks. The self is neither the aggregates, nor other than them, nor their possessor — like the chariot sought in seven ways. The Buddhas have taught 'self', have taught 'no-self', and have taught neither self nor no-self; 'I exist' and 'mine exists' are false in the ultimate sense.
- **lin:pudgalavada**: A person (pudgala) exists, designated in dependence on the present aggregates, neither the same as nor different from them, inexpressible — as fire in dependence on fuel; it is the bearer of the burden of the aggregates (the middle position; see dsp:pudgala).
- **lin:tathagatagarbha**: 'Self' in the true sense means the tathāgatagarbha: all beings have buddha-nature; seeing the Tathāgata as impermanent, suffering, non-self and impure is inverted; the dharmakāya has the perfection of self. (Other Mahāyāna texts read this 'self' as a skilful name for emptiness — see dsp:buddha-nature-self-or-emptiness.)
- **lin:carvaka**: (As reported) Only the living body qualified by consciousness; nothing survives it.
- **lin:sramana**: (As reported) Pakudha: the soul is one of seven eternal, unmoving bodies.
- **lin:ajnana**: (As reported) Not declared: 'who knows whether the soul exists?'
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: A self distinct from the body exists: consciousness is absent in the corpse.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: the Buddhist analysis negates a self found among or apart from the aggregates — which Advaita also calls not-self — while Advaita's witness is said never to be an object of such analysis. Rejected by Śāntarakṣita (who refutes the Upaniṣadic self too) and by Advaita (which asserts the self as being-consciousness, not an absence).
  - P2-standpoint (the Jains' own synthesis): permanent from the substance standpoint, momentary from the mode standpoint, so the Nyāya and Buddhist theses each state one aspect. Rejected by both Nyāya and the Buddhists as combining incompatible claims.
  - P4-stage (Kṣemarāja, PH 8): each doctrine of the self is a role of the one consciousness reached at a given level — body, buddhi, prāṇa, void, prakṛti, Īśvara. An inclusivist ranking that the ranked schools do not accept.
  - P5-neyartha: Mahāyāna passages on a 'true self' (tathāgatagarbha) read as provisional by some, definitive by others (see dsp:buddha-nature-self-or-emptiness).
  - P6-upaya: the Buddha's silence to Vacchagotta (SN 44.10) and MMK 18.6 ('self' and 'no-self' both taught) read as teaching fitted to the hearer; Nyāya and Advaita do not accept that their self is merely a teaching device.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: all parties agree that the body, senses and changing cognitions are not the true self, but they disagree whether anything beyond them — an enduring conscious subject — exists.

## RQ-172 — Is there a subtle body that transmigrates? (a debate among Sāṃkhya teachers)  
`dsp:is-there-a-subtle-body` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: Yes: a constant subtle body transmigrates, perfumed by the dispositions (classical; Pañcādhikaraṇa's permanent conveying body; Patañjali's successive subtle bodies).
- **lin:samkhya**: No: since the organs are all-pervading, birth is their functioning at the seed-place and death its abandonment (Vindhyavāsin).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: whether the vehicle of rebirth is conceived as a body or as the functioning of pervasive organs.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-173 — Is there another world, spontaneously born beings, and fruit of good and bad deeds?  
`dsp:is-there-another-world` · skeleton

- **lin:carvaka**: (Pāyāsi, and Ajita Kesakambalī as reported) There is no other world, no spontaneously born beings, no fruit of deeds; experiments with dying criminals show nothing leaving the body.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: (Kumārakassapa) The other world cannot be seen with the fleshly eye; the dead cannot return to tell; similes of the dream, the blind man, the conch-blower show the flaws in the 'experiments'.
- Candidate readings:
  - No principle proposed; see the Cārvāka material of U33.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: materialism and the doctrine of rebirth are opposed.

## RQ-U33-2 — Do good and bad deeds bear fruit (kiriyavāda) or not (akiriyavāda)?  
`dsp:is-there-fruit-of-action` · skeleton

- **lin:sramana**: (As reported) Pūraṇa: the doer of any violence does no evil, the giver gains no merit; Pakudha: the seven bodies are unmade and unaffected, so no one kills; Ajita: there is no fruit of good and bad deeds.
- **lin:carvaka**: (As reported) There is no unseen potency of deeds; things differ by own-nature; merit and demerit are not; the rites bear no fruit.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: Deeds bear fruit; the doctrine of action is the safe course; Gosāla's 'no deed' is the worst doctrine.
- **lin:jainism**: The true kiriyavāda knows influx, bondage and the wearing away of karma; the akiriyavādins, who call the world barren and fixed, are like a blind man with a lamp.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: 'no doer, no killer' (Pakudha) might be read as a statement about unchanging substance (ultimate) against the conventional reality of harm — the Buddhist and Jain reporters reject this as licence for killing.
  - P5-neyartha: none — no source treats the akiriya teaching as provisional.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the deniers explicitly reject any fruit of deeds; the affirmers make it the ground of the holy life.

## RQ-175 — Is there rebirth (and another world)?  
`dsp:is-there-rebirth-caraka` · skeleton

- **lin:carvaka**: The deniers (nāstika) who hold perception alone valid; others who make the parents, nature, creation by another or chance the cause of birth.
- **lin:ayurveda**: Caraka: rebirth is established by all four means — scripture of the sages of divine sight, perception (inequalities of birth, untaught infant behaviour, memory of former births), inference from karma (daiva), and yukti (no sprout without seed); denial is the worst of sins.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: the denial as confined to what is perceptible at the conventional level (the Cārvāka would reject this)
  - record only: the Cārvāka view is here reported by an opponent (Caraka)
- Note: Not yet reconciled: an affirmation and a denial of rebirth; no principle is applied here.

## RQ-U50-10 — Is there an Īśvara — a Lord who makes the world and dispenses the fruits of action? Is he proved by reason, known only from scripture, a special puruṣa for meditation, or not to be accepted at all?  
`dsp:isvara` · skeleton

- **lin:nyaya**: There is an all-knowing, imperishable maker (Īśvara): the world is an effect, like a pot, so it has an intelligent maker; the combination of atoms, the support of the world, the arts and the Veda's authority require him; he brings about the fruit of human effort according to karma (Udayana answers five objections in five clusters).
- **lin:vaisesika**: At the end of Brahmā's life Maheśvara, wishing to give rest to beings wearied by transmigration, dissolves the world, and at his will creation begins again from the atoms according to the unseen merit of souls.
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Īśvara is a special puruṣa untouched by afflictions, actions, their fruition and residues, in whom the seed of omniscience is unsurpassed; devotion to him (praṇidhāna) brings samādhi near, since won over by it he favours the yogin — a model and a support, not the world's maker.
- **lin:samkhya**: Īśvara is not established: whether free or bound he could not create; unconscious prakṛti acts for puruṣa's release as unconscious milk flows for the calf; the fruit of action comes from karma without a governor.
- **lin:mimamsa**: There is no creator: no time without action is known; with no beings yet there would be none to pity, and a compassionate creator would make only happy beings; the Veda needs no author; in the rite the sacrifice, not the deity, is principal, and dharma itself yields the fruit.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Brahman as Lord is the cause of the world's origin, maintenance and dissolution, known from scripture alone — the Veda is breathed out by him; he is not partial or cruel, since like rain he gives according to the seeds of beings' karma; the fruit of works comes from him. (Īśvara belongs to Brahman as related to adjuncts.)
- **lin:visistadvaita**: Inference cannot prove a single omniscient maker of the world; Brahman, the Lord with infinite auspicious qualities, qualified by souls and matter, is known only from scripture (śāstrayonitvāt).
- **lin:dvaita**: Viṣṇu alone is the independent reality from whom come creation, maintenance, dissolution, control, knowledge, ignorance, bondage and release.
- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: The world undergoes origination, maintenance and dissolution and so has an agent; unconscious māyā and karma cannot organize themselves to give each soul its due, so an omniscient Lord is required.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: There is no permanent means of knowledge or permanent cause: what is permanent cannot produce effects successively; 'being an effect' does not prove a single intelligent maker of mountains and sprouts; the Blessed One is a means of knowledge by having become so, not as an eternal lord (Dharmakīrti, Śāntarakṣita, Ratnakīrti). Belief in Īśvara as cause arises from the error of a permanent self (Vasubandhu).
- **lin:theravada**: There is no god, no Brahmā, who makes this round of rebirths; only bare dhammas occur through the conjunction of conditions; the wheel of becoming is without a maker.
- **lin:jainism**: The maker-god of the Nyāya-Vaiśeṣika cannot be: an unembodied maker cannot act, an embodied one would be limited; if he made the world, where was he before and what was his material? — obstinate fancies. Haribhadra: the doctrine that Īśvara is the maker can be accepted in a true sense — the soul that attains the highest perfection is 'Īśvara', and every soul is the 'maker' of its own condition through its karma; a world-creating Lord is refuted.
- **lin:upanisadic**: Some sages say inherent nature is the cause, others time — they are deluded; it is the greatness of God in the world; 'we have found him, the supreme great Lord of lords'.
- **lin:carvaka**: (As reported) The only lord is the king known to the world; variety comes from own-nature.
- **lin:jainism**: Those who say the world was made by gods, Brahmā, Īśvara, pradhāna or the self-born do not know the truth; the world never perishes.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: If Brahmā were lord of the world, why did he make it unhappy and unjust? Creation by God is a tenet that ends in inaction.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint (Haribhadra's own): 'Īśvara is the maker' true in the sense that the perfected soul is Īśvara and each soul makes its own condition by karma — rejected by Nyāya, whose Lord is eternally free and the maker of the world, and it concedes no world-creator.
  - P3-path (Yoga): devotion to Īśvara as one of the means ('or', YS 1.23) for those of devotional temperament, without deciding creation — the non-theistic Sāṃkhya and the Buddhists do not accept an Īśvara even as an object of devotion.
  - P1-level (Advaita): the Lord is real at the empirical level (Brahman with adjuncts), transcended in the ultimate — rejected by the theistic Vedāntas and the Śaivas, for whom the Lord is ultimately real.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: Nyāya-Vaiśeṣika, Vedānta and Śaiva Siddhānta affirm a Lord (by inference or by scripture), Yoga admits a special puruṣa for devotion but not as maker, and Sāṃkhya, Mīmāṃsā, the Buddhists and the Jains reject a creator.

## RQ-177 — Does Sāṃkhya deny Īśvara, or only deny that an eternal creator is proved?  
`dsp:isvara-within-samkhya` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: No Īśvara is established as creator (classical; the Sāṃkhya Sūtra; Aniruddha as recalled), though emergent lords exist.
- **lin:samkhya**: The denial is a concessive argument; Īśvara is known from scripture (Vijñānabhikṣu, recalled).
- **lin:epic-teaching**: A Lord as twenty-sixth principle above the twenty-five (epic Sāṃkhya).
- Candidate readings:
  - P5-neyartha: Vijñānabhikṣu's own reading — the denial is provisional (for argument's sake), the scriptural Lord definitive; classical commentators take the denial at face value.
  - P4-stage: denial of an eternal Lord to cut attachment to lordship in aspirants (recalled as Vijñānabhikṣu's explanation; verify).
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-178 — Is there another world, and is dharma to be followed for its sake? (Rāmāyaṇa 2.108–110) (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:jabali-rama` · skeleton

- **lin:carvaka**: Jābāli (as voiced in the Rāmāyaṇa): no one is anyone's kin; there is no other world; offerings to the dead are waste; the texts enjoining sacrifice and gifts were composed to induce giving; hold to the perceptible.
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Rāma: truth is lord in the world and the root of all; he will keep his father's word; the one who denies the other world is to be shunned.
- Note: The text itself says Jābāli spoke only as a means to bring Rāma back, knowing well the coming and going of the world (Vasiṣṭha, 2.110.1); the reconciliation concerns Jābāli's intention only.

## RQ-179 — In mahābandha, is the throat lock (jālandhara) to be applied, or the tongue pressed at the root of the front teeth instead?  
`dsp:jalandhara-in-mahabandha` · skeleton

- **lin:hatha-yoga**: Apply the throat lock: in mahābandha the chin is set firmly on the chest while the perineum is contracted and the mind fixed in the middle (HYP 3.19-21); the Gheraṇḍa likewise holds the breath in jālandhara in mahābandha (GS 3.18-20).
- **lin:hatha-yoga**: Some teachers hold that the throat lock should be avoided in mahābandha and that the lock made with the tongue pressed at the root of the front teeth (rājadanta) is best (reported in HYP 3.22).
- Candidate readings:
  - Two lines of instruction (sampradāya) are recorded; each practitioner follows the guru who gives the traditional instruction (HYP 3.129).
  - The tongue-lock at the rājadanta belongs with the tongue-practices (ŚS 3.73; GŚ 64) and may be meant for practitioners of khecarī.
  - Brahmānanda's Jyotsnā on HYP 3.22 may decide between them (not checked by this unit).
- Note: The Haṭhapradīpikā records both instructions side by side without deciding; not yet reconciled.

## RQ-180 — Is an action 'done' while it is being done? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:jamali-schism` · skeleton

- **lin:jainism**: Mahāvīra: what is being done is (in a respect) done, what is moving has moved — each moment of the process accomplishes its part.
- **lin:jainism**: Jamāli (the 'Bahurata' schism): what is being done is not yet done; only the completed act is done — as the bed being spread was not yet spread.
- Note: Later Jain commentators explain the dispute by standpoints: Mahāvīra's statement holds from the momentary (ṛjusūtra-type) standpoint, Jamāli's from the everyday standpoint of the whole act; Jamāli's fault was to hold his view as the only truth.

## RQ-181 — Who is the most learned in brahman? — the theological contest at Janaka's court (BAU 3) (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:janaka-court-brahmodya` · sourced

- **lin:upanisadic**: The challengers (Aśvala, Ārtabhāga, Bhujyu, Uṣasta, Kahola, Gārgī, Uddālaka, Śākalya) test Yājñavalkya on the ritual means of passing beyond death, the graspers, the fate of the Pārikṣitas, the self within all, what the worlds are woven on, the thread and the inner controller, and the number of the gods.
- **lin:upanisadic**: Yājñavalkya: the self within all is the unseen seer, beyond hunger, sorrow and death; the inner controller; the imperishable, without knowing which all rites are finite; the self is 'not this, not this'.
- Note: The text does not reject the challengers' ritual and cosmological learning but places it below knowledge of the self within all (BAU 3.8.10).

## RQ-182 — Does a word denote the universal (jāti, ākṛti) or the individual (vyakti, dravya)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:jati-or-vyakti` · skeleton

- **lin:vyakarana**: Vājapyāyana: the universal form; Vyāḍi: the individual substance; Patañjali accepts both; Bhartṛhari: the universal is the true part, and ultimately all words denote Being (sattā) / the Real, as all ornament-words denote gold.
- **lin:mimamsa**: The word denotes the universal (ākṛti), which is real and eternal; the individual is understood through it.
- **lin:nyaya**: The meaning of a word is the individual, the form and the universal together.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: There are no real universals: a word signifies by excluding what is other (apoha).
- Note: Patañjali himself treats universal and individual as two grammatical standpoints, and Nyāya combines them; the realist schools are reconciled as standpoints.

## RQ-183 — What is the relation of the individual self to Brahman, according to the teachers named in BS 1.4.20–22?  
`dsp:jiva-brahman-relation-brahma-sutra` · skeleton

- **lin:vedanta**: Āśmarathya: the identity statement is an indication proving the promise (by knowing one, all is known) — read by Śaṅkara as difference-and-non-difference, like sparks and fire.
- **lin:vedanta**: Auḍulomi: the self becomes Brahman when it departs in liberation; before, it is different.
- **lin:vedanta**: Kāśakṛtsna: Brahman itself abides as the individual self (accepted by Śaṅkara).
- Candidate readings:
  - Advaita: Kāśakṛtsna final (identity), others provisional.
  - Bhedābheda (Bhāskara, Nimbārka): Āśmarathya or Auḍulomi closer to the truth.
  - P4-stage: Auḍulomi describes the bound state, Kāśakṛtsna the truth realized in liberation.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the sūtras record three views without explicit synthesis, and the Vedānta schools adopt different ones.

## RQ-U16-3 — Is liberation possible while living in the body?  
`dsp:jivanmukti` · skeleton

- **lin:bhedabheda**: Bhāskara: no; while the body lasts the real adjuncts remain; liberation comes after death.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Advaita: yes; knowledge destroys ignorance at once, and the body continues only by prārabdha karma (jīvanmukti).
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Gauḍīya: one who serves Hari in all conditions is called jīvanmukta; but the goal is not liberation, it is love.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level (Advaita's own) — rejected by Bhāskara
  - the Gauḍīya redefinition as a different referent for the same word
- Note: not yet reconciled: Bhāskara's denial rests on the reality of adjuncts, Advaita's affirmation on their unreality.

## RQ-185 — Is liberation while living (jīvanmukti) possible, or only liberation at death?  
`dsp:jivanmukti-possible` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Śaṅkara, Vidyāraṇya, most Advaitins: yes — on knowledge accumulated karma is destroyed and future karma does not cling; the body continues by prārabdha like a potter's wheel; the knower is free while living.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Maṇḍana Miśra (reported): the so-called jīvanmukta is an advanced seeker; full liberation comes only with the body's fall.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: Viśiṣṭādvaita and Dvaita: liberation is attained only after death, when the soul reaches the Lord's abode; embodied liberation is not real liberation.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: freedom from bondage in knowledge (ultimate) vs continued empirical embodiment (conventional).
  - Some Advaitins posit a 'trace of ignorance' (avidyā-leśa) to explain the continuing body, conceding part of Maṇḍana's point.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Vaiṣṇava schools and Maṇḍana deny what Advaita affirms; candidate reconciliation by level is rejected by the other schools.

## RQ-186 — Can liberating knowledge be combined with ritual action (jñāna-karma-samuccaya), or does knowledge alone liberate? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:jnana-karma-samuccaya` · sourced

- **lin:bhedabheda**: Action enjoined by the Veda is a duty to the end and assists knowledge; liberation comes from knowledge combined with prescribed action.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Ignorance is the root of rebirth and only knowledge removes it; action, which depends on agent, act and instrument born of ignorance, is opposed to knowledge and cannot be combined with it; action purifies the mind beforehand and is then given up.
- **lin:bhedabheda**: Bhartṛprapañca: knowledge must be combined with Vedic works throughout life; both together liberate.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Maṇḍana Miśra: works performed without desire are helpful — they purify and assist the rise of knowledge — and knowledge must be made direct by contemplation; a qualified combination.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Śaṅkara and Sureśvara: knowledge alone liberates; works (without desire) only purify the mind and generate the desire to know, and are then renounced; works and knowledge cannot be combined since works presuppose agency which knowledge negates.
- **lin:bhamati**: Bhāmatī: sacrifice etc. produce the desire to know (vividiṣā), not knowledge itself.
- **lin:vivarana**: Vivaraṇa: works, through purification, contribute to knowledge itself (vedana), not merely to the desire.
- **lin:bhedabheda**: Bhartṛprapañca and Bhāskara: knowledge must be combined with lifelong performance of prescribed works; works are not dropped after knowledge.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Śaṅkara and Sureśvara: knowledge alone liberates; works purify the mind beforehand but cannot be combined with knowledge, whose object is the actionless self.
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Gauḍīya: neither works nor knowledge but pure devotion is the means; works and the pursuit of impersonal knowledge 'cover' devotion (BRS 1.1.11); prescribed duties may be abandoned by one devoted to Kṛṣṇa.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: works belong to the beginner's stage (mind purification), knowledge to the qualified (Śaṅkara's own framework).
  - P3-path: works and knowledge as paths for different temperaments (Īśa 1–2 as read by Śaṅkara).
  - Bhāmatī–Vivaraṇa difference (desire vs knowledge) as a difference of emphasis within the preparatory stage.
- Note: The Rāma Gītā itself assigns action to the stage before knowledge (purification) and renunciation to the stage of knowledge.

## RQ-187 — If the chart shows fate, what room is left for human effort and for remedial rites? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:jyotisa-fate-and-effort` · skeleton

- **lin:jyotisa**: Varāhamihira: fate is karma made in another birth; it alone gives birth in a good family and the like, but after childhood success needs fate and effort together, like farming; neither favourable planets alone (else the astrologer would be king) nor rites alone (else the priest would be king) suffice. Yet timing matters: an act begun under ill-placed planets destroys its doer, one begun well succeeds with slight effort.
- **lin:dharmasastra**: Yājñavalkya: success rests on fate and human effort; fate is only the human effort of a former body made manifest; as a chariot does not move on one wheel, fate does not succeed without effort.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: The Yoga Vāsiṣṭha: effort is everything; fate has no form, act or power and is only a name for the fruit of one's own former effort, which present effort overcomes; reliance on fate is delusion.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: The Nakkhatta Jātaka: the fool who waits on the lucky star misses his goal; the goal is the goal's own star — what can the stars do?
- Note: Varāhamihira, Yājñavalkya and the Yoga Vāsiṣṭha all define fate as one's own former action; they view one karmic process from the past (fate) and the present (effort), differing in the weight given to each.

## RQ-U21-2 — Is liberation reached by the yogī's body-discipline, or by the Name and the inner guru that the Sants set above it?  
`dsp:kabir-and-the-yogis` · skeleton

- **lin:natha**: The yogī reaches the supreme through the guru, restraint, bindu, the unstruck sound and sahaja; the Nāths themselves reject outward marks and sectarian worship ('neither temple nor mosque').
- **lin:kabir-panth**: Earrings, ash, matted hair and bodily yoga do not bring one to Rām; only love of the Name and the true guru's word do. In the Kabīr–Gorakh Goṣṭhī Kabīr is shown overcoming Gorakh.
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path: body-breath (Nāth) and devotion to the Name (Sant) as paths for different temperaments; both reject outward marks.
  - Shared vocabulary (sahaj, śūnya, anahad) suggests a common goal named from two paths.
- Note: not yet reconciled; the Kabīr side is to be stated from the Bījak by the owning unit (U27)

## RQ-189 — Was Kabīr a disciple of Rāmānanda?  
`dsp:kabir-guru-ramananda` · skeleton

- **lin:ramanandi**: Yes: Kabīr was one of Rāmānanda's twelve disciples; lying on the steps of the Pañcagaṅgā ghāṭ he received the Name 'Rām' when Rāmānanda stepped on him; his Rām is Rāmānanda's.
- **lin:kabir-panth**: No human guru was needed: Kabīr is the Sat Puruṣ incarnate who came in every age; he accepted Rāmānanda outwardly only to establish the custom of taking a guru (some panth accounts even make Rāmānanda his disciple).
- Candidate readings:
  - P6-upaya: the Kabīr Panth's own reading — Kabīr modelled discipleship as a skilful means; rejected by Rāmānandīs, who hold the discipleship real
  - P2-standpoint: outward initiation by Rāmānanda (social standpoint) and the inner satguru (the Lord) are both affirmed; rejected by the Kabīr Panth insofar as it makes Kabīr a disciple
  - Scholarly chronology (metadata only): Rāmānanda's traditional dates (c. 1299–1410) make direct discipleship difficult for a Kabīr of c. 1440–1518
- Note: Not yet reconciled: a question of lineage fact on which the two traditions' accounts differ.

## RQ-190 — Was Kabīr a Hindu or a Muslim? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:kabir-identity-hindu-or-muslim` · skeleton

- **lin:ramanandi**: A Vaiṣṇava devotee of Rām and disciple of Rāmānanda.
- **lin:kabir-panth**: Neither: the Sat Puruṣ appeared as a child on a lotus at Lahartārā and was raised by the Muslim weavers Nīrū and Nīmā; at his death only flowers were found, divided for a samādhi and a tomb at Maghar.
- **lin:sant**: Kabīr's own verses disown both: Hindu and Turk fight over Rām and Rahīm and neither knows the secret.
- Note: Under P2, 'Muslim weaver' (by upbringing), 'devotee of Rām' (by devotion) and 'neither' (by his own teaching) are statements from different standpoints.

## RQ-191 — Is the Rām of the Sants the avatāra, Rāma son of Daśaratha? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:kabir-ram-and-the-avatara` · skeleton

- **lin:sant**: No: 'the son of Daśaratha is praised in the three worlds, but the secret of Rām's name is other'; Rām is the unborn Lord in every body.
- **lin:sant-mat**: The avatāras come from the regions of Kāl to keep order; the Saints come from the highest region; the Saints are above the avatāras.
- **lin:kabir-panth**: Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Maheś are Kāl's sons; Kabīr, not the avatāras, frees souls.
- **lin:ramanandi**: Yes: the one whom the Vedas sing and sages meditate on is the son of Daśaratha, lord of Kosala, for the sake of his devotees; there is no difference between saguṇa and nirguṇa.
- Note: Under P3-path the same Rām is approached through the Name within (Sants) and through the avatāra's form (Rāmānandīs); Tulsīdās's own 'no difference between saguṇa and nirguṇa' supports this from the saguṇa side.

## RQ-U35-kala-as-dravya — Is time (kāla) a real, separate substance?  
`dsp:kala-as-dravya` · skeleton

- **lin:digambara**: Yes: time is a sixth substance, made of innumerable time-atoms, one in each space-point of the universe, which assist the continuance and change of all things; conventional time (instants, muhūrtas) is measured by the luminaries.
- **lin:svetambara**: Some teachers hold time to be a substance, others only the modes of souls and non-souls; the sūtra itself says 'time too, according to some'.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: time as substance (dravyārthika) vs time as the succession of modes (paryāyārthika).
  - Both sects accept conventional time; the dispute concerns only ultimate (substantial) time.
  - The Śvetāmbara tradition itself records both views as optional ('according to some').

## RQ-193 — Is the Kālacakra an authentic Buddhist tantra — and, for the Jonang, the definitive one?  
`dsp:kalacakra-authenticity` · skeleton

- **lin:jonang**: The Kālacakra with its bodhisattva commentaries is the pinnacle of the tantras and teaches the definitive meaning — the other-empty ultimate, the emptiness endowed with all supreme aspects.
- **lin:sakya**: (Rendawa, early in his career, as recalled) The Kālacakra's cosmology and some of its doctrines conflict with the Abhidharma and other tantras, so it should not be accepted as an authentic Buddhist tantra.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Kālacakra's cosmology as the 'outer' Kālacakra, a presentation for its own purpose, beside the Abhidharma's (compare Kongtrul's threefold cosmology).
  - P5-neyartha: the Jonang reading of the Kālacakra as definitive versus other schools' readings.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-194 — Is the Kāpālika way (skull vow, insignia, liquor, consort, Bhairava rites) a path to liberation?  
`dsp:kapalika-path-validity` · skeleton

- **lin:kapalika**: As reported: knowing and wearing the insignia, meditation on the self in the yoni, and the Lord's own path of liquor and the beloved lead to release (voiced only through opponents and satirists).
- **lin:visistadvaita**: Release comes only from knowing Brahman (Vāsudeva); these practices are outside the Veda.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: The Kāpālikas are deluded opponents defeated by Śaṅkara (hagiography) and by Viveka (allegorical drama).
- Candidate readings:
  - Await any first-hand Kāpālika/Somasiddhānta text before assessing.
  - P6-upaya: the skull vow as penance modelled on the Lord's own act (Kūrma P. 2.31) - a reading the critics do not accept.
- Note: Not yet reconciled - and not fairly reconcilable while the Kāpālika side survives only in opponents' words.

## RQ-195 — Must the body be perfected and made deathless (kāya-citti, kaṟpam) for liberation, or is knowledge alone enough, the body being a pot to be seen through? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:kaya-siddhi-or-jnana` · skeleton

- **lin:tamil-siddha**: The body must be perfected: when it perishes the life perishes and firm true knowledge cannot be reached; nourishing the body nourishes the life (TM 724); kāya-citti removes karma (Tiruvaḷḷuvar ñāṉam 14); vāci and kaṟpam give a lasting body (TM 722, 727).
- **lin:tamil-siddha**: Knowledge and grace alone: for the knower who has seen the true thing, what need of kaṟpam or yoga? (Kuṭampai 2, 22); stilling the mind is higher than every power (Tāyumāṉavar); the only medicine against death is never forgetting the Lord's feet (Paṭṭiṉattār); the body is a leaking pot to be left (Aḻukaṇṇi 8).
- Note: P4-stage: body-perfection is a means for the seeker (TM 724 names it as the condition for reaching knowledge); Kuṭampai speaks to the one who has already reached it.

## RQ-196 — In the omniscient, do knowledge (jñāna) and perception (darśana) occur in sequence, simultaneously, or are they one? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:kevala-jnana-darsana-sequence` · skeleton

- **lin:svetambara**: In sequence (kramavāda): following the canon, the omniscient knows in one moment and perceives in the next, since two cognitive activities cannot occur at once.
- **lin:digambara**: Simultaneously (yugapadvāda): since the obscurations of both are destroyed together, the omniscient knows and perceives at once, as the sun gives light and heat at once.
- **lin:svetambara**: They are identical (abhedavāda): in the omniscient the distinction of general grasping (perception) and particular grasping (knowledge) falls away; there is one cognition (Siddhasena Divākara).
- Note: Yaśovijaya's Jñānabindu relates the sequential, simultaneous and identity views to different standpoints (recalled; to be checked).

## RQ-U34-01 — Does the omniscient (kevalin) take food?  
`dsp:kevalin-eats` · skeleton

- **lin:svetambara**: Yes: while his gross (audārika) body lasts, the omniscient takes food and bears hunger, thirst and the other hardships that arise from feeling-producing karma.
- **lin:yapaniya**: Yes, with systematic arguments (Śākaṭāyana's Kevalibhuktiprakaraṇa).
- **lin:digambara**: No: the omniscient is free of hunger and thirst, has no bodily pleasure or pain, and his body is sustained without morsel-eating.
- **lin:svetambara**: Yes: the embodied omniscient keeps the feeling (vedanīya) karma and a gross body that is sustained by food; scripture narrates Mahāvīra's eating and illness; the Tattvārthasūtra says eleven hardships occur in the Jina.
- **lin:yapaniya**: Yes: the Yāpanīya grammarian Śākaṭāyana's Kevalibhuktiprakaraṇa argues that eating does not contradict omniscience.
- **lin:digambara**: No: eating presupposes desire and the working of delusion, which the omniscient has destroyed; feeling karma without delusion is powerless, like a seed without water; the omniscient's body is sustained without eating; TS 9.11 is figurative.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Digambara account speaks of the omniscient as he is in himself (free of desire and suffering, beyond the senses), the Śvetāmbara of the persisting physical body — both agree he has no desire or delusion
  - P1-level: 'eleven in the Jina' holds at the conventional level of the body's karmic state, 'no hunger' at the level of the soul's nature
  - P2-standpoint: the two sects read TS 9.11 from different standpoints (literal fruition of feeling karma vs its powerlessness without delusion).
  - P1-level: Digambaras speak of the omniscient's inner freedom from desire, Śvetāmbaras of the conventional bodily level — but Digambaras deny eating at every level, so this reading is rejected by them.
  - A factual disagreement about the omniscient's body that no principle may dissolve; keep both.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: both sides describe the same omniscient body and state incompatible facts about it.

## RQ-198 — Can valid cognition, the means of knowledge and the categories be coherently defined (and so difference established)?  
`dsp:khandana-definability` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Śrīharṣa: every proposed definition fails; the Advaitin can argue on the basis of commonly accepted usage without committing to the reality of the categories; only self-luminous consciousness is beyond refutation.
- **lin:nyaya**: Nyāya (and later Navya-Nyāya, esp. Gaṅgeśa): definitions can be refined to meet the objections; the categories and difference are real and knowable.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: Nyāya's definitions hold for empirical usage (which Śrīharṣa also allows), not as ultimate truths.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: a dialectical contest over the definability of knowledge.

## RQ-199 — What appears in erroneous cognition? (theories of error)  
`dsp:khyativada` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: The existent-and-non-existent (sadasatkhyāti): the erroneous object is sublated as there and not sublated as real elsewhere.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: The indescribable (anirvacanīyakhyāti): the erroneous object is neither real nor unreal.
- **lin:nyaya**: Appearance-as-other (anyathākhyāti): a real thing elsewhere appears as something it is not.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: each theory describes error from a different ontological standpoint (realist, indeterminist, relocation).
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-200 — Do deeds have fruit, and does effort matter — or are experiences fated, caused by God, uncaused, or ended at death?  
`dsp:kiriyavada-akiriyavada` · skeleton

- **lin:ajivika**: Makkhali Gosāla: there is no cause for the defilement or purification of beings; all are purified by wandering through saṃsāra by fate (niyati); effort does not affect it.
- **lin:sramana**: Pūraṇa Kassapa: there is no evil in killing nor merit in giving (akiriya); Ajita Kesakambalī: nothing survives death, there is no fruit of deeds (uccheda).
- **lin:early-buddhism**: The Buddha teaches the doctrine of kamma and of effort: kamma is intention, deeds bear fruit, and striving is fruitful; the tenets that all is due to past deeds, to God's creation, or to chance lead to inaction.
- Candidate readings:
  - No principle proposed; the Ājīvika and materialist sides survive only in hostile reports (see U33).
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the śramaṇa teachers' positions are known only through opponents' reports and are rejected by the Buddhist texts.

## RQ-201 — Kōan (critical-phrase) introspection or silent illumination? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:koan-or-silent-illumination` · skeleton

- **lin:yangqi**: Awakening is the standard; one must raise great doubt on the critical phrase ('wu') throughout daily life until it shatters; 'silent-illumination Chan' that teaches people to sit like cold ash and dead wood, and teachers who deny awakening, cut off the life of buddha-wisdom.
- **lin:caodong**: In silence the words are forgotten and illumination is clear; silence and illumination are one, knowing without touching things; the original field is already present and is revealed by letting go, not produced by striving.
- **lin:yangqi**: Wumen too lists 'keeping the mind clear and still' as heretical silent-illumination Chan among the faults of practice.
- Note: Both sides reject quietist 'silence without illumination'; they differ as methods suited to different temperaments and in whether awakening is framed as a breakthrough event.

## RQ-202 — Is Kṛṣṇa a form of Kālī, or is the Goddess a power of Kṛṣṇa? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:krsna-and-kali` · skeleton

- **lin:kalikula**: (Toḍala) The ten Mahāvidyās are the ten avatāras; 'Bhagavatī Kālī herself arose as the form of Kṛṣṇa' (10.11); Kālī is the source of Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Śiva (Nirvāṇa Tantra 10.42-43; Karpūrādistotra 12).
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: (Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇava) Kṛṣṇa is the Lord himself ('kṛṣṇas tu bhagavān svayam', Bhāgavata 1.3.28); Durgā is his one power of creation, maintenance and dissolution, sustaining the worlds like his shadow and acting by his will (Brahmasaṃhitā 5.44).
- Note: Each tradition describes the relation of Goddess and Kṛṣṇa from the standpoint of its chosen deity (iṣṭa-devatā); both agree the two are inseparable as power and power-holder.

## RQ-U50-14 — Is kuṇḍalinī — and the absorption it leads to — awakened by the yogin's effortful practice or by grace: the guru's, or the Lord's descent of power (śaktipāta)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:kundalini-effort-grace` · skeleton

- **lin:hatha-yoga**: Kuṇḍalinī sleeps at the mouth of the door of Brahman; to awaken her the yogin should practise the mudrās 'with every effort'; success comes only to one who practises — not from reading or wearing the garb. Yet the same text says the sleeping kuṇḍalī awakens 'by the grace of the guru', and the Gheraṇḍa has samādhi come through the guru's grace and one's own devotion. (Methods are restricted; summary only.)
- **lin:natha**: Kuṇḍalinī, coiled above the bulb and closing the door of Brahman, is to be awakened with the fire of yoga by mind and breath (Gorakṣaśataka); but the Amaraughaśāsana, also ascribed to Gorakṣa, says liberation marked by the raising of kuṇḍalinī from the root is not liberation — it lies in innate (sahaja) absorption.
- **lin:hatha-yoga**: The Amanaska: breath-control, mudrās, bandhas, mantra and meditation on objects are laborious and do not bring the mindless state; that arises without effort through the guru's grace and a gaze resting on nothing.
- **lin:kashmir-saivism**: Grace (śaktipāta) depends on the Lord's freedom alone — not on a balance of karma or the ripening of impurity — and is intense, middling or mild; for the highest grace a single hearing of the teacher's word suffices (anupāya); the śāmbhava, śākta and āṇava means correspond to lessening grace, the āṇava working through breath, bodily seals and places; grace may come by degrees or suddenly. 'The upsurge (udyama) is Bhairava'; 'the teacher is the means'. The signs of immersion are bliss, leaping up, trembling, sleep and whirling.
- **lin:kaula**: In the Kaula path there are two: the made (kṛtaka) kuṇḍalī, impelled by seals and mantras, and the innate (sahaja), abiding in sameness; the guru's initiations by touch, sight and — rarest — by piercing (vedha) awaken the disciple; the yogin rouses kuṇḍalinī 'by the method learned from the guru'.
- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: Grace descends when the soul's two deeds come into balance and its mala has ripened; the Lord, not the mala, is the cause, and he acts through the guru in initiation.
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: The mind's activities are stilled by practice and dispassion; practice is effort toward stability, firm when long, uninterrupted and devoted; for those of intense ardour samādhi is near — or through devotion to Īśvara, who favours the yogin.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: effort belongs to the practitioner's (conventional) side, grace to the Lord's side of one event — Śiva Sūtra 1.5 ('the upsurge is Bhairava') points this way; the haṭha texts do not frame it so, and the Siddhānta keeps the soul's ripening as the occasion.
  - P6-upaya: effortful methods as means for those who need them, discarded when the innate state dawns (Akulavīra, Amanaska) — rejected by the haṭha texts that make practice the cause of success.
- Note: The texts themselves pair effort and grace — the Haṭhapradīpikā has kuṇḍalinī wake 'by the guru's grace' and prescribes the mudrās 'with every effort' — and Abhinavagupta orders the means by the intensity of grace, from the means-less for the highest to bodily practice for the lesser (P4-stage; P3-path); whether effortful arousal is itself the way to liberation or is surpassed by the innate, effortless state remains not yet reconciled.

## RQ-204 — Is there an intermediate existence between death and rebirth?  
`dsp:kv-antarabhava` · skeleton

- **lin:pudgalavada**: There is an intermediate existence (held, per the commentary, by the Pubbaseliyas and Sammitīyas).
- **lin:theravada**: There is no intermediate existence: rebirth-linking follows death immediately; the intermediate state is none of the three existences.
- **lin:sarvastivada**: An intermediate being (gandharva) exists between death and birth (Sarvāstivāda Abhidharma, as known from its own texts; U38).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the gandhabba of MN 38 read as a being about to be reborn (Theravāda) or as an intermediate being (other schools)
  - Tibetan bardo teachings (U45) as a later elaboration of the intermediate state
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-205 — Can an arahant fall away from arahantship? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:kv-arahant-falling-away` · skeleton

- **lin:sarvastivada**: An arahant can fall away from arahantship (held, per the commentary, by Sammitīyas, Vajjiputtakas, Sabbatthivādins and some Mahāsaṅghikas).
- **lin:theravada**: One whose fetters are destroyed by the path cannot fall away; only mundane attainments (jhānas, 'temporary liberation') can be lost.
- Note: What can be lost is the pleasant abiding of attainments, not the destruction of the fetters — the two sides may speak of different things.

## RQ-206 — Does the arahant have imperfections (the 'five points')?  
`dsp:kv-arahant-imperfections` · skeleton

- **lin:andhaka**: An arahant can be made to emit semen by deities, can have ignorance and doubt, can be informed by others, and one may utter speech while in jhāna (held, per the commentary, by the Pubbaseliyas and Aparaseliyas (Andhakas)).
- **lin:theravada**: The arahant has abandoned lust, ignorance and doubt; none of these imperfections can belong to him.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: the opponents may have spoken of kinds of ignorance (of worldly matters) that the Theravāda would also allow in an arahant
  - The Mahāsāṅghika tradition's own sources (not read) may frame the points differently
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-207 — Did the Buddha really live in the human world and himself teach?  
`dsp:kv-buddha-in-human-world` · skeleton

- **lin:vetulyaka**: It should not be said that the Buddha lived in the human world, nor that he himself taught the Dhamma (a created form taught it) (held, per the commentary, by the Vetulyakas).
- **lin:theravada**: The Buddha was born, lived, awakened and taught in the human world; there are shrines, parks and villages where he dwelt.
- Candidate readings:
  - P6-upaya / P2-standpoint: a later doctrine of Buddha-bodies (U39) distinguishing the manifested from the dharma-body
  - The Theravāda would reject both readings as departing from the Buddha's human life
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-208 — Do Buddhas stand in all directions?  
`dsp:kv-buddhas-in-all-directions` · skeleton

- **lin:mahasanghika**: Buddhas stand in all the directions (held, per the commentary, by the Mahāsaṅghikas).
- **lin:theravada**: The Buddha does not say which direction or name such Buddhas; only one Buddha arises in one world-system at a time.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: one world-system versus countless world-systems
  - The Mahāyāna Buddha-field teachings (U39, U43) as a development of the thesis
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-209 — Is everything due to kamma?  
`dsp:kv-everything-due-to-kamma` · skeleton

- **lin:andhaka**: Everything is due to kamma (held, per the commentary, by Rājagirikas and Siddhatthikas).
- **lin:theravada**: Not everything is due to kamma: kamma itself is not due to kamma; experiences arise also from bile, phlegm, wind, seasons, careless conduct and assault (five orders of nature).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: 'everything' may mean everything experienced as result versus everything whatever
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-210 — Are the four truths penetrated gradually or all at once? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:kv-gradual-penetration` · skeleton

- **lin:sarvastivada**: The truths are penetrated gradually (one after another) (held, per the commentary, by Andhakas, Sabbatthivādins, Sammitīyas and Bhadrayānikas).
- **lin:theravada**: Each path penetrates all four truths in a single moment of knowledge.
- Note: Preliminary insight takes the truths separately; the path moment penetrates them together — the two theses may describe different phases.

## RQ-211 — Is there an intermediate jhāna between the first and second? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:kv-jhanantarika` · skeleton

- **lin:sarvastivada**: There is an intermediate stage of jhāna (jhānantarikā) (held, per the commentary, by some schools (names not asserted)).
- **lin:theravada**: There is no interval; the Abhidhamma's fivefold scheme already counts a jhāna with sustained but without applied thought as a jhāna, not an interval.
- Note: The state the opponents call an intermediate jhāna corresponds in content to the second jhāna of the Abhidhamma's fivefold count.

## RQ-212 — Can a householder be an arahant? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:kv-lay-arahant` · skeleton

- **lin:uttarapathaka**: A householder can be an arahant (held, per the commentary, by the Uttarāpathakas).
- **lin:theravada**: An arahant has no householder's fetter; one who attains arahantship does not remain a householder.
- Note: Attainment may occur while in lay dress, but the state is not lived as a householder's life.

## RQ-213 — Is the person (puggala) found as a real and ultimate fact? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:kv-puggala` · skeleton

- **lin:pudgalavada**: The person is apprehended as a real and ultimate fact (held, per the commentary, by the Vajjiputtakas and Sammitīyas (personalists)).
- **lin:theravada**: The person is only a conventional designation on the aggregates; it is not found as a real and ultimate fact as the aggregates are.
- Note: Both sides accept the person at the conventional level; they differ on whether it has any further reality.

## RQ-214 — Do past and future dhammas exist (does 'everything exist')?  
`dsp:kv-sabbam-atthi` · skeleton

- **lin:sarvastivada**: Everything exists — past, future and present dhammas (held, per the commentary, by the Sabbatthivādins).
- **lin:theravada**: Only present dhammas exist; the past has ceased and the future has not arisen.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: existence 'by own-nature' (Sarvāstivāda) versus existence 'as occurring' (Theravāda) as two senses of 'exists'
  - P1-level: both agree that only present dhammas are efficacious
- Note: Not yet reconciled: a difference about the being of dhammas in time.

## RQ-215 — Do the departed live on what is given here? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:kv-transfer-of-gifts` · skeleton

- **lin:andhaka**: Beings in the other world subsist on what is given here (held, per the commentary, by some schools (names not asserted)).
- **lin:theravada**: The robe or food given here is not itself used there.
- Note: The Theravāda denies a literal transfer of the gift while its practice of dedicating merit (with the departed rejoicing) continues.

## RQ-216 — Are space and dependent origination unconditioned?  
`dsp:kv-unconditioned-dhammas` · skeleton

- **lin:mahisasaka**: Dependent origination and space are unconditioned (held, per the commentary, by Pubbaseliyas, Mahiṃsāsakas and Uttarāpathakas).
- **lin:theravada**: Only nibbāna is unconditioned; if these were unconditioned they would be a refuge, the deathless.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the fixedness (niyāmatā) of the law of dependent origination versus the conditioned links themselves
  - Different counts of asaṃskṛta among the schools (U38)
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-217 — Is the Abhidhammatthavibhāvinī-ṭīkā a reliable guide to the Abhidhamma?  
`dsp:ledi-vibhavini` · skeleton

- **lin:ledi**: Ledi Sayadaw's Paramatthadīpanī points out places where the Vibhāvinī departs from the canon and commentaries.
- **lin:theravada**: The Vibhāvinī, long the standard sub-commentary in Burma, was defended by other Burmese scholars.
- Candidate readings:
  - Collate the specific points in Phase D
- Note: Not yet reconciled (points of dispute not yet collected).

## RQ-218 — Is Kaula practice — the Kula worship with the five tattvas, the circle and the cremation ground — a legitimate path to liberation, or a departure from dharma? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:legitimacy-of-kaula-practice` · skeleton

- **lin:kaula**: The Kaula path is the highest (Kulārṇava 2.7-8), both enjoyment and yoga (2.23-24), Vedic in essence (2.85), misunderstood by the deluded as musk is taken for mud (2.109); the realized Kaula is beyond injunction and prohibition (9.55-58). But it is harder than a sword's edge (2.122), must be given only to the tested (2.36; 14.9-14), and is condemned when practised for pleasure: wine alone gives no perfection (2.117), the wilful and uninitiated go to hell (5.96-105), and abandoning ritual out of false learning is heresy (9.130).
- **lin:kalikula**: (Rudrayāmala, voiced by Vasiṣṭha) The Siddhas of Mahācīna drink, eat meat and live with women 'outside the Veda' — how can perfection come without Vedic rites? The text answers through the Buddha that the Kula path begins in purity and the paśu disposition and is Vedic in its own way; the question is the orthodox objection it sets out to meet.
- **lin:srividya**: (Samaya Śrīvidyā) The sixty-four (Kaula) tantras beguile the world, aim at powers and are outside the Veda; the three higher classes should follow only the Vedic Samaya way (Lakṣmīdhara on Saundaryalaharī 31).
- **lin:kalikula**: (Kālīvilāsa) In the Kali age the Kaula (vīra) disposition does not exist; worship must be pure and meatless (4.2-3).
- Note: The Kaula texts address different eligibilities (P4) and speak of freedom from injunction only for the realized, from the ultimate standpoint (P1), while binding everyone else by the samayas and warnings.

## RQ-U44-legitimacy-of-tantra — Are the tantras the word of the Buddha and a legitimate Buddhist path?  
`dsp:legitimacy-of-tantra` · skeleton

- **lin:vajrayana**: Yes: the tantras were taught by the Buddha in the form of Vajradhara; they share the Mahāyāna goal and view and excel in means.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: No (as reported): the tantras are not the Buddha's word; their rites and conduct violate the Vinaya and resemble non-Buddhist practice.
- Candidate readings:
  - P5-neyartha: the tantras' offensive statements are provisional/intentional speech (the tradition's own reading) — answers the conduct objection, not the authority objection
  - P6-upaya: the tantras as skilful means for particular disciples (tīkṣṇendriya)
  - P4-stage: tantra restricted to consecrated and qualified practitioners (Atiśa's position)
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the critics deny the tantras' authority, which no hermeneutic principle internal to the tantras can establish for them.

## RQ-220 — At liberation, are the Lord's qualities transferred to, possessed by or produced in the soul - or is the soul's own Śiva-nature manifested?  
`dsp:liberated-soul-qualities` · skeleton

- **lin:pasupata**: The liberated gains the Lord's qualities: powers of knowing and acting, the marks of perfection, union with Rudra ('attainment of the qualities' as the gain of the fifth stage).
- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: Liberation is the manifestation of the soul's own Śiva-nature, not the transfer, possession or production of qualities; it comes through initiation, the Lord's act.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: described from the Lord's giving (Pāśupata) or from the soul's latent nature (Siddhānta).
  - No reconciliation: the Siddhānta's rejection is explicit.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Siddhānta explicitly rejects accounts in which the Lord's qualities come to the soul from outside.
