## RQ-221 — Does the liberated soul become identical with Śiva, or only equal to Śiva (śivasamatva) while remaining distinct?  
`dsp:liberation-identity-or-equality-with-siva` · skeleton

- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: Sanskrit exegetes: the freed soul becomes a Śiva, equal in omniscience and omnipotence, its own powers manifested; it remains distinct and does not perform the cosmic acts; the liberations of other schools (isolation, extinction, merger) are refuted.
- **lin:tamil-saiva-siddhanta**: Tamil school: release is inseparable union (advaita as ananya) — the soul, its malas powerless, abides in Śiva enjoying his bliss, 'one, other and together'; it neither becomes Śiva nor stands as a second Lord; 'Śivasamavāda' and 'Saṅkrāntavāda' are refuted.
- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: The liberated soul becomes equal to Śiva in omniscience and omnipotence but remains numerically distinct; the five cosmic acts remain Śiva's alone.
- **lin:pratyabhijna**: There is only one Maheśvara, the self of all beings (ĪPK 4.1); the bound individual already performs the five acts (PH 10); liberation is the recognition of that identity, culminating in lordship over the circle of one's own powers (PH 20). The Śiva Sūtra's 'he becomes like Śiva' (3.25) is read by Kṣemarāja in this sense.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: 'equality' from the standpoint of plurality of knowers, 'identity' from the standpoint of the one consciousness.
  - Treat as a case of dsp:souls-one-or-distinct (U50) and reconcile there.
- Note: Within the Siddhānta the two sides may be complementary standpoints (analytic: the soul's manifest powers; experiential: its enjoyment in Śiva); not yet reconciled with the non-dualists.

## RQ-222 — Is the Liṅgāyata/Vīraśaiva tradition a religion distinct from 'Hinduism' and the Vedic fold, or a Śaiva sampradāya within it — and are 'Liṅgāyata' and 'Vīraśaiva' one tradition or two?  
`dsp:lingayata-hindu-identity` · skeleton

- **lin:sarana-vacana**: Liṅgāyata is a distinct religion founded by Basava; it rejects the Veda, caste, temple worship and brāhmaṇical ritual; 'Vīraśaiva' names a Vedic-Āgamic Śaiva sect that is not the same as Basava's Liṅgāyata (the position of many Basava-centred writers and some Virakta maṭhas).
- **lin:virasaiva**: Vīraśaiva and Liṅgāyata are two names of one tradition, which has its own identity and scriptures (a position taken by many who reject the split of the names, whether or not they regard the tradition as outside Hinduism).
- **lin:pancacarya**: Vīraśaivism is a Śaiva sampradāya within Sanātana dharma, agreeing with the Veda and Vedānta, whose scripture includes the Āgamas, the SSM and the Śrīkarabhāṣya (the Pañcācārya position as generally stated).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the question is answered differently from a doctrinal standpoint (relation to Veda) and a communal one (distinct institutions and rites).
  - The names track the two strands (śaraṇa and Pañcācārya) rather than two religions.
- Note: not yet reconciled; recorded as the tradition's internal debate without taking sides

## RQ-223 — Are the transgressive statements and practices of the tantras to be taken literally or as intentional language? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:literal-or-symbolic-tantric-conduct` · skeleton

- **lin:arya-guhyasamaja**: Read by the six alternatives: many statements are intentional and not literal, pointing to completion-stage yoga (e.g. 'the bhagas of the vajra-women' = gnosis that destroys afflictions).
- **lin:mahasiddha**: The observance is actually lived by the realized yogin — cremation ground, outcaste companions, equal vision of pure and impure — but only after realization and as a hidden observance.
- **lin:kadam**: For monastics the literal higher consecrations are forbidden; the master's consecration, study and ritual are allowed (Atiśa).
- Note: By the tantras' own hermeneutic (P5) many statements are intentional; where conduct is meant literally it is restricted by stage and vow (P4): only the realized, and not monastics.

## RQ-224 — Where does ignorance (avidyā) reside — in the individual self (jīva) or in Brahman? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:locus-of-avidya` · skeleton

- **lin:bhamati**: Ignorance resides in the individual selves, with Brahman as its object; there are as many ignorances as jīvas.
- **lin:vivarana**: Pure consciousness (Brahman) is both the locus and the object of the one ignorance.
- Note: The tradition itself treats both as empirical-level explanatory methods (prakriyā) converging on the one self; at the ultimate level there is no ignorance to locate.

## RQ-225 — Should the liberated renouncer act for the world's good — teach, take disciples, follow scriptural conduct? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:lokasangraha-for-the-renouncer` · sourced

- **lin:sannyasa**: No: he undertakes nothing for the world's welfare, takes no disciples, keeps no books, gives no lectures; gathering disciples binds.
- **lin:sannyasa**: Yes, freely: though his work is done, he may keep to the scriptural path out of a wish to help the world — it does him no harm.
- Note: The prohibitions bind the renouncer on the way; the Avadhūta speaks of the one whose work is done, and the Sannyāsa's rule itself exempts disciples taken out of compassion.

## RQ-226 — Can the Lord be only the efficient cause of the world, as the Māheśvaras teach? (Brahma Sūtra 2.2.37ff, paśupati-adhikaraṇa)  
`dsp:lord-only-efficient-cause` · skeleton

- **lin:pasupata**: The Lord is the cause; the effect - vidyā, kalā, paśu - is eternal and depends on him; Rudra is the efficient cause, his Kalā the other cause (as Yāmuna reports).
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Brahman is both the material and the efficient cause; the view of a Lord who is only ruler is non-Vedic and inconsistent (partiality, no possible relation, no support for ruling insentient matter, finitude or non-omniscience).
- **lin:visistadvaita**: The Paśupati doctrine is contrary to the Veda and internally inconsistent; its practices (insignia, skull-eating, corpse-ash) are non-Vedic.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Pāśupata analyses the world as eternal effect under a ruler (causal standpoint); Vedānta speaks of Brahman as the substance of all (substance standpoint).
  - No reconciliation: Vedānta treats the non-material-cause view as contrary to śruti.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the disagreement concerns whether the Lord is also the material cause of the world.

## RQ-227 — What is the 'luminous mind' — the life-continuum, or the knowing heart known in meditation?  
`dsp:luminous-citta-reading` · skeleton

- **lin:theravada**: The luminous mind is the life-continuum consciousness, luminous because pure of defilements, which are adventitious.
- **lin:thai-forest**: The luminous citta is the knowing heart met in deep practice; its radiance itself can harbour ignorance and must be transcended (Ajahn Maha Boowa).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: analytic (Abhidhamma) versus experiential (forest teachers) description of the same passage
- Note: Not yet reconciled: a difference of reading rather than a recorded formal debate.

## RQ-228 — Are the scriptural passages Madhva quotes from works not otherwise known (e.g. 'Bhāllaveya śruti', 'Brahmatarka', 'Paramaśruti') authentic scripture?  
`dsp:madhva-sources-authenticity` · skeleton

- **lin:dvaita**: They are genuine scripture — quoted from Vedic recensions, Pañcarātra texts and Purāṇa portions available to Madhva but now rare or lost; Madhva, as Vāyu's avatāra who received the Vedas' meaning from Vyāsa, had access to them.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Critics of Madhva have questioned the authority of quotations that cannot be traced in known texts and declined to accept them as scripture.
- Note: Not yet reconciled — a question of textual history, outside the reconciliation principles.

## RQ-229 — Is the Madhyamaka a nihilism — does 'everything is empty' destroy karma, the path and the four truths? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:madhyamaka-nihilism-charge` · skeleton

- **lin:sarvastivada**: If all is empty there is no arising or ceasing, so the four truths, their knowledge and cultivation, the fruits, the Saṅgha, the Dharma and the Buddha, dharma and adharma and all worldly conventions are destroyed.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: The view of the śūnyavādin is contradicted by all the means of knowledge and deserves no detailed refutation; a world experienced by every pramāṇa cannot be denied.
- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: Kumārila examines and rejects the doctrine of the void (śūnyavāda) together with the doctrine that cognitions have no object: without real objects and cognitions there is no basis for ritual, merit or knowledge.
- **lin:yogacara**: Some who misunderstand the Perfection of Wisdom deny even the inexpressible reality that is the basis of designation; this 'wrongly grasped emptiness' is worse than a view of self. Something must exist for anything to be empty of something.
- **lin:madhyamaka**: The objector does not understand the purpose, nature and meaning of emptiness. Emptiness is dependent origination; for whom emptiness is possible everything is possible, while if things had own-nature there could be no arising, suffering, path or awakening. The Mādhyamika is not a nihilist: he does not deny action, agent and fruit but their own-nature.
- Note: Under P1-level the Madhyamaka affirms karma, path and the truths as conventional truth while denying their own-nature ultimately; the charge fits a nihilist reading that the Madhyamaka itself rejects (MMK 24.11).

## RQ-230 — Is consciousness ultimately real while external objects are not (mind-only), or is mind too empty? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:madhyamaka-yogacara` · skeleton

- **lin:yogacara**: Only consciousness exists; the external objects it appears as do not. The dependent nature (the constructing mind) is real and empty of the imagined subject–object duality; the perfected nature is that emptiness. The Saṃdhinirmocana's third turning, which distinguishes what exists from what does not, is the definitive meaning, while 'all dharmas lack own-nature' needs interpretation.
- **lin:prasangika**: Consciousness is no more real than its objects: both are conventionally accepted and ultimately empty. Reflexive awareness and the storehouse are not established; 'the three worlds are mind only' denies a creator other than mind and is of provisional meaning.
- **lin:svatantrika**: Bhāviveka rejects the Yogācāra's real dependent nature and accepts external objects conventionally.
- **lin:yogacara-madhyamaka**: Mind-only is correct as the account of conventional reality; ultimately even mind lacks own-nature. Riding both chariots one is a true Mahāyānist.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: mind-only as a step for students before the full teaching of emptiness (MAL 92).
  - P5-neyartha: the provisional/definitive division applied in opposite directions by each school.
- Note: Śāntarakṣita's synthesis reconciles the two under P1-level: mind-only holds at the conventional level, emptiness of mind at the ultimate.

## RQ-U26-05 — May the gods (devatās) be worshipped, and must caste and Vedic rites be kept, by one who seeks liberation?  
`dsp:mahanubhava-and-the-devatas` · skeleton

- **lin:mahanubhava**: The devatās are bound beings who give only worldly fruit; only Parameśvara and his incarnations are to be worshipped; caste and Vedic ritual have no place among seekers.
- **lin:dharmasastra**: The gods, rites and varṇa duties enjoined by śruti and smṛti bind all; a sect that rejects them is outside dharma.
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path (iṣṭa-devatā): each worships the chosen form — explicitly rejected by the Mahānubhāvas.
  - P1-level: devatā-worship as conventional, Parameśvara as ultimate — closer to the Mahānubhāva view but it forbids, not merely ranks, such worship.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Mahānubhāvas reject the plurality of worshipful gods that a P2/P3 reconciliation would assume.

## RQ-U34-05 — Was Mahāvīra's embryo transferred from Devānandā to Triśalā?  
`dsp:mahavira-embryo-transfer` · skeleton

- **lin:svetambara**: Yes: at Śakra's command Hariṇaigameṣin moved the embryo from the brāhmaṇī Devānandā to the kṣatriyāṇī Triśalā, since Arhats are born in kṣatriya families; the brāhmaṇa conception was the fruit of the pride Mahāvīra had felt as Marīci.
- **lin:digambara**: No: Mahāvīra was conceived by Triśalā alone; the transfer story is not accepted.
- Candidate readings:
  - The Śvetāmbara tradition itself classes the event as an extraordinary 'wonder', not the normal course
  - P2-standpoint (weak): the Śvetāmbara account teaches the karmic consequence of pride of lineage; the Digambara account stresses the purity of the Tīrthaṅkara's birth
- Note: Not yet reconciled; a difference in the sacred biography.

## RQ-U34-06 — Did Mahāvīra marry?  
`dsp:mahavira-marriage` · skeleton

- **lin:svetambara**: Yes: he married Yaśodā and had a daughter, Priyadarśanā (Anojjā), who married Jamāli.
- **lin:digambara**: No: he renounced as an unmarried prince (kumāra).
- Candidate readings:
  - Some Śvetāmbara lists call five Tīrthaṅkaras, including Mahāvīra, 'kumāra' at renunciation; Śvetāmbara commentators read this as 'not having ruled as king', Digambaras as 'unmarried' (low confidence on the list)
- Note: Not yet reconciled; a difference in the sacred biography.

## RQ-234 — Is the Mahāyāna the word of the Buddha?  
`dsp:mahayana-as-buddhavacana` · skeleton

- **lin:early-buddhism**: (As reported by Mahāyāna authors:) the Mahāyāna is not the Buddha's word; it is not found in the recited collections, and it teaches non-arising where the Buddha taught extinction.
- **lin:madhyamaka**: The Mahāyāna is the Buddha's word: the non-arising it teaches and the extinction taught to śrāvakas are one in meaning; the bodhisattva's aspirations and conduct are not taught in the śrāvaka scriptures; whatever reason establishes your scripture establishes the Mahāyāna; the Buddha taught according to each hearer's capacity.
- Candidate readings:
  - P6-upaya / P4-stage: the Buddha taught different vehicles to hearers of different capacity (RĀ 4.94–96; the Mahāyāna reading).
  - P5-neyartha: the Mahāyāna treats the śrāvaka teaching of extinction as agreeing in meaning with non-arising (RĀ 4.86).
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Mahāyāna's own principles (graded teaching, provisional meaning) include the śrāvaka teachings, but the śrāvaka traditions do not accept the Mahāyāna texts as canon.

## RQ-U39-4 — Are the Mahāyāna sūtras the word of the Buddha?  
`dsp:mahayana-buddhavacana` · skeleton

- **lin:mahayana**: Yes: they were taught by the Buddha to bodhisattva assemblies (and some kept by nāgas until beings could receive them); rejecting them is grave karma.
- **lin:theravada**: No: the Buddha's word is what was recited at the councils; the 'Vetulla' (Vaitulya) teachings are not the Buddha's word.
- **lin:yogacara**: Yes, by seven reasons: the Buddha did not warn of it as a future danger; it arose together with the disciples' vehicle; its content is beyond the reach of reasoners; it is established (by its results); whether or not a Mahāyāna exists, the conclusion follows; it is the antidote to afflictions; its meaning is not its letter (Mahāyānasūtrālaṃkāra 1.7); much of the Buddha's word has been lost (Vyākhyāyukti).
- **lin:early-buddhism**: No: the Buddha's word is what is found in the sūtras and vinaya as recited; the Mahāyāna teachings are not found there and contradict them (as the objector is presented in these texts).
- Candidate readings:
  - P4: sūtras addressed to disciples of higher capacity, not recited in the śrāvaka councils (the Mahāyāna's own account).
  - P5: the Mahāyāna sūtras as the definitive intent of what the earlier sūtras taught provisionally (Mahāyāna reading) — rejected by Theravāda.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: an authenticity question that none of the eight principles decides.

## RQ-236 — Is the root impurity (āṇava mala) a real substance removable only by ritual initiation, or is it ignorance - a contraction of consciousness - removed by knowledge?  
`dsp:mala-substance-or-ignorance` · skeleton

- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: Āṇava mala is a real, beginningless substance (dravya) that covers the soul's powers of knowing and acting; as a substance it cannot be destroyed by knowledge but only by an act - initiation (dīkṣā) empowered by Śiva - after its ripening; the liberated soul becomes equal to Śiva but remains distinct.
- **lin:trika**: Impurity is ignorance, the cause of the sprouting of saṃsāra (MVT 1.23); ignorance is not the absence of knowledge but its incomplete manifestation (TĀ 1.25-26); knowledge is the sole cause of liberation (TĀ 1.22); the āṇava impurity is consciousness losing its freedom (ĪPK 3.2.4); grace does not depend on the ripening of impurity or on a balance of karma but on the Lord's freedom (TĀ 13); initiation works because it is itself a form of knowledge.
- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: Mala is a substance (dravya); knowledge cannot remove it any more than knowing a cataract cures it; Śiva removes it through the initiation rite.
- **lin:tamil-saiva-siddhanta**: Āṇava is eternal and is never destroyed; grace makes its power ineffective, like a snake's poison stilled by a mantra; knowledge given by grace (not the soul's own effort) accompanies this.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: 'substance' and 'ignorance' describe the same bondage from an ontological (ritual) and an epistemic (gnostic) standpoint.
  - P4-stage: Abhinavagupta himself keeps initiation for those whose grace is not intense enough for knowledge alone, so the Siddhānta account could be read as addressed to that stage.
  - Not reconcilable while one side holds souls eternally distinct and the other holds them identical with Śiva.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the two schools disagree on what mala is.

## RQ-U34-04 — Was the nineteenth Tīrthaṅkara, Malli, a woman?  
`dsp:mallinatha-gender` · skeleton

- **lin:svetambara**: Yes: Mallī, daughter of King Kumbha, was a woman Tīrthaṅkara — one of the ten 'wonders' of this era — born female because of a small deceit in a past life.
- **lin:digambara**: No: Mallinātha was a man, like all Tīrthaṅkaras; a woman cannot reach the stages required.
- Candidate readings:
  - Śvetāmbara tradition itself marks a woman Tīrthaṅkara as an exceptional 'wonder', which limits the claim without denying it
- Note: Not yet reconciled; the question depends on the wider debate on women's liberation (dsp:women-caste-liberation).

## RQ-238 — Is the mantra way superior to the perfection way? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:mantra-and-paramita-ways` · skeleton

- **lin:vajrayana**: Same goal, but the mantra way is superior: unconfused, rich in means, not difficult, for the sharp; it gives buddhahood in this very life.
- **lin:mahasiddha**: Neither mantra nor pāramitā as systems sees clear-light mahāmudrā; the innate is beyond both.
- **lin:mahayana**: The perfection way is itself a complete path to buddhahood over three incalculable aeons (the sūtra position as presented in the tantric doxographies).
- Note: All sides agree the goal is one; the difference is in means suited to temperament and faculty (P3) and in the practitioner's stage and qualification (P4).

## RQ-U33-3 — Does anything survive the body's death — is there another world and rebirth, or is consciousness a product of the body that ends with it?  
`dsp:materialism-and-the-other-world` · skeleton

- **lin:carvaka**: (As reported) Consciousness arises from the four elements formed into a body, like intoxicating power, and perishes with them; there is no one who goes to another world, so there is none; the burnt body does not return; no one comes back to tell.
- **lin:sramana**: (As reported) Ajita: the person of four elements is annihilated at death; there is no other world.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: There is another world; the denier loses the wager either way; Kumāra Kassapa answers each of Pāyāsi's arguments with a simile; Śāntarakṣita argues that a first cognition presupposes a prior one, so mind is a beginningless continuum.
- **lin:jainism**: The soul is other than the body: Kesi answers Paesi; the Sūtrakṛtāṅga answers the five-element and soul-is-body teachers.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: The self is distinct from the body: consciousness is absent in the corpse, and what experiences the elements cannot be one of them.
- **lin:nyaya**: Having granted a knower that lasts through the body, the suśikṣita Cārvāka cannot show a cause of its destruction; so it is eternal and passes to other bodies.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: the Lokāyata speaks only of what perception establishes (the vyāvahārika world) — but it denies, not merely brackets, the other world.
  - P2-standpoint: the suśikṣita Cārvāka's 'a knower lasting as long as the body' concedes part of the opponents' case (Jayanta) — a partial convergence on the living knower only.
  - P6-upaya: the tradition's account that Bṛhaspati taught the doctrine to delude the asuras (Maitrī Up. 7.9) makes it a deliberate device — a reading the Cārvākas as reported would reject.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Lokāyata explicitly denies another world and a surviving self; every other party affirms them.

## RQ-240 — Is māyā the Goddess's real power (śakti), or an indeterminable ignorance that knowledge sublates? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:maya-sakti-or-avidya` · skeleton

- **lin:sakta**: Māyā is the Goddess's own power: she is Mahāmāyā, queen of the supreme Brahman, who transforms herself into mind and the elements, and is 'of the form of knowledge and ignorance'; even ignorance is knowledge, the world is the Lord (Kaula Upaniṣad).
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Māyā is the Lord's power but is beginningless ignorance, neither real nor unreal, removed by knowledge of Brahman; duality is mere māyā.
- **lin:srividya**: Within Śrīvidyā the Sahasranāma also calls the Goddess 'the substratum of the illusory world', a name read in Advaita terms by Advaita-inclined commentators.
- Note: At the conventional level the world is the Goddess's real play and power; at the ultimate level only she (Brahman) is — the Sahasranāma itself holds both names.

## RQ-241 — What does the injunctive form (liṅ etc.) convey, and what prompts a person to act?  
`dsp:meaning-of-injunction` · skeleton

- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: Kumārila: bhāvanā — the verbal force of the injunction (śābdī) prompting the person's activity (ārthī) toward a result, by means and procedure; the person acts from desire for the result.
- **lin:prabhakara-mimamsa**: Prabhākara: the injunction conveys niyoga, the command / what is to be done (kārya), which itself prompts the qualified person, and is identical with the apūrva.
- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: Maṇḍana Miśra: the injunction conveys that the act is a means to what is desired (iṣṭasādhanatā).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: prompting (śābdī bhāvanā), command (niyoga) and means-to-the-desired (iṣṭasādhanatā) may describe one injunction from the side of the word, of the hearer's duty and of the goal; the schools themselves argue these are exclusive.
- Note: Not yet reconciled; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-242 — How many members does a demonstration (inference for others) need?  
`dsp:members-of-inference` · skeleton

- **lin:nyaya**: Five: proposition, reason, example, application, conclusion; together they embody all four means of knowledge. A ten-member view (adding desire to know, doubt etc.) is rejected; stating fewer is the point of defeat 'saying too little'.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Fewer members suffice: Dignāga states thesis, reason and example; Dharmakīrti holds that for the competent the reason with its pervasion and presence in the subject is enough, and stating superfluous members is itself a fault in debate.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Three members suffice — either the first three or the last three (Vedāntaparibhāṣā); Mīmāṃsakas also hold three.
- **lin:jainism**: The number depends on the listener: two members for the sharp, more (up to ten) for others (Jain logicians, as usually reported).
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage / P6-upaya: the number of stated members depends on the hearer's capacity — a principle the Jain side states explicitly and Nyāya approaches by distinguishing inference for oneself (no verbal members) from inference for others.
  - The shared logical core (pervasion plus the reason's presence in the subject) is common to all sides.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: each side treats the other's practice as a fault in debate.

## RQ-U38-13 — Does a gift to the Buddha yield more merit than a gift to the Saṅgha?  
`dsp:merit-of-gifts-buddha-or-sangha` · skeleton

- **lin:dharmaguptaka**: Yes: though included in the Saṅgha, the Buddha as a separate field yields greater fruit; offerings to stūpas bring great fruit (Vasumitra).
- **lin:mahisasaka**: No: the Buddha is included in the Saṅgha, so a gift to the Saṅgha has great fruit (Vasumitra).
- **lin:sarvastivada**: Merit depends on giver, thing and field; merit arises even at a stūpa where no one receives.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Buddha as member of the Saṅgha (Mahīśāsaka) or as its unsurpassed field (Dharmaguptaka).
  - The Kośa's criterion: the field is distinguished by qualities; the highest gift is from the liberated to the liberated.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the relative merit of gifts to the Buddha and to the Saṅgha.

## RQ-244 — Does consciousness arise from the body alone, so that death is the end?  
`dsp:mind-body-rebirth` · skeleton

- **lin:carvaka**: Yes: consciousness arises from the four elements when combined as a body, like the intoxicating power from fermenting ingredients; there is no other world.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: No: breath, senses and cognition do not arise from the body alone without a prior moment of their own kind; the first cognition of a life comes from a previous cognition; hence rebirth, and the cultivation of compassion over many lives, are possible.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Lokāyata speaks only of what perception establishes; Dharmakīrti argues from the causal requirement of a homogeneous prior cause — the disagreement concerns the admissibility of inference.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Lokāyata and the Buddhists disagree about whether mind depends on the body alone.

## RQ-245 — In Prāsaṅgika Madhyamaka, is the ultimate only a non-affirming negation of true existence, or the union beyond all four extremes in which nothing is apprehended? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:mipham-gelug-madhyamaka` · skeleton

- **lin:nyingma**: Mipham: the enumerated ultimate is the negation of true existence, but the actual (non-enumerated) ultimate is beyond all four extremes, and in equipoise nothing — not even emptiness — is grasped; Madhyamaka has no thesis from the ultimate standpoint.
- **lin:gelug**: Gelug scholars (e.g. Pari Rabsel, Drakar Lobzang Palden) defended Tsongkhapa: the object of negation is true existence specifically; emptiness is a non-affirming negation and is apprehended in equipoise; to negate without qualification risks nihilism.
- Note: Mipham himself reconciles by levels: the Gelug non-affirming negation is valid as the enumerated ultimate for analysis; the union beyond extremes is the non-enumerated ultimate of equipoise.

## RQ-246 — Is everything that exists momentary?  
`dsp:momentariness` · skeleton

- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Yes: whatever exists is momentary, since existence is causal efficacy and an enduring thing could produce its effects neither all at once nor successively; recognition is a construction over a series.
- **lin:nyaya**: No: there is no reason for such a universal rule; things should be accepted as they are seen; causes of arising and destruction are observed only where things arise and perish; recognition shows enduring things and an enduring self; an enduring thing produces effects successively as auxiliaries join it.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Two proofs: from destruction — destruction has no cause, so a thing perishes by nature as soon as it arises (Dharmakīrti); from existence — whatever exists is momentary, since an enduring thing could be causally efficacious neither successively nor at once (Ratnakīrti: yat sat tat kṣaṇikam).
- **lin:jainism**: A real is characterized by origination, destruction and permanence together: substances endure while their modes arise and perish (Tattvārtha Sūtra 5.30, as recalled).
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Recognition and memory require an enduring knower; universal momentariness cannot even be stated coherently (Śaṅkara on BS 2.2.25, as recalled).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: momentariness as the standpoint of modes, endurance as that of substance (Jain).
  - P1-level: continuity (santāna) as conventionally valid in Buddhist accounts.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-247 — Is the mind one abiding thing with many objects, or only momentary cognitions each fixed to one object? (YBh 1.32)  
`dsp:momentary-cognition-vs-abiding-mind` · sourced

- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Vyāsa: the mind is one, has many objects and abides; otherwise every mind would be one-pointed, no mind distracted; memory, the enjoyer of the karma-store, and the single notion 'I' would be unaccountable.
- **None**: The unnamed opponent in the bhāṣya: the mind is only a cognition fixed to each object (pratyartha-niyata) and momentary; the one-pointedness of a stream of like cognitions belongs to the stream or to its parts. Vyāsa does not name the opponent.
- Candidate readings:
  - a dispute about the nature of mind stated inside one text; other traditions' own texts are needed for the opponent's side
- Note: not yet reconciled: recorded as an argument within the text; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-248 — May celibate monastics receive (and practise) the secret and wisdom empowerments? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:monastic-higher-initiations` · skeleton

- **lin:kadam**: No: the Ādibuddha tantra strongly prohibits the secret and wisdom empowerments for those who keep celibacy; taking them breaks the vow, incurs defeat, leads to the lower realms and yields no accomplishment; with the master's empowerment the celibate may still hear and teach all tantras and perform fire offerings.
- **lin:gelug**: Monastics may receive the four empowerments of Highest Yoga Tantra, the higher ones with a visualised wisdom-consort, and practise the completion stage without breaking celibacy; practice with an actual consort belongs only to a very advanced stage and the tradition's monks refrain from it.
- Note: Both forbid the celibate the literal practice; they differ on whether the empowerment itself may be taken in visualised form.

## RQ-249 — Is complete nudity required of the renunciant (and for liberation)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:monastic-nudity` · skeleton

- **lin:digambara**: Yes: the fully renounced monk is naked like a newborn; a clothed person cannot be liberated even if he were a Tīrthaṅkara; clothes are possessions.
- **lin:svetambara**: No: possession is attachment; clothes and bowls kept for restraint are not possessions; the canon allows monks three, two, one or no garments; nakedness belonged to the Jinakalpa, which lapsed after Jambū.
- **lin:yapaniya**: Nudity is the normal sign, but a garment may be kept by exception, and clothed persons and women are not excluded from liberation.
- Note: Both agree that the naked mode is the highest and the Jinas' own; they differ on whether it is required now of every renunciant — a difference the Ācārāṅga and Uttarādhyayana themselves frame by capacity and era.

## RQ-250 — Is awakening reached by the effortful arousal of kuṇḍalinī, or does it lie in the innate (sahaja), which such practices do not reach? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:natha-effort-or-sahaja` · skeleton

- **lin:natha**: Kuṇḍalinī, lying coiled at the door of Brahman, must be awakened by the fire of yoga with mind and breath and the five seals; then she rises through suṣumṇā and time is cheated (the haṭha position of the Gorakṣaśataka; the Haṭhapradīpikā adds that she wakes 'by the guru's grace').
- **lin:yogini-kaula**: The Kaula path is twofold: the kuṇḍalī impelled by seals and mantras is 'made' (kṛtaka); the innate (sahaja) abides in samarasa; the Akulavīra is not in any support, channel or breath-practice.
- **lin:natha**: The Amaraughaśāsana (also ascribed to Gorakṣa) denies that liberation is the movement of kuṇḍalinī raised from the root: liberation is where the mind sees the mind in sahaja-samādhi.
- Note: The haṭha texts themselves present effortful practice as a stairway to rājayoga/sahaja (HYP 1.1, 4.3–4, 4.114), so arousal of kuṇḍalinī belongs to the stage of practice and sahaja to the goal.

## RQ-251 — Do caste duties and Vedic/temple ritual bind the yogī, or are they bondage he must leave behind? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:natha-varna-and-ritual` · skeleton

- **lin:natha**: For one who knows the supreme there is no caste, sacrifice, fast, pilgrimage, image or rite; worldly and pledge-conduct are 'made bonds'; knowledge alone is to be relied on; the yogī meditates where there is neither temple nor mosque.
- **lin:dharmasastra**: There are four varṇas and no fifth; each life-stage has its duties, and renunciation comes in due order after the other stages.
- **lin:mimamsa**: Dharma is known from Vedic injunction; its enjoined acts are to be performed.
- Note: The Nāth rejections are spoken of the knower (the 'one in whom this liberation abides') from the ultimate level, while Dharmaśāstra and Mīmāṃsā address the one within the social order — and Dharmaśāstra itself exempts the renouncer from ritual duties.

## RQ-252 — What is the 'non-seeing' (adarśana) that is the occasion of the conjunction of seer and seen? (YBh 2.23)  
`dsp:nature-of-adarsana` · sourced

- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Views listed by Vyāsa as found in the śāstra: the office of the guṇas; the non-arising of the principal mind of the master to whom objects have been shown, that is, the absence of seeing while his own seen is present; the purposiveness (arthavattā) of the guṇas; ignorance stilled with its mind as seed of the mind's arising; the manifestation of the impression of motion on the exhaustion of that of stillness.
- **None**: Others in the śāstra: seeing-power itself is non-seeing; non-seeing is a property of both; knowledge itself is non-seeing.
- Candidate readings:
  - Vyāsa lists but does not decide; the passage is compressed (confidence low)
- Note: not yet reconciled: recorded as an argument within the text; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-253 — What is liberation?  
`dsp:nature-of-liberation` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: Isolation (kaivalya): the complete cessation of the threefold suffering; puruṣa has no properties, so liberation is not bliss, motion, annihilation or powers.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Liberation is realizing one's nature as Brahman, which is bliss.
- **lin:nyaya**: Liberation is absolute release from pain (Nyāya Sūtra 1.1.22), with the cessation of the self's specific qualities (Nyāya-Vaiśeṣika).
- **lin:jainism**: The liberated soul rises to the summit of the world (Tattvārtha Sūtra 10.5, recalled).
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level / P2-standpoint: descriptions by negation (Sāṃkhya, Nyāya: cessation of suffering) vs by affirmation (Advaita: bliss) of a state all agree is beyond saṃsāra.
  - P7-arthavāda: Sāṃkhya itself reads 'bliss' as figurative praise (SS 5.67–68).
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-254 — Is the Buddha's bodily existence supramundane, or human and impure (sāsrava)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:nature-of-the-buddha` · skeleton

- **lin:lokottaravada**: Supramundane: the Buddha's conduct, merit, postures, body, robes and food are beyond the world; his human acts are only conformity to the world.
- **lin:mahasanghika**: All Tathāgatas are supramundane and without outflows; body, power and life are limitless (Vasumitra).
- **lin:sarvastivada**: The Buddha's physical body is the result of former karma and can be the object of others' defilements, hence with outflows; one takes refuge not in it but in the non-learner's dharmas; Buddhas differ in lifespan and stature.
- **lin:theravada**: The Buddha lived in the human world; it is wrong to deny it (against the Vetulyakas per the commentary).
- Note: Both sides place what is truly 'Buddha' in the pure qualities (dharmakāya / the supramundane) and treat his worldly appearance as conventional — the Lokottaravāda explicitly as skilful conformity (lokānuvartanā).

## RQ-255 — Are the Vedic gods embodied persons, or are they what the mantras name? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:nature-of-vedic-deities` · sourced

- **lin:vedic-srauta**: Yāska reports three views: the gods are like persons (they are praised and addressed as sentient beings with limbs and belongings); they are not like persons (what is seen of them is fire, wind, sun, earth); or both.
- **lin:mimamsa**: For Mīmāṃsā the deity of a rite is what the mantra-word designates as recipient of the offering; embodied gods who eat the offerings and grant rewards are not needed — the rite produces its result (apūrva) itself.
- **lin:vedanta**: The gods are embodied, can take several bodies at once, and are eligible for the knowledge of Brahman (Brahma Sūtra 1.3.26–33, with Śaṅkara).
- Note: Yāska's own third answer — the gods may be 'of both kinds' — treats the personal and the non-personal descriptions as standpoints (as addressed in praise, as seen in nature).

## RQ-256 — Is nibbāna a true self (attā) or not-self?  
`dsp:nibbana-atta-or-anatta` · skeleton

- **lin:dhammakaya**: Nibbāna is the true self and a realm where the Dhammakāyas abide; the Dhammakāya within is the real refuge (the Dhammakāya tradition's teaching as reported by its critics and in its publications).
- **lin:theravada**: All dhammas, including nibbāna, are not-self; nibbāna is the unconditioned element, not a self: 'there is extinction but no one extinguished'.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: 'self' used as a name for the unconditioned (experiential language) versus 'self' as a metaphysical entity (denied)
  - P5-neyartha: positive language about nibbāna as provisional
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U14-05 — Is the Lord's saving grace without any cause in the soul, or does it take the soul's surrender as its occasion? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:nirhetuka-or-sahetuka-krpa` · skeleton

- **lin:tenkalai**: Grace is causeless (nirhetuka); nothing the soul does earns or occasions it ('mārjāra-nyāya').
- **lin:vadakalai**: Grace is free but takes surrender as its occasion (vyāja), lest the Lord be partial or cruel in not saving all ('markaṭa-nyāya').
- Note: Both deny that surrender earns grace; the Vaṭakalai 'occasion' is not a cause, so the difference concerns the seeker-side description of one divine act.

## RQ-258 — Do obligatory (nitya) rites have a result, and why are they performed?  
`dsp:nitya-karma-result` · skeleton

- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: They destroy sins done earlier and avert the future demerit that omission would cause; the seeker of release performs them to avoid that demerit.
- **lin:prabhakara-mimamsa**: They are performed because they are enjoined; the command itself prompts the person whose qualification is simply being alive, without a separate fruit.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Obligatory rites, done without desire, purify the mind and so prepare for knowledge; they do not themselves liberate.
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path / P4-stage: the same rites are described by their function for different aspirants (avoiding demerit, fulfilling a command, purifying the mind).
- Note: Not yet reconciled; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-U33-1 — Is everything fixed by destiny (niyati), or do effort and deeds shape a being's course?  
`dsp:niyati-or-effort` · skeleton

- **lin:ajivika**: (As reported by opponents) Everything is fixed: there is no cause for defilement or purification, no exertion, strength, energy or human effort; beings are transformed by destiny, circumstance and nature and reach the end of suffering after a fixed round that no vow or austerity can shorten.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: Deeds, action and energy are effective; all Buddhas teach it; there is an element of initiative, and beings are seen to initiate. Destiny, God's creation and no-cause alike end in inaction.
- **lin:jainism**: Effort exists: the Ājīvika potter makes his pots by effort and would punish one who broke them; a god gained his state by exertion. In the developed doctrine niyati is one of five co-causes, wrong only when taken alone.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Jain synthesis (Sanmati 3.53) treats niyati and effort as co-causes seen from different standpoints — the tradition's own reconciliation, but it rejects exactly the Ājīvika claim that effort is nothing.
  - P1-level: fixity might describe the whole round seen at once, effort the being's lived course — no text proposes this for the Ājīvikas.
  - P4-stage: none recorded.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Ājīvika position as reported denies effort any role, and the Buddhist position rejects destiny as a cause; no principle joins them without erasing one side.

## RQ-260 — Is levirate (niyoga) permitted? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:niyoga` · sourced

- **lin:dharmasastra**: Permitted under strict rules: a sonless wife or widow, appointed by the elders, may conceive a son by the husband's brother or a sapiṇḍa (MDh 9.59–63; also earlier Dharmasūtras).
- **lin:dharmasastra**: Forbidden: the learned condemn it as fit for animals, introduced under the wicked king Vena (MDh 9.64–68).
- Note: The commentators read Manu's permission and prohibition as belonging to different ages — possible for earlier people, not for those of the Kali age (a verse ascribed to Bṛhaspati is quoted to this effect); later lists count niyoga among kalivarjya practices.

## RQ-261 — How many kinds of consciousness are there — six, eight or nine?  
`dsp:number-of-consciousnesses` · skeleton

- **lin:sarvastivada**: Six: the five sense-consciousnesses and mental consciousness.
- **lin:yogacara**: Eight: the six, the afflicted mind and the store-consciousness.
- **lin:shelun**: Nine: beyond the store-consciousness there is a stainless consciousness (amalavijñāna) identical with suchness.
- **lin:faxiang**: Eight only: 'amala' is a name of the purified eighth consciousness, not a ninth (Kuiji).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the ninth consciousness may be the eighth considered in its purified aspect (as Faxiang says), so that the difference is one of standpoint.
  - P4-stage: six are taught to disciples, eight to bodhisattvas (the Saṃdhinirmocana's 'not taught to the childish').
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the count of consciousnesses differs among schools.

## RQ-262 — Are the instruments thirteen or eleven? (a debate among Sāṃkhya teachers)  
`dsp:number-of-instruments-samkhya` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: Thirteen: buddhi, ahaṃkāra, manas and ten external capacities, each inner organ with its own function (classical).
- **lin:samkhya**: Eleven: the functions of construction, self-assertion and determination are one; all objects are apprehended in manas (Vindhyavāsin).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: a counting convention — one inner organ with three functions vs three organs.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U50-15 — How many means of valid knowledge (pramāṇa) are there? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:number-of-pramanas` · skeleton

- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Two: perception and inference, because the object is twofold — the particular, capable of causal efficacy, grasped by perception, and the universal, grasped by inference; testimony and the rest are inference or not means of knowledge.
- **lin:vaisesika**: Two: perception and inference; verbal testimony, comparison, presumption and the other proposed means are included in inference, since they work in the same way.
- **lin:samkhya**: Three: perception, inference and reliable testimony, since all other means are included in these (presumption and inclusion in inference, absence in perception; tradition is testimony only if its speaker is reliable).
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Three: perception, inference and testimony are the means of valid cognition.
- **lin:jainism**: Valid cognition is knowledge that illumines itself and its object; it is of two kinds, direct (pratyakṣa) and indirect (parokṣa): of the five kinds of knowledge, sensory and scriptural knowledge are indirect, clairvoyance, mind-reading and omniscience direct; memory, recognition, reasoning and inference are forms of sensory knowledge.
- **lin:nyaya**: Four: perception, inference, comparison and verbal testimony; tradition (aitihya) is not different from testimony, and presumption, inclusion (sambhava) and absence are not different from inference. (Bhāsarvajña's Nyāyasāra, within Nyāya, counts three, comparison not being separate.)
- **lin:prabhakara-mimamsa**: Five: perception, inference, verbal testimony, comparison and presumption — neither fewer nor more; absence is not a separate means.
- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: Six: the five, and non-apprehension (abhāva/anupalabdhi), which knows the non-existence of a thing where the five positive means do not arise; the Vṛttikāra's list in Śabara already names six.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Six, following the Bhāṭṭas in empirical matters: perception, inference, comparison, verbal testimony, presumption and non-cognition; valid cognition is of an object not already known and not contradicted. Śrīharṣa: the Advaitin may debate on the practices both parties accept without committing to the reality of the means of knowledge.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: Three: perception, inference and scripture; comparison and the rest are included in these.
- **lin:dvaita**: Valid knowledge accords with its object: knowledge itself (kevala) and its means (anupramāṇa); the means are three — perception (sevenfold, with the witness), inference and authoritative word; presumption and comparison are kinds of inference.
- **lin:pasupata**: Three: perception (of the senses and of the self), inference, and scripture (āgama) handed down from Maheśvara.
- **lin:ayurveda**: Examination is fourfold: authoritative testimony, perception, inference and yukti (reasoning that sees effects produced by many combined causes).
- **lin:puranic**: Eight (as the Naiyāyika doxography reports): the Paurāṇikas add inclusion (sambhava) and tradition (aitihya) to the six.
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: In the matter of the transcendent, perception and inference are fallible; revelation (śabda) alone is decisive, and the Purāṇas complete the Veda.
- **lin:madhyamaka**: If the means of knowledge establish their objects, what establishes the means? Neither by other means (regress) nor by themselves (fire does not illumine itself) nor by their objects; 'if I apprehended anything by perception and the rest I would affirm or deny' — the Mādhyamika fixes no count.
- **lin:carvaka**: (As reported) Perception alone — inference fails because pervasion cannot be known.
- **lin:carvaka**: (Purandara) Inference as known in the world is valid; only inference to the transcendent is rejected.
- **lin:carvaka**: (Suśikṣita Cārvākas; Jayarāśi) The number cannot be fixed; no definition of any means of knowledge can be made good.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Perception and inference; one who denies inference uses it to convey his intention, and the world itself infers causes from effects.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint for the Cārvāka: some Cārvākas (Purandara) accept inference 'as known in the world' and reject only inference to the transcendent — a narrower dispute about the supersensible (see U33's sides); Nyāya and the Buddhists reject the restriction.
  - P1-level for Madhyamaka and Śrīharṣa: the means of knowledge are accepted conventionally, as the world uses them, and not established ultimately — the realist schools reject the ultimate/conventional split for pramāṇas.
- Note: Largely reconciled by the schools' own device of inclusion (antarbhāva): the Nyāya Sūtra folds tradition into testimony and presumption, inclusion and absence into inference; Vaiśeṣika, Sāṃkhya, Madhva and the Viśiṣṭādvaitins fold the extra means into fewer — differences of classification from different analytic standpoints (P2); the Cārvāka denial of inference, the status of testimony for the supersensible, and Madhyamaka's refusal to fix any means remain not yet reconciled.

## RQ-264 — How many tastes (rasa) are there? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:number-of-tastes` · skeleton

- **lin:ayurveda**: One (Bhadrakāpya) — not other than water; two (Śākunteya) — eliminating and pacifying; three (Pūrṇākṣa Maudgalya); four (Hiraṇyākṣa) by wholesomeness and sweetness; five (Kumāraśiras) by the elements; six (Vāyorvida) by qualities; seven (Nimi) adding alkali; eight (Baḍiśa) adding the unmanifest; innumerable (Kāṅkāyana) by substrates.
- **lin:ayurveda**: Ātreya: six — sweet, sour, salt, pungent, bitter, astringent; water is their source; alkali is a substance, the unmanifest a state, and innumerability belongs to the substrates.
- Note: Ātreya shows that most counts classify tastes by another standpoint (source, action, element, quality, substrate) while rejecting alkali and the unmanifest as tastes.

## RQ-265 — Is the world false (mithyā), and can the Advaita definitions of falsity withstand Vyāsatīrtha's critique? (The Nyāyāmṛta–Advaitasiddhi controversy)  
`dsp:nyayamrta-advaitasiddhi` · skeleton

- **lin:dvaita**: The world is real. None of the five Advaita definitions of falsity is coherent; the inferences from 'perceivable', 'insentient' and 'limited' fail; perception, scripture and above all the witness (sākṣin) establish the world's reality, and what the witness establishes is never sublated. The Upaniṣads do not teach an impartite meaning; the flawless Brahman is absolutely different from the flawed souls; liberation is not mere cessation of ignorance, and the liberated are graded.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: The world is mithyā: not ultimately real, since it is sublated by the knowledge of Brahman, yet not unreal like a hare's horn, since it appears and functions; Madhusūdana defends each of the five definitions of falsity point by point. Perception shows only empirical existence and does not contradict the Upaniṣadic teaching of non-duality, whose purport is the one Brahman; difference belongs to the empirical order; liberation is the cessation of ignorance by knowledge of identity.
- Candidate readings:
  - {"principle": "P1-level", "reading": "Difference and the world are real at the empirical (vyāvahārika) level, as Advaita itself grants, and non-difference holds at the ultimate level."}
  - {"principle": "P2-standpoint", "reading": "Dvaita speaks from the standpoint of the dependent self and its objects, Advaita from the standpoint of the absolute; each is true within its standpoint."}
  - {"principle": "P4-stage", "reading": "The reality of difference is taught to the seeker; non-difference is realized at the end."}
- Note: Not yet reconciled: both sides reject the level, standpoint and stage readings.

## RQ-266 — Do objects exist independently of the mind, or are they only constructions of cognition?  
`dsp:object-independent-of-mind` · skeleton

- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: The object is common to many minds and stands on its own; it is not constructed by one mind or many.
- **lin:yogacara**: The three worlds are cognition only (vijñapti-mātra); objects appear without external referents, as in dreams.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: Yogācāra's 'mind-only' as a claim about the ultimate, Yoga's realism about the conventional (Yoga would reject the two-level frame).
  - P4-stage: Yogācāra texts present mind-only as a stage of meditative insight.
  - Leave distinct: YBh 4.14–23 is an explicit refutation.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: Yoga and Yogācāra each explicitly reject the other's account of objects.

## RQ-267 — Is devotion directed to the Lord as other, to the one self, or to both? (the Śāṇḍilya Sūtra's three teachers) (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:object-of-bhakti-sbs` · skeleton

- **lin:bhakti-sastra**: Kāśyapa (as reported in the Śāṇḍilya Sūtra): devotion is directed to the Lord's majesty (aiśvarya), because he is other than the soul.
- **lin:vedanta**: Bādarāyaṇa (as reported in the Śāṇḍilya Sūtra): devotion is directed to the self as one.
- **lin:bhakti-sastra**: Śāṇḍilya: devotion is directed to both, on the grounds of scripture and reasoning.
- Note: The sūtra's own conclusion ('both') treats the Lord-as-other and the self-as-one as two standpoints on devotion's object, each supported by scripture.

## RQ-U47-1 — What does Madhyamaka reasoning negate: inherent existence only, or all four extremes including existence itself?  
`dsp:object-of-negation-madhyamaka` · skeleton

- **lin:gelug**: The object of negation is inherent existence (existence from the object's own side, not posited by conceptual designation). Things are not negated conventionally; emptiness is a non-affirming negation of inherent existence, and conventional existence, cause and effect and valid cognition remain. To negate existence as such is to negate too much and fall into nihilism.
- **lin:sakya**: The final view is freedom from elaborations: at the level of ultimate analysis all four extremes — existence, non-existence, both, neither — are negated without qualification, and clinging to emptiness as a mere negation is itself an extreme; Tsongkhapa's system, by adding the qualifier and holding the ultimate to be a non-affirming negation, is 'Madhyamaka of the nihilist extreme' and also leaves a subtle grasping.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: Gelug speaks of the object ascertained by a reasoning consciousness (a non-affirming negation), Gorampa of the meditative equipoise in which no extreme is grasped — possibly two standpoints on one realisation.
  - P4-stage: Gorampa himself treats the negation of true existence as a step; the Gelug also holds that in direct realisation emptiness appears without dualistic elaboration.
  - P5-neyartha: each side reads the other's key citations as provisional.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: both traditions insist the difference is real and doctrinal.

## RQ-269 — What are the objects of the bliss (ānanda) and I-am-ness (asmitā) forms of samprajñāta samādhi, and who are the videhas and prakṛtilayas?  
`dsp:objects-of-samprajnata` · skeleton

- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Vācaspati Miśra: reasoning and reflection have the grasped (gross and subtle elements) as object; bliss has the grasping (the senses, sattva-dominated and pleasant) as object; I-am-ness has the grasper, the sense of 'I' in which the puruṣa is mixed with buddhi; sub-varieties 'with bliss' and 'with I-am-ness' are admitted.
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Bhoja: sānanda is cultivation of the inner organ's sattva tinged by rajas and tamas (grasping-coalescence) — those content there are the 'videhas'; sāsmita is cultivation of pure sattva, mere being — those content there are 'prakṛtilayas'; discernment of the supreme puruṣa is grasper-coalescence.
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Vijñānabhikṣu: bliss is a joy arising from preponderant sattva, taken as object; it is not the senses; asmitā is awareness with the self alone as object (the individual self or the Supreme Self); there are no 'with bliss / without bliss' sub-varieties.
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Vyāsa: the bodiless (videha) are gods who experience a quasi-kaivalya with a mind using its own impressions (YBh 1.19); bliss is joy, I-am-ness awareness of a single self (YBh 1.17).
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: read the accounts as finer or coarser maps of the same ascent.
  - P2-standpoint: objective (grāhya–grahaṇa–grahītṛ) vs experiential (joy, 'I am') descriptions.
  - Leave distinct: Vijñānabhikṣu explicitly refutes the senses-as-object view.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the commentators assign different objects to the upper samprajñāta levels and identify the videhas differently.

## RQ-270 — Can any person be omniscient, in particular directly know dharma?  
`dsp:omniscience` · skeleton

- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: No person perceives dharma: an omniscient is not seen now, cannot be shown to have existed, cannot be established by his own scripture without circularity, and could not be recognized by the non-omniscient; an 'omniscience' by all six means of knowledge would not be denied, but perception never grasps tastes by the eye nor the future dharma.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: The Buddha is an authoritative person whose knowledge of what is to be abandoned and adopted, with its means, is established; Śāntarakṣita and Kamalaśīla examine the 'seer of supersensible things' at length against Kumārila.
- **lin:jainism**: The Jina's pure knowledge (kevala-jñāna) directly knows all substances and modes; subtle, hidden and remote things are perceptible to someone (Samantabhadra); Akalaṅka and later Jain logicians answer Kumārila's arguments.
- **lin:nyaya**: Īśvara is omniscient; yogic perception, arising from special merit, grasps the supersensible.
- **lin:yogacara-madhyamaka**: Yes: the mind's clarity is by nature and defilements are adventitious, so by cultivation the knowledge of supersensible things can be perfected; the Buddha is such a knower (Tattvasaṃgraha, last chapter).
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: What matters is not that the Buddha knows everything (e.g. the number of insects) but that he knows what is to be abandoned and adopted with its means (Dharmakīrti); later authors (Ratnakīrti's Sarvajñasiddhi) prove full omniscience.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: Kumārila concedes that an omniscience by all six means of knowledge would not be refuted (ŚV codanā 111); if Buddhist and Jain claims are read as knowledge of what matters for liberation rather than perception of Vedic dharma, part of the dispute narrows — each tradition would reject this narrowing of its own claim.
  - P3-path: the claims concern different paths (ritual dharma vs liberating insight).
- Note: Not yet reconciled; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-271 — Is the conscious self one or many? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:one-or-many-purusas` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: Many: each being has its own puruṣa.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: One: the self is one; apparent plurality comes from limiting adjuncts, like space in pots.
- Note: Sāṃkhya's own reading (SS 1.154): non-duality texts speak of sameness of kind, plurality of number — one and many under different standpoints.

## RQ-272 — Are the puruṣas many, or is there one Puruṣa? (MBh 12.294–296, 12.338) (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:one-or-many-purusas-epic` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: The Sāṃkhya-Yoga thinkers hold that there are many puruṣas and do not accept a single one (as reported in the Mokṣadharma).
- **lin:epic-teaching**: The many puruṣas have one source, the universal Puruṣa of the Puruṣa-sūkta (12.338.3–24); the twenty-fifth, awakening, attains the state of the twenty-sixth (12.296.7–12), and Vasiṣṭha contrasts the knowledge of plurality with that of unity.
- Note: The Mokṣadharma itself frames the answer as 'many as individuals, one as their source' (12.338.3), a difference of standpoint (the individuals vs their ground).

## RQ-273 — Is there one prakṛti for all puruṣas, or one for each? (a debate among Sāṃkhya teachers)  
`dsp:one-prakrti-or-many` · skeleton

- **lin:samkhya**: One prakṛti, common to all (classical).
- **lin:samkhya**: A separate pradhāna for each puruṣa, all following the pradhāna of the great-bodied one (Paurika).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: cosmic prakṛti (one) vs individual prakṛti as each puruṣa's field of experience (many).
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-274 — Is there one rasa underlying all, and if so which? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:one-rasa-or-many` · skeleton

- **lin:alankara**: Bharata: eight (or nine) distinct rasas, four of which generate the other four.
- **lin:alankara**: Bhavabhūti: compassion (karuṇa) alone is rasa, appearing as the others as water appears as eddies, bubbles and waves.
- **lin:alankara**: Bhoja: the one rasa is śṛṅgāra understood as self-regard (abhimāna, ahaṃkāra).
- **lin:alankara**: Nārāyaṇa (cited by Viśvanātha): wonder is the essence of all rasa, so the marvellous alone is rasa.
- **lin:alankara**: Abhinavagupta: śānta is the natural state from which all emotions arise and into which they dissolve.
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Rūpa Gosvāmī: love for Kṛṣṇa (bhakti-rasa), culminating in the erotic-devotional (madhura), is the supreme rasa.
- Note: Each 'one rasa' names the ground of aesthetic experience from a different standpoint — pathos, self-regard, wonder, peace, love of God — while Bharata's list describes the variety as it appears.

## RQ-U39-2 — Is the one vehicle the final truth and the three vehicles a skillful means — or are the three vehicles real and the 'one vehicle' spoken with an intention?  
`dsp:one-vehicle-or-three` · skeleton

- **lin:mahayana**: One vehicle only: the three vehicles are the Buddha's skillful means to lead beings into the one buddha-vehicle (Lotus; Śrīmālā); the arhat's nirvāṇa is a conjured city.
- **lin:yogacara**: The three vehicles correspond to real differences of lineage; the 'one vehicle' was taught with an intention (the sameness of the dharma-realm, of no-self and of liberation), to attract the indeterminate.
- Candidate readings:
  - Lotus/Tiantai: the three are upāya (P6), the one is nītārtha.
  - Yogācāra/Faxiang: the one is abhiprāya-speech (P5), the three are nītārtha.
  - Stage reading (P4): three vehicles for beings at different stages, one vehicle as the eventual end for all.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: both sides apply upāya/neyārtha, in opposite directions.

## RQ-U38-11 — Is the mind by nature luminous and only adventitiously defiled?  
`dsp:original-purity-of-mind` · skeleton

- **lin:mahasanghika**: Yes: the nature of mind is originally pure, defiled by adventitious defilements (Vasumitra).
- **lin:vibhajyavada**: Yes (as reported in the Vibhāṣā).
- **lin:sarvastivada**: No: a mind with defilement is not pure; purity and defilement are successive states.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: luminous in its nature (ultimate), defiled in its occurrence (conventional) — rejected by the Sarvāstivāda, for which a defiled mind is not pure in any respect.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: whether the mind is by nature luminous.

## RQ-277 — Are other mind-streams established? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:other-minds-buddhist` · skeleton

- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Yes: another's mind is inferred from observed action and speech, as one's own actions are preceded by intention; the proof holds for proponents of external objects and of cognition-only alike (Dharmakīrti).
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Not ultimately: once all appearances are one's own cognition, the inference fails at the ultimate level; others are admitted conventionally (Ratnakīrti).
- Note: P1: Dharmakīrti's proof works at the conventional level; Ratnakīrti's denial is at the ultimate level and he himself allows others conventionally.

## RQ-278 — Are things established by their own nature (svabhāva), or is everything relative and empty?  
`dsp:own-nature-of-things` · skeleton

- **lin:nyaya**: Things are established by their own nature; the claim that nothing is so established because everything is relative is self-defeating; 'everything is non-existence' is rejected.
- **lin:madhyamaka**: Things lack own-nature because they arise dependently; emptiness is not nothingness but dependent origination (Nāgārjuna).
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: Madhyamaka affirms conventional existence of things while denying own-nature ultimately.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-279 — Are the five 'M's to be used literally, replaced by substitutes, interiorized, or rejected? (Summary only.) (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:pancamakara-literal-or-substitute` · skeleton

- **lin:kaula-srividya**: Bliss is Brahman's form in the body and the five 'M's manifest it; they are used in secret worship; when unavailable the daily worship continues without them.
- **lin:samaya-srividya**: Rejected: the Samaya way admits no Kaula rites or worship of a young woman's triangle.
- **lin:srividya**: The right-hand (dakṣiṇa) mode worships with substitutes (anukalpa) or with inner equivalents; the Goddess is worshipped on both right and left paths.
- Note: The traditions themselves assign the modes by qualification and stage, and the Kalpasūtra's continuation of worship without the 'M's shows they are not strictly indispensable.

## RQ-280 — Do Brahma Sūtras 2.2.42–45 reject or uphold the Pāñcarātra?  
`dsp:pancaratra-brahma-sutra` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Śaṅkara: they reject the Pāñcarātra doctrine that the jīva (Saṅkarṣaṇa) and mind originate from Vāsudeva, while accepting its worship and meditation.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: Rāmānuja: the sūtras state an objection (2.2.42–43) and answer it (2.2.44–45), upholding the Pāñcarātra as authoritative; 'origination' refers to the Lord's forms (vyūhas), not the soul's creation.
- Candidate readings:
  - Both agree that the individual soul is not created; they differ on whether the Pāñcarātra teaches its creation.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: opposite readings of the same sūtras.

## RQ-281 — Does the Lord act independently of the karma of souls?  
`dsp:pasupata-isvara-karma` · skeleton

- **lin:pasupata**: The Lord depends neither on karma nor on the soul; he produces the effect at will, all at once or in sequence.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: The Lord's dispensation depends on beings' karma; otherwise he would be partial and cruel; and a Lord who is merely the efficient cause cannot escape either partiality or circular dependence on karma.
- **lin:nyaya**: The Lord is the cause who makes human effort fruitful; results do not arise without human action (Nyāya Sūtra 4.1.19-21 as read by its commentators).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Pāśupata speaks from the divine standpoint (the Lord's freedom), Vedānta and Nyāya from the causal standpoint (karma's regularity); the SDS reply itself gives karma a fruit-bearing role under the Lord's will.
  - P1-level: at the conventional level karma governs results; the Lord's freedom concerns the level of the ultimate cause.
  - No reconciliation: the traditions disagree about whether the Lord could act otherwise than karma requires.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Pāśupatas deny that the Lord depends on karma; Vedānta holds that denying dependence makes him partial.

## RQ-282 — Is the Pāśupata (and wider Atimārga) teaching within or outside the Veda?  
`dsp:pasupata-vedic-status` · skeleton

- **lin:pasupata**: The Pāśupata path is the best of all paths, the true path, others being bad paths; its scripture comes from Maheśvara through the teachers; it uses the Vedic brahma-mantras and is for brahmins; only from the Pañcārtha comes true ascertainment of reality.
- **lin:puranic**: The Vedic Pāśupata vow of the Atharvaśiras is to be followed; the Pāśupata, Soma, Lākula, Vāma and Bhairava scriptures are non-Vedic and were taught for delusion.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: All four Māheśvara groups are outside the Veda: they contradict the Vedic Brahman as material and efficient cause and devise āśramas outside the Vedic order.
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Pāśupata is one of five systems of knowledge (with Sāṃkhya, Yoga, Pañcarātra and the Vedas), taught by Śiva Śrīkaṇṭha; Nārāyaṇa is the foundation in all.
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path: the Nārāyaṇīya's own stance - several systems for different temperaments with one foundation.
  - P4-stage: the Pāśupata presents itself as a state beyond the āśramas for qualified brahmins (atyāśrama).
  - No reconciliation: the Kūrma Purāṇa and Yāmuna explicitly deny Vedic authority to these scriptures.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Pāśupatas claim the highest path; Purāṇic and Vaiṣṇava critics place the Pāśupata scriptures outside the Veda.

## RQ-283 — Is Speech at the level of paśyantī (the grammarians' Word-Brahman) the ultimate reality?  
`dsp:pasyanti-brahman` · skeleton

- **lin:vyakarana**: The beginningless and endless Brahman is the principle of the Word (śabdatattva), from which the world unfolds; paśyantī, the undivided 'seeing' speech, is the highest level of speech (Bhartṛhari).
- **lin:pratyabhijna**: What the grammarians call paśyantī is only the knowledge-power at the level of Sadāśiva (Śivadṛṣṭi 2.1); the ultimate is Śiva with will, knowledge and action; speech as reflexive awareness is 'supreme speech' (parā vāk) beyond paśyantī (ĪPK 1.5.13).
- **lin:vyakarana**: Yes: Brahman is the beginningless Word-principle; paśyantī, undivided and with all sequence withdrawn, the inner self-light, is the supreme station of speech; all cognition is word-natured.
- **lin:pratyabhijna**: No: paśyantī is only the power of knowledge at the level of Sadāśiva; the ultimate is Śiva with will, knowledge and action, and supreme speech (parā vāk) is reflexive awareness itself, beyond paśyantī.
- **lin:mantrasastra**: The mantra manuals, accepting the four levels, place parā above paśyantī and define śabda-brahman as the consciousness of all beings rather than as word or meaning.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: both accept paśyantī as a real level of speech; the Śaivas place parā vāk above it, so the question narrows to whether a level above paśyantī is admitted.
  - P2-standpoint: Utpaladeva's parā vāk and Bhartṛhari's Word-principle may name the same reflexive consciousness from different standpoints.
  - P1-level: all accept paśyantī as a real level; the question narrows to whether a level above it (parā) is admitted — later grammarians (Nāgeśa) themselves admit parā.
  - P2-standpoint: Bhartṛhari's word-nature of awareness (pratyavamarśa) and Utpaladeva's reflexive awareness (vimarśa) may name one reflexivity of consciousness from the grammarian's and the Śaiva's standpoints.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: which level of speech is ultimate.

## RQ-U22-pattinattar — Is the Siddhar Paṭṭiṉattār the same person as Paṭṭiṉattup Piḷḷaiyār of the eleventh Tirumuṟai?  
`dsp:pattinattar-identity` · skeleton

- **lin:tamil-siddha**: The tradition: one Paṭṭiṉattār (Tiruveṇkāṭar of Kāvirippūmpaṭṭiṉam) composed both the Tirumuṟai poems and the Siddhar songs; his life is one story from the eyeless needle to Tiruvoṟṟiyūr.
- **lin:nayanmar**: The scholarly account (recorded as metadata): the Tirumuṟai poet (c. 10th c.) and the author(s) of the Siddhar songs (later, in a different idiom) are distinct; the songs may have more than one author.
- Candidate readings:
  - Tradition's account: one poet.
  - Scholarly account: two or more poets sharing a name and a legend.
- Note: not yet reconciled: a historical question of authorship to which the eight principles do not apply

## RQ-U14-07 — Is kaivalya (the soul's isolated enjoyment of itself) a permanent state?  
`dsp:permanence-of-kaivalya` · skeleton

- **lin:vadakalai**: No: those who attain kaivalya eventually go on to seek the Lord and attain him.
- **lin:tenkalai**: Yes: it is a permanent state at the edge of the eternal realm, from which there is no return.
- Candidate readings:
  - P5-neyartha: one side's statement read as provisional
  - both agree kaivalya is inferior to the Lord's service
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-286 — Is the Tathāgata permanent (his parinirvāṇa a display), or is parinirvāṇa the final end, the Tathāgata after death being undeclared? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:permanence-of-the-tathagata` · skeleton

- **lin:mahayana**: The Tathāgata's lifespan is immeasurable; he awakened aeons ago and shows parinirvāṇa as skillful means; his body is the dharmakāya, the vajra body, permanent.
- **lin:theravada**: At parinibbāna the Tathāgata is released from reckoning by the aggregates; whether he exists after death is not declared; like a fire gone out he cannot be said to have gone anywhere.
- Note: Both deny that the Tathāgata can be reckoned by the aggregates; the Mahāyāna calls the undescribable dharmakāya 'permanent' at the ultimate level and the parinirvāṇa an upāya.

## RQ-287 — When a clay pot is baked, is the change of colour in the atoms or in the whole pot?  
`dsp:pilupaka-pitharapaka` · skeleton

- **lin:vaisesika**: In the atoms (pīlupāka): the pot disintegrates into atoms, which change colour under heat, and a new pot is formed.
- **lin:nyaya**: In the whole (piṭharapāka): the colour of the pot changes without its being destroyed, heat reaching the parts through the pores.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: atomic vs. gross-object description of one process.
- Note: Not yet reconciled (an internal Nyāya-Vaiśeṣika debate).

## RQ-288 — Can one object be known by more than one means of knowledge?  
`dsp:pramana-samplava` · skeleton

- **lin:nyaya**: Yes (saṃplava): fire is known from testimony, inferred from smoke and perceived when near; some objects are restricted (heaven to scripture), and knowledge culminates in perception.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: No (vyavasthā): perception grasps only unique particulars and inference only universals; the two means have distinct objects.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the disagreement follows from different ontologies of the object (enduring thing with universals vs. momentary particular).
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U35-pranayama — Does breath-control (prāṇāyāma) serve meditation and liberation?  
`dsp:pranayama-and-liberation` · skeleton

- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Yes, as a preparatory limb: after posture, regulating the breath thins the covering of the light and makes the mind fit for concentration.
- **lin:hatha-yoga**: Yes: when the breath moves the mind moves; when the breath is still the mind is still — so the breath must be controlled.
- **lin:svetambara**: No, not as a means to liberation: restraining the breath causes pain, pain disturbs the mind, and the laborious filling, holding and expelling distract; stillness comes from indifference, not force (Hemacandra).
- **lin:digambara**: Breath-control can steady the body and reveal omens, but pursued for powers it brings distress and is not the cause of liberation (Śubhacandra).
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: Patañjali places prāṇāyāma among the outer, preparatory limbs; Hemacandra speaks of the direct means of liberation — the two may not conflict.
  - P2-standpoint: Haribhadra gives prāṇāyāma an inner meaning (expelling outer states, taking in inner ones), a different sense of the same word.
  - A genuine difference over whether forcible breath restraint steadies or disturbs the mind; keep both.

## RQ-U50-08 — Should a Mādhyamika establish emptiness by autonomous inferences (svatantra-anumāna) or only by consequences (prasaṅga) that draw out untenable consequences of the opponent's own thesis — and does this difference of method reflect a difference of view? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:prasangika-svatantrika` · skeleton

- **lin:madhyamaka**: Buddhapālita: things do not arise from themselves, because their arising would be pointless (what already exists need not arise again) and would never end — the argument proceeds by drawing out consequences (prasaṅga) of the opponent's thesis.
- **lin:madhyamaka**: Bhāviveka: Buddhapālita's argument is inadequate — it states no reason and example, does not answer the faults the opponent would raise, and being a consequence it implies by reversal that things arise from another, against the Mādhyamika's own tenet. The Mādhyamika should state an independent inference: 'ultimately the inner sense-bases do not arise from themselves, because they exist, like consciousness'.
- **lin:madhyamaka**: Candrakīrti: for one who is a Mādhyamika it is not right to state an autonomous inference, since he accepts no other position; as Āryadeva said, one who has no position — existent, non-existent or both — cannot be refuted; Nāgārjuna: 'if I had a thesis the fault would be mine; I have none'. Consequences suffice to show the opponent the untenable consequences of his own thesis; Buddhapālita was right.
- **lin:yogacara-madhyamaka**: Śāntarakṣita: entities lack own-nature because they are neither one nor many, like a reflection; the conventional is what is agreeable only when unexamined; relying on mind-only one sees there are no external things, relying on the Middle Way that even mind lacks self — riding both vehicles one reaches the Mahāyāna.
- **lin:gelug**: Tsongkhapa: the difference is one of view, not only of method — the Svātantrikas accept that things have their own character conventionally and therefore use inferences whose subject appears alike to both parties; the Prāsaṅgikas accept no inherent nature even conventionally, so no such common subject exists and they lead the opponent by consequences and inferences 'renowned to the other'; Prāsaṅgika is the higher view, and the object of negation must be identified exactly.
- **lin:sakya**: Gorampa: Tsongkhapa's adding of the qualifier 'inherently/truly' to what is negated is not in Nāgārjuna and Candrakīrti, who negate arising and existence without qualification; the Madhyamaka ultimate is freedom from all four extremes, and the negation of true existence alone is only a step.
- **lin:nyingma**: Mipham: for the provisional, enumerated ultimate, emptiness as the negation of true existence is taught (the Svātantrika emphasis); the actual, non-enumerated ultimate is freedom from all elaboration (the Prāsaṅgika emphasis); from the standpoint of that ultimate there is no thesis to hold, while conventionally the Mādhyamika asserts; the two are to be approached in sequence.
- **lin:gelug**: The difference is in the view, not only in method: Svātantrikas accept that phenomena exist by their own character conventionally and therefore use autonomous inferences; Prāsaṅgikas deny this even conventionally and so use consequences and 'other-acknowledged' inferences. Hence Prāsaṅgika's object of negation is subtler.
- **lin:sakya**: The two differ only in how the view is generated in another's mind — by autonomous inference or by consequence — not in the view realised, which is freedom from elaboration for both (Gorampa).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: Svātantrika speaks from the standpoint of conventional valid cognition shared with the opponent, Prāsaṅgika from the standpoint of the ultimate with no thesis — rejected by Tsongkhapa as blurring a real difference of view.
  - P5-neyartha: the Svātantrika presentation as provisional, the Prāsaṅgika as definitive — the Gelug ranking, which Svātantrika authors themselves would not accept.
