- Note: Partly reconciled in the Nyingma and Sakya traditions' own terms: the Svātantrika approach addresses the enumerated ultimate as a step and the Prāsaṅgika the non-enumerated freedom from extremes (P4-stage), and whether one argues by autonomous inference or by consequence is a matter of how the opponent is led (P6-upaya); the Gelug position that the two differ in view — acceptance of own-character conventionally — is not reconciled.

## RQ-291 — Does a Prāsaṅgika assert any thesis of his own? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:prasangika-theses` · skeleton

- **lin:gelug**: Prāsaṅgikas have no thesis asserting inherent existence, but they do assert theses conventionally — e.g. that phenomena lack inherent existence — and uphold them with valid cognition; Nāgārjuna's 'I have no thesis' means no thesis that is inherently existent.
- **lin:sakya**: In ultimate analysis the Mādhyamika holds no thesis at all, following Vigrahavyāvartanī 29; conventional statements are made only in accordance with others' perspective, without ultimate commitment (Gorampa and the earlier Tibetan Prāsaṅgikas).
- Note: Both deny any thesis at the ultimate level and allow statements at the conventional level; they differ on whether conventional assertions are the Mādhyamika's own and established by valid cognition.

## RQ-292 — After understanding the mahāvākya, is repeated meditative contemplation (prasaṅkhyāna) required for liberating knowledge? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:prasankhyana` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Maṇḍana Miśra: yes — verbal knowledge is indirect and does not remove deep impressions; repeated contemplation makes it direct.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Śaṅkara (US 18) and Sureśvara: no — for the qualified hearer the understood sentence itself gives direct knowledge; contemplation serves only to remove obstacles where understanding is incomplete.
- Note: Śaṅkara's own gloss (BSBh 4.1.2) makes repetition needed for those who do not understand at once and useless for those who do — a stage-relative reading.

## RQ-293 — Are there only momentary cognitions without an enduring subject, or is there one conscious knower - the Lord - whose powers of cognition, memory and exclusion make experience possible?  
`dsp:pratyabhijna-vs-buddhist-logicians` · skeleton

- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: There is only the cognition that shows the particular and the conceptual cognition joined to words; no permanent seer appears in them; the notion 'I' rests on the body; memory arises from impressions left by earlier cognitions, so no permanent self is required (as presented by Utpaladeva, ĪPK 1.2.1-3, 1.3.1).
- **lin:pratyabhijna**: Memory cannot make the past object appear if the remembering cognition is separate from the original experience; the unity of cognitions at different times is the knower (ĪPK 1.4.3); cognition, memory and exclusion require one consciousness containing the universe within (1.3.7); determinate awareness arises by exclusion, which the Pratyabhijñā accepts as a power of the Lord (1.6.5); consciousness is reflexive awareness (1.5.11-13).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: momentariness describes cognitions as modes, the enduring knower their substance (dravyārthika/paryāyārthika).
  - Handle within dsp:is-there-a-self (U50).
- Note: Not yet reconciled; a particular case of dsp:is-there-a-self.

## RQ-294 — Is devotion a means to liberation, or is love of God a goal beyond liberation? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:prema-beyond-moksa` · skeleton

- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Gauḍīya: prema is the fifth and supreme goal; devotees refuse even the five liberations without service; liberation is made trivial by pure devotion.
- **lin:pustimarga**: Puṣṭimārga: the grace-soul seeks only service and absorption; liberation (sāyujya) is a lesser fruit of the maryādā path.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Advaita: devotion purifies the mind and prepares for knowledge, which alone liberates; liberation is the highest end.
- Note: Advaita places devotion at a stage before knowledge; the bhakti schools place knowledge of the impersonal below love — each ranks the other's goal by its own path.

## RQ-U38-01 — Is there a person (pudgala) that is neither the same as nor different from the aggregates?  
`dsp:pudgala` · skeleton

- **lin:pudgalavada**: There is a person, real, designated in dependence on the internal, appropriated, present aggregates — neither identical with them nor different, as fire is designated in dependence on fuel; it is inexpressible, the fifth kind of knowable; it bears the burden of the aggregates and transmigrates.
- **lin:sautrantika**: There is no person apart from the series of aggregates; 'self' and 'person' are designations of that series. A person that is neither real nor a designation is incoherent; the five-knowables list cannot even be stated; the burden-bearer is the earlier aggregates.
- **lin:sarvastivada**: There is no self; only aggregates conditioned by defilements and karma go from life to life through the intermediate existence, like a flame.
- **lin:theravada**: The person is not found as a real and ultimate fact (saccikaṭṭha-paramattha).
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: 'person' is valid at the conventional level for both; the dispute concerns only its ultimate status — rejected by the Pudgalavādins, who deny the person is a mere designation.
  - P2-standpoint: the 'inexpressible' as a standpoint neither of identity nor of difference, comparable to other tetralemma answers — rejected by Vasubandhu as a fifth category unknown to the Buddha.
  - P5-neyartha: sūtras speaking of 'a person' (the burden-bearer) are provisional — accepted by the Sarvāstivāda and Theravāda, rejected by the Pudgalavāda.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: both sides accept that the person is spoken of in dependence on the aggregates, but disagree whether it is anything beyond a designation.

## RQ-296 — Is the Pure Land a real land in the West, or the purified mind only? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:pure-land-real-or-mind-only` · skeleton

- **lin:pure-land**: The Western land is to be sought really; birth there is the 'birth of non-birth'; mind-only and the Western land are not two, and those whose power is not sufficient must be born there.
- **lin:chan**: The Pure Land is the pure mind; 'as the mind is pure the buddha-land is pure'; Amitābha is one's own nature (as reported by Pure Land writers answering the view).
- **lin:jodo-shu**: The Pure Land is pointed to in a direction and given form (Shandao) for ordinary beings; it is a reward land, not merely mind.
- **lin:mahayana**: The Buddha seen in the samādhi neither comes nor goes; mind makes the buddha (Pratyutpanna; Contemplation Sūtra 8).
- Note: Chinese Pure Land masters reconcile mind-only (ultimate level) with the Western land (conventional level, a skilful means for those of insufficient power) — a reconciliation made within the tradition.

## RQ-297 — Are the rules of ritual purity and conventional conduct binding on the knower? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:purity-impurity-kaula` · skeleton

- **lin:dharmasastra**: Purity and impurity, and the rules of varṇa and āśrama, are part of dharma and binding.
- **lin:trika**: The purity prescribed by those of little knowledge is impurity in Śiva's view; nothing is pure or impure to the thought-free (VBT 123); rules based on difference are not a direct means to Śiva, though the samaya is to be kept by those who need it (TĀ 4.258-270); the knower eats whatever comes and is untouched by merit and sin (Paramārthasāra 69-71).
- Note: The Trika itself keeps rules as valid for those in the state of difference and declares them non-binding only for the knower.

## RQ-298 — Are Pūrva and Uttara Mīmāṃsā one continuous śāstra, so that inquiry into dharma must precede inquiry into Brahman?  
`dsp:purva-uttara-mimamsa-unity` · skeleton

- **lin:visistadvaita**: Yes: the Vṛttikāra cited by Rāmānuja says the inquiry into Brahman follows the knowledge of rites, and that the Śārīraka is joined with Jaimini's work of sixteen chapters to form one śāstra; Vedānta Deśika's Seśvaramīmāṃsā reads the Mīmāṃsā Sūtra accordingly.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: No: the 'then' (atha) of BS 1.1.1 follows the fourfold qualification (discrimination, dispassion, the six virtues, longing for release), not the inquiry into dharma; the two inquiries differ in object, fruit and eligible person.
- **lin:mimamsa**: Mīmāṃsā authors acknowledge Vedānta as a related study: Upavarṣa deferred the proof of the self to the Śārīraka (per Śaṅkara), Kumārila says Vedānta makes self-knowledge firm, and later Bhāṭṭas read the Upaniṣads as serving rites and meditations.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: ritual inquiry as a preparation for some students, not a necessary precondition for all — Advaita accepts this for purification, Viśiṣṭādvaita insists on the continuity of the śāstra.
- Note: Not yet reconciled; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-U26-03 — Who composed the Rādhā-sudhānidhi — Hita Harivaṃśa or Prabodhānanda Sarasvatī?  
`dsp:radha-sudhanidhi-authorship` · skeleton

- **lin:radhavallabha**: Hita Harivaṃśa composed it; it expresses the Rādhāvallabha doctrine of Rādhā's supremacy.
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Prabodhānanda Sarasvatī, a Gauḍīya ascetic of Vṛndāvana, composed it.
- Candidate readings:
  - Manuscript and stylistic evidence may settle it (sourcing phase).
- Note: Not yet reconciled: an authorship question; no reconciliation principle applies.

## RQ-U26-01 — Is the Rāma whose name saves the embodied son of Daśaratha, or a formless Rām who was never born?  
`dsp:rama-of-the-name` · skeleton

- **lin:ramanandi**: The formless and the embodied are not different: the unborn Brahman became Daśaratha's son for love of his devotees; the name stands between the two forms and makes both known; those who preach devotion while reviling the Vedas and Purāṇas are misled.
- **lin:kabir-panth**: Rām is formless and unborn; the Rām of the name is not the son of Daśaratha whom the world praises; avatāras and images are not the Rām within.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: Tulsī's own reconciliation — one Brahman seen as formless and embodied, the name mediating (Mānas 1.116, 1.21); the Kabīr-panth rejects the avatāra this affirms.
  - P1-level: nirguṇa as ultimate, saguṇa as conventional — rejected by Tulsī, who denies any hierarchy between the forms.
  - P3-path: a difference of temperament (knowledge vs devotion) — weak, since both sides are devotional and name-centred.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: both sides centre on the name but differ on whether its Rām took birth.

## RQ-U26-02 — Does the Rāmānandī order descend from Rāmānuja's Śrīvaiṣṇava lineage, or is it an independent sampradāya founded by Rāmānanda with its own line from Rāma?  
`dsp:ramanandi-descent-from-ramanuja` · skeleton

- **lin:ramanandi**: (Older lineage lists) Rāmānanda was a disciple of Rāghavānanda in Rāmānuja's line and founded his own branch after a dispute over commensality; the order is part of the Śrī sampradāya.
- **lin:ramanandi**: (Independent faction, 20th c.) Rāmānanda founded an independent Rāmāvat sampradāya whose line runs from Rāma through Sītā and Hanumān; he wrote his own Brahma Sūtra commentary (Ānandabhāṣya); Rāma, not Nārāyaṇa, is supreme.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: Śrīvaiṣṇava institutions have at times claimed the northern Rāmānandīs as a branch of their sampradāya.
- Candidate readings:
  - Both lists may preserve a real Śrīvaiṣṇava connection alongside a distinct northern identity (historical reading).
  - P2-standpoint: doctrinal continuity with Viśiṣṭādvaita vs institutional independence as two standpoints.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: a question of lineage history on which the sources are late and partisan.

## RQ-U50-07 — Is the ultimate empty of its own nature like all phenomena (rang stong, self-emptiness), or empty only of what is other than it — the adventitious stains — while not empty of its own nature and qualities (gzhan stong, other-emptiness)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:rangtong-shentong` · skeleton

- **lin:jonang**: Dolpopa: buddha-nature — the dharmakāya, the dharmadhātu — is the ultimate truth; it is empty of all conventional phenomena, which are adventitious and other than it, but not empty of its own nature (other-emptiness). Conventional phenomena are empty of their own nature, like a rabbit's horns. An emptiness that is nothing whatsoever is annihilatory emptiness and not the ultimate. The final-wheel sūtras, the Maitreya treatises and the Kālacakra teach the definitive meaning; the dharmakāya is the supreme self.
- **lin:jonang**: Tāranātha: the thoroughly established nature is self-aware, self-illuminating primordial wisdom free of subject and object, unchanging, realized in meditative equipoise and not empty of itself; self- and other-emptiness agree that all conventional phenomena are empty of own-nature and differ over the ultimate — with twenty-one further differences following.
- **lin:gelug**: Tsongkhapa and Khedrup Je: nothing whatsoever, the ultimate included, is established by its own nature; the ultimate truth is the mere absence of inherent existence, a non-affirming negation, and emptiness itself is empty. An ultimate that is truly established, permanent and empty only of other would be an object of grasping at true existence — the extreme of permanence, like the self of the non-Buddhists; the Saṃdhinirmocana's three natures do not teach such an ultimate.
- **lin:sakya**: Gorampa: three views are held in Tibet to be Madhyamaka — the eternalist extreme (Dolpopa: the ultimate empty of other but not of itself), the nihilist extreme (Tsongkhapa: the ultimate as mere negation of true existence) — and the Madhyamaka free from all extremes, which is Nāgārjuna's; clinging to emptiness as mere negation is itself an extreme.
- **lin:karma-kagyu**: Rangjung Dorje: the beginningless element, basis of all phenomena and of nirvāṇa, is mind's nature, luminous and empty, obscured by adventitious stains.
- **lin:nyingma**: Mipham: buddha-nature is the union of luminosity and emptiness — its emptiness keeps it from being an existent self, its luminosity means the qualities are primordially present; 'other-emptiness' as the Nyingma accept it — the ultimate wisdom empty of adventitious conditioned phenomena — does not conflict with self-emptiness, since that ultimate too is not established by its own nature.
- **lin:rime**: Jamgön Kongtrul: the self-emptiness Madhyamaka of Nāgārjuna's reasonings and the other-emptiness Great Madhyamaka of the Maitreya treatises are each valid in their own sphere — the self-empty approach cuts through elaboration in study and reflection, the other-empty view suits meditative experience; the element is empty of separable stains and not empty of inseparable qualities.
- **lin:tathagatagarbha**: The Indian scriptural ground cited by both sides: the tathāgatagarbha is empty of the separable defilements and not empty of the inseparable buddha-qualities (Śrīmālādevī); nothing is to be removed from it and nothing added (Ratnagotravibhāga 1.154-155); yet the Laṅkāvatāra says the tathāgatagarbha is taught meaning emptiness, not the tīrthika self.
- **lin:gelug**: Only 'self-emptiness' is correct: every phenomenon, the ultimate included, is empty of its own inherent existence; an ultimate empty only of other (adventitious) things but not of itself would be truly existent and a form of eternalism.
- **lin:sakya**: Gorampa classes Dolpopa's other-emptiness as 'Madhyamaka of the eternalist extreme'; Shākya Chokden, in contrast, held that self-emptiness is the view for cutting conceptual elaboration and other-emptiness the view for meditative experience, both valid.
- Candidate readings:
  - P5-neyartha: which wheel is definitive — the second (emptiness) or the third (buddha-nature) — decides the matter (dsp:which-turning-is-definitive); the parties apply the provisional/definitive distinction in opposite directions.
  - P1-level (Dolpopa's own assignment): conventional phenomena self-empty, the ultimate other-empty — rejected by Gelug, for whom the ultimate too is empty of own-nature.
  - P4-stage (Kongtrul): self-emptiness in study, other-emptiness in meditation — accepted in Rimé and much Kagyu and Nyingma teaching, rejected by Gelug and by strict Jonang.
- Note: Partly reconciled in the Rimé and Nyingma traditions' own terms — self-emptiness for cutting elaboration in study and reflection, other-emptiness for meditative experience (Kongtrul), and other-emptiness understood as not self-established compatible with self-emptiness (Mipham); the Jonang–Gelug disagreement over whether the ultimate is truly established remains not yet reconciled.

## RQ-303 — Are the dualist Siddhānta scriptures or the non-dual Bhairava tantras the higher revelation?  
`dsp:rank-of-siddhanta-and-bhairava-tantras` · skeleton

- **lin:trika**: Śiva's teaching in ten, eighteen and sixty-four divisions has the Trika as its essence and the Mālinīvijayottara as the essence of that; the Śiva-, Rudra- and Bhairava-tantras teach difference, difference-and-non-difference and non-difference in ascending order.
- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: The Siddhānta of the upper stream is Śiva's definitive teaching; the soul remains distinct and in liberation becomes equal to Śiva; non-dual readings of the scriptures are rejected.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: the Kashmirian ranking treats the three classes as graded teachings for graded hearers (cf. Kiraṇa 10.28–31 and Kāmika 1.103: one speaker, many through the hearers).
  - P2-standpoint: difference and non-difference as standpoints on one reality.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-304 — How is another family's revelation to be placed — as a lower stage of one's own, as a deliberate delusion, as a subdued power, or as a partial standpoint?  
`dsp:ranking-of-other-revelations` · skeleton

- **lin:pratyabhijna**: All philosophies are roles of the one conscious Lord: the Buddhists rest in the intellect-principle, the Jains in the qualities, the Vedic knowers in the Person, the Pāñcarātrikas in the unmanifest; the Trika alone knows the self as both transcendent and immanent.
- **lin:vajrayana**: Maheśvara and the worldly gods are subdued by Vajrapāṇi and given places in the Buddha's maṇḍala; they are worldly powers, not the goal.
- **lin:puranic**: The Buddha and the naked ascetics' teachers are Viṣṇu's deluding descent, sent to lead the enemies of the gods away from the Veda.
- **lin:jainism**: Every other doctrine is a one-sided standpoint; the Jina's word is the aggregate of all the wrong views, each true only when joined with the others.
- **lin:madhyamaka**: Whatever is acceptable in the Vedāntins' teaching is acceptable only as the emptiness the Buddha taught.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage and P2-standpoint applied symmetrically: every ranking is made from within one tradition's standpoint, so the rankings do not conflict as descriptions of their authors' own maps.
  - Record as mutual subordination and leave unreconciled, since each tradition's final goal is defined against the others.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: each tradition applies stage and standpoint to the others while rejecting its own subordination.

## RQ-U38-07 — Is unmanifest matter (avijñapti) a real entity?  
`dsp:reality-of-avijnapti` · skeleton

- **lin:vaibhasika**: Yes: unmanifest matter carries vows and non-vows through time, even when mind is absent.
- **lin:sautrantika**: No: shape and avijñapti are not entities; the course of action is the transformation of the series produced by volition.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: 'unmanifest matter' as the conventional name for the karmic continuity — rejected by the Vaibhāṣika.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Vaibhāṣika holds a real entity where the Sautrāntika sees a designation.

## RQ-U38-06 — Is possession (prāpti) a real entity?  
`dsp:reality-of-prapti` · skeleton

- **lin:vaibhasika**: Yes: a real dissociated force linking dharmas to a series; the state of an ordinary person is non-possession of the noble path.
- **lin:sautrantika**: No: 'possession' is the intact seed in the series; the ordinary person is the series in which the noble dharmas have not arisen.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: 'possession' as a valid conventional description of the seed-state — rejected by the Vaibhāṣika, who holds it a dravya.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Vaibhāṣika holds a real entity where the Sautrāntika sees a designation.

## RQ-U38-08 — Are the unconditioned dharmas (space, the two cessations) real entities?  
`dsp:reality-of-the-unconditioned` · skeleton

- **lin:vaibhasika**: Yes: three real, eternal unconditioned dharmas; nirvāṇa is cessation through discernment, a real disjunction.
- **lin:sautrantika**: No: all the unconditioned is unreal — space is absence of resistance, nirvāṇa the non-arising of further defilement and birth.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level / apophatic reading: nirvāṇa described positively (as a dharma) or negatively (as absence) — the Vaibhāṣika insists on its reality, the Sautrāntika on its unreality.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Vaibhāṣika holds a real entity where the Sautrāntika sees a designation.

## RQ-308 — Are universals (jāti, sāmānya) real?  
`dsp:reality-of-universals` · skeleton

- **lin:vaisesika**: Yes: universals are real, eternal, one and present in many, inhering in individuals; being is the highest; genuine universals are limited by six impediments.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: No: universals are conceptual constructions; words signify by excluding what is other (apoha); only unique particulars are real.
- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: Universals are real but in identity-in-difference with their individuals (Kumārila, as usually reported).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: Jain many-sidedness treats universal and particular as two aspects of one real.
  - P1-level: the Buddhist grants universals conventional usefulness while denying ultimate reality.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U47-3 — Are universals real entities (the Tibetan debate on Dharmakīrti)?  
`dsp:reality-of-universals-tibetan` · skeleton

- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Chapa Chökyi Senge and the Sangphu logicians (Kadam scholastic line) treated certain universals and relations as real (reported by their critics).
- **lin:sakya**: Sakya Paṇḍita: only particulars are real; universals are conceptual constructs formed by exclusion, as Dharmakīrti taught.
- **lin:gelug**: Universals (generally characterised phenomena) exist conventionally as permanent, non-functioning phenomena, though they are not real in the sense of functioning things (Gelug pramāṇa textbooks).
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: 'real' as ultimately (functionally) real vs. conventionally existent may dissolve part of the disagreement between the Sakya and Gelug sides.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-310 — How does the unchanging puruṣa 'know' the mind's activities — by a single reflection of consciousness in the buddhi, or by mutual reflection?  
`dsp:reflection-single-or-mutual` · skeleton

- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Vācaspati Miśra: the buddhi, transformed into the object's form, receives a reflection (chāyā) of consciousness; the resulting 'cognition' is a property of the buddhi alone; the puruṣa is not reflected into.
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Vijñānabhikṣu: reflection is mutual — consciousness is reflected in the buddhi and the buddhi's activity is reflected back in consciousness; the fruit of cognition is consciousness qualified by that reflection.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: two analytic models of one relation (buddhi-side vs puruṣa-side description of knowing).
  - Textual: decide by the bhāṣya's wording at 1.7 ('pauruṣeyaś cittavṛttibodhaḥ') and 4.22.
  - Leave distinct: Vijñānabhikṣu explicitly rejects the single-reflection view.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: both commentators interpret YS 4.22 and YBh 1.7 and each rejects the other's model.

## RQ-311 — Should Yudhiṣṭhira renounce the kingdom for the forest, or rule? (MBh 12.7–36) (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:renounce-or-rule` · skeleton

- **lin:epic-teaching**: Renunciation: after the slaughter, Yudhiṣṭhira wishes to abandon kingship and live as a mendicant in the forest; he quotes Janaka's saying that nothing of his burns when Mithilā burns.
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Rule: his brothers, Draupadī, Vyāsa and Kṛṣṇa argue that the householder's and king's duty, protecting the people with the rod, is his dharma; Sahadeva teaches that 'mine' is death and 'not mine' the eternal Brahman — renunciation is inner.
- Note: In the narrative Yudhiṣṭhira is persuaded: renunciation proper belongs to its stage of life, and inner non-possession ('na mama') is possible while ruling; the Mokṣadharma then teaches liberation to the ruling king.

## RQ-312 — Should the renouncer keep outer marks — triple staff, topknot, sacred thread — or abandon them (one staff, or none)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:renunciant-marks-ekadanda-tridanda` · sourced

- **lin:sannyasa**: Advaitin Saṃnyāsa Upaniṣads: the paramahaṃsa abandons topknot and thread (knowledge is his topknot and thread); the staff of knowledge makes the true ekadaṇḍin; the highest renouncers bear no marks.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: Vaiṣṇava tridaṇḍin: the renouncer keeps triple staff, topknot and thread till death; they are Viṣṇu's marks, and abandoning them is a grave sin.
- Note: Both sides also read the staff inwardly (three restraints; staff of knowledge), and the Advaitin texts grade marks by kind of renouncer, but the Śāṭyāyanīya binds all kinds to the marks.

## RQ-313 — Is the renunciation of action or the yoga of action better? (BhG 3.1–8, 5.1–6, 6.1–4, 18.1–12) (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:renunciation-or-action-gita` · skeleton

- **lin:epic-teaching**: Renunciation of actions, which Arjuna hears praised alongside yoga (5.1), brings the highest good (5.2); the place reached by the Sāṃkhyas is reached by the Yogins too (5.5); renouncing all actions with the mind, the embodied one dwells at ease in the city of nine gates (5.13).
- **None**: Renunciation of actions is a path distinct from the yoga of action; Sāṃkhya and Yoga are separate; the renouncer is known by the outward marks of formal renunciation — giving up the ritual fires and rites.
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Both renunciation and the yoga of action bring the highest good, but the yoga of action is superior (5.2); true renunciation is inner — neither hating nor desiring, giving up the fruit and intention — and renunciation and yoga are one in result.
- **lin:epic-teaching**: If understanding (buddhi) is held superior to action (cf. 2.49), there is no reason to engage in terrible action; Kṛṣṇa's words seem mixed, and Arjuna asks for the one thing by which he may reach the good.
- **lin:epic-teaching**: There is a twofold commitment, jñāna-yoga for the sāṃkhyas and karma-yoga for the yogins; freedom from action is not reached by not acting, nor perfection by renunciation alone; one must act, for sacrifice and for holding the world together, without attachment.
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Renunciation: action should be abandoned as an evil (the view the Gītā reports at 18.3); the path of knowledge of the Sāṃkhyas; mere abstention appeals to Arjuna, who asks why he is urged to terrible action if understanding is superior (3.1).
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Action: no one can remain without acting (3.5); mere renunciation does not bring perfection (3.4); the yoga of action excels renunciation of action (5.2); acts of sacrifice, giving and austerity are not to be abandoned (18.5).
- Note: The text itself says both reach the same goal (5.2, 5.4–5) and ties the means to the student's stage — action for one ascending, calm for one ascended (6.3); commentators still divide on whether the superiority of karma-yoga in 5.2 is relative to stage or absolute.

## RQ-314 — Kōan introspection and kenshō (Rinzai) or just sitting as practice-realization (Sōtō)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:rinzai-or-soto` · skeleton

- **lin:rinzai**: Kenshō must be attained through great doubt on kōans and then deepened by post-awakening training; quiet sitting without the kōan becomes dead sitting; practice in activity surpasses practice in stillness.
- **lin:soto**: Practice and realization are one; just sitting is itself the enactment of buddhahood, not a means to a later kenshō; students should just sit rather than rely on kōan stories.
- **lin:rinzai**: Bankei (within Rinzai) rejects both manufactured doubt and effortful method: simply abide in the Unborn.
- Note: Different methods for different temperaments and stages within one zazen-centred practice.

## RQ-315 — Is conceptual analysis part of meditation, or must all thought be stopped? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:role-of-analysis-in-meditation` · skeleton

- **lin:gelug**: Analytical meditation is indispensable: faith, renunciation, bodhicitta and the view are produced by analysis; insight arises only when analysis itself induces pliancy; stopping all thought is not realisation of emptiness but a blank state.
- **lin:kadam**: Conceptualisation is great ignorance and saṃsāra itself; after ascertaining by scripture and reasoning that phenomena are unborn, one meditates without conceptualisation (Atiśa).
- **lin:sakya**: Sakya Paṇḍita: a Mahāmudrā of mere thoughtlessness without the wisdom of empowerment is the cause of animal or formless rebirth; genuine non-conceptual wisdom comes through empowerment and the two stages.
- Note: All three place reasoned ascertainment before non-conceptual meditation; Atiśa's non-conceptual meditation follows analysis (v60), which is the Gelug sequence of analysis leading to non-dual realisation.

## RQ-316 — Does RV 1.164.32 ('having many offspring he has entered Nirṛti') speak of repeated births? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:rv-1-164-32-rebirth-reading` · sourced

- **lin:vedic-srauta**: The wandering ascetics (parivrājakāḥ): 'one with many births comes to misery' — the verse speaks of the embodied being entering misery through many births.
- **lin:vedic-srauta**: The etymologists (nairuktāḥ): the verse refers to the action of rain — the cloud (born of the mother, the atmosphere), 'rich in offspring', comes down to the earth (nirṛti read as bhūmi).
- Note: The Vedic interpretive tradition itself reads verses on several planes (of the rite, of the deities, of the self); the rain reading is the adhidaivata plane, the many-births reading the adhyātma plane.

## RQ-U01-02 — Does the funeral hymn enjoin the widow to rise to the living, or (by a variant reading) to enter the fire?  
`dsp:rv-10-18-7-widow-reading` · sourced

- **lin:vedic-srauta**: The transmitted text and the Padapāṭha read 'let the wives first (agre) ascend the seat' (10.18.7), and the next verse bids the widow 'rise to the world of the living' (10.18.8); the Atharvaveda describes her lying down beside the dead by 'ancient dharma' and then rising (AVŚ 18.3.1–2).
- **lin:dharmasastra**: A variant reading 'yonim agneḥ' ('the seat of fire') was cited in later times as Vedic sanction for a widow's self-immolation.
- Candidate readings:
  - P8-six-marks: the hymn's context (the next verse bids the widow rise to the living) and the Padapāṭha's 'agre' settle the purport of the passage.
  - The Atharvaveda's 'ancient dharma' of lying beside the dead and rising was a symbolic rite of the funeral, not a death.
  - The variant arose in later transmission; the recitation lineages themselves preserve 'agre'.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: a dispute over one word of the transmitted text.

## RQ-318 — What does Ṛgveda 9.83.1 ('pavitraṃ te vitataṃ brahmaṇas pate… ataptatanūr na tad āmo aśnute') enjoin?  
`dsp:rv-9-83-1-pavitra` · skeleton

- **lin:pancacarya**: 'Pavitra' is the liṅga and 'brahman' names it; the 'unheated body' is one without initiation, who cannot attain the liṅga — so the verse enjoins wearing the liṅga after dīkṣā.
- **lin:pancaratra**: The verse enjoins the heating (tapta) of the body with the seals of Viṣṇu's conch and discus as part of Vaiṣṇava initiation (the SSM commentary itself names tapta-mudrā-dhāraṇa as Pāñcarātra practice; Śrīvaiṣṇava and Mādhva usage to be sourced by U08/U14/U15).
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path: the same injunction to consecrate the body read through different chosen deities (liṅga for Śaivas, the seals for Vaiṣṇavas) — each tradition holds its reading exclusive.
- Note: not yet reconciled: competing ritual readings of one mantra

## RQ-319 — Does killing enjoined as part of a Vedic rite incur demerit?  
`dsp:sacrificial-killing` · skeleton

- **lin:mimamsa**: Violence is prohibited, and rites whose aim is violence (the Śyena) are harmful; but the Veda states such rites only as means for one who wishes to harm, not as duties. (The Bhāṭṭa position, generally reported, is that killing enjoined as a subsidiary of a rite is not sin, the specific injunction limiting the general prohibition.)
- **lin:samkhya**: The Vedic means are impure (aviśuddhi, Sāṃkhya Kārikā 2): the killing that helps the rite still brings demerit to the person, since the general and the specific injunction concern different ends.
- **lin:jainism**: All killing is violence and binds karma, whatever the motive of ritual.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: Animal sacrifice is not a wholesome act.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage / P3-path: different ethical demands for the ritual householder and the renunciant or ascetic; each tradition rejects this as relativizing its ethic.
- Note: Not yet reconciled; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-U50-13 — Is the ultimate personal and with qualities (saguṇa), impersonal and without qualities (nirguṇa), or both? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:saguna-nirguna` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Brahman is without attributes (nirguṇa): not even by place (adjunct) can it have a twofold character, for everywhere it is taught as attributeless; it is formless, that being the principal purport; texts about form are not purposeless, like light taking shapes. Brahman is known in two forms — qualified by adjuncts, as the object of worship in the state of ignorance, and free of them, as the object of knowledge; of 'is, shines, is dear, name and form', the first three are Brahman. (Madhusūdana Sarasvatī nonetheless closes: 'I know no reality higher than Kṛṣṇa'.)
- **lin:visistadvaita**: No means of knowledge establishes an undifferentiated object — even the first, non-conceptual perception grasps a qualified thing; Brahman is ever qualified, free of all defects and full of auspicious qualities (twofold mark, ubhaya-liṅga); 'not so, not so' denies only that Brahman is limited to what was just described. The texts calling Brahman nirguṇa deny bad qualities.
- **lin:dvaita**: All Viṣṇu's qualities are real; he is full of infinite qualities and free of defects; even the names of other gods in the Veda are his names; his descents are not births among beings.
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: The knowers of reality call the one non-dual knowledge by three names — Brahman, Paramātman and Bhagavān: realised without its distinctions and powers it appears as Brahman, the effulgence of Bhagavān's body; Bhagavān, Kṛṣṇa, is its fullest disclosure. Caitanya reads the sūtras and Upaniṣads as speaking directly of the personal Lord.
- **lin:pustimarga**: Brahman is the locus of contradictory attributes (viruddha-dharmāśraya): scripture's statements that it is attributeless and full of qualities, formless and with form, are all true of it.
- **lin:epic-teaching**: The Gītā ch. 12: those who worship the Lord with form, ever yoked with supreme faith, are held the most yoked; those who worship the imperishable, indefinable, unmanifest also reach him, but greater is the difficulty of those set on the unmanifest, for that goal is hard for the embodied. 'I am the foundation of Brahman'; the supreme person is beyond the perishable and the imperishable.
- **lin:sant**: Kabīr: repeat the name of the attributeless (nirguṇa) Rām; fix the mind where there is no form or sign; the son of Daśaratha is praised in the three worlds, but the secret of Rām's name is other; if worshipping a stone brought Hari I would worship a mountain.
- **lin:ramanandi**: Tulsīdās: there is no difference at all between the embodied (saguṇa) and the formless (aguṇa), sing the sages; Bhuśuṇḍi, taught the formless by Lomaśa, kept asking for the Rāma with form and was cursed and then blessed for his devotion.
- **lin:kashmir-saivism**: In truth Bhairava is not the ninefold, not the mass of sounds, not the three-headed god, not made of nāda and bindu — these are told to the unawakened, like a mother's sweets, to make them take up practice; the Tāntrikas hold the self transcends the universe, the Kula schools that it is the universe, the Trika that it is both.
- **lin:srividya**: The Goddess is without support, stainless, eternal, formless, without qualities — and she is the manifest and the unmanifest; meditation on her is of two kinds, with form and formless.
- **lin:upanisadic**: One God hidden in all beings, all-pervading, the inner self of all, overseer of actions, the witness, pure consciousness, alone and without qualities (Śvetāśvatara) — the Upaniṣad names the personal God with the attributeless predicates.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: saguṇa worship as preparation for nirguṇa knowledge (Advaita, Rāma Gītā 7.5.58-62) or nirguṇa realization as a partial stage below loving service of Bhagavān (Gauḍīya) — the two rankings run in opposite directions.
  - P2-standpoint (Vallabha): Brahman as locus of all contradictory attributes, so both descriptions are literally true — rejected by Advaita (qualities through adjuncts only) and by Madhva (no attributelessness).
- Note: Reconciled in part in the traditions' own terms: the Gītā has both the worshipper of the Lord with form and the worshipper of the unmanifest reach him, the latter with greater difficulty (P3-path); Advaita places the qualified Brahman as the object of worship and the unqualified as the object of knowledge (P1-level); the Bhāgavata names one non-dual reality Brahman, Paramātman and Bhagavān by approach, Tulsīdās and the Trika hold both true (P2-standpoint); which is higher remains not yet reconciled.

## RQ-321 — Is the Sahajiyā path (and those of the Bāuls and Kartābhajās) a true form of Caitanya's Vaiṣṇavism?  
`dsp:sahajiya-and-gaudiya-orthodoxy` · skeleton

- **lin:vaisnava-sahajiya**: Yes: Caitanya (Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa in one body) and the Gosvāmīs taught the sahaja doctrine esoterically; the Caitanya-caritāmṛta has an inner Sahajiyā meaning; parakīyā love is to be realized by human partners who see each other as Kṛṣṇa and Rādhā, turning kāma into prema.
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: No: parakīyā belongs only to Kṛṣṇa's līlā and may never be imitated by humans; kāma (desire for one's own pleasure) and prema (desire for Kṛṣṇa's) are opposites; the Gosvāmīs were strict renunciants. Later orthodoxy lists Āul, Bāul, Kartābhajā, Neḍā, Daraveś, Sāṃi, Sahajiyā, Sakhībhekī, Smārta, Jāt-gosāñi, Ativāḍī, Cūḍādhārī and Gaurāṅga-nāgarī as deviant sects (apasampradāya).
- **lin:kartabhaja**: Caitanya himself returned as Āulcānd to reveal the secret sahaja religion to the poor; outward conformity hides the true teaching.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: parakīyā true of the divine līlā but not of human conduct — this is the orthodox position itself and is rejected by the Sahajiyā
  - P5-neyartha: the Sahajiyā reading of the Caitanya-caritāmṛta as the definitive (nītārtha) meaning behind a provisional surface — the Sahajiyā's own hermeneutic, rejected by the Gauḍīyas
  - P3-path / P4-stage: different qualifications (adhikāra) for different practitioners — rejected by the orthodox as licensing transgression
- Note: Not yet reconciled: each side rejects the other's reading of the same Caitanya literature.

## RQ-322 — Are the images (ākāra) appearing in cognition real or false?  
`dsp:sakara-nirakara` · skeleton

- **lin:satyakaravada**: Real: whatever appears, appears as cognition's own form; the variegated image is one non-dual cognition; even buddha-cognition has images.
- **lin:alikakaravada**: False: the images are superimposed by beginningless imprints; only image-free luminous awareness is real.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: the images may be real as cognition at the level of cognition-only, false at the level of the imageless.
  - P4-stage: Laṅkāvatāra 10.256–258 places passing beyond the 'imageless' as a further stage, which both sides read their way.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Vikramaśīla masters disagreed about the reality of cognition's images.

## RQ-323 — Do things possess a distinct causal power (śakti)?  
`dsp:sakti-causal-power` · skeleton

- **lin:mimamsa**: Yes: a distinct power in things explains efficacy — e.g. fire does not burn when a gem or mantra is present because its power is obstructed (Prābhākaras count śakti as a separate category).
- **lin:nyaya**: No: an effect follows from the totality of causes, which includes the absence of counteragents; the gem or mantra is a counteragent whose absence is a cause; capacity, similarity and number are not separate categories.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: 'power in the thing' and 'totality of conditions including absent obstacles' may describe the same causal facts from the standpoint of substance and of relations.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-324 — Should Śrīvidyā be practised only in the inner Samaya way, or also in the Kaula way with outer worship and Kaula conduct? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:samaya-or-kaula` · skeleton

- **lin:samaya-srividya**: Worship only in the inner space of the heart and crown; outer worship on a physical support is 'Kaula' and is rejected, as is the worship of a young woman's triangle; the sixty-four tantras are non-Vedic and aimed at worldly powers, the Candrakalā tantras 'mixed'; only the five Śubhāgamas teach the Vedic way.
- **lin:kaula-srividya**: Outer worship of the cakra is performed by Śiva himself and by every knower, because it produces the conviction of non-difference; Kaula conduct, including the five 'M's, is used in secret, and daily worship continues without them when unavailable; the Goddess is both Kaulinī and intent on Samaya conduct.
- Note: Both sides grade practice by the student's qualification (Lakṣmīdhara assigns modes by eligibility; the Yoginīhṛdaya grades aparā, parāparā and parā worship), so the modes can be read as different stages and temperaments of one path.

## RQ-U34-10 — Does Ṣaṭkhaṇḍāgama 1.1.93 allow human females the stages of full restraint?  
`dsp:samjada-controversy` · skeleton

- **lin:digambara**: One party of Digambara scholars: the sūtra's word 'saṃjada' (restrained) belongs in the text, so the sūtra allows the stages of restraint for 'manuṣinī' — understood, with the Dhavalā, of psychological sex (bhāva-veda).
- **lin:digambara**: The other party: the word is an interpolation or is to be read without implying liberation for women in a female body, which Digambara doctrine denies.
- Candidate readings:
  - The bhāva-veda reading lets both keep the text and the doctrine
- Note: Not yet reconciled; a 20th-century textual controversy within the Digambara community (recent).

## RQ-326 — Must one petition Amida with body, speech and mind (sangō kimyō) to be saved?  
`dsp:sango-wakuran` · skeleton

- **lin:shinshu-honganji-ha**: (The Honganji academy's heads, as usually reported) One should rely on Amida with the three acts — bowing, calling for help and wishing — as the form of entrusting.
- **lin:jodo-shinshu**: (Their provincial critics) Entrusting is the one thought of shinjin given by Amida; requiring the three acts is self-power.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: three acts as the outward form of shinjin vs as its condition
- Note: Recorded as a historical controversy; not reconciled here.

## RQ-327 — Is a living master necessary, and how is the true successor known?  
`dsp:sant-mat-living-master-and-succession` · skeleton

- **lin:radha-soami-satsang-beas**: A living Sant Satguru is indispensable; each master designates his successor; Jaimal Singh was authorized by Soamiji to initiate in the Punjab, and the Beas line continues by designation.
- **lin:radhasoami-agra**: The Agra lines trace the true succession through Soamiji's own circle (Rai Salig Ram and after, or Soamiji's family at Soamibagh, or the Dayalbagh line) and have not accepted the other lines' claims.
- **lin:dharamdasi-kabir-panth**: The Dharamdāsī Kabīr Panth locates authority in a hereditary line of forty-two ācāryas promised by Kabīr, not in spiritual designation.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage / P3-path: different lines serving different disciples — rejected by each line as denying the uniqueness of the true master
- Note: Not yet reconciled: each line claims the true succession.

## RQ-328 — Is śānta (peace) a rasa, and what is its stable emotion? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:santa-rasa` · skeleton

- **lin:alankara**: Yes: the Nāṭyaśāstra's śānta passage (in some recensions) makes calm its stable emotion and liberation its aim; Ānandavardhana makes it the principal rasa of the Mahābhārata; Abhinavagupta makes knowledge of reality — the self — its stable basis and the ground of all rasas; Mammaṭa (dispassion) and Viśvanātha (calm) count it the ninth.
- **lin:alankara**: No, not in drama: Bharata counts eight rasas; calm, being the cessation of all activity, cannot be developed or shown on stage (Dhanañjaya and Dhanika).
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Rūpa Gosvāmī counts śānta as the first and least intimate of the five primary devotional rasas — awareness of the Lord's greatness without personal love.
- Note: Much of the disagreement concerns the medium: the deniers speak of dramatic performance (nāṭya), the affirmers of poetry and of the audience's experience; in poetry both sides grant śānta.

## RQ-329 — Was there being (sat) or non-being (asat) in the beginning? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:sat-or-asat-in-the-beginning` · sourced

- **lin:vedic-srauta**: Being was born from non-being (RV 10.72.2–3), a view the Taittirīya Upaniṣad repeats ('in the beginning this was non-being; from it being was born', TU 2.7).
- **lin:vedic-srauta**: Then there was neither non-being nor being (RV 10.129.1); the seers found the bond of being in non-being (10.129.4).
- **lin:upanisadic**: In the beginning this was being alone, one only; how could being come from non-being? (Uddālaka in the Chāndogya, rejecting those who say asat was first).
- Note: Śaṅkara reads the 'non-being' of the creation texts as the unmanifest (name and form not yet distinguished), not absolute nothing, so 'asat' and 'sat' describe the same origin from the standpoints of the manifest and the unmanifest.

## RQ-U32-sayana-nirayana — Should the zodiac be reckoned from the moving equinox (sāyana) or from the fixed stars (nirayana), and with which ayanāṃśa?  
`dsp:sayana-nirayana` · skeleton

- **lin:jyotisa**: Nirayana: the siddhāntas and Indian horoscopy and almanacs reckon the signs and asterisms from a fixed star-point, treating the solstices' shift as an oscillation; schools differ on the amount of the correction.
- **lin:jyotisa**: Sāyana: the seasons, the solstices and the sun's courses follow the equinox, and some authorities and reformers hold that festivals tied to seasons (and the signs) should follow it.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: sidereal reckoning for asterism-based judgment, tropical for seasonal rites.
  - Recent: competing ayanāṃśas (Lahiri's Citrāpakṣa adopted after the 1950s Calendar Reform Committee; Raman; Krishnamurti) — recent: true.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-331 — Are the seeds in the store-consciousness innate, newly perfumed, or both? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:seeds-innate-or-perfumed` · skeleton

- **lin:yogacara**: Innate only: all seeds exist from beginningless time and are merely nourished by perfuming (Candrapāla, as reported in the Cheng weishi lun).
- **lin:yogacara**: Newly perfumed only: seeds are produced by perfuming; there is no seed without prior experience (Nanda, as reported).
- **lin:yogacara**: Both: some seeds are innate — including the pure seeds of the lineage — and some are newly perfumed (Dharmapāla, adopted by Xuanzang).
- Note: P2: Dharmapāla's own synthesis assigns innate seeds to the lineage and pure dharmas and perfumed seeds to experience; the two single-cause views each describe one standpoint.

## RQ-332 — Were the hymns 'seen' by the seers or 'fashioned' by poets? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:seen-or-made-hymns` · sourced

- **lin:vedic-srauta**: Seen: the seers had direct vision of dharma and handed on the mantras (Nirukta 1.20); 'a ṛṣi from seeing — he saw the hymns' (2.11); speech was found entered into the seers (RV 10.71.3); the Veda is authorless (Sāyaṇa).
- **lin:vedic-srauta**: Fashioned: the poets say they 'fashioned' (takṣ) their praise 'as a skilled craftsman a chariot' (RV 5.2.11), offer 'new' hymns, and speak of making them with mind and heart.
- Note: The hymns themselves hold both: speech is found entered into the seers (source, 10.71.3) and the wise fashion it with mind like grain sifted in a sieve (utterance, 10.71.2) — seen as to its origin, fashioned as to its form.

## RQ-333 — Is a cognition aware of itself?  
`dsp:self-awareness-of-cognition` · skeleton

- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Yes: self-awareness is a kind of perception, the result of the means of knowledge; memory of having seen shows that the cognition was aware of itself (Dignāga, Dharmakīrti).
- **lin:yogacara**: Cognition has a self-witnessing part — and, for Dharmapāla, a fourth part witnessing that (Cheng weishi lun).
- **lin:prasangika**: No: just as a sword does not cut itself, cognition cannot be its own object; memory does not prove reflexive awareness (Candrakīrti; Śāntideva, as recalled).
- **lin:nyaya**: No: a cognition is known by a subsequent inner perception (anuvyavasāya) 'I know this', produced through the mind.
- **lin:prabhakara-mimamsa**: Yes: every cognition reveals at once itself, its knower and its object (triple perception).
- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: A cognition is not perceived but inferred from the 'knownness' it produces in the object.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Consciousness is self-luminous (svaprakāśa) and never an object.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Cognition is reflexively aware of itself (svasaṃvedana).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the schools may be answering different questions — how a cognition is manifest versus how it becomes an object of report.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-334 — Is the self known as an object (of the 'I'-cognition), or only as the subject of every cognition?  
`dsp:self-known-as-object` · skeleton

- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: Kumārila: the self is grasped by the mind as the object of the 'I'-cognition (ahaṃ-pratyaya).
- **lin:prabhakara-mimamsa**: The self is never an object: it appears in every cognition of objects as its subject, as the goer is agent, not object, of going.
- **lin:mimamsa**: The Vṛttikāra: the self is known to itself (svasaṃvedya) and cannot be shown to another.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: 'self-known' (Vṛttikāra), 'object of the I-notion' (Kumārila) and 'subject in every cognition' (Prabhākara) may describe the same self-awareness from different analyses; Kumārila's side is recorded from memory and should be checked.
- Note: Not yet reconciled; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-335 — Is awakening reached by one's own practice (self-power) or by the power of Amitābha's vow (other-power)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:self-power-or-other-power` · skeleton

- **lin:chan**: Awakening is to see one's own nature; the Pure Land is in the purified mind and Amitābha is one's own nature; seeking a Buddha outside is delusion (as the Pure Land texts report the objection).
- **lin:pure-land**: In an age without a buddha one cannot reach non-retrogression by self-power; the easy path is to be born by riding the power of the vow; Amitābha's vow is the dominant condition.
- **lin:jodo-shu**: Birth is by other-power through the nenbutsu that accords with the vow; one keeps calling all one's life.
- **lin:jodo-shinshu**: Nothing is self-power: shinjin and the nenbutsu are given; the self-power good person is not the object of the vow until he turns; self-power nenbutsu leads only to the transformed land.
- Note: The Pure Land tradition itself presents the holy path and the Pure Land as two gates suited to different capacities and ages (Daochuo), a reconciliation by temperament and stage of the student.

## RQ-336 — How does a sentence convey its meaning: by the connection of word-meanings already expressed (abhihitānvaya) or by words that express meanings already connected (anvitābhidhāna)?  
`dsp:sentence-meaning` · skeleton

- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: Words convey their own meanings and cease; the expressed meanings, through expectancy, compatibility and proximity, yield the sentence-meaning, the word-meanings being an intermediate step like the flame in cooking.
- **lin:prabhakara-mimamsa**: Words express their meanings only as connected with one another and with the 'to-be-done', since word-meaning is learned from elders' action-guiding usage; the Bhāṭṭa appeal to secondary signification ('the sentence-meaning is always indicated') is rejected.
- **lin:vyakarana**: The sentence is an indivisible unit (vākya-sphoṭa) whose meaning flashes as an intuition (pratibhā); word-meanings are abstractions.
- **lin:vyakarana**: The sentence is a single partless sphoṭa; its meaning flashes as pratibhā; word-meanings are abstractions (apoddhāra) from it; Bhartṛhari lists eight rival definitions of the sentence.
- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: Words express their own meanings, which then combine through expectancy, compatibility and proximity (abhihitānvaya).
- **lin:prabhakara-mimamsa**: Words express their meanings only as already connected with one another (anvitābhidhāna).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the two Mīmāṃsā accounts describe the same understanding from the side of words (Prābhākara) and of meanings (Bhāṭṭa); each school denies the other's analysis of the mechanism.
  - P2-standpoint: the grammarian describes the understanding as it is experienced (one flash), the Mīmāṃsakas the analysis of how it is produced.
- Note: Not yet reconciled; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-337 — Sudden awakening followed by gradual cultivation (Jinul) or sudden awakening with sudden cultivation (Seongcheol)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:seon-sudden-cultivation-debate` · skeleton

- **lin:seon**: Jinul: all sages first awaken, then cultivate; habit energies are removed gradually after sudden awakening; even 'sudden/sudden' rests on past-life cultivation.
- **lin:seon**: Seongcheol: true Chan awakening is realization in which cultivation is complete — sudden awakening, sudden cultivation; Jinul's awakening is only understanding.
- Note: Jinul himself allows sudden/sudden for the highest faculties and treats sudden/gradual as the path of most practitioners.

## RQ-U22-siddha-ayurveda — Is Siddha medicine an independent Tamil science from Śiva and Agastya, or a regional form of the Āyurvedic system?  
`dsp:siddha-ayurveda-relation` · skeleton

- **lin:siddha-medicine**: Siddha medicine was revealed by Śiva through Nandi to the Siddhars and set down by Agastya in Tamil; its 96 principles, pulse lore, 4,448 diseases, mineral-metal pharmacy and kaṟpam are its own; the Kuṟaḷ already names the three humours in Tamil.
- **lin:ayurveda**: Āyurveda traces medicine from Brahmā through the Aśvins and Indra to Bharadvāja and Dhanvantari; the tridoṣa, seven dhātus and rasāyana are its categories, found in Caraka and Suśruta.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the two describe one humoral physiology from different transmissions (a reading the Siddha tradition rejects as subordinating it).
  - Scholarly account (metadata only): shared humoral vocabulary with distinct Tamil developments in pulse diagnosis and mineral-metal pharmacy.
- Note: not yet reconciled: the question is one of origin and priority, on which the traditions give incompatible accounts

## RQ-339 — Can the Veda be a means of knowledge about an already-existing thing (siddha), or only about what is to be done (sādhya / kārya)?  
`dsp:siddha-or-sadhya` · skeleton

- **lin:prabhakara-mimamsa**: Vedic sentences are authoritative about what is to be done; words mean in connection with the kārya, and scripture does not make known accomplished things for their own sake — so, e.g., a blissful liberation is not established by Upaniṣadic statements.
- **lin:mimamsa**: Passages that do not enjoin (arthavāda) are meaningful by forming one sentence with injunctions; the Brahma Sūtra reports Jaimini as holding that the self taught in the Upaniṣads is subsidiary to action and fruit-statements for knowledge are praise.
- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: Later Bhāṭṭas grant that Upaniṣads teaching the agent-self serve ritual injunctions, while those teaching the non-transmigrating self have their own purport as required by injunctions of meditation; self-knowledge serves both rite and person.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: The Upaniṣads are authoritative about Brahman, an existent reality, because their purport rests there; knowledge of Brahman is not enjoined and its fruit, liberation, is not produced by action.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: the ritual sections address the one eligible for action, the Upaniṣads the one eligible for knowledge (Śaṅkara's adhikāra-bheda) — accepted by Advaita, rejected by the Prābhākaras, for whom the issue is how sentences mean, not who hears them.
  - P2-standpoint: the later Bhāṭṭa view (Upaniṣads have their own purport as required by meditation) narrows the gap to the question whether meditation or knowledge is enjoined.
  - P8-six-marks: both sides use the same interpretive criteria but apply them to different passages; a text-by-text comparison may settle particular cases.
- Note: Not yet reconciled; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-U16-9 — Who is eligible for instruction in the inner perfected identity (siddha-praṇālī) and meditation on the eightfold play? (recent)  
`dsp:siddha-pranali-eligibility` · skeleton

- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: The lineages of Nityānanda and Advaita and the Vraja bābājīs: the guru transmits the inner identity at or after initiation; spontaneous practice begins with eager longing, supported by this instruction.
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī and the Gauḍīya Maṭha (recent): the identity manifests of itself when the heart is purified by chanting; formal instruction given to the unqualified produces imitation (a form of Sahajiyā error).
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: both agree the practice depends on qualification; they differ on how qualification is judged
- Note: not yet reconciled.

## RQ-341 — Are temple, image, Āgamic ritual and initiation the way to Śiva, or are they to be abandoned for the Lord within? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:siddha-vs-agamic-saivism` · skeleton

- **lin:tamil-siddha**: The Lord is within the body; the planted stone does not speak; temple and tank are in the mind; karma does not go by Kāśī or the Gaṅgā; chiselled stone and moulded image are not the Real; the realized need no hymns, mantras or seals.
- **lin:tamil-saiva-siddhanta**: Śiva is worshipped in the temple image and in his devotees; the Āgamas are his word; initiation, temple service and ritual (caryā and kriyā) are the first two of the four paths, and even the liberated worships the devotees and the temple form as Śiva himself (Civañāṉa Pōtam 12).
- Note: P4-stage: outer worship is for the lower rungs (caryā, kriyā), the Siddhars speak from the stage of the realized for whom such means are done with.

## RQ-U22-siddhar-rebirth — Are the dead born again?  
`dsp:siddhar-rebirth` · skeleton

- **lin:tamil-siddha**: Sivavākkiyar: as drawn milk does not return to the udder and fallen fruit does not return to the tree, the dead are not born again — 'no, no, no'.
- **lin:tamil-siddha**: Paṭṭiṉattār and most Siddhar voices: we have had countless mothers, fathers and births and more are to come unless the Lord frees us; one's own deed returns to burn oneself.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: the verse speaks of the realized, who having died to ego are not reborn (a reading the verse does not state).
  - P7-arthavada / polemical context: the verse attacks rites for the dead and the priestly trade in rebirth, not the doctrine of karma as such.
  - Taken literally, a genuine denial of rebirth within the Siddhar corpus, to be recorded as such (compare the Cārvāka position, not asserted here).
- Note: not yet reconciled: whether Sivavākkiyar's verse denies rebirth outright is itself disputed

## RQ-343 — Do the Siddhars accept the Veda (and scripture generally) or reject it? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:siddhar-veda-and-scripture` · skeleton

- **lin:tamil-siddha**: Kaṭuveḷi: 'stand by the rule of the Veda; go the way the good follow'; climb to the open space of pure Vedānta. The Tiruvaḷḷuvar ñāṉam: when the Veda becomes true knowledge the Lord's grace gives the state; its good meaning is like ghee in milk (12).
- **lin:tamil-siddha**: Pattirakiriyār: abiding alone means leaving all Vedānta and Veda; the four Vedas cry out but cannot see the secret. Sivavākkiyar and Pāmbāṭṭi scorn recitation and learning.
- Note: P4-stage: scripture governs the seeker's conduct (Kaṭuveḷi 8 is addressed to the beginner) and is left behind in abiding (niṭṭai), as Pattirakiriyār 32 says.

## RQ-344 — Is the fourth Mahāpurāṇa the Śiva Purāṇa or the Vāyu Purāṇa?  
`dsp:siva-or-vayu-mahapurana` · sourced

- **lin:puranic**: The Śaiva (Śiva) Purāṇa is the fourth Mahāpurāṇa.
- **lin:puranic**: The Vāyavīya (Vāyu) Purāṇa is the fourth Mahāpurāṇa.
- **lin:puranic**: Both are named, the 'Vāyavīya' being identified with the eighteenth, the Brahmāṇḍa.
- Candidate readings:
  - The Matsya's 'Vāyavīya of 24,000 verses with Rudra's glory' and the lists' 'Śaiva of 24,000' name one Śaiva Purāṇa proclaimed by Vāyu (the Śiva Purāṇa's own Vāyavīya Saṃhitā supports a link).
  - The Kūrma's identification of Vāyavīya with Brahmāṇḍa suggests the two once formed one Vāyu-proclaimed text, leaving the Śiva as fourth.
  - Sectarian preference (Śaiva vs other) shaped which title each list preserved.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the lists themselves differ and none of the eight principles decides which text is meant.

## RQ-345 — Who is supreme in the epic — Nārāyaṇa/Viṣṇu or Śiva? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:siva-or-visnu-epic` · skeleton

- **lin:bhagavata-early**: Nārāyaṇa-Vāsudeva is supreme: the culmination seen in all five systems, including Pāśupata (12.337.63–67); the Viṣṇu Sahasranāma names him the one refuge (13.135); in the Gītā Śaṃkara is one of Kṛṣṇa's manifestations (10.23).
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Śiva is supreme: Kṛṣṇa himself learns Śiva's thousand names from Upamanyu and obtains his wishes by devotion to him (13.14–17); Śiva proclaims the Pāśupata knowledge (12.337.62), grants Arjuna the Pāśupata weapon (3.38–41) and teaches Umā dharma (13.126–134).
- Note: The epic states that Rudra and Nārāyaṇa are one being made twofold, manifest in all actions (12.328.24), so the praises of each are read as the one being praised under two forms.

## RQ-346 — Is Śakti supreme, is Śiva supreme, or are they equal? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:siva-sakti-primacy` · skeleton

- **lin:sakta**: Śakti is the power without which Śiva cannot even stir; the gods act on her command; she is the queen of the supreme Brahman.
- **lin:kaula**: Uttara-Kaula: there is no Śiva-principle apart from the Śakti-principle; Śiva is included in Śakti and she alone is to be worshipped. Pūrva-Kaula: the Goddess is Śiva's body, the relation of principal and subsidiary common to both.
- **lin:samaya-srividya**: Śiva and Śakti are of equal primacy, sharing a fivefold equality of seat, state, action, form and name; neither is subordinate.
- **lin:srividya**: The ultimate is the oneness of Śiva and Śakti — light and self-awareness fused — while worship is directed to the Goddess seated on Kāmeśvara's lap.
- Note: From the standpoint of worship and power (śakti) the Goddess is primary; from the standpoint of the ultimate the texts speak of the fusion or equality of Śiva and Śakti — different standpoints on one union.

## RQ-347 — Is the mind (citta) only of the size of the body, contracting and expanding, or all-pervading with only its function contracting and expanding?  
`dsp:size-of-citta` · sourced

- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: The mind is like a lamp placed in a jar or a palace: it contracts and expands and is only of the size of the body; thus an intermediate state (antarābhāva) and saṃsāra are logical.
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: It is the function (vṛtti) of the all-pervading (vibhu) mind that contracts and expands; this depends on dharma and the like as occasion, external or internal.
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Some (apare): the mind, like a lamp placed in a jar or a palace, contracts and expands to the size of the body, which makes the intermediate state and transmigration intelligible.
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: The teacher (ācārya): the mind is all-pervading; only its activity contracts and expands, depending on merit and other causes.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: substance (all-pervading mind) vs mode (its contracting activity).
  - Textual: identify 'the ācārya' and 'others' from the sub-commentaries.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the bhāṣya states the two positions and takes the second; no principle is applied here.

## RQ-U50-04 — Are the individual souls one with Brahman/God, eternally distinct from him and from one another, or both different and non-different?  
`dsp:souls-one-or-distinct` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: The individual self is Brahman alone: 'that you are', 'I am Brahman', 'this self is Brahman'. The one Lord is the knower of the field in all fields; the plurality and transmigration of souls belong to adjuncts. Of the three early teachers in the Brahmasūtra, Kāśakṛtsna's view — that the supreme Lord himself abides as the individual self — is the one in accord with scripture.
- **lin:dvaita**: Difference is real and eternal — fivefold (pañca-bheda): between the Lord and souls, the Lord and matter, soul and soul, soul and matter, and matter and matter. 'Tat tvam asi' does not teach identity (it is read as 'sa ātmā atat tvam asi', 'you are not that', or of the soul's dependence on and likeness to the Lord); those who, proud with 'I am Brahman', read the Veda as oneness go to darkness. Liberated souls remain distinct and graded.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: Souls are eternal and many and are modes and body of Brahman, inseparable from him (apṛthak-siddhi). In 'tat tvam asi' both words denote Brahman — 'that' as the cause of the world, 'you' as having the soul as its body; scripture's texts of difference, of non-difference and the mediating texts are all true. The self is a part of Brahman as an attribute is part of what it qualifies; the Gītā's 'never was there a time when I was not, nor you' teaches the eternal plurality of selves.
- **lin:bhedabheda**: The soul is a part (aṃśa) of Brahman, different from it by its limiting adjuncts and non-different in essence (Bhāskara: difference adventitious).
- **lin:dvaitadvaita**: Because scripture declares both, the soul is a part of Brahman both different and non-different by nature (svābhāvika), like the snake and its coils; the released soul abides in non-separation from Brahman while remaining a distinct knower.
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: The souls are the Lord's marginal power, eternally distinct from yet dependent on him — inconceivable difference and non-difference (acintya-bhedābheda).
- **lin:vedanta**: The Brahmasūtra itself records three earlier views on the passages that identify the self with Brahman: Āśmarathya (identity as the fulfilment of the promise that by knowing one all is known), Auḍulomi (identity when the soul departs in liberation), Kāśakṛtsna (the supreme self abides as the individual self). The soul is called a part 'because of the declarations of difference and otherwise'.
- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: The Lord is one with souls and yet other than them, and with them — like the soul with the body, the vowel with the consonants; non-duality means inseparability, not identity; the liberated soul becomes equal to Śiva but remains a soul.
- **lin:kashmir-saivism**: Maheśvara alone is the self of all beings, of the form of the universe, full of the undivided awareness 'I'; the liberated becomes (like) Śiva.
- **lin:vaisesika**: Selves are many, not one: though pleasure, pain and cognition arise alike, their distinct states (bondage, release, experience) show plurality.
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Though the seen has ceased for the one whose purpose is fulfilled, it has not ceased for others, being common to them: puruṣas are many.
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Asked whether there are many puruṣas or one, the Mokṣadharma reports that the Sāṃkhya-Yoga thinkers hold many, and teaches that all are grounded in the one Puruṣa.
- **lin:jainism**: Souls are infinitely many, each an independent substance; absolute non-duality is refuted.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level (Advaita's own): difference is real for the empirical self, identity is the ultimate truth — rejected by Madhva, for whom difference is real in every state, and by Rāmānuja, who denies identity of essence.
  - P2-standpoint: souls one with the Lord as consciousness (substance standpoint), many as individual centres (mode standpoint) — the bhedābheda schools' own form; rejected by Dvaita as a denial of real difference and by Advaita as a denial of non-duality.
  - P3-path (devotional vs knowledge): the devotee's 'I am yours' (dāsoham) and the knower's 'I am that' (so'ham) as two paths — Śāṇḍilya Bhakti Sūtra 29-31 records Kāśyapa (difference), Bādarāyaṇa (identity) and Śāṇḍilya (both); rejected by Dvaita as making difference merely devotional.
  - P4-stage: dāsoham ripening into so'ham (see dsp:dasoham-soham) — rejected by Dvaita and Viśiṣṭādvaita, for whom the servant relation is eternal.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: identity (Advaita), eternal difference (Dvaita) and qualified or natural difference-in-non-difference (Viśiṣṭādvaita, Bhedābheda, Śaiva Siddhānta) are each defended as the purport of the same texts.

## RQ-349 — Was haṭha yoga taught by Śiva (Ādinātha) through the Nāth siddhas, or did its earliest methods arise in a Buddhist tantric milieu?  
`dsp:source-of-hatha-yoga` · skeleton

- **lin:hatha-yoga**: Haṭha was taught by Śrī Ādinātha (Śiva) and known by Matsyendra, Gorakṣa and the other great siddhas, from whom Svātmārāma received it.
- **lin:mahasiddha**: The Tibetan canon places the Amṛtasiddhi among the siddhas' works, opening with homage to Heruka and ascribed to the great master Virūpa.
- Candidate readings:
  - Several strands: a Buddhist-milieu bindu yoga (Amṛtasiddhi) and Śaiva/Kaula kuṇḍalinī and khecarī strands, joined in the later haṭha compilations (scholarly account).
  - A shared siddha milieu in which the same names (Virūpa/Virūpākṣa, Mīna, Cauraṅgī) belong to both traditions (see cpt:siddha-identifications).
- Note: Not yet reconciled: a question of origins on which the two traditions' accounts differ.

## RQ-350 — Who composed the Spandakārikā - Vasugupta or his disciple Kallaṭa?  
`dsp:spanda-karika-authorship` · skeleton

- **lin:spanda**: Kṣemarāja: Vasugupta composed the kārikās to make the Śiva Sūtra's teaching accessible; the closing verse naming Vasugupta supports this.
- **lin:spanda**: Bhāskara (Vārttika 1.5: Kallaṭa explained three sections of the Śiva Sūtra 'with his own Spanda-sūtras'), Rāmakaṇṭha (Spandavivṛti) and Bhagavadutpala (Spandapradīpikā): Kallaṭa composed them, conveying his teacher Vasugupta's teaching.
- Candidate readings:
  - Kallaṭa versified the teaching he received from Vasugupta, so that Vasugupta is the source and Kallaṭa the composer.
  - The closing verse names Vasugupta as the teacher, not necessarily as the author.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: an attribution question to which the reconciliation principles do not apply.

## RQ-351 — Is there a sphoṭa — a unitary word-entity distinct from the letters — that conveys meaning?  
`dsp:sphota` · skeleton

- **lin:vyakarana**: Yes. The letters, perishing one after another, can convey meaning neither singly nor together; the word is a partless, sequence-less sphoṭa manifested by the sounds, grasped when the last sound, helped by the impressions of the earlier ones, makes it clear; finally only the sentence-sphoṭa is real.
- **lin:mimamsa**: No. 'The letters alone are the word' (Upavarṣa): the last letter, together with the impressions left by the earlier ones, conveys the meaning; a sphoṭa is never perceived and is a needless assumption.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Śaṅkara follows Upavarṣa against the sphoṭa, while accepting that the world is preceded by the Vedic word (words as eternal universals).
- **lin:nyaya**: No separate sphoṭa: the letters are apprehended in sequence and their meaning is understood from the final letter with the recollection of the earlier ones; sound is a non-eternal quality of ether.
- **lin:mimamsa**: No: the word 'gauḥ' is the letters g, au, ḥ (Upavarṣa); meaning arises from the letters (through the impression of the earlier ones), as fire is known from smoke; a sphoṭa is never perceived and is superfluous.
- **lin:vyakarana**: Yes: the letters, perishing in sequence, cannot jointly convey meaning; the indivisible sphoṭa, manifested by the sounds, conveys it (Bhartṛhari); Maṇḍana Miśra's Sphoṭasiddhi defends this against Kumārila.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the grammarians describe the word as understood (one), the Mīmāṃsakas the word as heard (letters); both agree that meaning is grasped only at the end of the utterance with the help of impressions.
  - P1-level: Bhartṛhari's sphoṭa belongs to the level of the undivided Word-principle, the letters to the level of articulated speech (vaikharī).
  - P2-standpoint: the letter-sequence (as heard) and the unitary word (as understood) may be two standpoints on one process — the Mīmāṃsakas reject a separate entity, the grammarians a merely serial conveyance.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: whether a unitary meaning-bearer exists over and above the letters.

## RQ-352 — Do śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas realize the emptiness (selflessness) of phenomena?  
`dsp:sravaka-realization-of-emptiness` · skeleton

- **lin:prasangika**: Yes: without understanding that the aggregates lack own-nature they could not abandon the view of self, for as long as there is grasping at the aggregates there is grasping at 'I' (RĀ 1.35); they differ from bodhisattvas in the extent of their cultivation, not in the object understood (Candrakīrti, MAbh on 1.8).
- **lin:svatantrika**: No: śrāvakas realize only the selflessness of persons; the selflessness of phenomena is the special realization of bodhisattvas.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: both agree the bodhisattva's realization is more complete; they differ on whether the difference is of kind or extent.
  - P5-neyartha: the Hīnayāna scriptures' brief statements on the emptiness of form read as definitive (Candrakīrti) or as not teaching phenomenal selflessness (Bhāviveka).
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the sides disagree on what liberation from saṃsāra requires.

## RQ-353 — Does the hearing of the Upaniṣadic sentence itself produce immediate knowledge (so that hearing is the principal means), or does the mind perfected by contemplation produce it? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:sravana-alone-liberates` · skeleton

- **lin:vivarana**: Hearing is principal (aṅgin), enjoined by a restrictive injunction; the mahāvākya directly gives immediate knowledge, as 'you are the tenth' does.
- **lin:bhamati**: Contemplation is principal; verbal knowledge is mediate; the mind, perfected by contemplation, is the instrument of direct realization ('to be seen by the mind alone').
- Note: The tradition's own compendium (Siddhāntaleśasaṅgraha) records both as methods leading to the same knowledge; both agree hearing, reflection and contemplation are all to be practised.

## RQ-U42-srenika — Is there a permanent mind-nature that leaves the perishing body at death?  
`dsp:srenika-heresy` · skeleton

- **lin:chan**: (Reported view of certain 'southern' teachers) The knowing nature is permanent and has never arisen or perished; the body perishes like a snake's shed skin while the nature departs.
- **lin:chan**: Nanyang Huizhong: this is no different from the heretic Śreṇika, who holds that a spirit leaves the house of the body when it burns.
- **lin:soto**: Dōgen: body and mind are one, nature and form are not two; the Śreṇika view is not the buddha-dharma.
- Candidate readings:
  - P5-neyartha: talk of a permanent nature (from the Nirvāṇa-sūtra) is provisional and misread as a soul.
  - P1-level: permanence of buddha-nature at the ultimate level confused with a conventional entity that transmigrates.
- Note: The first side is known only through its critics; not yet reconciled.

## RQ-355 — Did Śrīkaṇṭha teach qualified non-dualism as final, or as a step toward Advaita? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:srikantha-advaita-or-visistadvaita` · skeleton

- **lin:siva-visistadvaita**: The text of Śrīkaṇṭha's commentary: Śiva with his śakti, souls and world as his body, and liberation as equality (not identity) with Śiva — qualified non-dualism as final.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Appayya Dīkṣita (Śivādvaitanirṇaya): Śrīkaṇṭha's real intent is pure non-dualism; he taught the qualified form for devotees attached to Śiva with attributes.
- **lin:virasaiva**: Vīraśaiva authors (e.g. Nīlakaṇṭha's Kriyāsāra) claim Śrīkaṇṭha as a Vīraśaiva teacher of Śakti-qualified non-dualism.
- Note: Appayya's own reading places the qualified doctrine at a stage for devotees (P4).

## RQ-356 — Is Nirañjan the stainless supreme or a lower, deceiving power (Kāl)?  
`dsp:status-of-niranjan` · skeleton

- **lin:natha**: Alakh Nirañjan, the stainless unseen, is the ultimate of the yogīs.
- **lin:niranjani**: Nirañjan is the formless stainless Lord, object of devotion and yoga.
- **lin:dadu-panth**: Dādū bows first to Nirañjan in his Vāṇī.
- **lin:kabir-panth**: Nirañjan is Kāl, the fallen son of the Sat Puruṣ, creator and jailer of the three worlds.
- **lin:sant-mat**: Jot Nirañjan rules the first region (Sahasdal Kanwal) and belongs to Kāl's domain; those who stop there take a lower lord for the highest.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: Sant Mat's own reading — those who reached only the first region named its lord supreme; rejected by Nāths and Nirañjanīs
