  - P1-level: nirañjana as the absolute (stainless) vs Nirañjan as the ruler of the created worlds (cosmic function); rejected by the Kabīr Panth, for whom Kāl is not the Sat Puruṣ in any aspect
  - Homonymy: the name denotes different things in different lineages (so no single question); weakened if, as some scholars suggest, the Kabīr Panth myth deliberately answers the Nāth/Nirañjanī theology (scholarly suggestion, metadata only)
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the same name is the highest for some lineages and a lower power for others.

## RQ-357 — What is Rādhā's status — scriptural basis, relation to Kṛṣṇa, and place in worship? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:status-of-radha` · skeleton

- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Rādhā is Kṛṣṇa's hlādinī-śakti, one in self with him, the embodiment of mahābhāva and the object of the highest service; the Bhāgavata hints at her (10.30.28 'anayārādhitaḥ'); Caitanya is Rādhā–Kṛṣṇa combined.
- **lin:dvaitadvaita**: Rādhā, daughter of Vṛṣabhānu, is worshipped with Kṛṣṇa as the eternal divine couple (Daśaślokī 5).
- **lin:pustimarga**: Vallabha's own works centre on Kṛṣṇa and the gopīs as a group; Rādhā as Svāminī is emphasised by Viṭṭhalanātha.
- **lin:radhavallabha**: The Rādhāvallabha tradition gives Rādhā the supreme place (lineage owned by U26; summary only).
- Note: All three Vaiṣṇava schools worship Rādhā with Kṛṣṇa; their differing emphases follow their chosen devotional moods (iṣṭa and rasa).

## RQ-U14-03 — What is Śrī's rank: all-pervading and of the Lord's rank (Īśvara-koṭi), or atomic and of the rank of souls (jīva-koṭi)? Is she a means or only the mediator?  
`dsp:status-of-sri` · skeleton

- **lin:vadakalai**: Śrī is all-pervading like Viṣṇu, of the Lord's rank, and with him the means and goal; the divine couple is the object of surrender.
- **lin:tenkalai**: Śrī is the mediator (puruṣakāra) who reconciles soul and Lord; the Lord alone is the means; she is commonly reported as atomic in essence and of the rank of souls, though never separate from him.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: Śrī's pervasiveness and lordship (V) spoken of her essence as the Lord's consort; her mediation (T) spoken of her function toward souls
  - shared ground: both make her inseparable from the Lord, the mother of all and the first refuge in the Śaraṇāgati Gadya
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U50-11 — What is the status of the Veda: authorless and eternal (apauruṣeya), breathed out or composed by the omniscient Lord, or without authority?  
`dsp:status-of-veda` · skeleton

- **lin:mimamsa**: The Veda is authorless (apauruṣeya) and eternal: the relation of word and meaning is original, not made by any person; names in the Veda are of expounders, not authors, or mere similarity of sound; since faults in testimony arise from dependence on a speaker, a speakerless Veda is free of them and its cognitions are never overturned; every study of the Veda is preceded by the teacher's study; no omniscient author can be established.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: The Veda is eternal — no independent author of it is remembered, and the world with its fixed forms arises from the Vedic word; yet Brahman is its source, from which it is breathed out effortlessly, and it is the only means of knowing Brahman. For the knower, in the highest state, 'the Vedas are no Vedas'; the Veda is the lower knowledge, the knowledge of the imperishable the higher.
- **lin:dvaita**: The Veda is authorless; no author of it is known, and to assume one would be needless; the Ṛg and other Vedas, the Bhārata and the Pañcarātra are the scriptures that teach Viṣṇu.
- **lin:nyaya**: The Veda is valid because it is the word of a reliable, omniscient speaker (the Lord) — like mantras and Āyurveda, whose validity is seen; the charges of falsehood, self-inconsistency and repetition fail. Nyāya is foremost among sciences because it protects the Veda's authority.
- **lin:vaisesika**: The authority of the sacred tradition follows from its being the statement of 'that' (the Lord / the seer); the composition of sentences in the Veda is preceded by understanding.
- **lin:samkhya**: The Vedas are not eternal, since scripture speaks of their being produced; nor are they of personal authorship, since there is no author; their validity is intrinsic.
- **lin:epic-teaching**: The Gītā: the Vedas have the three guṇas as their domain — be free of the three; for the knowing brāhmaṇa there is as much use in all the Vedas as in a well when there is flood all around; yet the one who casts aside scripture's injunctions attains neither perfection nor happiness; 'I alone am to be known by all the Vedas'.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: An authorless Veda cannot be shown true in meaning: truth of speech depends on the qualities of a speaker, and without a speaker there is no guarantee; the Mīmāṃsā denial of any being who sees supersensible things is refuted; the Veda's claims about the supersensible have no means of knowledge behind them.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: The brāhmaṇas versed in the three Vedas teach a path to union with Brahmā though neither they nor their teachers to the seventh generation have seen Brahmā — like a line of blind men.
- **lin:jainism**: The Vedas, the epics and other non-Jain scriptures are 'false scripture' (mithyāśruta) for one of wrong view, though they can serve right knowledge for one of right view; the Jain twelve-limbed canon 'never was not, never is not, never will not be'. Not by the syllable Oṃ is one a brāhmaṇa, but by conduct.
- **lin:mantramarga**: The Veda and the Āgama are both true, both the Lord's scripture — one general, the other special (Tirumantiram); Śrīkaṇṭha sees no difference between the Veda and the Śivāgama; the Kaula texts make the Vedic auxiliaries the limbs of the Kula (see dsp:agama-veda-authority).
- **lin:sant**: Do not say the Vedas and the Books are false; false is the one who does not reflect (Kabīr); I speak of what I have seen, you of what is written on paper; the Bāuls follow the present (bartamān), not the inferred; Basava will put the Veda to the touchstone; Tukārām: we alone know the Veda's meaning, others carry its burden.
- **lin:carvaka**: (As reported) Rejected: the Veda is tainted by falsehood, inconsistency and repetition; its makers were buffoons, knaves and demons; its rites are a livelihood of priests; the Bārhaspatyas call it a mere cover.
- **lin:sramana**: (As reported) Ajita: there is no gift, no sacrifice, no offering.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: Sacrifice of animals and claims of birth-purity are refuted; the priests are cheats (Bhūridatta).
- **lin:nyaya**: The charge of falsehood, inconsistency (vyāghāta) and repetition fails: a failed rite is due to defects in the act, agent or means.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint (Vedānta's middle): from the standpoint of its source the Veda issues from the omniscient; from the standpoint of its form and sequence it is never freely composed — rejected by Mīmāṃsā (no Lord) and by Nyāya (the Lord composes it anew).
  - P1-level / P4-stage (the Upaniṣads' and Gītā's own relativization): the Veda is the lower knowledge, of use until the flood of knowledge — for the knower 'the Vedas are no Vedas'; Mīmāṃsā does not accept a stage beyond the injunctions.
  - P7-arthavada: the Sants' and Tantras' disparagements of Veda-reciters read as censure of misuse, not of the Veda (Kabīr: 'do not say the Vedas are false') — the śramaṇa critics reject the Veda itself, not only its misuse.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Vedic schools agree the Veda is valid but disagree whether it has an author (Mīmāṃsā, Vedānta vs Nyāya), and the śramaṇa, Cārvāka and some bhakti and Sant voices deny or relativize its authority.

## RQ-U50-06 — Is awakening sudden — an all-at-once seeing of the mind's nature — or gradual, reached by stages of cultivation and analysis; and after sudden awakening, is gradual cultivation still needed? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:sudden-or-gradual` · skeleton

- **lin:heze**: Shenhui (Southern school): Bodhidharma's robe and the direct transmission passed to Huineng; Shenxiu's line is collateral, and its teaching — 'freezing the mind to enter samādhi, fixing the mind to view purity, activating the mind to illumine outside, concentrating the mind to realise within' — is gradual; the true teaching is sudden awakening.
- **lin:chan**: The Platform Sūtra: the Dharma has no sudden or gradual; people's natures are sharp or dull — the deluded cultivate gradually, the awakened accord suddenly. Its gate takes no-thought as its tenet, no-form as its substance, non-abiding as its root; for one awakened to self-nature there is 'sudden awakening, sudden cultivation, without gradual steps', while Shenxiu's three trainings suit smaller faculties. Guishan: a beginner who suddenly awakens to principle in one thought still has beginningless habit energies to clear — that is cultivation.
- **lin:northern-chan**: The Northern school in its own words: the buddhas have a great expedient for entering the Way — with one thought of the pure mind one suddenly transcends to the buddha-ground; viewing the mind, if it is pure, is called the ground of the pure mind; awakening is the mind's substance free of thought, equal to space. (The Southern texts report Shenxiu's verse — the body the bodhi tree, the mind a bright mirror to be wiped — and his 'fix the mind, view stillness'.)
- **lin:chan**: Moheyan (Chinese party at the Tibetan court): all beings are bound by deluded, discriminating thought; when thoughts arise, do not follow them — 'not thinking, not examining' — and the originally pure mind appears; the six perfections are included in this non-examining; entry into non-conceptuality is simultaneous, not by steps.
- **lin:yogacara-madhyamaka**: Kamalaśīla: there is no way other than correct analysis (bhūta-pratyavekṣā) to reach non-recollection and non-attention; mere absence of thought cannot cause non-conceptual wisdom, or one who has fainted would have it. Seeing by analysis that no dharma has arisen in the three times, the yogin enters non-conceptual wisdom; the path is gradual — calm abiding and insight, the perfections and the grounds.
- **lin:theravada**: The Kathāvatthu rejects the thesis that the truths are penetrated gradually (anupubbābhisamaya): each path penetrates the four truths at once.
- **lin:sarvastivada**: The truths are realized in sixteen successive moments of acceptance and knowledge on the path of seeing — a graded, momentary sequence.
- **lin:mahayana**: The Laṅkāvatāra: the stream of what is seen as one's own mind is purified gradually — as mangoes ripen — and also all at once — as a mirror shows all forms at once. The Avataṃsaka: at the first arousal of the mind one attains unsurpassed awakening. The Śūraṅgama: in principle awakening is sudden, in phenomena removal is gradual.
- **lin:heze**: Zongmi: sudden and gradual, awakening and cultivation, seem opposed but accord and support one another; if one cultivates on the basis of awakening it is understanding-awakening, if one awakens through cultivation it is realization-awakening; 'first sudden awakening, then gradual cultivation' is like the sun rising at once while frost melts gradually. Over past lives there is only the gradual.
- **lin:seon**: Jinul: though there are many gates, in essence they are two — sudden awakening and gradual cultivation; even the 'sudden awakening, sudden cultivation' of the highest faculties rests on gradual cultivation in past lives; the principle is awakened suddenly, habit energies are removed step by step.
- **lin:seon**: (Recent) Seongcheol: the patriarchs' awakening is realization-awakening in which cultivation is also complete — sudden awakening, sudden cultivation; Jinul's formula describes only understanding-awakening.
- **lin:sakya**: Sakya Paṇḍita: the Mahāmudrā of his day and the 'Chinese Great Perfection' are essentially the same as the Hashang's teaching rejected in Tibet; true Mahāmudrā is the wisdom born of empowerment and the two stages.
- **lin:vajrayana**: The Ājñāsamyakpramāṇa: by the difference of persons' minds there are the gradual and the simultaneous; the medicine of one is the poison of the other — teach the simultaneous to one with the residue of former training, the gradual to beginners.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: (An analogue in the Upaniṣadic yoga texts) The Varāha Upaniṣad distinguishes Śuka's path of the bird — immediate liberation through inquiry into the great sayings — from Vāmadeva's path of the ant — gradual liberation over births through yoga — and says both reach Brahman.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage for Samye: the simultaneous approach for those of sharp faculties or with former training, the gradual for beginners (Ājñāsamyakpramāṇa; Samten Migdrön's ranking) — rejected by Kamalaśīla ('no way other than correct analysis').
  - P6-upaya: 'not thinking, not examining' as a pointer to the ever-present mind rather than a technique — not accepted by the Tibetan critics, who read it as a method that leads to dullness.
  - Kathāvatthu vs Sarvāstivāda: 'at once' of the path-moment vs sixteen moments of the path of seeing — a question of how moments are counted within one path; neither school states this reconciliation.
- Note: Reconciled within Chan, Seon and the tantric Tengyur by their own principle that sudden and gradual lie in the practitioner's faculties, not in the Dharma (P4-stage), and by the reading 'principle is awakened suddenly, phenomena are removed gradually' (P1-level); the Samye question — whether non-conceptuality is reached by non-examining or only through analysis — remains not yet reconciled.

## RQ-361 — Can a king be liberated while ruling — and may a woman renunciant test him? (MBh 12.308)  
`dsp:sulabha-janaka` · skeleton

- **lin:epic-teaching**: Janaka Dharmadhvaja: taught by Pañcaśikha, he is liberated while ruling; a mendicant woman entering a king's mind by yoga commits an impropriety, mixing what should be kept apart.
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Sulabhā: one still attached to royal insignia, possessions and power is not free as he claims; analysing the body she shows it to be a composite that no one owns; she has done no wrong in entering his understanding by yoga.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: Janaka's claim concerns inner knowledge, Sulabhā's critique concerns conduct and attachment — both may hold from their standpoints
  - the dialogue's own weight falls on Sulabhā's reply, which the narrative gives the last word (to be confirmed in Phase D)
  - link to the general question whether women may renounce and teach (dsp:women-caste-liberation)
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the dialogue records the challenge to the ruling king's claim of liberation and the objection to a woman renunciant's conduct.

## RQ-362 — Is Viṣṇu or Śiva (or the Goddess) the supreme deity? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:supremacy-visnu-or-siva` · sourced

- **lin:puranic**: Viṣṇu/Kṛṣṇa is supreme: Kṛṣṇa is Bhagavān himself; Brahmā and Śiva are his functions; Śambhu is the foremost Vaiṣṇava.
- **lin:puranic**: Śiva is supreme: he alone is niṣkala and worshipped in the liṅga; the column of fire whose ends Brahmā and Viṣṇu cannot find.
- **lin:sakta**: The Goddess is supreme: she gives Viṣṇu, Brahmā and Śiva their bodies; beyond Hari and Hara.
- **lin:puranic**: Hari and Hara are one (the Kūrma's Īśvara Gītā; Gajendra's hymn reached Hari as all the gods).
- Note: Each Purāṇa exalts the worshipper's chosen deity (P3) with praise-language (P7); several Purāṇas themselves state the unity of the forms (VP 1.2.66; Kūrma), a standpoint reading (P2).

## RQ-U33-5 — Should one refuse to affirm or deny anything about what lies beyond experience (the other world, fruit of deeds, the perfected one after death)?  
`dsp:suspension-of-judgment` · skeleton

- **lin:ajnana**: (As reported) Yes: to every such question, in each of its four forms, 'I do not say it is so, nor thus, nor otherwise, nor not so, nor not not so'; knowing is useless and ignorance better.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: It is eel-wriggling born of fear or dullness; yet the Buddha himself sets some questions aside as not leading to the goal and declares what he knows beyond all views.
- **lin:jainism**: The ignorant cannot settle their inquiry in ignorance or teach others; they are blind leading the blind.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: Sañjaya's refusal of four alternatives resembles the Jain 'avaktavya' and the later catuṣkoṭi — a resemblance noted by scholars (metadata only), rejected by both traditions.
  - P4-stage: the Buddha's setting-aside is tied to what benefits the hearer — not a general refusal.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Ajñānika refuses to assert; Buddhist and Jain both assert, and both insist their own reticence (the undeclared points; the 'inexpressible' of the sevenfold predication) differs in kind from his.

## RQ-364 — Can Mahāmudrā be introduced and realized apart from tantric empowerment — Gampopa's 'sūtra Mahāmudrā', the 'white self-sufficient remedy' — or is Mahāmudrā only the wisdom arising from empowerment and the two stages?  
`dsp:sutra-mahamudra` · skeleton

- **lin:kagyu**: Mahāmudrā is the nature of one's own mind; a realized guru can point it out through blessing to a ripe disciple, also on the basis of the sūtra teaching of buddha-nature, and recognizing it suffices ('the white self-sufficient remedy'); the tantric path of method is a second, complementary path, not the only door.
- **lin:sakya**: Mahāmudrā, in the tantras, is the wisdom born from the empowerments and the completion stage; a 'Mahāmudrā' taught without them is not the Buddha's Mahāmudrā; the 'white self-sufficient remedy' is the teaching of the Chinese Hashang, refuted at Samye; the path needs the gradual accumulation of merit and wisdom.
- **lin:gelug**: There is both a sūtra Mahāmudrā — the emptiness of mind realized by a mind that has established emptiness by reasoning — and a tantric Mahāmudrā — the clear light of great bliss; the Ganden (Gelug) Mahāmudrā tradition teaches both (First Panchen Lama).
- **lin:sakya**: Sakya Paṇḍita: the Mahāmudrā taught by the Buddha is only the wisdom born of empowerment and the two stages; the present-day 'white self-sufficient remedy' taught without empowerment is the Chinese (Hwashang) Great Perfection under another name, and a fool's meditation on it leads to animal or formless rebirth or the śrāvaka cessation.
- **lin:kagyu**: The Kagyu teach a Mahāmudrā of pointing out the nature of mind that can be introduced on the basis of sūtra (the Ratnagotravibhāga and Maitrīpa's teaching of non-attention), as Gampopa did; later Kagyu scholars answered Sakya Paṇḍita that his target was not their teaching.
- **lin:ganden-oral-lineage**: The First Panchen Lama: Mahāmudrā has a sūtra approach (emptiness of mind as Nāgārjuna taught) and a mantra approach (clear light of great bliss); the various Mahāmudrā instructions converge in one intent.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: 'sūtra Mahāmudrā' is for persons of the simultaneous type with prior training (Ājñāsamyakpramāṇa 271a.3); Sakya objection — the Hashang made the same claim.
  - P1-level / terminological: the word names two things — the emptiness-luminosity of mind recognized through blessing, and the tantric wisdom of the fourth empowerment; if so the dispute is partly about the name; Sakya objection — the name is proper only to the tantric referent; Kagyu objection — the referent is one.
  - P6-upaya: blessing functions as an empowerment for the ripe disciple; Sakya objection — formal empowerment is required.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the sides disagree whether the guru's blessing without formal empowerment can be the cause of Mahāmudrā realization.

## RQ-U16-4 — Is the gopīs' (and Rādhā's) love for Kṛṣṇa paramour love (parakīyā) or wedded love (svakīyā) in truth?  
`dsp:svakiya-parakiya` · skeleton

- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Rūpa (Ujjvalanīlamaṇi) and Viśvanātha Cakravartin (Ānandacandrikā): paramour love in Vraja is the summit of amorous rasa, uniquely permitted there because Kṛṣṇa brought the gopīs down to taste rasa; Viśvanātha holds it eternal.
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: Jīva Gosvāmī: the gopīs are Kṛṣṇa's own internal potencies (Brahma Saṃhitā 5.37), hence svakīyā in truth; their apparent marriage to others is Yogamāyā's arrangement in the manifest play; the Gopālacampū ends with their marriage.
- **lin:dvaitadvaita**: Nimbārka tradition: Rādhā is Kṛṣṇa's eternal consort worshipped on his left (commonly described as svakīyā).
- Candidate readings:
  - Jīva's own two-level reading (svakīyā in the unmanifest truth, parakīyā in the manifest play) — analogous to P1-level; rejected by Viśvanātha
  - Viśvanātha's reading of Jīva's svakīyā passages as written at others' wish (Locanarocanī verse on intent) — a P6-upaya-type reading; rejected by svakīyā proponents
  - P3-path: different rasika temperaments — rejected by both parties as making a truth-claim a matter of taste
- Note: not yet reconciled: the Gauḍīya tradition itself records two positions, and the parakīyā party rejects Jīva's two-level resolution.

## RQ-366 — Is consciousness reflexively aware of itself (svasaṃvedana)?  
`dsp:svasamvedana` · skeleton

- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Every cognition is aware of itself as it is aware of its object; this self-awareness is the result of the means of knowledge, and memory of the experience proves it.
- **lin:yogacara-madhyamaka**: Accepted conventionally: consciousness arises as the opposite of the inert, and its not being inert is its self-awareness; it is not an act of an agent on an object.
- **lin:prasangika**: Rejected even conventionally: mind does not see mind, as a sword's edge does not cut itself; memory is explained without self-awareness.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: self-awareness as conventional description only, never ultimately (Śāntarakṣita).
  - P2-standpoint: epistemological (pramāṇa) standpoint vs. soteriological analysis of the self.
  - P6-upaya: accepted as a device for refuting external objects, then discarded.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Prāsaṅgika refuses self-awareness even at the conventional level, so P1-level alone does not bridge the positions.

## RQ-367 — Is the validity of a cognition intrinsic (svataḥ) or extrinsic (parataḥ)?  
`dsp:svatah-paratah-pramanya` · skeleton

- **lin:nyaya**: Validity is extrinsic: it is produced by an excellence in the causes of a cognition and is known not with the cognition itself but by inference — from the success of the activity it prompts or other marks; invalidity likewise comes from defects and is known extrinsically.
- **lin:mimamsa**: Every cognition is valid by itself: validity arises from the very conditions that produce the cognition and is apprehended with it; invalidity is extrinsic, known from a defect in the causes or a later sublating cognition. A power not present in a thing by itself cannot be produced by another.
- **lin:samkhya**: Both validity and invalidity are intrinsic — as reported in later doxography.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Invalidity is intrinsic and validity extrinsic — as reported in the same doxographic scheme (Buddhist sources themselves are more differentiated).
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: Validity is known through practical activity (vyavahāra) — whether the cognition leads to the object's efficacy (Dharmakīrti, PV Pramāṇasiddhi 5, conventional).
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: There is no single rule: some cognitions (self-awareness, inference, habituated perception, cognitions of efficacy) are valid by themselves, others only extrinsically (Śāntarakṣita and Kamalaśīla, as recalled).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Mīmāṃsaka thesis may describe the default acceptance of a cognition when no doubt has arisen, the Naiyāyika thesis the justification of validity once doubt arises.
  - P2-standpoint: both distinguish the arising (utpatti) from the ascertainment (jñapti) of validity; agreement may be possible on the first and not the second.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the schools disagree about how validity is established.

## RQ-U34-03 — How did the Śvetāmbara–Digambara division arise?  
`dsp:svetambara-digambara-origin` · skeleton

- **lin:digambara**: During a twelve-year famine foreseen by Bhadrabāhu, the saṅgha went south with him and Candragupta; those who stayed in the north under Sthūlabhadra (or Rāmilla and Sthūlācārya) took up a half-cloth to beg in hard times and later a full white garment, becoming the Śvetāmbaras (at Valabhī, per Devasena, 136 years after Vikrama).
- **lin:svetambara**: The Digambaras (Boṭikas) arose from Śivabhūti, who at Rathavīrapura, 609 years after the nirvāṇa, angered over a blanket, declared the naked Jinakalpa the only true renunciation; because his sister could not go naked, his followers denied liberation to women.
- Candidate readings:
  - Both narratives agree on a famine, a dispersal of the saṅgha and a dispute over clothing; they differ on who departed from the original practice
  - Scholarly account (labelled, not a reconciliation): a gradual differentiation over several centuries rather than a single event; the Mathurā inscriptions and images show a clothed tradition by the Kuṣāṇa period
- Note: Not yet reconciled: each account makes the other the seceding group.

## RQ-U33-4 — Is the soul the same as the body, or different from it?  
`dsp:tajjiva-taccharira` · skeleton

- **lin:carvaka**: (As reported) The same: the soul is bounded by the skin; no soul can be drawn out and shown like a sword from its sheath; when the body is burnt nothing arises again.
- **lin:jainism**: Different: the soul is other than the body (Kesi to Paesi).
- **lin:early-buddhism**: Neither view is taught: 'soul is body' and 'soul is other than body' both make the holy life impossible; the Tathāgata teaches by the middle, dependent origination.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Buddhist 'middle' is itself offered as avoiding two standpoints — but it does not accept either side's claim.
  - P5-neyartha: none recorded.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the materialist and the Jain affirm opposite answers; the Buddhist declines both.

## RQ-U25-tamil-veda — Can hymns in Tamil, composed by human poets — one of them born outside the brahmin class, one a woman — have the authority of the Veda?  
`dsp:tamil-prabandham-as-veda` · skeleton

- **lin:visistadvaita**: Yes: the Tiruvāymoḻi is the essence of the four Vedas in Tamil, the 'Tamil Upaniṣad', equal in authority to the Sanskrit and revealing its heart; Nammāḻvār's four works are the four Vedas, Tirumaṅkai's six the aṅgas; the Prabandham is recited in temples as Veda.
- **lin:mimamsa**: The Veda is authorless and eternal in its word–meaning relation; human compositions (smṛti) are authoritative only as inferring a Vedic source and are set aside where they conflict with it.
- Candidate readings:
  - P8-six-marks: the Prabandham shares the Upaniṣads' purport (opening and close, repetition, result) and so carries authority as to content without being apauruṣeya in form
  - P3-path: the Tamil Veda as the scripture of those (women, śūdras) barred from Sanskrit Veda-study — same goal by another door
  - Mīmāṃsā-style: the Prabandham as smṛti rooted in the Veda (authoritative by inference), which the Śrīvaiṣṇavas would accept only in part
- Note: not yet reconciled

## RQ-371 — Is release won by Śiva's grace and worship, or by the Jina's path of self-discipline? (the Tamil Śaiva–Jain contests)  
`dsp:tamil-saiva-and-jain-contests` · skeleton

- **lin:nayanmar**: Śiva alone saves by grace; the Jain monks' austerities, nakedness and denial of the Lord and the Veda are fruitless; the Lord's name, ash and temples protect the devotee.
- **lin:jainism**: Release is gained by right faith, knowledge and conduct, by one's own effort and austerity, without a creator's grace; non-violence is the highest dharma (position as held in Jain texts; in the Śaiva sources it is reported by opponents).
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path: complementary paths for different temperaments
  - P4-stage: ethics shared, divergence in the ultimate
- Note: Not yet reconciled: a P3 reading (grace for the devotional temperament, self-discipline for the ascetic) is a candidate only.

## RQ-U14-12 — Are the Tamil hymns of the Āḻvārs (above all the Tiruvāymoḻi) Veda, equal in authority to the Sanskrit Upaniṣads?  
`dsp:tamil-veda-status` · skeleton

- **lin:visistadvaita**: Yes: the Tiruvāymoḻi is the Tamil Upaniṣad (Dramiḍopaniṣad), the Veda's essence in Tamil; the teachers comment on both as one Vedānta (ubhaya-vedānta).
- **lin:mimamsa**: As the objection is reported in Śrīvaiṣṇava works: only the authorless Sanskrit Veda is śruti; a composition by a human author, in Tamil, by one not a brahmin, can at most be a devotional smṛti.
- Candidate readings:
  - P8-six-marks: the Tamil hymns share the Upaniṣads' purport
  - P3-path: the Tamil Veda as the devotional expression of the same truth
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-373 — What does 'tat tvam asi' (Chāndogya Upaniṣad 6.8.7) teach — identity of soul and Brahman, or something else?  
`dsp:tat-tvam-asi` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: It teaches identity: 'that' (Brahman) and 'you' (the soul) are one consciousness; the words, stripped of their incompatible parts (by implication that drops part and keeps part), point to pure consciousness, as 'this is that Devadatta' identifies one person.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: It teaches co-ordinate predication: both words denote Brahman — 'that' as the cause of the world, 'you' as having the individual soul as its body; the soul is a mode of Brahman, one with it as body with embodied self, yet distinct.
- **lin:dvaita**: It teaches difference: read 'sa ātmā atat tvam asi', 'you are not that', repeated nine times with examples that show difference (the bird tied to its post, honey from many trees, rivers in the sea); or 'aitadātmya' as being under the Lord's control and 'tat tvam asi' as dependence or likeness. Identity contradicts experience ('no one experiences I am omniscient, lord of all') and the conceit of it is the asuras' error.
- Candidate readings:
  - {"principle": "P2-standpoint", "reading": "Identity (of consciousness), inseparability (of body and embodied) and difference (of substance) are each true from a standpoint."}
  - {"principle": "P8-six-marks", "reading": "Apply the six marks of purport to the chapter as a whole to decide its purport — a method all three sides accept but apply with opposite results."}
- Note: Not yet reconciled: all three sides read the same passage with the same exegetical rules and reach opposite purports.

## RQ-U35-tattvartha-bhasya — Is the Tattvārthabhāṣya Umāsvāti's own commentary on the Tattvārthasūtra?  
`dsp:tattvartha-bhasya-authorship` · skeleton

- **lin:svetambara**: Yes: the bhāṣya is Umāsvāti's own (svopajña); its praśasti names the author and his lineage; Siddhasenagaṇi and Haribhadra comment on it as such.
- **lin:digambara**: No: the Digambaras follow the sūtra text fixed in the Sarvārthasiddhi and do not accept the bhāṣya as the author's.
- Candidate readings:
  - A historical question of authorship to which no reconciliation principle applies; record the tradition's accounts and the scholarly account separately (many scholars accept the bhāṣya as auto-commentary).

## RQ-375 — Are the ten practices of the Vajjian monks (including accepting gold and silver) allowable?  
`dsp:ten-points-vesali` · skeleton

- **lin:mahasanghika**: (The Vajjian monks of Vesālī, as reported by the Theravāda Vinaya) Ten practices are allowable, among them carrying salt in a horn, eating when the shadow is two fingers past noon, and accepting gold and silver.
- **lin:theravada**: (Yasa, Revata, Sabbakāmī and the council of seven hundred) None of the ten points accords with the Dhamma and discipline.
- Candidate readings:
  - See U38's dsp:cause-of-the-first-schism for the Mahāsāṅghika and Sarvāstivāda accounts.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the schools remember the Second Council and the first schism differently.

## RQ-376 — Are saving life and giving to the unrestrained part of spiritual dharma? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:terapanthi-daya-dana` · skeleton

- **lin:terapanthi**: No: dharma is only what stops karmic inflow and fosters self-restraint; saving a being's bodily life by force or feeding those without vows is worldly (laukika) duty, not spiritual (lokottara) dharma, and may even support further harm.
- **lin:sthanakavasi**: Yes: compassion (anukampā, dayā) that protects life and giving to the needy are religious merit and belong to dharma; the Jina's first teaching is non-harm and care for beings.
- **lin:murtipujaka**: Yes, with the Sthānakavāsīs: compassion and giving are meritorious.
- Note: Bhikṣu's own distinction between worldly and supramundane dharma is a distinction of level: his opponents affirm compassion as merit (puṇya) and good conduct, which he does not forbid as social duty; he denies only that it is the spiritual dharma that stops karma.

## RQ-377 — Do the paths taught by the brahmins of the three Vedas lead to union with Brahmā?  
`dsp:tevijja-union-with-brahma` · skeleton

- **lin:vedic-srauta**: The various paths taught by the brahmins versed in the three Vedas — Addhariya, Tittiriya, Chandoka, Bavhārija — all lead to union with Brahmā, as many paths lead to one village.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: None of the brahmins or their teachers has seen Brahmā; their teaching is like a line of blind men; the path to companionship with Brahmā is the four divine abidings of a mind without hatred, like Brahmā's.
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path: the Upaniṣadic and Buddhist traditions both teach companionship with Brahmā through inner qualities; the dispute concerns which path — but the Buddhist side treats the Brahmā world as within saṃsāra, not as liberation
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the Buddha denies that the three Vedas' paths lead to Brahmā and substitutes the brahmavihāras.

## RQ-378 — Is there a real whole (avayavin) over and above the aggregation of atoms? (YBh 1.43)  
`dsp:the-whole-and-its-parts` · sourced

- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Vyāsa: the whole exists — one, great or small, touchable, active, impermanent; ordinary dealings are carried out with it; it is the object of nirvitarkā coalescence; otherwise almost all cognition would be false and even right knowledge would have no object.
- **None**: The unnamed opponent: the aggregation of atoms is not a real thing (avastuka) and the subtle cause is imperceptible, so there is no whole.
- Candidate readings:
  - difficult passage (confidence low); the reading of the opponent's position is the minimal sense
- Note: not yet reconciled: recorded as an argument within the text; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-379 — What happens in erroneous cognition (khyāti)?  
`dsp:theory-of-error` · skeleton

- **lin:nyaya**: Misplaced apprehension (anyathākhyāti): a real property located elsewhere is apprehended as qualifying the thing before one (silver in mother-of-pearl).
- **lin:prabhakara-mimamsa**: Non-apprehension (akhyāti): two cognitions — a perception and a memory — are not distinguished; no cognition is strictly erroneous.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Apprehension of the indeterminable (anirvacanīyakhyāti): the silver is neither real nor unreal but an appearance produced by ignorance.
- **lin:yogacara**: Self-apprehension (ātmakhyāti): cognition's own form is taken as external.
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: Advaita's account presupposes levels of reality that Nyāya does not recognize; within the empirical level the accounts overlap in treating error as a presentation of what is not there.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-380 — Are the three vows (individual liberation, bodhisattva, mantra) one in essence, or distinct systems to be kept separately? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:three-vows-one-essence` · skeleton

- **lin:drikung-kagyu**: The three vows are one in essence and differ only in aspect; they are kept together as one discipline, the higher including the lower (the 'single intent').
- **lin:sakya**: The three vows differ in how they are taken, kept and restored and must not be confused; Sakya Paṇḍita's Discrimination of the Three Vows sets out their distinct rules and criticizes teachings that blur them.
- Note: From the standpoint of essence (the motivation and view that the vows serve) they are one; from the standpoint of mode (ritual, rules, restoration) they are distinct — the Gongchig itself grants distinct aspects.

## RQ-U34-07 — Are there three categories — soul, non-soul and 'no-soul'?  
`dsp:trairasika` · skeleton

- **lin:svetambara**: The orthodox teaching (Śrīgupta): reality is soul and non-soul only; the twitching tail of a lizard is part of the soul's field, not a third kind.
- **lin:svetambara**: Rohagupta (Ṣaḍulūka), as reported by the Jain tradition: there are three heaps — soul, non-soul and no-soul; he refused to recant and, the tradition says, went on to found the Vaiśeṣika system of six categories.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: 'no-soul' as a name for a separated part of a living body considered as a mode, not a substance
- Note: Not yet reconciled; recorded as the Jain tradition reports it (the schismatic's own words are not preserved).

## RQ-382 — Is the cause of birth the nenbutsu (with the three minds) or shinjin?  
`dsp:true-cause-of-birth` · skeleton

- **lin:jodo-shu**: The nenbutsu is the rightly determined act and cause of birth; the three minds accompany it.
- **lin:jodo-shinshu**: Shinjin is the true cause (shinjin shōin); the nenbutsu is the expression of gratitude (shōmyō hōon).
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: from the practice side the nenbutsu, from the entrusting side shinjin — Hōnen's 'believe with one calling, practise with the whole life'
  - Shinran's own claim that the name and shinjin are inseparable (the name as the cause, shinjin as the condition, or the reverse)
- Note: Not yet reconciled between the schools.

## RQ-383 — Should one aspire to Maitreya's Tuṣita or to Amitābha's Sukhāvatī? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:tusita-or-sukhavati` · skeleton

- **lin:faxiang**: (Aspirants to Tuṣita, as reported) Maitreya is the next buddha; being born in Tuṣita one hears him and descends with him; the ten goods suffice.
- **lin:pure-land**: Tuṣita is in the desire realm, a place of retrogression and sensual attachment, without a vow to receive beings; in Sukhāvatī all are non-retrogressive and the vow and light embrace reciters.
- Note: Both are Mahāyāna aspirations to be near a buddha; the Pure Land texts rank them by ease and non-retrogression rather than denying Tuṣita's value.

## RQ-U14-01 — What divides the northern (Vaṭakalai) and southern (Teṅkalai) schools of Śrīvaiṣṇavism — the 'eighteen differences' (aṣṭādaśa-bheda)?  
`dsp:vadakalai-tenkalai` · skeleton

- **lin:vadakalai**: Surrender is a means the soul performs once, with its limbs, and the occasion of the Lord's grace ('the young monkey clings'); Śrī is all-pervading, of the Lord's rank, and with him the means and goal; the Lord overlooks faults rather than relishing them; scriptural duties bind the surrendered as the Lord's command, and deliberate offences need expiation; kaivalya is not permanent.
- **lin:tenkalai**: The Lord alone is the means; surrender is not a means but acceptance fitting the soul's dependence ('the kitten is carried'); grace is causeless; Śrī is the mediator, not the means; the Lord relishes his devotees' faults; duties are kept as service and example, not as means or under threat; the teacher's regard saves; kaivalya is permanent.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Teṅkalai speak from the divine standpoint (the Lord alone saves), the Vaṭakalai from the seeker's (surrender is what the seeker does)
  - P4-stage: the Teṅkalai stress what fits the utterly helpless; the Vaṭakalai what scripture enjoins on the qualified
  - shared ground: both hold the Lord as siddhopāya, surrender once, no last remembrance needed, release at the end of the body, eternal service
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the schools disagree on whether the soul's surrender is a means and on Śrī's rank; see the sub-disputes, some partially reconciled.

## RQ-385 — Which system — the Vedic Vaikhānasa or the tantric Pāñcarātra — is the proper basis of Viṣṇu temple worship, and must a Vaiṣṇava receive the branding initiation?  
`dsp:vaikhanasa-pancaratra` · skeleton

- **lin:vaikhanasa**: The Vaikhānasa Sūtra, composed by Vikhanas (Nārāyaṇa/Brahmā), is the first and best of sūtras; its worship is wholly Vedic; Vaikhānasas are Vaiṣṇavas from the womb through the viṣṇubali rite and need no branding; its followers are the highest.
- **lin:pancaratra**: The Pāñcarātra is the Lord's own word, governing the chief self-manifest shrines (Śrīraṅgam, Melkote, Kāñcī), with the five sacraments as the mark of a Vaiṣṇava; only Veṅkaṭādri is excepted.
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path: two valid ritual paths to the same Viṣṇu, suited to hereditary Vedic eligibility (Vaikhānasa) and to initiatory eligibility (Pāñcarātra).
  - P4-stage: the Pādma's claim that Pāñcarātra eligibility comes 'at the end' of Mīmāṃsā learning is one side's framing of adhikāra.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: each community holds its own system to be the better-founded.

## RQ-386 — Is inference a valid means of knowledge?  
`dsp:validity-of-inference` · skeleton

- **lin:carvaka**: No (as reported by opponents): only perception is valid; pervasion cannot be established — perception cannot survey all cases, inference would presuppose inference, and testimony is itself inferential; Jayarāśi's scepticism extends even to perception.
- **lin:nyaya**: Yes: the alleged deviations of marks (river swelling from damming etc.) involve different marks; the nāstika who uses an example has already given up his position, and one who refutes others must use inference.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: some later Cārvākas (e.g. Purandara, as reported by Kamalaśīla) accepted inference about the perceptible world and rejected only inference to the supersensible — narrowing the disagreement to the unseen (adṛṣṭa, the other world, Īśvara).
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-387 — Is the Pāñcarātra a valid scripture, and does the Brahma Sūtra refute it?  
`dsp:validity-of-pancaratra` · skeleton

- **lin:mimamsa**: The Pāñcarātra is invalid: not rooted in the Veda, practised by Sātvatas/Bhāgavatas who are a low temple-serving class, classed by smṛti with heretical systems, and refuted by Bādarāyaṇa.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: The worship of Vāsudeva by the five daily practices is acceptable, but the doctrine that the soul (Saṅkarṣaṇa) is born from Vāsudeva is refuted, and the system shows contradiction and opposition to the Veda (Śāṇḍilya turning to it after not finding the highest good in the Vedas).
- **lin:pancaratra**: The Pāñcarātra is fully valid, spoken by the Lord; the Bhāgavatas are brahmins; the sūtras 42–43 state an objection that 44–45 answer, since the vyūhas are Brahman itself and the Pāñcarātra denies the origination of the soul.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: if the vyūhas are read as forms of the one Lord (Rāmānuja) rather than as the origination of souls (Śaṅkara's report), the Advaitin objection loses its object; Śaṅkara already accepts the worship portion.
  - P6-upaya: the tradition itself holds different scriptures suited to different devotees.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the sides read the same sūtras and the same vyūha doctrine differently.

## RQ-388 — Is varṇa (and eligibility for austerity and teaching) fixed by birth, or made by conduct and qualities?  
`dsp:varna-by-birth-or-conduct-epic` · skeleton

- **lin:epic-teaching**: Birth and nature: the varṇas' duties arise from their nature and one should not abandon the work born with one (BhG 18.41–48; 3.35); service is the śūdra's dharma (MBh 12.60.27–28; 5.40.26); Vidura, born of a śūdra womb, will not teach beyond this (5.41.5); in the Rāmāyaṇa the śūdra Śambūka's austerity is held to cause a child's death and Rāma kills him (7.74–76).
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Conduct and qualities: one in whom truth, generosity, forbearance, good conduct, non-cruelty, restraint and compassion are seen is a brāhmaṇa (3.177.16); the whole world is of Brahman and came to varṇa by actions (12.181.10); the varṇas were made by guṇa and action (BhG 4.13); knowledge may be taken even from a śūdra (12.306.85); a meat-seller teaches a brāhmaṇa (3.198–206) and a merchant an ascetic (12.253–256); the wise see the same in a brāhmaṇa and a dog-eater (BhG 5.18); women, vaiśyas and śūdras too reach the highest goal (9.32).
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: sameness in the self and in the Lord (BhG 5.18; 9.29) at the ultimate level, varṇa duties at the conventional level
  - guṇa-karma reading of BhG 4.13 (varṇa by qualities and work) against the birth reading of the classical commentators, who take svabhāva as formed by past lives
  - bhakti reading: devotion and refuge open liberation to all regardless of birth (BhG 9.32; 18.66), while social duties remain
  - yuga reading (Rāmāyaṇa 7.74): eligibility for austerity differs by age of the world
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the epics record both a birth-based order of duties and eligibility and repeated teachings that conduct and qualities make varṇa and that liberation is open to all.

## RQ-U01-04 — Are the four varṇas given by birth from the cosmic Person (RV 10.90.12)?  
`dsp:varna-origin-purusa-sukta` · sourced

- **lin:vedic-srauta**: The Puruṣa Sūkta: the brāhmaṇa was the Person's mouth, the rājanya his arms, the vaiśya his thighs, the śūdra born from his feet — the order of society belongs to the cosmic sacrifice.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: The Buddha mocks the brahmins' claim to be 'born from Brahmā's mouth' when they are seen born from women (Aggañña Sutta), and teaches that one is a brahmin not by birth but by action (Vāseṭṭha Sutta).
- **lin:vedic-srauta**: Within the Vedic literature, the seers accepted Kavaṣa Ailūṣa, 'son of a slave woman', when the waters and gods showed him favour (Aitareya Brāhmaṇa 2.19, low confidence).
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level: the Puruṣa hymn speaks of the order of the manifest world (conventional), not of the worth of the self (the hymn also says the Person is all this).
  - P2-standpoint: cosmic-functional (the body of society) vs personal-ethical (conduct) standpoints.
  - The traditions themselves (Buddhist, and the bhakti and Siddha critics) reject any reading that keeps birth as the criterion; the Dharmaśāstras reject readings that drop it.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-390 — Is the Yoga smṛti refuted by the Vedānta ('etena yogaḥ pratyuktaḥ', Brahma Sūtra 2.1.3)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:vedanta-on-yoga-smrti` · skeleton

- **lin:vedanta**: Brahma Sūtra 2.1.3 declares that Yoga is refuted by the same reasoning as Sāṃkhya; Śaṅkara explains that Yoga is rejected where it teaches an independent pradhāna and a plurality of selves contrary to śruti, while yoga as a discipline and means is accepted.
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Yoga holds a real prakṛti and many puruṣas (YS 2.17–25, 2.22); commentators answer that Vyāsa's Brahma Sūtras refuted the independence and reality claimed for pradhāna and a real difference of consciousness, not yoga as meditative cultivation, which the Gītā holds higher (Nārāyaṇatīrtha).
- Note: Both Śaṅkara's reading of BS 2.1.3 and the late Yoga commentators accept yoga as a path of practice while leaving the metaphysical disagreement standing (P3).

## RQ-391 — Is the killing prescribed in Vedic ritual an injury (hiṃsā) that brings demerit?  
`dsp:vedic-ritual-killing-and-ahimsa` · sourced

- **lin:dharmasastra**: No: the injury prescribed by the Veda is non-injury, for dharma shone forth from the Veda (MDh 5.44); animals were created for sacrifice.
- **lin:dharmasastra**: Abstention is better: meat cannot be had without injury, killing does not lead to heaven (MDh 5.48); eating meat is natural activity, abstention bears great fruit (MDh 5.56).
- **lin:samkhya**: The Vedic means are impure (aviśuddhi) because they involve killing; their fruit is also perishable (Sāṃkhyakārikā 2 as its commentators explain).
- **lin:jainism**: Injury to living beings is never dharma, whether in ritual or not; the Śramaṇa traditions reject animal sacrifice.
- Candidate readings:
  - Mīmāṃsā: a specific injunction removes the general prohibition for its own case (no demerit)
  - Sāṃkhya: both apply — the rite yields its fruit and the killing its demerit
  - P4-stage reading of MDh 5.56: ritual meat is permitted at the level of pravṛtti, abstention is the higher nivṛtti
  - yuga-dharma: later authorities forbid animal (cow) sacrifice in the Kali age
  - Brāhmaṇa substitution: the sacrificial essence passed from animals into rice and barley (ŚB 1.2.3.6–9)
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U23-vedic-status-of-tantra — Are the Śākta tantras Vedic and authoritative?  
`dsp:vedic-status-of-tantra` · skeleton

- **lin:samaya-srividya**: Only the five Śubhāgamas, which teach the Vedic Samaya way, are fully authoritative for the twice-born; the sixty-four tantras aim at worldly powers, deceive even the learned, are far from the Vedic path and meant for others; the eight Candrakalā tantras are mixed.
- **lin:kaula-srividya**: Śrīvidyā is itself taught in śruti — the Tripurā, Bhāvanā, Kaula and Bahvṛca Upaniṣads — and the Gāyatrī is the Śrīvidyā; the Kalpasūtra calls itself a great Upaniṣad whose study makes one the performer of all sacrifices; the vidyā is hidden within all schools while the Veda is public. Bhāskararāya defends this with Mīmāṃsā reasoning.
- **lin:bhatta-mimamsa**: Texts not accepted by those who know the three Vedas — including the Pāśupata and Pāñcarātra āgamas — have no authority as sources of dharma.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage: all sides already distribute texts by eligibility (adhikāra); authority could be read as relative to the qualified practitioner.
  - P7-arthavāda: the Kalpasūtra's claim to be a great Upaniṣad and the Saundaryalaharī's disparagement of the sixty-four tantras may be praise and blame rather than doctrine.
  - P5-neyārtha: Lakṣmīdhara's classification may be provisional teaching for Vedic householders.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the sides disagree about which texts are Vedic, not only about who may use them.

## RQ-393 — Where the Visuddhimagga departs from the Vimuttimagga (e.g. on the number of temperaments), which account holds? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:vimuttimagga-visuddhimagga` · skeleton

- **lin:abhayagiri**: Fourteen kinds of behaviour, including mixed types (Vimuttimagga, as reported).
- **lin:mahavihara**: Six temperaments in brief; counting combinations would be endless (Visuddhimagga, which cites the other view as 'some').
- Note: A difference of counting: both recognize the same basic temperaments and their mixtures.

## RQ-394 — May one kill in battle as one's own dharma — and what of non-violence as the highest dharma?  
`dsp:violence-and-svadharma` · skeleton

- **lin:epic-teaching**: Arjuna: killing kinsmen is sin, destroys family dharma and leads to hell; it is better to be killed unresisting or to live by begging (BhG 1.31–47; 2.5).
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Kṛṣṇa: the self neither slays nor is slain; for a kṣatriya nothing is better than a righteous war; acting without ego one does not slay even in slaying; the warriors are already slain by Time (BhG 2.18–38; 11.32–34; 18.17).
- **lin:epic-teaching**: Non-violence is the highest dharma (MBh 1.11.12; 13.116; Tulādhāra 12.253–256; Vicakhnu 12.257); Satyavat argues against even capital punishment (12.259).
- **lin:epic-teaching**: The meat-seller: no one lives without harming beings — even farmers and non-violent ascetics — so one's own inherited work done rightly is dharma (MBh 3.199).
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage (adhikāra): the kṣatriya's duty belongs to his station; the ideal of non-violence to renunciants and ekāntins
  - P1-level: 'it neither slays nor is slain' (2.19) at the ultimate level, while the conventional duty of the warrior stands
  - action without ego and attachment does not bind (18.17)
  - allegorical reading of the battle as the inner struggle (Gandhi; recent) — rejected by the classical commentators, who read the war literally
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the epic records both the kṣatriya's duty to fight a righteous war and non-violence as the highest dharma.

## RQ-395 — Does liṅga-initiation abolish caste and pollution distinctions among devotees? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:virasaiva-caste-and-pollution` · skeleton

- **lin:sarana-vacana**: Yes: among liṅga-wearers there is no caste — Basava is the son of a cobbler's house and every devotee is 'ours'; the five pollutions do not apply; the intercaste marriage of Haraḷayya's son and Madhuvarasa's daughter enacted this.
- **lin:pancacarya**: Devotion, not birth, makes the bhakta; the māheśvara drops jāti-dharmas opposed to the liṅga; at union brāhmaṇa and outcaste are alike and the realized are untouched by pollution — yet the practitioner eats with twice-born of his own jāti on the path, and the ordering of varṇa and āśrama is twofold (Śiva's and Brahmā's).
- **lin:dharmasastra**: Varṇa and the rules of purity bind all; marriage across varṇa in the pratiloma direction is forbidden — the objection of the orthodox in the Kalyāṇa narrative (as reported in Vīraśaiva hagiography).
- Note: Within the SSM, commensality rules for practitioners (9.29) and equal vision for the realized (14.19–20) are assigned to different stages by the text itself (5.22–23); between the vacana strand, the SSM and dharmaśāstra orthodoxy the question is not yet reconciled.

## RQ-396 — Did the Vīraśaiva/Liṅgāyata tradition begin with Basava and the śaraṇas of twelfth-century Kalyāṇa, or is it a beginningless Śaiva dharma renewed in every age by the five ācāryas?  
`dsp:virasaiva-origins` · skeleton

- **lin:pancacarya**: Vīraśaiva dharma is anādi: Śiva taught it in the later portion of the Āgamas and to the Goddess and Nandin; Reṇuka emerged from the Someśvara liṅga at Kollipāki and taught Agastya; the five ācāryas appear from liṅgas in every yuga and founded the five pīṭhas. Basava is a great devotee and reviver within this stream.
- **lin:sarana-vacana**: The tradition was inaugurated by Basava with Allama Prabhu, Cennabasava, Akka Mahādēvi and the śaraṇas in Kalyāṇa; the vacanas are its founding scripture; the Pañcācārya narratives and the Sanskrit Āgamic framing come later.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Pañcācārya account speaks from the standpoint of the dharma's eternal transmission, the śaraṇa account from that of its historical renewal — each side rejects this as diminishing its account.
  - Two streams — a Śaiva ācārya/maṭha stream and the Kalyāṇa devotional movement — later merged (a scholarly hypothesis, labeled; it also invokes a Kālāmukha background, see brw:kalamukha-to-virasaiva).
  - Basava as reviver of an older dharma (the Pañcācārya reading) — rejected by the śaraṇa strand.
- Note: not yet reconciled: the two accounts differ on a historical question (who began the tradition and when) to which the eight principles do not apply as a hermeneutic

## RQ-397 — Is Śiva to be worshipped in temples and installed images (the fixed), or in the personal liṅga and the living devotee (the moving)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:virasaiva-sthavara-jangama` · skeleton

- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: Installed liṅgas in temples are proper seats of Śiva's worship for the community (temple worship as taught in the Āgamas).
- **lin:sarana-vacana**: The fixed shall perish, the moving endures; the body is the temple; to feed the stone snake and drive off the living devotee is hypocrisy.
- **lin:pancacarya**: Graded: palm-worship of the iṣṭaliṅga excels pedestal worship; the inner liṅga excels stone liṅgas and clinging only to the outer is delusion; yet the nirmālya of fixed liṅgas is not to be used and fixed liṅgas are to be protected.
- Note: Within the Vīraśaiva texts the forms of worship are graded by stage (outer liṅga for all, inner for the advanced; 6.48; 15.44); vis-à-vis Āgamic temple Śaivism the question is not yet reconciled.

## RQ-398 — What authority do the Veda and the Āgamas have for Vīraśaivas? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:virasaiva-veda-agama-authority` · skeleton

- **lin:pancacarya**: The Veda is the chief authority; the Śaiva Siddhānta Āgama is 'made of the Veda', one with it in meaning and as authoritative; the Vīraśaiva doctrine is taught in the Āgamas' later portion and agrees with Veda and Vedānta — though sacrifices and austerities without Śiva-devotion are fruitless and not worth a fraction of liṅga-worship.
- **lin:sarana-vacana**: Scripture is to be tested by experience: Basava will put the Veda to the touchstone, fetter the śāstras and cut the nose of the Āgamas; the śaraṇas' vacanas and anubhava are the authority.
- **lin:aradhya-saiva**: The Ārādhyas wear the liṅga and worship Śiva exclusively while keeping the sacred thread and Vedic rites.
- Note: Within the SSM, reverence for the Veda and the subordination of Vedic rites to liṅga-worship are reconciled by stage (5.22–23; 9.39–40; 15.30–36); between the SSM and the vacanas the question is not yet reconciled.

## RQ-399 — Is the self the body (as seen in its reflection)? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:virocana-body-as-self` · sourced

- **lin:upanisadic**: Virocana, for the asuras: the bodily self alone is to be worshipped and served here; by serving it one wins both worlds — hence the dead are adorned with food, clothes and ornaments.
- **lin:upanisadic**: Prajāpati's final teaching as received by Indra: the body is mortal, the base of the immortal bodiless self, which is free from evil, old age, death and sorrow and appears in its own form in the highest light.
- Note: The body-self is the first of four successive teachings given to a student; Virocana stopped at the first stage, Indra went on to the last.

## RQ-U01-01 — The rivalry of the priestly houses of Viśvāmitra and Vasiṣṭha  
`dsp:visvamitra-vasistha-rivalry` · sourced

- **lin:vedic-srauta**: Viśvāmitra's house: Viśvāmitra is priest of the Bharatas and of Sudās (RV 3.33, 3.53); the closing imprecations of 3.53 are traditionally taken as aimed at Vasiṣṭha, and Vasiṣṭha's descendants do not recite them.
- **lin:vedic-srauta**: Vasiṣṭha's house: Vasiṣṭha is Sudās's priest in the Battle of the Ten Kings (7.18, 7.33) and swears 'may I die today if I am a sorcerer', against one who falsely called him so (7.104.15).
- Candidate readings:
  - The epic account (Rāmāyaṇa, Bāla-kāṇḍa) resolves it by Viśvāmitra's tapas and Vasiṣṭha's final acknowledgement of him as brahmarṣi — a P4-stage reading (change of standing through attainment).
  - The two houses served one king at different times (a historical, non-doctrinal reading).
  - The attributions of the imprecations and of the sorcery charge are commentarial and may not concern Vasiṣṭha at all.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: a rivalry of lineages, remembered differently by each house and by the epics.

## RQ-U01-03 — Is the Vrātya an exalted cosmic figure or an outsider to be brought into the Vedic fold?  
`dsp:vratya-status` · sourced

- **lin:sakha-saunaka**: The Atharvaveda's Vrātya book exalts the Vrātya: he stirs Prajāpati, becomes Mahādeva and Īśāna, and a king should honour the knowing Vrātya above himself.
- **lin:vedic-srauta**: The Sāmaveda Brāhmaṇa tradition prescribes the vrātyastoma rites by which vrātyas — people living outside Vedic rules — are admitted to Vedic society.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Atharvaveda speaks of an ideal, cosmic Vrātya 'who knows thus'; the Brāhmaṇa of actual vrātya groups.
  - P7-arthavāda: the exaltation of the Vrātya is praise of the knower, not a description of vrātya groups.
  - Commentators (e.g. Sāyaṇa) take the Vrātya of AVŚ 15 to be a learned, revered figure or the Supreme (recalled, low confidence).
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-402 — What is mastery of the senses (indriya-jaya)? (YBh 2.55)  
`dsp:what-is-mastery-of-senses` · sourced

- **None**: Non-addiction to sound and other objects (attachment is addiction, since it throws one off the better).
- **None**: Contact with sound and the rest at one's own wish; or knowledge of them empty of pleasure and pain when attachment and aversion are absent.
- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Jaigīṣavya: non-engagement (apratipatti) through one-pointedness of mind; Vyāsa adds that the mastery following sense-withdrawal is supreme because the senses, stilled with the mind, need no other effort-made means.
- Candidate readings:
  - four views are listed in one bhāṣya passage; Vyāsa gives Jaigīṣavya's as the last, and says the yogin's mastery is supreme
- Note: not yet reconciled: recorded as an argument within the text; candidate readings listed.

## RQ-403 — What sees — the eye, consciousness, or neither? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:what-sees` · skeleton

- **lin:vaibhasika**: The eye sees, as the support of consciousness, since forms behind a wall are not seen.
- **lin:sautrantika**: Neither: visual consciousness arises from eye and form; 'the eye sees' and 'consciousness cognizes' are conventions over a mere set of dharmas without activity.
- Note: The Sautrāntika itself accepts 'the eye sees' as a valid convention; disagreement remains on whether it is also true in the ultimate sense.

## RQ-404 — May one renounce directly, at any stage of life, or only after passing through the householder's duties? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:when-to-renounce` · sourced

- **lin:sannyasa**: Renounce on the very day dispassion arises, from studentship, household or forest; true renunciation is inner union of jīva and Self.
- **lin:dharmasastra**: Renunciation comes in due order, after the three debts (study, offspring, sacrifice) are paid; seeking liberation before paying them leads downward.
- Note: The texts tie the timing to the renouncer's qualification: in due order for most, at once for one of intense dispassion; and the Maitreya distinguishes outer ritual renunciation from inner renunciation.

## RQ-405 — Which 'Bhāgavata' is the Mahāpurāṇa of the lists - the Śrīmad (Viṣṇu) Bhāgavata or the Devī Bhāgavata?  
`dsp:which-bhagavata-is-the-mahapurana` · sourced

- **lin:puranic**: The Śrīmad Bhāgavata is the Mahāpurāṇa: it begins with the Gāyatrī-marked verse, contains Vṛtra's slaying and has 18,000 verses (Matsya 53.20-22), calls itself the essence of all Vedānta and the fruit of the Veda-tree.
- **lin:sakta**: The Devī Bhāgavata is the Mahāpurāṇa 'Bhāgavata'; the Viṣṇu Bhāgavata is an Upapurāṇa (some polemicists alleged it was composed by Vopadeva).
- Candidate readings:
  - P3-path: each is the 'Bhāgavata' for its community of worshippers (Vaiṣṇava, Śākta) - both traditions would reject this as evading the question.
  - The lists' 'Bhāgavata' predates both extant texts and the title was later claimed by each.
  - Scholarly metadata: modern scholars date both later than the lists' earliest layers.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: both sides apply the same Matsya definition to different texts; no principle decides identity of a title.

## RQ-406 — Which deity is the supreme Brahman — Śiva, Viṣṇu/Nārāyaṇa, or the Goddess? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:which-deity-is-supreme` · sourced

- **lin:pasupata**: Śiva/Rudra is supreme: he alone was in the beginning; as Śarabha he subdued Nṛsiṃha; Śiva alone is to be meditated on.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: Nārāyaṇa is supreme: Brahmā and Rudra are born from him; Nārāyaṇa alone existed in the beginning.
- **lin:srividya**: The Goddess is supreme: she alone was in the beginning and gave birth to Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Rudra.
- Note: The identity Upaniṣads read the names as one reality (Viṣṇu the heart of Śiva, Śiva of Viṣṇu), and each sectarian text's supremacy claim can be read as praise of the chosen deity.

## RQ-407 — Which aim of life is foremost — dharma, artha, kāma, their combination, or liberation? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:which-purusartha-is-foremost` · sourced

- **lin:dharmasastra**: The combination, with dharma weightiest: the settled view is that the good is the triad (MDh 2.224); dharmaśāstra prevails over arthaśāstra (YS 2.21); self-knowledge leads to the supreme good (MDh 12.85).
- **lin:arthasastra**: Artha is foremost, for dharma and kāma are rooted in artha (Kauṭilya).
- **lin:kamasastra**: The three are to be pursued in balance over the stages of life, each earlier weightier, artha first for a king; kāma needs its own śāstra.
- **lin:carvaka**: Lokāyatikas (as reported by Vātsyāyana): dharma should not be practised, its fruit being future and doubtful — better a pigeon today than a peacock tomorrow.
- Note: The texts themselves distribute the aims over the stages of life and over active and quiescent paths (Kāmasūtra 1.2.2–4; MDh 12.88–90).

## RQ-U39-3 — Which turning of the dharma-wheel is of definitive meaning (nītārtha): the teaching of emptiness (second) or the 'well-distinguished' teaching of the three natures and of buddha-nature (third)?  
`dsp:which-turning-is-definitive` · skeleton

- **lin:madhyamaka**: The sūtras teaching emptiness, the signless, no self and no being are definitive; those teaching self, a being, or a truly existent base are provisional (Akṣayamati); the tathāgatagarbha is emptiness taught with an intention (Laṅkāvatāra).
- **lin:yogacara**: The first two turnings are surpassable and provisional; the third, which distinguishes the three natures and three naturelessnesses, is unsurpassed and definitive (Saṃdhinirmocana ch. 7).
- **lin:jonang**: The third turning's tathāgatagarbha sūtras and the Ratnagotravibhāga are definitive: the ultimate (dharmakāya, buddha-nature) is empty of what is other than it — the adventitious stains — and not empty of its own qualities (shentong).
- Candidate readings:
  - Rimé reading (Jamgön Kongtrul, U48): the second turning is definitive about the emptiness of the relative, the third about the ultimate — both definitive in their own sphere.
  - Stage reading (P4): the turnings addressed disciples of different capacities (Saṃdhinirmocana itself).
  - Madhyamaka reading: third-turning statements are provisional (P5) but purposeful (RGV 1.156-157, P6).
- Note: Not yet reconciled: P5 is the Buddhist traditions' own hermeneutic and all sides accept it, but it names the distinction without settling its application.

## RQ-409 — What makes one a brāhmaṇa — birth, body, learning, action, or realization of the Self? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:who-is-a-brahmana` · sourced

- **lin:upanisadic**: Not jīva, body, birth, learning, action or dharmic deeds; the brāhmaṇa is one who has realized the non-dual Self and is free of faults; caste belongs to convention.
- **lin:dharmasastra**: Varṇa is by birth, with conduct and rites determining standing within it.
- Note: The Nirālamba itself places caste in convention (vyavahāra) and the true brāhmaṇa at the level of realized truth, so both can hold on different levels.

## RQ-U01-05 — Who may learn and recite the Veda?  
`dsp:who-may-learn-the-veda` · sourced

- **lin:vedic-srauta**: The recitation lineages transmit the Veda to the initiated (upanayana) twice-born; the Purāṇa says the triple Veda is not for the hearing of women, śūdras and lapsed twice-born, for whom Vyāsa composed the Mahābhārata (Bhāgavata 1.4.25); the Brahma Sūtra denies śūdras the right to Vedic study (1.3.34–38).
- **lin:vedic-srauta**: The Saṃhitās themselves record women seers (ṛṣikās) of hymns, a maiden's brahmacarya (AVŚ 11.5.18), a seer 'son of a slave woman' accepted by the gods (AB 2.19), and a verse speaking auspicious speech to brahmin, rājanya, śūdra, ārya, one's own and the stranger (VS 26.2).
- **lin:arya-samaj**: Dayānanda (recent): Vedic study and initiation are open to all, women and men of every birth, citing VS 26.2.
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage (adhikāra): the tradition's own frame — different texts and practices (Veda; epic and Purāṇa; the divine name) for different eligibilities — which the restricted parties and later reformers reject when eligibility is by birth.
  - Historical: women's Vedic study (brahmavādinīs) is attested in the Saṃhitās and early sūtras and was later restricted.
  - VS 26.2 read narrowly (addressing all with auspicious words at the rite) or broadly (teaching the Veda to all).
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-411 — Who is eligible to renounce?  
`dsp:who-may-renounce` · sourced

- **lin:sannyasa**: Restrictive: lists of the ineligible (the impotent, fallen, deformed, heretics, the unlearned and others); the ineligible may renounce only when at the point of death.
- **lin:sannyasa**: Open: anyone dispassionate — with or without vows or fires — may renounce; eligibility is having found desires like vomit.
- Candidate readings:
  - {"reading": "P4-stage / P1-level: rules for ritual (krama) renunciation vs the knower's inner renunciation open to anyone dispassionate", "rests_on": ["tea:maitreya-upanisad:2.17"]}
  - {"reading": "The Nāradaparivrājaka's own clause that the ineligible who have renounced are still fit for the great saying", "rests_on": ["tea:naradaparivrajaka-upanisad:3.1-11-eligibility"]}
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the restrictive lists govern ritual renunciation, the open statements define the renouncer by inner dispassion; whether one limits the other is not settled here.

## RQ-412 — Is a whole (avayavin) something real over and above its parts?  
`dsp:whole-and-parts` · skeleton

- **lin:nyaya**: Yes: the whole is a new substance inhering in its parts, perceived as one; otherwise nothing could be perceived (atoms being imperceptible) and a thing could not be held or pulled as one.
- **lin:pramana-buddhist**: No: only the parts (ultimately momentary particulars) are real; the 'whole' is a conceptual construction over an aggregate.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: the Jain distinction of a standpoint of the enduring whole (dravyārthika) from one of parts and modes (paryāyārthika) would treat the two as partial views.
  - P1-level: Buddhist two-truth doctrine allows wholes conventional (not ultimate) reality.
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-413 — May (or should) a widow follow her husband onto the pyre?  
`dsp:widow-anvarohana` · sourced

- **lin:dharmasastra**: The widow's dharma is lifelong chastity and restraint (MDh 5.156–160); Medhātithi (on Manu) holds that following the husband in death is self-destruction, not dharma, comparable to a rite performed to harm.
- **lin:dharmasastra**: It is permitted as an option: chastity or ascending the pyre (Viṣṇusmṛti 25.14); the Mitākṣarā (on YS 1.86) defends it as a meritorious optional act.
- Candidate readings:
  - Medhātithi's Mīmāṃsā argument that a rite motivated by desire and involving killing is not dharma
  - the Mitākṣarā's reading as an optional (kāmya) act
  - later kalivarjya-style prohibitions and the Śākta tantras' prohibition (outside this unit)
- Note: Not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U50-12 — Can women, and people of every birth (varṇa, jāti), attain liberation — and are they eligible for the scriptures, initiation and renunciation that lead to it?  
`dsp:women-caste-liberation` · skeleton

- **lin:dharmasastra**: The orthodox order: four classes with distinct duties; the śūdra is not twice-born and may not hear, study or perform the Veda — severe penalties are laid down for one who listens to it; a woman is guarded by father, husband and sons and is not independent; eligibility for the Vedic rite belongs to the twice-born, husband and wife performing jointly. The Veda is not within hearing of women and śūdras — hence the Bhārata was composed for them.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Śūdras are not eligible for the knowledge that comes through Vedic study (BS 1.3.34-38); but Śaṅkara grants that those like Vidura and Dharmavyādha attained knowledge through the impressions of former lives, that the fruit of knowledge once attained cannot be withheld, and that all four classes may hear the Itihāsa and Purāṇa. In the Manīṣāpañcaka, whoever has realized the one consciousness is the teacher, be he caṇḍāla or brāhmaṇa.
- **lin:upanisadic**: Maitreyī was a discusser of Brahman; Satyakāma, who did not know his father, was accepted as a brāhmaṇa for his truthfulness; Jānaśruti, addressed as 'śūdra', was taught by Raikva.
- **lin:digambara**: Women cannot attain liberation in a female body: they cannot give up clothing and so cannot be without possessions; they lack the strongest bodily constitution needed for the highest pure meditation; as they cannot fall to the lowest hell they cannot rise to the highest state; the scripture's 'female siddhas' means psychological, not physical, sex (Prabhācandra); subtle living beings arise in their bodies (Aṣṭapāhuḍa). The monk's sign is 'like the newborn' — naked.
- **lin:svetambara**: Women can attain liberation: the nineteenth Tīrthaṅkara, Mallī, was a woman; the liberated include those liberated in a female form (Prajñāpanā's fifteen kinds); nuns keep their robes by monastic law.
- **lin:yapaniya**: Śākaṭāyana: there is liberation for women as for men, since its cause — the three jewels — is present in them; a woman is not a non-soul, not incapable of liberation, not opposed to right faith, not non-human.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: Women who go forth are capable of the fruits up to arahantship (the Buddha to Ānanda), though it is impossible that a woman be a fully awakened Buddha; Mahāpajāpatī's ordination came with eight principles of respect. Somā: what does womanhood matter when the mind is concentrated? Class is not by birth: among humans there is no difference of species; one is a brāhmaṇa by deeds.
- **lin:mahayana**: The nāga king's eight-year-old daughter attains buddhahood (the Lotus Sūtra, in which she turns into a man); the goddess in Vimalakīrti's house shows Śāriputra that 'woman' has no fixed mark; Amitābha's thirty-fifth vow; in Zen Dōgen urges bowing to anyone who has attained the Way, man or woman.
- **lin:epic-teaching**: The Gītā: those who take refuge in me, even of sinful birth — women, vaiśyas and śūdras — reach the highest goal; the learned see the same in a brāhmaṇa, a cow, an elephant, a dog and a dog-eater.
- **lin:puranic**: Devotion knows no distinction of caste, learning, beauty, family or wealth; those of every birth down to the lowest are qualified for it; a dog-cooker who has offered all to the Lord is better than a learned brāhmaṇa without devotion; Rāma tells Śabarī that neither sex, caste, name nor stage of life is the cause of worship.
- **lin:alvar**: The Lord's devotees, even the lowest of the low below the four castes, are to be honoured as he is; the pāṇar Tiruppāṇ Āḻvār was carried into Śrīraṅgam (the tradition's account).
- **lin:virasaiva**: Wherever devotion to Śiva is strong, low or high, that one is a devotee; among those joined in the one contemplation of Śiva there is no brāhmaṇa or outcaste. Dāsimayya: they call it woman if they see breasts and long hair, man if beard and whiskers — but the self in between is neither.
- **lin:sant**: No one is low by birth; the mud of low deeds makes one low (Ravidās); do not ask a sādhu's caste — ask his knowledge (Kabīr). (The same Kabīr corpus also contains sayings warning against women as a snare — recorded, not harmonised.)
- **lin:tamil-siddha**: What makes a woman a Paṟaicci or a brāhmaṇī — is there any difference in flesh, skin or bone? (Civavākkiyar); learning, caste distinctions and ritual observances are to be burnt away (Pāmpāṭṭi); mankind is one family and God is one (Tirumantiram).
- **lin:kaula**: When the Bhairavī circle has begun, all castes are twice-born; when it is over they are separate again; no caste distinction is to be made in the consecrated food of the supreme Self.
- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: The Kiraṇa, asked whether initiation should follow the ranking of the classes: for women and others incapable of the observances, the guru purifies the observances away and their liberation is through the yoga of devotion in the Śaiva scripture; for the capable the observances remain and knowledge is revealed to them.
- **lin:hatha-yoga**: Young, old or very old, sick or weak — whoever is diligent attains success in yoga through practice; women and śūdras too may practise breath-control, with a non-Vedic mantra (Yoga-Yājñavalkya).
- Candidate readings:
  - P4-stage (adhikāra): eligibility for particular disciplines (Vedic study, the naked vow) differs by birth or body while liberation itself does not — the orthodox and Digambara form; rejected by the bhakti, Sant, Vīraśaiva and Siddha critics as a restatement of birth-rank, and by the Digambaras as to liberation itself.
  - P2-standpoint: from the standpoint of the self (neither male nor female — Dāsimayya; the Advaita witness) no distinction; from the standpoint of the body and social order, distinctions — each side accepts only its own half.
  - P3-path: devotion open to all (NBS 72; BhG 9.32) as a distinct path from Vedic knowledge — accepted by many Vedāntins (Rāmānuja on 9.32) while retaining Vedic restrictions; rejected by the Sants as insufficient.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the orthodox Vedic order restricts eligibility for Vedic study and rite by birth and sex, the Digambaras deny liberation in a female body, while the Śvetāmbaras and Yāpanīyas, the Buddhists, the bhakti, Vīraśaiva, Sant and Siddha voices and the Kaula tantras open liberation (and in varying degrees initiation) to all.

## RQ-415 — Can a woman, in a woman's body, attain buddhahood? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:womens-bodies-and-buddhahood` · skeleton

- **lin:mahayana**: Womanhood is empty like all dharmas: dharmas are neither male nor female (the goddess in Vimalakīrti); Queen Śrīmālā teaches the one vehicle and is predicted to buddhahood; the goddess of the Ganges is predicted; the nāga princess attains buddhahood in an instant.
- **lin:mahayana**: A woman's body cannot be a buddha: Śāriputra objects so in the Lotus, and the nāga princess becomes male before appearing as a buddha; Amitābha's and Bhaiṣajyaguru's vows promise women release from the woman's state; the Pali Bahudhātuka Sutta (MN 115) says a woman cannot be a fully awakened buddha.
- Note: The Vimalakīrti itself places the question on two levels: ultimately dharmas are neither male nor female; conventionally bodies and their karmic conditions are spoken of.

## RQ-U50-09 — Is liberation won by ritual works, by knowledge alone, by knowledge combined with works, by devotion, or by the Lord's grace — and does grace wait on the soul's surrender (the 'monkey') or carry it without cause (the 'cat')? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:works-knowledge-grace` · skeleton

- **lin:mimamsa**: Dharma, known only from the Veda, yields its fruit; liberation is not enjoined as a fruit of ritual knowledge. For one who knows the self, when earlier actions are exhausted by experience and no new ones arise — by avoiding desire-prompted and forbidden acts and performing the obligatory ones — there is no further embodiment (Kumārila).
