The self is subsidiary to the rite as its agent (the prior view in BS 3.4.2).
- **lin:prabhakara-mimamsa**: One troubled by the sorrows of transmigration, not longing even for the pleasures mixed with them, turns from prohibited and desire-prompted acts, exhausts the merit and demerit already produced by experiencing them, and by self-knowledge supported by calm, restraint and the like destroys the store of karma and is released.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Knowledge alone liberates: liberation is not something produced, reached, modified or purified by action but the eternal nature of the self; knowledge of an existing thing depends on the thing, not on the person's will; action is not opposed to ignorance and cannot remove it. Works done without desire purify the mind and produce the desire to know (sacrifice, gifts and austerity, BĀU 4.4.22) and are then renounced; knowledge cannot be combined with works, since works require the notions of agent, act and fruit that knowledge removes.
- **lin:bhedabheda**: Bhāskara (and before him Bhartṛprapañca): knowledge must be combined with the lifelong performance of prescribed works (jñāna-karma-samuccaya); 'then' in 'then the inquiry into Brahman' means after the inquiry into ritual; release does not come while the body lasts.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: Liberating knowledge is not the understanding of a sentence but meditation — continuous, loving remembrance (bhakti) — to which works and knowledge are ancillary; the inquiry into Brahman follows the inquiry into ritual. Or, for those unable, the Lord's own words 'abandoning all dharmas, take refuge in me alone' teach surrender (prapatti).
- **lin:vadakalai**: Vedānta Deśika: surrender (prapatti) is an independent means the soul performs once, with its limbs, and is the occasion of the Lord's grace — as the young monkey clings to its mother (the 'monkey' analogy, markaṭa-nyāya).
- **lin:tenkalai**: Piḷḷai Lokācārya: the Lord alone is the means; surrender is not a means but the acceptance befitting the soul's dependence; his grace is without cause — as the mother cat carries the kitten (the 'cat' analogy, mārjāra-nyāya).
- **lin:dvaita**: Without Nārāyaṇa's grace there is no liberation, and without knowledge no abundant grace: grace won by ritual action is the lowest, by hearing and reflection middling, by direct vision the highest; liberation is won by his pleasure alone, with love preceded by knowledge of his greatness.
- **lin:puranic**: Devotion is greater than action, knowledge and yoga, being of the nature of the fruit; neither yoga, Sāṃkhya, dharma, study, austerity nor renunciation wins the Lord as strong devotion does; devotion is not an act, since like knowledge it does not depend on effort.
- **lin:epic-teaching**: The Gītā: a twofold commitment was declared of old — the yoga of knowledge for the Sāṃkhyas, the yoga of action for the yogins; renunciation and the yoga of action both bring the highest good; Janaka reached perfection by action; the sacrifice of knowledge is better than material sacrifice; not by the Vedas, austerity, gifts or sacrifice but by exclusive devotion can the Lord be seen; performing all actions, taking refuge in him, one attains by his grace; 'abandoning all dharmas, take refuge in me alone'.
- **lin:upanisadic**: The Upaniṣads cited by every side: the self is not attained by instruction, intellect or much learning but by the one whom it chooses; sacrifices are unsafe boats; those who worship ignorance (ritual) enter darkness, and greater darkness those delighting in knowledge alone — by both one crosses death; brāhmaṇas seek to know the self by Veda-study, sacrifice, gifts and austerity.
- **lin:kashmir-saivism**: Ignorance — not mere absence of knowledge but incomplete manifestation — is the cause of transmigration and knowledge the sole cause of liberation; the descent of power (śaktipāta) depends on the Lord's freedom alone, not on a balance of karma, and is intense, middling or mild; initiation leading to nirvāṇa happens for the one who knows this, without oblation.
- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: Bondage by āṇava-mala is a real substance that knowledge alone cannot remove; when the two deeds are balanced and mala has ripened, grace descends and the guru, as Śiva, removes the bonds in initiation — the rite liberates by Śiva's power.
- **lin:jainism**: Right view, right knowledge and right conduct together are the path to liberation; knowledge without action is lost and the action of the ignorant is lost. No creator's grace is admitted: the soul frees itself.
- **lin:pure-land**: In an age without a buddha one cannot reach non-retrogression by self-power; birth in the Pure Land is by the power of Amitābha's vow (other-power); Shinran: even the good are born there, how much more the evil — shinjin and the nembutsu are themselves given.
- Candidate readings:
  - P8-six-marks: the Gītā's purport by opening and close — knowledge (Śaṅkara, from 2.11 and 18.66 as renunciation), devotion (Rāmānuja, Madhusūdana's closing verse), action (recent readings) — see dsp:gita-primary-teaching.
  - P7-arthavada: praise of works, of knowledge or of devotion as 'greater than all others' read as praise rather than exclusive doctrine — each school reads the others' texts so and rejects that reading of its own.
  - P1-level: works valid at the empirical level, knowledge alone at the ultimate — Advaita's own form, rejected by Bhāskara and by the bhakti schools.
  - cat and monkey (P2-standpoint): from the Lord's side grace is causeless, from the soul's side surrender is its fitting response — offered within Śrīvaiṣṇavism but not accepted as dissolving the eighteen differences.
- Note: Reconciled in part by the texts' own ordering: works purify the mind and produce the desire to know (P4-stage — Advaita, Bhāmatī), the Gītā's two commitments for two kinds of people and the Uddhava Gītā's three yogas for different temperaments (P3-path); whether knowledge alone or knowledge with works finally liberates, whether devotion is a means or the goal beyond liberation, and whether grace takes the soul's surrender as its condition remain not yet reconciled.

## RQ-U50-03 — Is the world real, or an appearance (mithyā) that is neither real nor unreal, superimposed on the one reality — and is māyā an indeterminable ignorance or the Lord's real power?  
`dsp:world-real-or-appearance` · skeleton

- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: The world is mithyā: not real, since it is sublated by the knowledge of Brahman; not unreal, since it appears; indeterminable as either — like the silver seen in shell. Duality is mere māyā; non-duality is the ultimate truth. Three orders are distinguished: the ultimate (Brahman), the empirical (the world, real for all practical purposes — Śaṅkara defends external objects against the Buddhist idealist), and the illusory (dream, rope-snake). Falsity is defended under five definitions against the Dvaita critique.
- **lin:visistadvaita**: The world of souls and matter is real, the body of Brahman. The Advaitin's avidyā is untenable in seven ways — no locus, no power to conceal self-luminous Brahman, no coherent nature, no sense to 'indescribable', no means of knowledge, no remover, no removal; no means of knowledge establishes an undifferentiated reality; consciousness is always someone's and of something.
- **lin:dvaita**: The world is real: the witness (sākṣin) establishes it as unsublated; what is sublated produces no effect; even dream-objects are real products of impressions. None of the five Advaita definitions of falsity is coherent, and the inferences from 'perceivable', 'insentient' and 'limited' fail. Reality is twofold: the independent (Viṣṇu) and the dependent (souls and matter) — dependent, but real.
- **lin:bhedabheda**: The effect is real and both different and non-different from its cause; those who expound māyā lead people astray (Bhāskara).
- **lin:gaudiya-vaisnava**: The world is a real transformation of the Lord's external power; the souls are his marginal power, distinct yet dependent. To call the world false and the Lord's lordship imagined is the māyāvāda that the Gītā (16.8) describes and the Padma Purāṇa calls 'Buddhism in disguise'.
- **lin:kashmir-saivism**: The universe is the real self-manifestation of free consciousness on its own screen; māyā is the Lord's power of self-concealment (Māyā-śakti), his supreme freedom — not an indeterminable ignorance. Those who say 'in the beginning this was being' stand at the level of the Īśvara-tattva, and some knowers of the Upaniṣads take prāṇa as the self: partial roles of the one consciousness. (The Vijñāna Bhairava nevertheless teaches contemplating the universe as a magic show as one means to peace.)
- **lin:sakta**: Māyā is the Goddess's own power; she is the manifest and the unmanifest; her reality is beyond both duality and non-duality (see dsp:maya-sakti-or-avidya).
- **lin:saiva-siddhanta**: The world is real, produced from real māyā by Śiva as efficient cause; 'advaita' in the Śivajñānabodha means non-separateness, not numerical identity; the Advaita and other views are set out and refuted.
- **lin:samkhya**: The world is real, being produced by faultless causes and never sublated; bondage cannot be due to an unreal avidyā — and a real avidyā would abandon non-dualism.
- **lin:jainism**: Absolute non-duality is refuted: if all were one there would be no distinction of act and fruit, merit and demerit, this world and the next, knowledge and ignorance, bondage and release.
- **lin:nyaya**: The idealist and illusionist thesis lacks a reason: the notion of dream-objects depends on prior waking experience, as memory does; false cognition presupposes a real object mistaken.
- **lin:madhyamaka**: All dharmas, the fruits of the path and buddhahood itself are like an illusion, like a dream — appearing conventionally, empty of own-nature; form is emptiness and emptiness form (the Prajñāpāramitā background shared by the Mahāyāna schools).
- Candidate readings:
  - P1-level (Advaita's own): the world is real at the empirical level and false only from the standpoint of Brahman — rejected by Viśiṣṭādvaita and Dvaita, for whom whatever is, is real, and the empirical/ultimate distinction is not scriptural.
  - P2-standpoint: from the side of the one consciousness there is no second, from the side of the embodied soul the world is real — rejected by Madhva and Rāmānuja, who hold the world real from every standpoint, and by Advaita if it implies that the world's reality is ultimate.
  - P4-stage: Kṣemarāja ranks the Vedāntins' 'being' at the Īśvara-tattva, and the Vijñāna Bhairava prescribes seeing the world as a magic show as a practice (VBT 102, 133) — a practice-level use of the 'appearance' view that does not settle its truth.
  - vivarta vs ābhāsa (Advaita vs Kashmir Śaivism): both deny that the one consciousness changes; they differ whether its appearing is real self-display or indeterminable — kept apart by both.
- Note: Not yet reconciled: Advaita's own levels of reality make the world real empirically and false ultimately, but Viśiṣṭādvaita, Dvaita, Śaiva Siddhānta and Kashmir Śaivism reject the very distinction as applied to the world, each for its own reasons.

## RQ-418 — Did Pātañjala Yoga take its shared meditative and ethical vocabulary (the four attitudes, the five faculties, the afflictions, samāpatti, dharmamegha) from Buddhism, or do Yoga, Buddhism and Jainism draw on a common śramaṇa milieu?  
`dsp:yoga-buddhism-shared-milieu` · skeleton

- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Yoga is an ancient teaching (the tradition names Hiraṇyagarbha as its first teacher and Patañjali as its compiler); the Yoga text marks its difference from the Buddhists, rejecting the view that objects are constructions of a single mind; no dependence on Buddhism is acknowledged.
- **lin:early-buddhism**: The suttas themselves acknowledge that other wanderers taught the abandoning of the hindrances and the four immeasurables, and that the bodhisatta learned the formless attainments from other teachers; the Buddha's distinctiveness lies in knowing where these culminate and in the liberating insight beyond them.
- **lin:jainism**: The five great vows and the four reflections belong to the Jina's teaching; Haribhadra treats the yoga taught by others as one path of qualities under different names, correlating his eight views with the limbs beginning with yama.
- Candidate readings:
  - Common milieu: the object-class form of the four attitudes (YS 1.33 = TS 7.11, unlike the Pali formula) and the shared five vows suggest a common śramaṇa stock rather than one-way borrowing.
  - Specific borrowing: technical terms of Buddhist Abhidharma (anuśaya, the burnt seed, dharmamegha as a ground-name) may have been taken over by the Yoga authors (scholarly hypothesis).
  - P2-standpoint (doctrinal content only): the same practices described from different standpoints — boundless pervasion (Buddhist) versus attitudes suited to classes of beings (Yoga, Jain).
- Note: Not yet reconciled: a historical question on which the traditions' own accounts and the scholarly hypotheses differ; no principle settles history.

## RQ-419 — What place, if any, has yogic discipline (haṭha, aṣṭāṅga, kuṇḍalinī) on the Sant path of the Name? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:yoga-in-the-sant-path` · skeleton

- **lin:sant**: None as a means: sahaj is best — no closing of eyes, stopping of ears or mortification; the yogī dyes his clothes, not his mind; the Name and love suffice.
- **lin:dadu-panth**: Sundardās teaches aṣṭāṅga and haṭha yoga (postures, breath, cakras, the serpent power) as a stage within the Sant path, below devotion and knowledge.
- **lin:charandasi**: Caraṇdās teaches the eight limbs of yoga to his disciples as a support of devotion to Kṛṣṇa and the guru.
- **lin:sant-mat**: Only the yoga of the sound current, beginning at the eye centre, is needed; haṭha and kuṇḍalinī work on the lower centres is unnecessary and hazardous; surat-śabd yoga is the crown of yogas.
- Note: Sundardās and Caraṇdās themselves place yoga as a preparatory stage (P4) under the Name and devotion; temperaments differ (P3).

## RQ-420 — Is knowledge alone sufficient for liberation, or must bodily yoga accompany it? (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:yoga-or-knowledge-for-liberation` · sourced

- **lin:hatha-yoga**: Yoga and knowledge together: knowledge without yoga cannot liberate, and the mere knower is reborn until his body is 'ripened' by yoga.
- **lin:advaita-vedanta**: Knowledge alone gives kaivalya; bodily yoga is for the unawakened; realization is not samādhi or yogic attainment but oneness with Brahman.
- Note: The Śāṇḍilya names two ways of ending mind — yoga and knowledge — suited to different people, and the Varāha's bird and ant paths both reach Brahman.

## RQ-421 — Is Īśvara to be admitted, and is devotion to him a means to liberation? (Yoga against nirīśvara Sāṃkhya) (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:yoga-samkhya-isvara` · skeleton

- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: Īśvara is a special puruṣa, ever free, unsurpassed in knowledge, the teacher of the ancients, designated by Oṃ; devotion to him brings samādhi near.
- **lin:samkhya**: Liberation comes from discriminating knowledge of puruṣa and prakṛti; Īśvara is not established by any means of knowledge and is not needed to explain the working of prakṛti.
- Note: The Gītā (5.4–5) and the Mokṣadharma treat Sāṃkhya and Yoga as two approaches to one goal (P3), and Vijñānabhikṣu reads the Sāṃkhya denial of Īśvara as a provisional, concessive argument (P5).

## RQ-422 — Is cognition (the dependent nature) real, or is it empty of own-being like everything else? — Yogācāra and Madhyamaka (partially reconciled — the unreconciled remainder)  
`dsp:yogacara-madhyamaka` · skeleton

- **lin:yogacara**: The dependent nature — unreal imagination — exists; only the duality it projects is absent; emptiness is the absence of that duality and itself exists. Taken literally, 'all dharmas lack own-nature' is a provisional teaching (second turning); its intended meaning is the three naturelessnesses. To deny the dependent and the perfected as well is the worst nihilism.
- **lin:madhyamaka**: Cognition is as empty of own-being as its objects; if the dependent existed by own-being it could not originate dependently. The ālaya and cognition-only were taught provisionally for those who fear emptiness; reflexive awareness is incoherent, as a sword cannot cut itself.
- **lin:yogacara-madhyamaka**: Mind-only is correct conventionally and is the step by which external objects are negated; ultimately mind too is neither one nor many and so without own-being; the Laṅkāvatāra's verses show the sequence.
- **lin:alikakaravada**: Image-free luminous awareness is the ultimate meaning of both Yogācāra and Madhyamaka; the images alone are false.
- Note: P4/P1: the Laṅkāvatāra's own four-stage sequence and Yogācāra-Madhyamaka treat mind-only as a stage and a conventional truth on the way to the imageless; this reconciles the two as successive levels for those who accept that sequence.

## RQ-423 — Is the mind self-luminous (illuminating itself and its objects), or is it seen (an object of the seer)?  
`dsp:ys-citta-self-luminous-or-seen` · sourced

- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: The mind is not self-luminous because it is seen; it is grasped by puruṣa, whose consciousness, not passing over, takes the form of the intellect's modification; a mind grasped by another mind leads to regress and confusion of memory.
- **None**: The mind itself is self-luminous and illuminates its objects like fire (4.19 objection); in one moment the becoming is itself the action and the agent, so self and other are grasped together (4.20, 'kṣaṇika-vādin'); one mind may be grasped by the next (4.21 objection).
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the opposing views are known here only through Vyāsa's report and answer.

## RQ-424 — Is the object of cognition independent of any mind, or only a construction of cognition?  
`dsp:ys-object-independent-of-mind` · sourced

- **lin:patanjala-yoga**: The object is one, common to many minds, stands on itself, is not imagined by one or many minds, and is independent of them; minds differ though the object is the same, so the paths of object and cognition are separate; the object is common to all puruṣas and the minds work separately in each.
- **None**: The thing is a mere conceptual construction of cognition like a dream object (4.14); or the object arises together with cognition, being enjoyable like pleasure (4.15); or all this is mind only and there are no cows or pots (4.23).
- Note: Not yet reconciled: the opposing views are known here only through Vyāsa's report; the text names no school.

## RQ-U32-yuga-quarters — Are the four quarters of the yuga equal, or in the proportion 4:3:2:1?  
`dsp:yuga-quarters` · skeleton

- **lin:jyotisa**: Āryabhaṭa: the four quarters of the yuga are equal; the kalpa has 14 Manus of 72 yugas; the yuga has an ascending and a descending half.
- **lin:jyotisa**: Brahmagupta and the Sūrya Siddhānta: Kṛta, Tretā, Dvāpara and Kali are four, three, two and one tenths of the yuga, following the feet of dharma; 'not one of Āryabhaṭa's quarters agrees with the smṛti'.
- Candidate readings:
  - P2-standpoint: Āryabhaṭa's equal quarters serve computation (gaṇita) while the 4:3:2:1 scheme expresses the decline of dharma (smṛti) — but Brahmagupta explicitly rejects this separation.
  - Brahmagupta's reading: Āryabhaṭa simply erred against scripture.
  - Recent: Sri Yukteswar's 24,000-year cycle (The Holy Science, 1894) departs from both (recent: true).
- Note: Not yet reconciled.
